## A Primary-Source-Aligned Textbook and Study System

**Based on the official NASAA _Uniform Investment Adviser Law Examination (Series 65) Overview_, dated September 1, 2023**

**Legal and tax currency date:** August 11, 2026
**Edition:** Independent educational edition

---

## Important educational and professional disclaimer

> **READ BEFORE USING THIS BOOK**
>
> This book is an independent educational resource. It is not sponsored, endorsed, approved, reviewed, or produced by the North American Securities Administrators Association (NASAA), FINRA, the SEC, any state securities regulator, any testing vendor, or any other governmental or self-regulatory organization.
>
> **No guarantee.** Studying this book does not guarantee that any reader will pass the Series 65 examination, qualify for registration, obtain or retain a license, satisfy an employer, or achieve any professional or financial result. Examination specifications, laws, rules, interpretations, fees, procedures, and effective dates can change without notice. Always check the current official sources before acting.
>
> **No legal, tax, investment, accounting, compliance, or registration advice.** Nothing here is individualized advice or a substitute for counsel from a qualified attorney, tax professional, accountant, compliance professional, investment professional, sponsoring firm, or securities regulator. Examples are simplified and are not recommendations to buy, sell, hold, or use any security, product, account, strategy, adviser, or service. Tax consequences depend on facts and law; registration duties vary by jurisdiction.
>
> **Passing is not permission to practice.** A passing examination result is ordinarily only one component of state investment-adviser-representative registration. It does not itself confer registration, licensure, an exemption, or authority to transact securities or advisory business in any state.

## Source, scope, and copyright-respect note

This book follows the scope and four-domain weighting in NASAA’s official [Series 65 Examination Overview/Study Guide (September 1, 2023)](https://www.nasaa.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/NASAA-Series-65-Exam-Study-Guide.pdf), together with NASAA’s [Series 65 content outline effective June 12, 2023](https://www.nasaa.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/02/Series-65-Outline-June-2023.pdf). Current administrative facts should be checked against the [NASAA Exam FAQs](https://www.nasaa.org/exams/exam-faqs/), [FINRA’s Series 65 page](https://www.finra.org/registration-exams-ce/qualification-exams/series65), and [FINRA’s exam-enrollment page](https://www.finra.org/registration-exams-ce/qualification-exams/enroll).

NASAA examination questions are protected content. This book contains **no copied, recalled, reconstructed, solicited, or purportedly “real” examination questions**. Its practice items are newly written to teach public concepts. The objective checklist below identifies public testable topics so readers can audit coverage; it does not disclose examination content. Do not seek, share, memorize, reproduce, or distribute live examination questions.

## Exam at a glance

| Feature | Official structure |
|---|---:|
| Questions presented | **140** multiple-choice questions |
| Questions scored | **130** |
| Unscored pretest questions | **10**, unidentified and mixed throughout |
| Testing time | **180 minutes** |
| Passing criterion | **At least 92 correct of the 130 scored questions** |
| Domain I: Economic Factors and Business Information | **20 scored questions (15%)** |
| Domain II: Investment Vehicle Characteristics | **32 scored questions (25%)** |
| Domain III: Client Investment Recommendations and Strategies | **39 scored questions (30%)** |
| Domain IV: Laws, Regulations, and Guidelines, Including Prohibition on Unethical Business Practices | **39 scored questions (30%)** |

The examination is closed book. Because the ten pretest questions are not identified, treat every item as potentially scored. A straight-line pace is about **77 seconds per presented question** ($180\text{ minutes}/140$), leaving little time to research a single difficult item from memory.

### Why the passing score is not “70%”

NASAA’s current FAQ expressly describes the Series 65 threshold as a **raw criterion**, not a percentage: the candidate must answer **92 of the 130 scored questions correctly**. Although $92/130 \approx 70.77\%$, rounding that relationship to “70%” is misleading. For example, 91 correct answers equals exactly 70% of the scored questions and does **not** meet the 92-correct criterion. Nor should a candidate divide by all 140 presented questions, because ten are unscored and unidentified. Prepare to clear 92 scored answers, with a margin for uncertainty, rather than aiming at a rounded percentage.

## Enrollment, testing window, and registration mechanics

1. **Firm-associated candidates.** A firm with the appropriate CRD access generally requests the examination through an electronic **Form U4**. Consult the firm before independently enrolling.
2. **Independent candidates.** A person who is not U4-registered through an eligible firm may open a NASAA exam enrollment through FINRA’s **Test Enrollment Services System (TESS)** via the [official enrollment page](https://www.finra.org/registration-exams-ce/qualification-exams/enroll). Series 65 enrollment itself does not require sponsorship.
3. **Window.** Once processed, FINRA opens a **120-calendar-day window** in which the candidate must both schedule **and take** the exam. One enrollment generally provides one attempt. An expired window or another attempt generally requires a new enrollment and fee.
4. **Fee.** Fees can change. Verify the live amount and payment terms on [FINRA’s current Series 65 page](https://www.finra.org/registration-exams-ce/qualification-exams/series65) or the enrollment workflow rather than relying on a printed dollar figure in this book.
5. **Result versus registration.** A pass does not submit an application, resolve disclosure issues, create an exemption, or authorize business. Confirm the state-specific application, fee, qualification, notice, and continuing obligations with the relevant regulator through NASAA’s [regulator directory](https://www.nasaa.org/contact-your-regulator/).

## Retake waiting periods: dated transition

The waiting period is keyed to unsuccessful attempts on the **specific examination**. A new attempt requires a new eligible enrollment and applicable fee.

### Applicable enrollments opened through January 3, 2027: 30 / 30 / 180

- After the **first** failed attempt: wait at least **30 calendar days**.
- After the **second** failed attempt: wait at least **30 calendar days**.
- After the **third and each subsequent** failed attempt: wait at least **180 calendar days**.

### Applicable enrollments opened on or after January 4, 2027: 30 / 30 / 60

NASAA announced on August 10, 2026 that, effective **January 4, 2027**, the wait after a third or later unsuccessful attempt will fall from 180 to **60 calendar days**; the first two waits remain **30 days** each. NASAA’s transition guidance states that windows opened before January 4, 2027 remain subject to the rules under which they were opened; the shorter wait is not retroactively applied to an existing window. Check the [NASAA announcement](https://www.nasaa.org/82113/nasaa-approves-updated-waiting-period-for-series-63-65-and-66-exam-retests/?qoid=current-headlines) and [current FAQ](https://www.nasaa.org/exams/exam-faqs/) before re-enrolling.

## How to use this book

1. **Start with the checklist.** Mark each objective `New`, `Learning`, or `Ready`; do not confuse recognition with recall.
2. **Take a short diagnostic.** Use original questions without notes. Sort misses by domain and by cause: knowledge gap, rule exception, calculation, reading error, or time pressure.
3. **Study in weighted order.** Give roughly 60% of content time to Domains III and IV, 25% to Domain II, and 15% to Domain I, while repairing personal weaknesses.
4. **Use active retrieval.** After each topic, close the text and state the definition, rule, exception, risk, tax feature, and client use case from memory.
5. **Contrast near-neighbors.** Build small comparison tables: adviser versus IAR; broker-dealer versus agent; exempt security versus exempt transaction; open-end versus closed-end fund; time-weighted versus dollar-weighted return.
6. **Maintain an error log.** For every miss, write the governing principle and why each tempting distractor fails. Re-test the concept after one day, three days, and one week.
7. **Check current law at the source.** Treat statutes, rules, tax limits, retirement ages, fees, and administrative procedures as date-sensitive.
8. **Use the final practice exam as a simulation.** Attempt all 60 questions in about **77 minutes**, without notes, then review every rationale—including correct guesses.
9. **Finish with weak-area retrieval.** In the last 48 hours, avoid indiscriminate rereading. Rehearse the formula sheet, registration/exemption maps, fiduciary duties, and the error log; protect sleep and logistics.

## Six-week study plan

Assumption: six study days per week, about 90–150 minutes on weekdays and one longer weekend session.

| Week | Primary work | Retrieval and measurement |
|---|---|---|
| **1 — Foundations and diagnostic** | Read exam rules; take a diagnostic; cover Domain I: economics, financial reporting, analytical methods, and risk. Begin formulas. | Two 20-question timed sets; create the error log; explain rate/bond relationships aloud. |
| **2 — Investment vehicles I** | Domain II cash, fixed income, equity, valuation, offerings, and pooled products. | Build product comparison grids; complete two mixed 25-question sets; revisit Week 1 misses. |
| **3 — Investment vehicles II and client profiling** | Finish derivatives, alternatives, insurance, commodities, and digital assets. Begin Domain III client types, profiles, capital-market theory, and portfolio methods. | Match products to client constraints; one 40-question mixed set; spaced review of formulas. |
| **4 — Recommendations, tax, and accounts** | Finish Domain III: tax concepts, retirement/ERISA, special accounts, estate techniques, trading, and performance. | Complete client-case drills; calculate returns/yields; one 60-question timed mixed set. |
| **5 — Law, registration, and ethics** | Domain IV: advisers/IARs, broker-dealers/agents, securities/issuers, remedies, communications, fiduciary obligations, custody, conflicts, cybersecurity, and continuity. | Draw registration and exemption decision trees; do three 30-question law/ethics sets; source-check changing rules. |
| **6 — Integration and exam readiness** | Review all four domains by weight; take the 60-question final simulation early in the week; repair only demonstrated gaps. | Two additional full-length or proportional simulations from a reputable independent source; target stable performance above the minimum with time to spare; taper in final 24 hours. |

## Three-week accelerated study plan

Assumption: six study days per week, about three to five focused hours per day. Split long sessions into 50-minute blocks.

| Week | Days 1–3 | Days 4–6 | Weekly checkpoint |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | Diagnostic plus all Domain I; start fixed income and equity. | Finish Domain II, emphasizing characteristics, valuation, risks, costs, and tax treatment. | One 60-question weighted set; remediate every miss before moving on. |
| **2** | Client profile, theory, allocation, strategies, and tax. | Retirement/ERISA, special accounts, estate ownership, trading, and performance; begin adviser/IAR regulation. | One timed 90-question mixed set; reproduce formulas and client-profile workflow from memory. |
| **3** | Finish Domain IV and drill registration/exemptions, communications, and fiduciary conduct. | Take the final 60-question simulation; repair gaps; complete one final proportional simulation; taper. | Require consistent timed performance with a buffer, not one lucky score. Final day: light retrieval, logistics, sleep. |

## Test-taking strategy

### Before answering

- Read the stem’s **last sentence first**, then the facts. Identify whether it asks for the best answer, an exception, a prohibition, a definition, or a calculation.
- Translate names and stories into roles: state adviser, federal covered adviser, IAR, broker-dealer, agent, issuer, client, or administrator.
- Mark controlling words: **must**, **may**, **except**, **not**, **best**, **most**, **least**, **state**, **federal**, **custody**, **discretion**, **exempt security**, and **exempt transaction**.

### Choosing an answer

- Predict the rule before reading choices when possible.
- Eliminate choices that answer a different legal role, confuse registration with antifraud coverage, guarantee a result, or ignore a stated client constraint.
- For client cases, move in this order: **facts → objectives/constraints → risk capacity and tolerance → time/liquidity/tax needs → suitable strategy/product → disclosure and monitoring**.
- For law questions, separate four questions: Who is the person? What is the instrument or transaction? Where did conduct occur? Is the issue registration, disclosure, fiduciary duty, or fraud?
- Do not invent unstated facts. Choose the best answer under the stated facts, not a merely possible answer under a hypothetical exception.

### Time management

- Target three passes: (1) answer clear items, (2) return to calculations and close calls, (3) check flagged items and accidental omissions.
- At roughly 77 seconds per item, move on when additional thought is no longer producing a rule or calculation. A flag preserves time; a blank cannot earn credit.
- Use scratch work for formulas, role labels, and elimination—not for rewriting the question.
- Answer every question unless the testing interface explicitly says otherwise. There is no benefit to leaving an item unanswered.
- Do not change an answer merely from anxiety. Change it when you identify a specific overlooked fact, rule, or arithmetic error.

## Compact formula sheet

### Financial statements and ratios

- **Working capital:** $\text{Current assets}-\text{Current liabilities}$
- **Current ratio:** $\text{Current assets}/\text{Current liabilities}$
- **Quick ratio:** $(\text{Cash}+\text{marketable securities}+\text{receivables})/\text{Current liabilities}$
- **Debt-to-equity:** $\text{Total debt (or liabilities, as defined)}/\text{shareholders' equity}$
- **Earnings per share:** $(\text{Net income}-\text{preferred dividends})/\text{weighted-average common shares}$
- **P/E:** $\text{Market price per share}/\text{EPS}$
- **P/B:** $\text{Market price per share}/\text{book value per share}$

### Time value and valuation

- **Future value:** $FV=PV(1+r)^n$
- **Present value:** $PV=FV/(1+r)^n$
- **Net present value:** $NPV=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\frac{CF_t}{(1+r)^t}-\text{initial outlay}$; accept a stand-alone project when NPV is positive, all else equal.
- **Internal rate of return:** the discount rate that makes $NPV=0$.
- **Constant-growth stock model:** $P_0=D_1/(k-g)$, where $k>g$.
- **Approximate bond price sensitivity:** $\%\Delta P\approx-\text{modified duration}\times\Delta y$.
- **Rate relationship:** market yields rise → existing fixed-rate bond prices generally fall; longer duration generally means greater sensitivity.

### Returns, yields, and tax

- **Holding-period return:** $(\text{income}+\text{ending value}-\text{beginning value})/\text{beginning value}$
- **Expected return:** $E(R)=\sum p_iR_i$
- **Exact real return:** $(1+R_n)/(1+\pi)-1$; approximation: nominal return minus inflation.
- **Current yield:** $\text{annual coupon dollars}/\text{bond market price}$
- **After-tax taxable yield:** $Y(1-t)$
- **Tax-equivalent yield of tax-exempt income:** $Y_{exempt}/(1-t)$
- **Linked time-weighted return:** $\prod(1+R_i)-1$; removes the effect of external cash-flow timing.
- **Dollar-weighted return:** the portfolio IRR; sensitive to the timing and size of client cash flows.

### Statistics and portfolio theory

- **Arithmetic mean:** $\sum x_i/n$; **range:** maximum minus minimum.
- **Standard deviation:** dispersion of returns around the mean; higher generally means more total variability.
- **Correlation:** from $-1$ to $+1$; lower correlation generally increases diversification benefit.
- **Beta:** systematic sensitivity relative to the market; market beta is 1.
- **CAPM required/expected return:** $R_f+\beta(R_m-R_f)$
- **Alpha:** actual return minus the CAPM-implied return, consistently measured.
- **Sharpe ratio:** $(R_p-R_f)/\sigma_p$; higher is better for excess return per unit of total risk.

### Options and leverage essentials

- **Long call breakeven at expiration:** strike price + premium; maximum loss is premium.
- **Long put breakeven at expiration:** strike price − premium; maximum loss is premium.
- **Covered-call maximum gain:** call premium + (strike − stock cost), when called away.
- **Leverage:** magnifies gains and losses; it does not create diversification or cap downside.

## Complete official objective coverage checklist

Use each box as a retrieval test. Mark it complete only when you can define the topic, compare it with its closest alternatives, identify major risks or legal consequences, and apply it to a short fact pattern. Parenthetical examples and authorities mirror the public September 1, 2023 guide’s scope; they are not live questions.

### Domain I — Economic Factors and Business Information (20 scored questions; 15%)

#### 1. Basic Economic Concepts

- [ ] Business cycles.
- [ ] Monetary and fiscal policies.
- [ ] Global and geopolitical factors, including currency valuation, exchange-rate effects on investment returns, and sovereign debt.
- [ ] Inflation and deflation.
- [ ] Interest rates, yield curves, and credit spreads.
- [ ] Economic indicators, including gross domestic product, employment indicators, trade deficit, balance of payments, and consumer price index.

#### 2. Financial Reporting

- [ ] Financial reports, including income statement, balance sheet, statement of cash flows, auditor disclosures, corporate SEC filings, and annual reports.
- [ ] Accounting fundamentals, including audited versus unaudited financials and cash versus accrual accounting.

#### 3. Analytical Methods

- [ ] Time-value-of-money concepts, including internal rate of return and net present value.
- [ ] Descriptive statistics, including mean/median/mode differences, ranges, standard deviation, alpha, beta, Sharpe ratios, and correlations.
- [ ] Financial ratios and their uses, including current, quick, and debt-to-equity ratios.

### Coverage attestation

The checklist above includes every numbered component and every bullet shown in the official September 1, 2023 Series 65 overview across all four domains. It is a scope audit, not a claim that merely checking boxes establishes competence. Laws, rules, model rules, tax provisions, and administrative procedures must be checked for later amendments and jurisdiction-specific adoption.

---

# Part I — Economic Factors and Business Information

## Purpose and exam weight

Domain 1 accounts for **20 scored questions, or 15% of the Series 65 examination**. It tests whether an investment adviser representative can connect four layers of analysis:

1. The economic environment;
2. A company’s financial reports;
3. Quantitative and valuation tools; and
4. The risks borne by investors and creditors.

These layers should not be studied as isolated vocabulary. A change in inflation can alter interest rates. Interest rates affect bond prices, corporate borrowing costs, valuation multiples, and project values. Those effects appear in financial statements and ultimately influence investor risk.

The practical objective is not to predict every recession, currency move, or security price. It is to reason from evidence, recognize uncertainty, and avoid conclusions that the data cannot support.

---

## Chapter 1 — Basic Economic Concepts

## 1.1 Business cycles

An economy does not grow at a constant rate. Economic activity tends to move through recurring—but irregular—phases called the **business cycle**.

### The four principal phases

| Phase | Typical conditions | Common policy or market concerns |
|---|---|---|
| Expansion | Rising production, income, employment, and spending | Inflation, excessive credit growth, asset-price speculation |
| Peak | Activity is high, but growth begins losing momentum | Tight capacity, high input costs, restrictive policy |
| Contraction | Falling or slowing output, weaker hiring, reduced spending | Defaults, unemployment, declining profits |
| Trough | Activity reaches a low point before recovery begins | Weak confidence, unused capacity, policy support |

A **recession** is a substantial, broad decline in economic activity. Although people often use “two consecutive quarters of falling real GDP” as a shorthand, recession identification is broader than a single mechanical rule.

A **depression** is an unusually deep and prolonged contraction. It is a descriptive term rather than a standard threshold used in ordinary securities analysis.

### Expansion is not the same as accelerating growth

Suppose real GDP grows as follows:

- Year 1: 2%
- Year 2: 4%
- Year 3: 1%

The economy still expanded in Year 3 because output rose by 1%. It merely expanded more slowly.

Similarly, if inflation falls from 7% to 3%, prices are generally still rising. The inflation rate has slowed, but the price level has not declined.

### Why cycles occur

No single mechanism explains every cycle. Important drivers include:

- Changes in consumer and business confidence;
- Credit availability and interest rates;
- Inventory accumulation or liquidation;
- Technological investment;
- Housing and construction;
- Fiscal or monetary policy;
- Commodity-price shocks;
- Financial crises;
- Wars, sanctions, elections, or regulatory changes; and
- Foreign demand and exchange rates.

### Market relationships are tendencies, not rules

Markets often anticipate economic changes. Stock prices may recover before reported GDP improves because investors expect future earnings to rebound. Unemployment may remain high after production begins recovering because employers wait for stronger demand before hiring.

Therefore:

> **Economic statistics usually describe the economy with a delay, while security prices reflect changing expectations about the future.**

### Memory aid

**E-P-C-T**:

- **E**xpansion
- **P**eak
- **C**ontraction
- **T**rough

After the trough, a new expansion begins.

### Likely exam traps

- Slower growth is not necessarily contraction.
- Disinflation is not deflation.
- A rising stock market does not prove that current economic conditions are strong.
- Unemployment often lags turning points because hiring and layoffs respond after business conditions change.
- Business cycles recur, but their length and severity are not fixed.

---

## 1.2 Monetary policy

**Monetary policy** concerns the supply, availability, and price of money and credit. In the United States, the Federal Reserve conducts monetary policy, with the Federal Open Market Committee playing the central role in setting the policy stance.

### Principal objectives

The Federal Reserve’s statutory objectives are commonly summarized as:

- Maximum employment;
- Stable prices; and
- Moderate long-term interest rates.

These objectives can conflict in the short run. Stimulus intended to reduce unemployment may increase inflationary pressure, while restrictive policy intended to contain inflation may weaken employment.

### Expansionary monetary policy

Expansionary policy seeks to make financial conditions easier. Possible actions include:

- Lowering the target range for short-term policy rates;
- Purchasing securities;
- Reducing certain administered rates;
- Encouraging bank reserve availability; and
- Using forward guidance to signal that policy may remain accommodative.

Typical intended effects include:

1. Lower short-term interest rates;
2. Greater willingness to lend and borrow;
3. More spending on homes, durable goods, and business investment;
4. Higher aggregate demand; and
5. Stronger output and employment.

These outcomes are not guaranteed. Borrowers may remain cautious, banks may tighten underwriting, or inflation expectations may rise.

### Contractionary monetary policy

Contractionary policy seeks to restrain demand and inflation. Possible actions include:

- Raising policy rates;
- Selling securities or allowing holdings to decline;
- Increasing certain administered rates; and
- Tightening reserve or credit conditions.

Typical effects include:

- Higher borrowing costs;
- Slower credit growth;
- Reduced interest-sensitive spending;
- Lower demand pressure; and
- Eventually, slower inflation.

### Traditional policy tools

Series 65 questions may use the traditional three-tool framework:

1. **Open-market operations**
   Purchases add reserves and tend to lower short-term rates; sales remove reserves and tend to raise them.

2. **Discount rate**
   The rate charged on certain loans from a Federal Reserve Bank to depository institutions. A lower rate is generally easier policy; a higher rate is generally tighter policy.

3. **Reserve requirements**
   The amount institutions must hold against specified deposits. A lower requirement can support lending; a higher requirement can restrain it. Changing reserve requirements is a blunt instrument and is not ordinarily the fine-tuning tool.

Modern implementation also relies on administered rates, including interest paid on reserve balances. For exam reasoning, focus on the direction of the effect rather than assuming every historical tool is used with equal frequency.

### Bond-market mechanics

When a central bank purchases securities, demand for those securities rises. All else equal:

- Bond prices rise; and
- Bond yields fall.

When it sells securities:

- Bond supply available to the public rises;
- Prices tend to fall; and
- Yields tend to rise.

This follows the fundamental inverse relationship between bond prices and yields.

### Policy transmission is neither immediate nor exact

Monetary policy operates through multiple channels:

- Bank lending;
- Bond yields;
- Mortgage and consumer-credit rates;
- Equity prices;
- Exchange rates;
- Expectations; and
- Household and business confidence.

The effects may take months or longer to become fully visible. Policy can also have unintended consequences, such as excessive leverage during prolonged easy conditions or recession risk during aggressive tightening.

### Likely exam distinction

- **Buying government securities** is expansionary.
- **Selling government securities** is contractionary.
- **Lowering rates** generally encourages borrowing and spending.
- **Raising rates** generally restrains borrowing and spending.

### Memory aid

The central bank can **buy to boost** and **sell to slow**.

---

## 1.3 Fiscal policy

**Fiscal policy** uses government spending, taxation, and borrowing to influence economic activity. Fiscal decisions are made through the legislative and executive processes rather than by the Federal Reserve.

### Expansionary fiscal policy

Examples include:

- Increasing government purchases;
- Increasing transfer payments;
- Cutting taxes; and
- Increasing targeted subsidies or credits.

The intended result is higher aggregate demand. The effect depends on how quickly funds are spent and how recipients respond.

### Contractionary fiscal policy

Examples include:

- Reducing government spending;
- Raising taxes; and
- Reducing transfers.

These actions tend to lower aggregate demand and may reduce inflation pressure, but they may also weaken growth and employment.

### Automatic stabilizers

Some fiscal mechanisms operate without a new law each time the economy changes. These are **automatic stabilizers**.

Examples include:

- Progressive tax collections falling when incomes decline; and
- Unemployment-related payments rising during contractions.

Automatic stabilizers soften fluctuations by supporting disposable income during downturns and withdrawing some spending power during expansions.

### Deficits and debt

A **budget deficit** occurs when government outlays exceed revenues during a period. A **budget surplus** occurs when revenues exceed outlays.

Government debt is the accumulation of past borrowing, adjusted for repayments and other financing activity. A deficit is a **flow** measured over time; debt is a **stock** measured at a point in time.

### Crowding out

If government borrowing increases substantially, it may compete with private borrowers for available capital. All else equal, this can place upward pressure on interest rates and reduce private investment. This possible effect is called **crowding out**.

Crowding out is not automatic. Its strength depends on factors such as:

- Economic slack;
- Private demand for credit;
- Central-bank policy;
- International capital flows; and
- Investor demand for government securities.

### Monetary versus fiscal policy

| Feature | Monetary policy | Fiscal policy |
|---|---|---|
| Main authority | Central bank | Legislative and executive branches |
| Main tools | Interest rates, reserves, securities operations | Spending, taxation, borrowing |
| Typical expansionary action | Lower rates or purchase securities | Increase spending or reduce taxes |
| Typical contractionary action | Raise rates or sell securities | Reduce spending or raise taxes |
| Common limitation | Uncertain lags and credit transmission | Political delays and implementation timing |

### Likely exam trap

Do not assign tax rates or government spending decisions to the Federal Reserve. Do not assign open-market operations to Congress.

---

## 1.4 Inflation, disinflation, and deflation

### Inflation

**Inflation** is a sustained rise in the general price level. As prices rise, each unit of currency buys fewer goods and services.

Inflation can arise from several sources:

- **Demand-pull inflation:** aggregate demand grows faster than productive capacity.
- **Cost-push inflation:** wages, energy, materials, tariffs, or other input costs rise.
- **Monetary or expectations effects:** money and credit growth, or expectations of future inflation, influence wages and pricing.
- **Supply shocks:** production disruptions reduce available goods or services.

Not every price increase is inflation. A shortage that raises the price of one product is a relative-price change unless it spreads broadly through the economy.

### Disinflation

**Disinflation** means inflation remains positive but slows.

Example:

- Last year’s inflation: 6%
- This year’s inflation: 2%

Prices are still rising, but more slowly.

### Deflation

**Deflation** is a sustained decline in the general price level. It may sound beneficial because purchasing power rises, but broad deflation can be economically damaging.

Potential consequences include:

- Consumers delaying purchases in expectation of lower prices;
- Falling business revenue;
- Lower wages or employment;
- Rising real burdens on fixed debts; and
- Increased default risk.

If a borrower owes a fixed $100,000, deflation does not reduce the nominal debt. Instead, the dollars needed to repay it become more valuable.

### Stagflation

**Stagflation** combines weak economic growth or high unemployment with substantial inflation. It is difficult for policymakers because stimulus may worsen inflation, while restrictive policy may worsen unemployment.

### Nominal and real returns

A **nominal return** is measured in current dollars. A **real return** adjusts for inflation.

The exact relationship is:

$$
1+R_{\text{real}}
=
\frac{1+R_{\text{nominal}}}{1+i}
$$

Therefore:

$$
R_{\text{real}}
=
\frac{1+R_{\text{nominal}}}{1+i}-1
$$

where $i$ is the inflation rate.

For moderate rates, a useful approximation is:

$$
R_{\text{real}} \approx R_{\text{nominal}}-i
$$

### Calculation example

An investment earns 7% while inflation is 3%.

Approximation:

$$
7\%-3\%=4\%
$$

Exact result:

$$
\frac{1.07}{1.03}-1
=
0.038835
\approx 3.88\%
$$

The approximation is close but not exact.

### Investment effects

All else equal, unexpected inflation tends to:

- Harm holders of fixed-rate bonds because fixed payments lose purchasing power;
- Benefit some borrowers because debts are repaid with less valuable dollars;
- Increase nominal interest-rate demands;
- Raise uncertainty about business costs and margins; and
- Affect industries differently depending on pricing power.

Deflation tends to increase the real value of fixed payments, which may help creditors if borrowers remain solvent. However, rising defaults can offset that advantage.

### Likely exam traps

- Inflation means the general price level rises—not that every price rises.
- Disinflation means slower inflation—not falling prices.
- A positive nominal return can be a negative real return.
- Use the exact formula when answer choices are close.

---

## 1.5 Interest rates

An interest rate is the price paid for the use of money over time. Rates also function as discount rates used to value future cash flows.

### Components of a nominal rate

A simplified relationship is:

$$
\text{Nominal rate}
\approx
\text{Real risk-free rate}
+
\text{Expected inflation}
+
\text{Risk premiums}
$$

Possible risk premiums compensate for:

- Credit or default risk;
- Maturity and interest-rate risk;
- Liquidity risk;
- Reinvestment risk;
- Call or prepayment risk; and
- Political or currency risk.

### The Fisher relationship

A more exact relationship between a nominal risk-free rate $r_n$, real rate $r_r$, and expected inflation $\pi_e$ is:

$$
1+r_n=(1+r_r)(1+\pi_e)
$$

For ordinary exam calculations:

$$
r_n \approx r_r+\pi_e
$$

### Bond prices and market rates

A fixed-rate bond promises stated cash flows. When market rates change, those promised cash flows do not automatically change, so the bond’s price adjusts.

- Market rates rise $\rightarrow$ existing bond prices fall.
- Market rates fall $\rightarrow$ existing bond prices rise.

Suppose a bond pays a fixed $40 annual coupon. If newly issued comparable bonds begin paying $60, investors will not pay full face value for the older $40 coupon. Its price must fall until its yield becomes competitive.

Other things equal, interest-rate sensitivity is generally greater for:

- Longer-maturity bonds; and
- Lower-coupon bonds.

### Interest rates as discount rates

A higher required return reduces present value:

$$
PV=\frac{FV}{(1+r)^n}
$$

As $r$ increases, the denominator increases and $PV$ decreases.

This principle applies beyond bonds. Higher discount rates can reduce the present value of projected corporate earnings, dividends, real-estate income, and capital projects.

---

## 1.6 Yield curves

A **yield curve** plots yields against maturities for debt securities with comparable credit quality. The Treasury yield curve is commonly used as a reference because Treasury securities are generally treated as having minimal default risk in dollar terms.

### Principal shapes

#### Normal or upward-sloping curve

Long-term yields exceed short-term yields.

Possible explanations include:

- Expectations of stronger growth or higher future short-term rates;
- Expected inflation; and
- A maturity or liquidity premium demanded for longer commitments.

#### Flat curve

Short- and long-term yields are similar.

A flat curve may appear during a transition in policy or economic expectations.

#### Inverted curve

Short-term yields exceed long-term yields.

An inversion may indicate that investors expect future short-term rates to fall, often because of weaker growth or future monetary easing. Inversions have preceded recessions, but they do not guarantee one.

#### Humped curve

Intermediate maturities yield more than both short and long maturities. This may reflect temporary policy, supply, demand, or economic expectations concentrated in a particular maturity range.

### Yield-curve theories

Three broad explanations are useful:

1. **Expectations theory**
   Long-term rates reflect expected future short-term rates.

2. **Liquidity-preference theory**
   Investors usually demand additional yield for committing funds for longer periods.

3. **Market-segmentation theory**
   Supply and demand can differ by maturity because particular investors or borrowers prefer particular portions of the curve.

No single theory explains every observed curve.

### Likely exam trap

A normal curve does not mean every long-term bond is safer or better. It means yields are generally higher at longer maturities among the securities being compared.

---

## 1.7 Credit spreads

A **credit spread** is the yield difference between a debt security carrying credit risk and a benchmark security of similar maturity.

A simplified formula is:

$$
\text{Credit spread}
=
\text{Corporate bond yield}
-
\text{Comparable Treasury yield}
$$

### Example

A ten-year corporate bond yields 6.2%, while a comparable Treasury yields 4.4%.

$$
6.2\%-4.4\%=1.8\%
$$

The spread is **1.8 percentage points**, or **180 basis points**.

Because:

$$
1\text{ percentage point}=100\text{ basis points}
$$

### Widening and narrowing spreads

A **widening spread** often signals:

- Greater perceived default risk;
- Lower liquidity;
- Economic stress; or
- Reduced investor appetite for risk.

A **narrowing spread** often signals:

- Improved credit expectations;
- Strong investor demand;
- Better liquidity; or
- Greater risk appetite.

### Compare like with like

A meaningful spread comparison should account for:

- Maturity;
- Embedded call or put options;
- Seniority;
- Collateral;
- Tax treatment;
- Liquidity; and
- Currency.

A high yield does not automatically mean a bargain. It may be compensation for a real probability of loss.

---

## 1.8 Economic indicators

Economic indicators can be grouped by when they tend to move relative to the business cycle.

| Category | General behavior | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Leading | Often changes before the overall economy | New orders, building permits, initial unemployment claims, some financial-market measures |
| Coincident | Moves roughly with current activity | Production, income, payroll employment, real GDP as a measure of the period |
| Lagging | Often confirms a change after it begins | Unemployment duration, some inflation and credit measures |

Classification is not perfect. Indicators can behave differently across cycles, and publication delays affect how useful they are in real time.

---

## 1.9 Gross domestic product

**Gross domestic product**, or **GDP**, is the market value of final goods and services produced within a country during a specified period.

The expenditure formula is:

$$
GDP=C+I+G+(X-M)
$$

where:

- $C$ = personal consumption expenditures;
- $I$ = gross private domestic investment;
- $G$ = government consumption and investment;
- $X$ = exports; and
- $M$ = imports.

### Why imports are subtracted

Imported goods may first appear in consumption, investment, or government purchases. Subtracting imports prevents foreign production from being counted as domestic production.

### What GDP generally includes

- Newly produced final goods;
- Newly provided services;
- Business investment in equipment and structures;
- Residential construction;
- Changes in business inventories;
- Government purchases of goods and services; and
- Exports produced domestically.

### What GDP generally excludes

- Intermediate goods already embodied in final goods;
- Pure financial transactions such as buying an existing share of stock;
- Transfer payments by themselves;
- Most unpaid household work;
- Sales of previously produced used goods, except current services such as a dealer’s commission; and
- Production by domestic residents occurring outside the country.

### Nominal versus real GDP

- **Nominal GDP** values production at current prices.
- **Real GDP** adjusts for price changes.

If nominal GDP rises 6% while prices rise approximately 4%, real output has increased by roughly 2%, subject to the details of the official calculation.

### GDP limitations

GDP measures production, not necessarily:

- Wealth distribution;
- Environmental quality;
- Leisure;
- Household well-being;
- Sustainability; or
- The quality of every product.

An adviser should not treat GDP growth as a complete measure of client welfare or investment opportunity.

---

## 1.10 Employment indicators

### Labor force concepts

The civilian labor force generally consists of people who are:

- Employed; or
- Unemployed and actively seeking work.

The unemployment rate is:

$$
\text{Unemployment rate}
=
\frac{\text{Unemployed}}{\text{Labor force}}
\times 100
$$

### Calculation example

Suppose:

- 96 million people are employed;
- 4 million are unemployed and actively seeking work; and
- 3 million want work but have stopped looking.

The labor force is:

$$
96+4=100\text{ million}
$$

The unemployment rate is:

$$
\frac{4}{100}=4\%
$$

The 3 million people not actively seeking work are not included in this simplified unemployment-rate calculation.

### Important limitations

- A person working part-time is ordinarily classified as employed, even if full-time work is preferred.
- A discouraged worker who is not actively seeking work may not be counted in the labor force.
- The unemployment rate can fall because employment rises, but it can also fall because people leave the labor force.
- Payroll growth and the unemployment rate come from different surveys and need not move identically.

### Other labor indicators

Analysts may review:

- Payroll employment;
- Labor-force participation;
- Average weekly hours;
- Wage growth;
- Job openings;
- Initial unemployment claims; and
- Continuing claims.

Initial claims can provide relatively timely evidence of changing labor-market conditions. Employment is often a coincident or lagging indicator rather than a perfect advance signal.

---

## 1.11 Consumer Price Index

The **Consumer Price Index**, or **CPI**, measures changes over time in prices paid by consumers for a basket of goods and services.

A simplified inflation calculation is:

$$
\text{Inflation rate}
=
\frac{CPI_{\text{new}}-CPI_{\text{old}}}{CPI_{\text{old}}}
\times 100
$$

### Example

If the CPI rises from 250 to 257.5:

$$
\frac{257.5-250}{250}\times100
=
3\%
$$

### Headline and core CPI

- **Headline CPI** includes the full measured basket.
- **Core CPI** commonly excludes food and energy because those categories can be volatile.

Core CPI is intended to reveal persistent trends; it does not imply that food and energy are unimportant to households.

### CPI limitations

CPI is an average measure. A particular client’s experienced inflation may differ because of:

- Location;
- Housing situation;
- Health-care spending;
- Transportation needs;
- Age;
- Consumption patterns; and
- Tax circumstances.

### Exam distinction

CPI focuses on consumer prices. The GDP price measures cover a different and broader set of domestically produced goods and services.

---

## 1.12 Trade deficits and the balance of payments

### Trade balance

The trade balance is broadly:

$$
\text{Exports}-\text{Imports}
$$

A **trade deficit** occurs when imports exceed exports. A **trade surplus** occurs when exports exceed imports.

A trade deficit is not automatically evidence that an economy is failing. It may accompany:

- Strong domestic consumption;
- High investment;
- A strong currency;
- Foreign willingness to invest domestically; or
- Structural differences in saving and investment.

It can still create vulnerabilities, especially if financing depends on unstable capital inflows.

### Balance of payments

The **balance of payments** records transactions between residents of a country and the rest of the world. Its broad components include:

1. **Current account**
   - Trade in goods and services;
   - Cross-border income; and
   - Current transfers.

2. **Capital account**
   - Certain capital transfers and transactions in nonproduced, nonfinancial assets.

3. **Financial account**
   - Direct investment;
   - Portfolio investment;
   - Other financial flows; and
   - Reserve-related transactions.

Because the system uses double-entry accounting, cross-border transactions have offsetting entries. A current-account deficit is generally financed by corresponding financial or capital flows, subject to statistical discrepancies.

### Likely exam trap

A trade deficit is only part of the current account. It is not the entire balance of payments.

---

## 1.13 Currency valuation and exchange-rate effects

An exchange rate states the value of one currency in terms of another.

### Appreciation and depreciation

If the dollar appreciates:

- A dollar buys more foreign currency;
- Imported goods tend to become cheaper for U.S. buyers;
- U.S. exports tend to become more expensive for foreign buyers; and
- Foreign assets translate into fewer dollars, all else equal.

If the dollar depreciates:

- A dollar buys less foreign currency;
- Imports tend to become more expensive;
- U.S. exports may become more competitive abroad; and
- Foreign assets translate into more dollars, all else equal.

Actual company effects depend on sourcing, hedging, pricing power, debt currency, and where costs and revenues are earned.

### Foreign-investment return

For a U.S. investor, the dollar return on an unhedged foreign investment depends on:

1. The investment’s return in its local currency; and
2. The foreign currency’s change against the dollar.

Define $R_{FX}$ as the change in the dollar value of the foreign currency. Then:

$$
1+R_{\$}
=
(1+R_{\text{local}})(1+R_{FX})
$$

Thus:

$$
R_{\$}
=
(1+R_{\text{local}})(1+R_{FX})-1
$$

### Calculation example 1: currency loss

A foreign stock rises 8% in local currency, but the foreign currency falls 5% against the dollar.

$$
R_{\$}
=
(1.08)(0.95)-1
=
0.026
=
2.6\%
$$

The investor earns 2.6% in dollars, not 8% and not exactly 3%.

### Calculation example 2: currency gain

A foreign bond earns 4% locally, and the foreign currency rises 6% against the dollar.

$$
R_{\$}
=
(1.04)(1.06)-1
=
0.1024
=
10.24\%
$$

### Quotation trap

Exchange rates can be quoted in either direction. “Dollars per euro” and “euros per dollar” move inversely. Before calculating, define clearly which currency is appreciating and what $R_{FX}$ means.

### Hedging

Currency forwards, futures, options, and other arrangements may reduce exchange-rate exposure. Hedging can also:

- Cost money;
- Introduce counterparty or basis risk;
- Limit favorable currency gains; and
- Produce imperfect offsets.

---

## 1.14 Global and geopolitical factors

International developments can affect investments through:

- Currency movements;
- Commodity prices;
- Supply chains;
- Tariffs and trade restrictions;
- Sanctions;
- Military conflict;
- Elections and political instability;
- Nationalization or expropriation;
- Capital controls;
- Regulatory changes;
- Migration;
- Cyberattacks; and
- Changes in cross-border investment.

### Direct and indirect exposure

A company can have international exposure even if its headquarters and listing are domestic.

Consider:

- Foreign customers;
- Foreign suppliers;
- Imported raw materials;
- Overseas factories;
- Debt denominated in another currency;
- Foreign competitors; and
- Reliance on international shipping.

A domestic utility and a global manufacturer may react very differently to the same currency move.

### Translation, transaction, and economic exposure

- **Translation exposure:** foreign financial statements are converted into the reporting currency.
- **Transaction exposure:** a specific receivable, payable, or contract is denominated in a foreign currency.
- **Economic exposure:** long-run competitiveness and cash flows change because of currency or geopolitical developments.

---

## 1.15 Sovereign debt

**Sovereign debt** is debt issued or guaranteed by a national government.

### Principal risks

Sovereign borrowers can present:

- Default risk;
- Restructuring risk;
- Extension of maturities;
- Reduction of principal or interest;
- Inflation risk;
- Currency-devaluation risk;
- Capital controls;
- Political risk;
- Weak legal remedies; and
- Liquidity risk.

### Domestic- versus foreign-currency debt

A government borrowing in its own currency may have greater capacity to create money for nominal repayment, but that does not eliminate risk. Investors may instead suffer through:

- Inflation;
- Currency depreciation;
- Financial repression; or
- Reduced real purchasing power.

Foreign-currency debt creates an additional mismatch. If the borrower’s local currency weakens, more local currency is needed to service the same foreign-currency obligation.

### Sovereign spreads

The yield spread over a reference government security or benchmark can reflect:

- Default probability;
- Expected recovery;
- Currency risk;
- Political uncertainty;
- Market liquidity; and
- Global risk appetite.

A high sovereign yield may indicate expected inflation or currency loss rather than an unusually attractive real return.

### Responsible interpretation

Country labels alone are inadequate. Review:

- Debt currency;
- Maturity;
- Governing law;
- Seniority;
- Foreign reserves;
- Fiscal capacity;
- Monetary credibility;
- Political institutions; and
- The investor’s home currency.

---

## Chapter 2 — Financial Reporting

## 2.1 The financial-statement system

The principal financial statements are:

1. Income statement;
2. Balance sheet;
3. Statement of cash flows; and
4. Statement of shareholders’ equity.

The notes are integral to the statements. They explain accounting policies, contingencies, debt terms, segment information, estimates, related-party transactions, and other matters that the face of the statements cannot fully communicate.

A single statement rarely tells the full story.

---

## 2.2 Income statement

The **income statement** reports financial performance over a period.

A simplified structure is:

$$
\text{Revenue}
-\text{Cost of goods sold}
=
\text{Gross profit}
$$

$$
\text{Gross profit}
-\text{Operating expenses}
=
\text{Operating income}
$$

After interest, nonoperating items, and taxes:

$$
\text{Pretax income}
-\text{Income tax expense}
=
\text{Net income}
$$

### Important terms

- **Revenue:** amounts earned from providing goods or services.
- **Cost of goods sold:** direct costs associated with products sold.
- **Gross profit:** revenue minus cost of goods sold.
- **Operating expenses:** selling, administrative, research, and similar operating costs.
- **Operating income:** profit from core operations before specified nonoperating items.
- **Net income:** residual accounting profit after recognized expenses.

### Earnings per share

Basic earnings per common share is generally:

$$
EPS
=
\frac{\text{Net income}-\text{Preferred dividends}}
{\text{Weighted-average common shares outstanding}}
$$

Diluted EPS reflects the potential effect of convertible securities, options, or other instruments that may increase common shares.

### Example

A company reports:

- Revenue: $5,000,000
- Cost of goods sold: $3,000,000
- Operating expenses: $1,200,000
- Interest expense: $200,000
- Tax expense: $150,000

Gross profit:

$$
5{,}000{,}000-3{,}000{,}000
=
2{,}000{,}000
$$

Operating income:

$$
2{,}000{,}000-1{,}200{,}000
=
800{,}000
$$

Net income:

$$
800{,}000-200{,}000-150{,}000
=
450{,}000
$$

### Income-statement limitations

Net income can differ from cash generated because it includes:

- Credit sales not yet collected;
- Expenses incurred but not yet paid;
- Depreciation and amortization;
- Estimates of bad debts or warranties;
- Deferred revenue recognition;
- Gains or losses from asset sales; and
- Other accrual adjustments.

### EBITDA trap

EBITDA excludes interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. It is not the same as:

- Net income;
- Cash from operations; or
- Free cash flow.

A capital-intensive business may show strong EBITDA while requiring substantial spending to replace equipment.

---

## 2.3 Balance sheet

The **balance sheet** reports financial position at a point in time.

The accounting equation is:

$$
\text{Assets}
=
\text{Liabilities}
+
\text{Shareholders' equity}
$$

### Assets

Common current assets include:

- Cash;
- Short-term marketable securities;
- Accounts receivable;
- Inventory; and
- Prepaid expenses.

Noncurrent assets may include:

- Property, plant, and equipment;
- Long-term investments;
- Goodwill;
- Patents and other intangible assets; and
- Deferred tax assets.

### Liabilities

Common current liabilities include:

- Accounts payable;
- Accrued expenses;
- Short-term borrowings; and
- The current portion of long-term debt.

Noncurrent liabilities may include:

- Long-term debt;
- Lease liabilities;
- Pension obligations; and
- Deferred tax liabilities.

### Shareholders’ equity

Equity may include:

- Common stock at stated accounting value;
- Additional paid-in capital;
- Retained earnings;
- Accumulated other comprehensive income or loss; and
- Treasury stock as a reduction.

### Retained earnings are not a cash account

Retained earnings broadly accumulate profits that have not been distributed as dividends, subject to accounting adjustments.

A company can have high retained earnings and little cash because profits may have been invested in:

- Inventory;
- Receivables;
- Buildings;
- Equipment;
- Acquisitions; or
- Debt repayment.

### Working capital

$$
\text{Working capital}
=
\text{Current assets}
-
\text{Current liabilities}
$$

Positive working capital may suggest short-term financial flexibility, but interpretation depends on asset quality. Obsolete inventory and doubtful receivables may not be readily available to pay obligations.

### Historical cost and book value

Many balance-sheet amounts are based on historical cost, adjusted under applicable accounting rules. Book value may therefore differ greatly from current market value.

Examples include:

- Land purchased decades earlier;
- Internally developed brands that are not recorded as acquired intangible assets;
- Goodwill from prior acquisitions; and
- Securities carried at different measurement bases.

---

## 2.4 Statement of cash flows

The **statement of cash flows** explains changes in cash over a period through three categories.

### Operating activities

Operating cash flows arise primarily from ordinary business operations.

Examples include:

- Cash collected from customers;
- Cash paid to suppliers and employees;
- Certain interest and tax payments; and
- Adjustments reconciling net income to operating cash flow.

### Investing activities

Investing activities generally involve long-term assets and investments.

Examples include:

- Purchasing equipment;
- Selling property;
- Acquiring a business;
- Buying long-term investments; and
- Receiving proceeds from their sale.

### Financing activities

Financing activities involve obtaining or returning capital.

Examples include:

- Issuing stock;
- Repurchasing stock;
- Issuing debt;
- Repaying debt principal; and
- Paying dividends.

### U.S. classification conventions

Under U.S. accounting conventions, interest paid and received is generally classified as operating, dividends received as operating, and dividends paid as financing. International accounting rules may permit different classifications, so use the framework specified in the question.

### Indirect operating-cash-flow method

The indirect method commonly begins with net income and adjusts for:

- Noncash expenses;
- Gains and losses classified elsewhere; and
- Changes in operating assets and liabilities.

Useful directional rules include:

- Increase in a current operating asset $\rightarrow$ subtract from operating cash flow.
- Decrease in a current operating asset $\rightarrow$ add.
- Increase in a current operating liability $\rightarrow$ add.
- Decrease in a current operating liability $\rightarrow$ subtract.

### Calculation example

Suppose a company reports:

- Net income: $100,000
- Depreciation: $20,000
- Increase in accounts receivable: $15,000
- Increase in accounts payable: $8,000

Then simplified operating cash flow is:

$$
100{,}000+20{,}000-15{,}000+8{,}000
=
113{,}000
$$

Why?

- Depreciation reduced net income but did not use current-period cash, so add it back.
- Receivables increased because some recognized revenue was not collected, so subtract the increase.
- Payables increased because some recognized expenses were not yet paid, so add the increase.

### Free cash flow

A commonly used—but not formally standardized—measure is:

$$
\text{Free cash flow}
=
\text{Cash flow from operations}
-
\text{Capital expenditures}
$$

If operating cash flow is $500,000 and capital expenditures are $180,000:

$$
500{,}000-180{,}000
=
320{,}000
$$

Because free-cash-flow definitions vary, confirm exactly what a source includes.

### Cash-flow traps

- Buying equipment is investing, not an operating expense on the purchase date.
- Issuing bonds is financing.
- Repaying bond principal is financing.
- Depreciation is noncash, but the original purchase of the asset used cash.
- Positive operating cash flow does not prove profitability, and net income does not prove positive operating cash flow.

---

## 2.5 How the statements connect

The statements form one accounting system.

### Example: credit sale

A company sells a product for $1,000 on credit. The product cost $600.

Immediate effects include:

- Income statement:
  - Revenue increases by $1,000.
  - Cost of goods sold increases by $600.
  - Pretax income increases by $400.

- Balance sheet:
  - Accounts receivable increases by $1,000.
  - Inventory decreases by $600.
  - Equity eventually increases through retained earnings, subject to taxes and distributions.

- Cash-flow statement:
  - No customer cash has yet been collected.
  - Under the indirect method, the increase in receivables offsets the portion of income not collected.

When the customer later pays:

- Cash rises;
- Accounts receivable falls; and
- No new revenue is ordinarily recognized merely because the receivable was collected.

### Example: equipment purchase

A company pays $120,000 cash for equipment expected to provide service for several years.

At purchase:

- Equipment rises by $120,000;
- Cash falls by $120,000;
- Investing cash flow falls by $120,000; and
- The full $120,000 ordinarily does not become an immediate operating expense.

Over time, depreciation allocates the asset’s cost to expense under the applicable accounting method.

---

## 2.6 Cash accounting versus accrual accounting

### Cash-basis accounting

Under cash-basis accounting:

- Revenue is generally recorded when cash is received.
- Expense is generally recorded when cash is paid.

Cash accounting is simple but may poorly match economic activity to the period in which it occurs.

### Accrual accounting

Under accrual accounting:

- Revenue is recognized when earned under the applicable recognition rules.
- Expenses are recognized when incurred or allocated to the periods benefiting from them.

Accrual accounting creates accounts such as:

- Accounts receivable;
- Accounts payable;
- Accrued expenses;
- Prepaid expenses;
- Deferred or unearned revenue; and
- Depreciation.

Public-company financial reporting generally uses accrual accounting.

### Example: prepaid service contract

A customer pays a company $1,200 on January 1 for 12 months of service.

On January 1:

- Cash increases by $1,200.
- Deferred revenue, a liability, increases by $1,200.
- The company has not necessarily earned the full amount.

After one month, assuming even service:

$$
\frac{\$1{,}200}{12}
=
\$100
$$

The company recognizes $100 of revenue and reduces deferred revenue by $100.

### Example: unpaid expense

Employees earn $20,000 during December but are paid in January.

Under accrual accounting, the company ordinarily records in December:

- Wage expense of $20,000; and
- An accrued liability of $20,000.

January’s cash payment reduces cash and the liability; it does not create a second December wage expense.

### Earnings quality

Accrual accounting requires judgment. Analysts should examine whether earnings are supported by cash generation.

Warning signs can include:

- Receivables growing much faster than sales;
- Inventory growing despite weak demand;
- Repeated “nonrecurring” charges;
- Aggressive capitalization of ordinary costs;
- Large changes in estimates;
- Weak operating cash flow despite reported profit; and
- Dependence on asset-sale gains.

None of these alone proves misconduct. They identify areas requiring investigation.

### Memory aid

- **Receivable:** earned, not collected.
- **Payable:** incurred, not paid.
- **Prepaid expense:** paid, not yet consumed.
- **Deferred revenue:** collected, not yet earned.

---

## 2.7 Audited versus unaudited information

### Audit

An independent financial-statement audit is designed to provide **reasonable assurance** that the statements are free of material misstatement, whether caused by error or fraud.

Reasonable assurance is high assurance, but it is not:

- Absolute assurance;
- A guarantee that no fraud exists;
- A guarantee that the company will remain solvent;
- An appraisal of investment value; or
- A prediction of stock performance.

Management prepares the statements and maintains internal control. The independent auditor evaluates evidence and expresses an opinion.

### Review

A review provides less assurance than an audit. It ordinarily relies more heavily on inquiry and analytical procedures and does not provide an audit opinion.

### Unaudited does not mean false

Quarterly financial statements are commonly labeled unaudited, although they may be subject to an independent accountant’s review. Unaudited information can still be useful, but it does not carry the same level of assurance as an audit.

### Auditor opinions

#### Unmodified opinion

An **unmodified**, or “clean,” opinion indicates that the financial statements are presented fairly, in all material respects, under the applicable reporting framework.

It does not mean the company is financially healthy or the security is attractive.

#### Qualified opinion

A **qualified opinion** generally means that, except for a specified matter, the statements are fairly presented.

The issue is material but not so pervasive that the entire set of statements is unreliable.

#### Adverse opinion

An **adverse opinion** means material misstatements are both serious and pervasive enough that the statements are not fairly presented.

#### Disclaimer of opinion

A **disclaimer** means the auditor does not express an opinion, commonly because sufficient appropriate evidence could not be obtained or independence was impaired.

### Going-concern disclosures

If substantial doubt exists about an entity’s ability to continue operating for the relevant assessment period, the financial statements and auditor’s report may include going-concern-related disclosures.

A going-concern paragraph is not automatically the same as an adverse opinion. It highlights uncertainty about continued operation.

### Critical audit matters

For issuers subject to the applicable requirements, the auditor may discuss matters involving especially challenging, subjective, or complex judgments. A critical audit matter does not by itself mean the accounting is wrong.

### Likely exam hierarchy

From a favorable opinion to increasingly serious reporting problems:

1. Unmodified;
2. Qualified;
3. Adverse.

A disclaimer is different: the auditor declines to express an opinion.

---

## 2.8 SEC filings and annual reports

Public companies file specified reports with the Securities and Exchange Commission. These filings are available through EDGAR.

### Form 10-K

The annual report on Form 10-K generally includes:

- Audited annual financial statements;
- Management’s discussion and analysis;
- Business description;
- Risk factors;
- Legal proceedings;
- Market information;
- Executive and governance disclosures or references; and
- Auditor reports.

### Form 10-Q

Form 10-Q is a quarterly filing containing interim financial and operating information. Its financial statements are generally unaudited, although they are ordinarily reviewed by the independent accountant.

### Form 8-K

Form 8-K reports specified material events, such as certain:

- Acquisitions or dispositions;
- Leadership changes;
- Auditor changes;
- Bankruptcy events;
- Financing developments; and
- Other significant corporate matters.

It is event-driven rather than a substitute for the regular annual or quarterly reports.

### Proxy statement

A proxy statement, commonly filed on Schedule 14A, provides information relevant to shareholder voting, such as:

- Director elections;
- Executive compensation;
- Governance;
- Related-party matters; and
- Shareholder proposals.

### Registration statements and prospectuses

When securities are offered, registration filings and prospectuses provide information concerning:

- The issuer;
- The offering;
- Use of proceeds;
- Risks;
- Management; and
- Financial statements.

### Insider-ownership filings

Forms such as Forms 3, 4, and 5 provide information about specified ownership and transaction reporting by directors, officers, and significant beneficial owners.

### Annual report versus Form 10-K

A shareholder annual report may be designed as a polished communication document with photographs, summaries, and management messaging. It may include or accompany the 10-K, but the terms are not always interchangeable.

For rigorous analysis:

1. Read the filed financial statements;
2. Read the notes;
3. Review management’s discussion;
4. Review the auditor’s report;
5. Compare non-GAAP presentations with GAAP results; and
6. Check subsequent Forms 8-K and later filings.

### Likely exam traps

- A 10-K is annual and includes audited financial statements.
- A 10-Q is quarterly and generally unaudited.
- An 8-K is event-driven.
- An attractive annual-report design is not audit evidence.
- Filing with the SEC does not mean the SEC approves the investment’s merits.

---

## Chapter 3 — Analytical Methods

## 3.1 Time value of money

The **time value of money** reflects the principle that a dollar available today is generally worth more than a dollar available later because today’s dollar can be invested, consumed, or retained for flexibility.

The core variables are:

- $PV$ = present value;
- $FV$ = future value;
- $r$ = interest or discount rate per period;
- $n$ = number of periods; and
- $C$ = periodic cash flow.

### Future value of a lump sum

$$
FV=PV(1+r)^n
$$

### Example

Invest $10,000 for three years at 6% annually.

$$
FV
=
10{,}000(1.06)^3
=
\$11{,}910.16
$$

The total compound gain is $1,910.16.

### Present value of a lump sum

$$
PV=\frac{FV}{(1+r)^n}
$$

### Example

An investor will receive $5,000 in four years. The appropriate annual discount rate is 5%.

$$
PV
=
\frac{5{,}000}{(1.05)^4}
=
\$4{,}113.51
$$

Paying more than $4,113.51 would produce a return below 5%, assuming the payment is certain and the discount rate is appropriate.

### Compounding frequency

If a quoted annual rate is compounded $m$ times per year:

$$
FV
=
PV\left(1+\frac{r}{m}\right)^{mn}
$$

The effective annual rate is:

$$
EAR
=
\left(1+\frac{APR}{m}\right)^m-1
$$

### Example

A 6% stated annual rate compounded monthly has an effective annual rate of:

$$
\left(1+\frac{0.06}{12}\right)^{12}-1
\approx 6.17\%
$$

### Match rates and periods

If cash flows are monthly:

- Use a monthly rate; and
- Count monthly periods.

Do not apply a 12% annual rate directly to each month.

---

## 3.2 Annuities

An **annuity** is a series of equal payments at regular intervals.

### Ordinary annuity

Payments occur at the end of each period.

Present value:

$$
PV_{\text{ordinary}}
=
C
\left[
\frac{1-(1+r)^{-n}}{r}
\right]
$$

Future value:

$$
FV_{\text{ordinary}}
=
C
\left[
\frac{(1+r)^n-1}{r}
\right]
$$

### Annuity due

Payments occur at the beginning of each period.

Because every payment compounds or is discounted for one fewer period:

$$
PV_{\text{due}}
=
PV_{\text{ordinary}}(1+r)
$$

$$
FV_{\text{due}}
=
FV_{\text{ordinary}}(1+r)
$$

### Example

An investment pays $1,000 at the end of each year for three years. The discount rate is 5%.

$$
PV
=
1{,}000
\left[
\frac{1-(1.05)^{-3}}{0.05}
\right]
\approx
\$2{,}723.25
$$

If the payments occur at the beginning of each year:

$$
PV_{\text{due}}
=
2{,}723.25(1.05)
\approx
\$2{,}859.41
$$

The annuity due is worth more because payments arrive sooner.

### Exam memory aid

- **Ordinary:** payment at the **end**.
- **Due:** payment is **due immediately**, at the beginning.

---

## 3.3 Net present value

**Net present value**, or **NPV**, compares the present value of future cash inflows with the present value of costs.

For an initial outlay $C_0$ and future cash flows $C_t$:

$$
NPV
=
-C_0
+
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\frac{C_t}{(1+r)^t}
$$

### Decision rule

- $NPV>0$: expected return exceeds the required return.
- $NPV=0$: expected return equals the required return.
- $NPV<0$: expected return falls below the required return.

### Calculation example

A project costs $10,000 and is expected to pay $4,000 at the end of each of the next three years. The required return is 8%.

$$
NPV
=
-10{,}000
+
\frac{4{,}000}{1.08}
+
\frac{4{,}000}{1.08^2}
+
\frac{4{,}000}{1.08^3}
$$

$$
NPV
\approx
-10{,}000
+
3{,}703.70
+
3{,}429.36
+
3{,}175.33
$$

$$
NPV
\approx
\$308.39
$$

Because NPV is positive, the project exceeds the 8% required return under the stated assumptions.

### NPV depends on assumptions

A positive NPV is not a guarantee. It depends on:

- Cash-flow estimates;
- Timing;
- Discount rate;
- Taxes;
- Terminal value;
- Inflation;
- Project life; and
- Risk.

Responsible analysis should test how results change under less favorable assumptions.

---

## 3.4 Internal rate of return

The **internal rate of return**, or **IRR**, is the discount rate that makes NPV equal to zero.

$$
0
=
-C_0
+
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\frac{C_t}{(1+IRR)^t}
$$

For the prior project, the IRR is approximately 9.7%.

### Decision rule

- If $IRR$ exceeds the required return, accept the project, all else equal.
- If $IRR$ is below the required return, reject it.
- If $IRR$ equals the required return, NPV is zero.

### Relationship between NPV and IRR

For a conventional project with one initial outflow followed by inflows:

- At discount rates below the IRR, NPV is positive.
- At the IRR, NPV is zero.
- At discount rates above the IRR, NPV is negative.

### IRR limitations

IRR can mislead when:

- Cash flows change sign more than once, creating multiple IRRs;
- Projects differ greatly in scale;
- Projects have different timing;
- Mutually exclusive projects produce conflicting rankings;
- Reinvestment assumptions are unrealistic; or
- No economically meaningful IRR exists.

For mutually exclusive projects, NPV generally gives the clearer measure of dollar value added when the discount rate is appropriate.

### Likely exam distinction

- **NPV** is a dollar amount.
- **IRR** is a percentage rate.
- IRR is the rate that sets NPV to zero.

---

## 3.5 Mean, median, mode, and range

Consider the observations:

$$
2,\ 4,\ 4,\ 6
$$

### Arithmetic mean

$$
\bar{x}
=
\frac{\sum x_i}{n}
$$

$$
\bar{x}
=
\frac{2+4+4+6}{4}
=
4
$$

The mean uses every observation but can be distorted by extreme values.

### Median

The median is the middle observation after sorting. With an even number of observations, average the two middle values.

The middle observations are 4 and 4:

$$
\text{Median}=4
$$

The median is often preferable when a distribution is highly skewed.

### Mode

The mode is the most frequent value.

Here:

$$
\text{Mode}=4
$$

A dataset may have:

- No mode;
- One mode; or
- Multiple modes.

### Range

$$
\text{Range}
=
\text{Maximum}
-
\text{Minimum}
$$

$$
6-2=4
$$

The range is simple but uses only two observations and is highly sensitive to outliers.

### Outlier example

For salaries of $40,000, $42,000, $44,000, and $500,000:

- Mean = $156,500;
- Median = $43,000.

The median better describes a typical employee in this small, highly skewed group.

### Weighted mean

Expected return is a probability-weighted mean:

$$
E(R)
=
\sum p_iR_i
$$

If an investment has:

- 30% probability of a 20% return;
- 50% probability of an 8% return; and
- 20% probability of a $-10\%$ return,

then:

$$
E(R)
=
(0.30)(20\%)
+
(0.50)(8\%)
+
(0.20)(-10\%)
$$

$$
E(R)=6\%+4\%-2\%=8\%
$$

An 8% expected return does not mean the investment will actually earn exactly 8%.

---

## 3.6 Variance and standard deviation

Standard deviation measures dispersion around the mean. In investment analysis, it is commonly used as a measure of total return volatility.

### Population variance

$$
\sigma^2
=
\frac{\sum (x_i-\mu)^2}{N}
$$

### Population standard deviation

$$
\sigma
=
\sqrt{
\frac{\sum (x_i-\mu)^2}{N}
}
$$

Using $2,4,4,6$, the mean is 4.

Squared deviations are:

$$
(2-4)^2=4
$$

$$
(4-4)^2=0
$$

$$
(4-4)^2=0
$$

$$
(6-4)^2=4
$$

Population variance:

$$
\frac{4+0+0+4}{4}
=
2
$$

Population standard deviation:

$$
\sqrt{2}
\approx
1.41
$$

### Sample standard deviation

When observations are treated as a sample used to estimate a larger population, the usual sample formula divides by $n-1$:

$$
s
=
\sqrt{
\frac{\sum (x_i-\bar{x})^2}{n-1}
}
$$

For the same observations:

$$
s
=
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}
\approx
1.63
$$

Use the formula specified by the problem.

### Interpretation

If two portfolios have the same expected return, the portfolio with lower standard deviation has exhibited less total volatility.

However, standard deviation:

- Treats favorable and unfavorable deviations alike;
- Depends on the period and data frequency;
- May understate extreme or non-normal risks; and
- Is not a promise about future losses.

### Exam distinction

- **Variance** is in squared units.
- **Standard deviation** is in the original units and is easier to interpret.

---

## 3.7 Beta

**Beta** measures a security’s sensitivity to broad market movements. It is a measure of **systematic risk**, not total risk.

A common expression is:

$$
\beta_i
=
\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(R_i,R_m)}
{\operatorname{Var}(R_m)}
$$

### Interpretation

- $\beta=1.0$: historically moves approximately in line with the market’s percentage movements.
- $\beta>1.0$: greater sensitivity to market movements.
- $0<\beta<1.0$: less sensitivity than the market.
- $\beta=0$: no measured linear market sensitivity.
- $\beta<0$: tendency to move opposite the market.

If a security has beta 1.4 and the market rises 5%, a simple beta-only estimate suggests a 7% market-related move:

$$
1.4\times5\%=7\%
$$

This is not a forecast or guarantee. Company-specific news can dominate the result.

### Beta traps

- A low-beta investment can still have high company-specific risk.
- Beta depends on the chosen market benchmark and measurement period.
- Beta measures sensitivity, not whether the investment is “good.”
- A beta of 2 does not mean the security always earns twice the market return.

---

## 3.8 Alpha

**Alpha** measures return above or below a benchmark-adjusted expected return.

Using the Capital Asset Pricing Model as a reference:

$$
E(R_i)
=
R_f+\beta_i(R_m-R_f)
$$

Then:

$$
\alpha_i
=
R_i-
\left[
R_f+\beta_i(R_m-R_f)
\right]
$$

### Calculation example

Suppose:

- Portfolio return: 11%;
- Risk-free rate: 3%;
- Market return: 9%;
- Portfolio beta: 1.2.

Required or benchmark-adjusted return:

$$
3\%+1.2(9\%-3\%)
$$

$$
3\%+1.2(6\%)
=
10.2\%
$$

Alpha:

$$
11\%-10.2\%
=
0.8\%
$$

The portfolio produced positive alpha of 0.8 percentage point relative to this model.

### Interpretation caution

Alpha depends on:

- Benchmark selection;
- Beta estimate;
- Time period;
- Fees;
- Taxes;
- Cash flows; and
- Model assumptions.

Positive historical alpha does not prove manager skill or predict future alpha.

---

## 3.9 Sharpe ratio

The **Sharpe ratio** measures excess return per unit of total volatility.

$$
\text{Sharpe ratio}
=
\frac{R_p-R_f}{\sigma_p}
$$

where:

- $R_p$ = portfolio return;
- $R_f$ = risk-free rate; and
- $\sigma_p$ = portfolio standard deviation.

### Calculation example

Portfolio A:

- Return = 10%
- Risk-free rate = 2%
- Standard deviation = 12%

$$
S_A
=
\frac{10\%-2\%}{12\%}
=
0.67
$$

Portfolio B:

- Return = 8%
- Risk-free rate = 2%
- Standard deviation = 7%

$$
S_B
=
\frac{8\%-2\%}{7\%}
\approx
0.86
$$

Portfolio B has the lower raw return but the higher Sharpe ratio. It produced more excess return per unit of measured total volatility.

### Sharpe-ratio cautions

- Compare ratios measured over compatible periods.
- Use consistent risk-free rates.
- Standard deviation may not capture illiquidity or severe tail risk.
- Negative Sharpe ratios require care because simple rankings can be unintuitive.
- The ratio is retrospective unless based on forecasts.

### Alpha, beta, and Sharpe comparison

| Measure | Main question | Risk concept |
|---|---|---|
| Beta | How sensitive is the investment to the market? | Systematic risk |
| Alpha | Did return exceed a benchmark-adjusted expectation? | Model-relative performance |
| Sharpe ratio | How much excess return was earned per unit of total volatility? | Total risk measured by standard deviation |

### Memory aid

- **Beta:** market **bounce**.
- **Alpha:** return **above** expectation.
- **Sharpe:** excess return per unit of risk—the larger, the **sharper** the trade-off.

---

## 3.10 Correlation

Correlation measures the direction and strength of the linear relationship between two variables.

$$
\rho_{XY}
=
\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}
{\sigma_X\sigma_Y}
$$

Correlation ranges from $-1$ to $+1$.

### Interpretation

- $+1$: perfect positive linear relationship.
- $0$: no linear relationship.
- $-1$: perfect negative linear relationship.

### Diversification implications

Combining assets with correlation below $+1$ can reduce portfolio volatility, depending on weights and individual volatilities.

- Perfect positive correlation provides the least diversification benefit.
- Lower positive, zero, or negative correlation generally provides more benefit.
- Perfect negative correlation can theoretically eliminate volatility at an appropriate weighting, though stable $-1$ correlations are rare.

### Important cautions

- Zero correlation does not prove independence.
- Correlation does not prove causation.
- Historical correlations can change during market stress.
- A low correlation estimate based on limited data may be unreliable.
- Illiquid assets may appear artificially stable or weakly correlated because prices update infrequently.

### Exam trap

Diversification does not require negative correlation. Any correlation below $+1$ may provide some volatility reduction.

---

## 3.11 Liquidity ratios

### Current ratio

$$
\text{Current ratio}
=
\frac{\text{Current assets}}
{\text{Current liabilities}}
$$

### Quick ratio

The quick ratio excludes less liquid current assets such as inventory and prepaid expenses.

$$
\text{Quick ratio}
=
\frac{
\text{Cash}
+
\text{Marketable securities}
+
\text{Accounts receivable}
}
{\text{Current liabilities}}
$$

An equivalent simplified form may be:

$$
\text{Quick ratio}
=
\frac{
\text{Current assets}
-
\text{Inventory}
-
\text{Prepaid expenses}
}
{\text{Current liabilities}}
$$

### Calculation example

A company reports:

- Current assets: $500,000
- Inventory: $180,000
- Prepaid expenses: $20,000
- Current liabilities: $250,000

Current ratio:

$$
\frac{500{,}000}{250{,}000}
=
2.0
$$

Quick ratio:

$$
