\frac{500{,}000-180{,}000-20{,}000}{250{,}000}
=
1.2
$$

### Interpretation

A current ratio above 1 means reported current assets exceed current liabilities. It does not guarantee timely payment because:

- Inventory may be obsolete;
- Receivables may be uncollectible;
- Current liabilities may mature before assets convert to cash; and
- Seasonal businesses may show large fluctuations.

A very high current ratio is not automatically optimal. It may indicate idle cash, excess inventory, or inefficient use of assets.

### Memory aid

The **quick** ratio removes assets that may not turn into cash quickly.

---

## 3.12 Debt-to-equity ratio

A common balance-sheet form is:

$$
\text{Debt-to-equity}
=
\frac{\text{Total liabilities}}
{\text{Shareholders' equity}}
$$

Some analysts define the numerator more narrowly as interest-bearing debt or long-term debt. Always use the definition specified by the question or source.

### Example

If total liabilities are $600,000 and equity is $400,000:

$$
\frac{600{,}000}{400{,}000}
=
1.5
$$

The company has $1.50 of liabilities for each $1.00 of reported equity.

### Interpretation

Higher leverage may:

- Magnify returns to equity holders when business returns exceed financing costs;
- Magnify losses when business performance weakens;
- Increase default risk;
- Increase sensitivity to interest rates;
- Restrict financial flexibility; and
- Cause creditors to demand higher yields or covenants.

Debt-to-equity comparisons should be made among similar businesses. Utilities, banks, manufacturers, and software companies can have very different normal capital structures.

If equity is very small or negative, the ratio may become unstable or economically meaningless.

---

## 3.13 Price-to-earnings ratio

The **price-to-earnings ratio**, or **P/E**, compares market price per share with earnings per share.

$$
P/E
=
\frac{\text{Market price per share}}
{\text{Earnings per share}}
$$

### Example

A stock sells for $48 and has trailing EPS of $4.

$$
P/E
=
\frac{48}{4}
=
12
$$

Investors are paying $12 for each $1 of reported trailing annual earnings.

### Trailing versus forward P/E

- **Trailing P/E** uses historical earnings.
- **Forward P/E** uses forecast earnings.

Forward P/E depends on estimates and can change sharply when forecasts are revised.

### Interpretation

A high P/E may reflect:

- Expected growth;
- High-quality or stable earnings;
- Low perceived risk;
- Low interest rates; or
- Overvaluation.

A low P/E may reflect:

- Undervaluation;
- Cyclical peak earnings;
- Slow expected growth;
- Financial distress;
- Litigation or regulatory risk; or
- Poor earnings quality.

P/E is generally not meaningful when earnings are zero or negative.

### Exam trap

A lower P/E is not automatically a better investment. Ratios must be interpreted in context.

---

## 3.14 Price-to-book ratio

The **price-to-book ratio**, or **P/B**, compares market price with book value per common share.

A simplified book value per common share is:

$$
BVPS
=
\frac{
\text{Common shareholders' equity}
}{
\text{Common shares outstanding}
}
$$

If preferred equity exists, it is generally removed from equity available to common shareholders:

$$
BVPS
=
\frac{
\text{Total equity}-\text{Preferred equity}
}{
\text{Common shares outstanding}
}
$$

Then:

$$
P/B
=
\frac{\text{Market price per share}}{BVPS}
$$

### Example

A company has:

- Common equity: $200 million
- Common shares: 20 million
- Market price: $15

Book value per share:

$$
\frac{\$200\text{ million}}{20\text{ million}}
=
\$10
$$

Price-to-book ratio:

$$
\frac{\$15}{\$10}
=
1.5
$$

### Interpretation

P/B may be especially relevant for businesses whose balance-sheet assets and liabilities are central to earning power, but it has limitations.

Book value may understate or overstate economic value because:

- Assets are recorded under accounting rules rather than continuously at market value;
- Internally developed intellectual property may not appear as an asset;
- Acquired goodwill may later be impaired;
- Asset quality may be uncertain; and
- Some liabilities or contingencies require judgment.

A P/B below 1 may indicate undervaluation, but it may instead signal that investors expect asset impairments, weak returns, or distress.

---

## 3.15 Ratio-analysis discipline

Never interpret a ratio in isolation. Compare it with:

- The company’s prior periods;
- Direct competitors;
- Industry norms;
- Management guidance;
- Cash flows;
- Accounting policies;
- Economic conditions; and
- The reason the ratio changed.

Ask whether the numerator or denominator created the change.

For example, debt-to-equity can rise because:

- Debt increased;
- Equity fell after losses;
- Shares were repurchased;
- Assets were impaired; or
- Several effects occurred together.

The same numerical ratio can therefore represent different economic stories.

---

## Chapter 4 — Risk Concepts

## 4.1 Systematic risk

**Systematic risk** affects broad portions of the market or economy and cannot ordinarily be eliminated through diversification within the same general capital market.

Examples include:

- Recession;
- Broad interest-rate changes;
- Economy-wide inflation;
- Major financial crises;
- Widespread geopolitical conflict;
- Broad tax or regulatory changes; and
- Marketwide shifts in risk appetite.

Beta is a common measure of systematic market sensitivity.

Diversification can change exposure to systematic risk by changing asset classes or sensitivities, but simply adding more similar securities does not eliminate market risk.

---

## 4.2 Unsystematic risk

**Unsystematic risk** is specific to a company, industry, project, or relatively narrow group.

Examples include:

- A product recall;
- Loss of a major customer;
- A factory fire;
- A management scandal;
- A strike at one company;
- Failure of a clinical trial;
- A company-specific lawsuit; and
- A local supply interruption.

Holding many securities across issuers and industries can reduce unsystematic risk because adverse company-specific events need not affect every holding.

### Diversification principle

Portfolio risk depends not only on the risk of each holding but also on how holdings move together.

A portfolio of 30 companies in one narrowly defined industry may remain heavily exposed to:

- The same regulation;
- The same commodity;
- The same customer base; or
- The same technological disruption.

Number of holdings is not the same as quality of diversification.

### Memory aid

- **Systematic:** system-wide.
- **Unsystematic:** unique.
- **Diversification:** reduces unique risk, not every risk.

### Likely exam trap

A company-specific product failure is unsystematic even if the stock falls dramatically. A broad increase in market interest rates is systematic even if companies are affected by different amounts.

---

## 4.3 Opportunity cost

**Opportunity cost** is the value of the best alternative forgone when a choice is made.

### Example

An investor holds cash earning 1% instead of a Treasury security earning 4% with acceptable maturity and liquidity.

The approximate opportunity cost of the cash choice is:

$$
4\%-1\%=3\%
$$

This does not prove the cash holding is wrong. Cash may provide valuable:

- Liquidity;
- Principal stability;
- Emergency access; or
- Optionality.

Opportunity cost makes the trade-off explicit.

### Investment application

Capital committed to one use cannot simultaneously be used for another. An investment should therefore be compared not merely with earning zero, but with an appropriate alternative having comparable:

- Risk;
- Liquidity;
- Time horizon;
- Tax characteristics; and
- Currency.

### Opportunity cost versus sunk cost

A **sunk cost** has already been incurred and cannot be recovered. Rational forward-looking decisions should focus on future costs and benefits, not on continuing an unsuitable investment merely because money has already been lost.

### Exam trap

Opportunity cost is not necessarily an accounting expense shown on the income statement. It is an economic cost of the forgone alternative.

---

## 4.4 Capital structure

A company’s **capital structure** is the mix of financing claims used to fund its assets and operations.

Major categories include:

- Secured debt;
- Senior unsecured debt;
- Subordinated debt;
- Preferred stock; and
- Common stock.

### Debt

Debt ordinarily provides:

- Contractual interest;
- A stated maturity;
- Priority over equity;
- Possible collateral;
- Covenants; and
- Legal remedies following default.

Interest is generally a contractual obligation. Failure to make required payments may constitute default.

### Preferred stock

Preferred stock is an equity security with priority over common stock for specified dividends and liquidation distributions.

Features may include:

- Fixed or adjustable dividends;
- Cumulative or noncumulative dividends;
- Convertibility;
- Callability; and
- Limited voting rights.

Preferred dividends are not the same as bond interest. Unless legally declared or otherwise required by the instrument, dividend treatment differs from a contractual debt payment.

### Common stock

Common shareholders are residual owners. They generally have:

- Voting rights;
- Potential dividends;
- Unlimited upside in the company’s residual value; and
- The lowest priority among the ordinary classes of capital providers.

Common dividends are discretionary and are paid only after required obligations and any preferred dividend priority are addressed.

---

## 4.5 Leverage and the risk-return trade-off

Debt can increase returns to common shareholders when operating returns exceed borrowing costs.

### Simplified example

A company acquires a $1 million asset that earns 10%, producing $100,000 before financing costs.

#### All-equity financing

If equity is $1 million:

$$
ROE
=
\frac{100{,}000}{1{,}000{,}000}
=
10\%
$$

#### Half debt, half equity

Suppose the company uses:

- $500,000 debt at 6%; and
- $500,000 equity.

Interest is:

$$
500{,}000(6\%)
=
30{,}000
$$

Income before taxes available after interest is:

$$
100{,}000-30{,}000
=
70{,}000
$$

Simplified return on equity:

$$
\frac{70{,}000}{500{,}000}
=
14\%
$$

Leverage increased the equity return from 10% to 14%.

Now suppose the asset earns only 2%, producing $20,000. Interest remains $30,000:

$$
20{,}000-30{,}000
=
-10{,}000
$$

Equity return becomes:

$$
\frac{-10{,}000}{500{,}000}
=
-2\%
$$

Leverage magnifies both favorable and unfavorable outcomes.

### Capital-structure considerations

More debt may provide:

- Lower financing cost than equity;
- No dilution of voting ownership; and
- Potential tax benefits, subject to applicable law.

But it also creates:

- Fixed payment obligations;
- Refinancing risk;
- Covenant restrictions;
- Greater earnings volatility for common holders;
- Default risk; and
- Lower flexibility during downturns.

---

## 4.6 Liquidation priority

When an issuer is liquidated, claims are paid according to contractual seniority and applicable law.

For Series 65 purposes, the simplified order among ordinary security classes is:

1. Secured creditors, to the extent of their collateral claims;
2. Senior unsecured creditors;
3. Subordinated creditors;
4. Preferred shareholders; and
5. Common shareholders.

Actual insolvency proceedings also involve administrative expenses, statutory claims, collateral valuation, and jurisdiction-specific rules. For exam questions comparing securities, focus on the relative ordering above.

### Calculation example

Assume, after relevant administrative and legal claims:

- Available proceeds: $80 million
- Secured debt: $30 million
- Senior unsecured debt: $40 million
- Subordinated debt: $20 million
- Preferred stock: $15 million
- Common stock: residual claim

Distribution:

1. Secured creditors receive $30 million.
2. Senior unsecured creditors receive $40 million.
3. Only $10 million remains for subordinated creditors.
4. Preferred shareholders receive nothing.
5. Common shareholders receive nothing.

Subordinated creditors recover:

$$
\frac{10}{20}
=
50\%
$$

### Priority is not the same as guaranteed payment

A senior claim has priority over junior claims, but it can still suffer loss if the issuer’s assets are insufficient.

A secured creditor also faces:

- Collateral-value risk;
- Legal and enforcement costs;
- Delay;
- Competing liens; and
- Any unsecured deficiency if collateral is inadequate.

### Income priority versus liquidation priority

During ordinary operation, the broad payment hierarchy is:

- Required debt interest before discretionary equity dividends;
- Preferred dividends before common dividends, subject to the security’s terms; and
- Common dividends last.

In liquidation, debt claims precede equity claims.

### Memory aid

**Debt before preferred; preferred before common. Common is the residual claimant.**

---

## Chapter 5 — Integrating Economic and Company Analysis

A strong Series 65 answer follows the chain of cause and effect instead of relying on a memorized slogan.

### Example: inflation and monetary tightening

Suppose inflation remains elevated and the central bank raises short-term rates.

Possible first-order effects:

- Short-term borrowing costs rise;
- Existing fixed-rate bond prices fall;
- Interest-sensitive spending weakens;
- The currency may strengthen if relative yields become more attractive; and
- Future cash flows are discounted at higher rates.

Possible company effects:

- Floating-rate interest expense rises;
- Refinancing becomes more costly;
- Customers reduce financed purchases;
- Inventory turnover may slow;
- Credit losses may rise;
- P/E ratios may contract; and
- Highly leveraged issuers may experience widening credit spreads.

Possible financial-statement evidence:

- Higher interest expense on the income statement;
- Larger debt balances or reduced cash on the balance sheet;
- Weaker operating cash flow;
- More borrowing in financing activities; and
- Expanded liquidity or going-concern disclosures.

The correct conclusion depends on the company. A well-capitalized bank, a highly leveraged property company, and a cash-rich software business will not respond identically.

### Example: stronger domestic currency

A stronger dollar may:

- Reduce the dollar value of foreign investment returns;
- Reduce translated foreign revenue;
- Make imported inputs cheaper;
- Make domestic exports less competitive; and
- Reduce some inflation pressure.

A domestic retailer importing inventory may benefit, while an exporter earning foreign-currency revenue may be harmed. A company hedging its exposure may show a smaller effect.

### Example: recession risk

As contraction risk rises:

- The yield curve may flatten or invert before the downturn;
- Lower-quality credit spreads may widen;
- Employment may weaken with a delay;
- Corporate revenue estimates may fall;
- Receivables and inventory may become less liquid;
- Debt-to-equity may rise if losses reduce equity; and
- Unsystematic company problems may combine with systematic market stress.

A diversified portfolio can reduce company-specific damage, but it cannot remove the broad contraction itself.

### Responsible analytical sequence

1. **Identify the fact.**
   Example: the credit spread widened by 200 basis points.

2. **Identify the likely mechanism.**
   Investors may perceive higher default or liquidity risk.

3. **Check alternatives.**
   Did benchmark maturity, call features, or market liquidity change?

4. **Inspect the issuer.**
   Review debt terms, cash flow, covenants, refinancing schedule, and filings.

5. **Quantify where possible.**
   Recalculate ratios, present values, and scenario outcomes.

6. **State limitations.**
   Avoid presenting estimates as certainties.

---

## Chapter 6 — High-Yield Distinctions and Memory Aids

| Often confused | Correct distinction |
|---|---|
| Expansion vs accelerating growth | Output can expand at a slower rate |
| Inflation vs disinflation | Disinflation is slower price growth |
| Disinflation vs deflation | Deflation is an actual decline in the general price level |
| Monetary vs fiscal policy | Central-bank credit tools vs government taxing and spending |
| Deficit vs debt | Period flow vs accumulated stock |
| Trade deficit vs balance-of-payments deficit | Trade is one component of the broader international accounts |
| Nominal vs real return | Real return adjusts for inflation |
| Bond price vs yield | They move inversely |
| Clean audit opinion vs safe investment | Audit opinion addresses fair presentation, not investment merit |
| 10-K vs 10-Q | Annual audited filing vs quarterly generally unaudited filing |
| Income vs cash flow | Accrual profit can precede or follow cash |
| NPV vs IRR | Dollar value added vs discount rate setting NPV to zero |
| Mean vs median | Mean uses all values; median resists outliers |
| Beta vs standard deviation | Systematic sensitivity vs total volatility |
| Alpha vs Sharpe ratio | Benchmark-adjusted excess vs excess return per unit of total volatility |
| Current vs quick ratio | Quick ratio excludes less liquid current assets |
| P/E vs P/B | Price relative to earnings vs accounting book value |
| Systematic vs unsystematic risk | Marketwide risk vs diversifiable issuer-specific risk |
| Opportunity cost vs sunk cost | Best forgone future alternative vs unrecoverable past cost |
| Bondholder vs shareholder priority | Debt precedes equity |
| Preferred vs common | Preferred has priority; common receives the residual |

### Compact formula sheet

$$
GDP=C+I+G+(X-M)
$$

$$
R_{\text{real}}
=
\frac{1+R_{\text{nominal}}}{1+i}-1
$$

$$
R_{\$}
=
(1+R_{\text{local}})(1+R_{FX})-1
$$

$$
FV=PV(1+r)^n
$$

$$
PV=\frac{FV}{(1+r)^n}
$$

$$
NPV=-C_0+\sum_{t=1}^{n}\frac{C_t}{(1+r)^t}
$$

$$
\bar{x}=\frac{\sum x_i}{n}
$$

$$
\sigma
=
\sqrt{
\frac{\sum(x_i-\mu)^2}{N}
}
$$

$$
E(R_i)=R_f+\beta_i(R_m-R_f)
$$

$$
\alpha_i
=
R_i-
\left[
R_f+\beta_i(R_m-R_f)
\right]
$$

$$
\text{Sharpe}
=
\frac{R_p-R_f}{\sigma_p}
$$

$$
\text{Current ratio}
=
\frac{\text{Current assets}}{\text{Current liabilities}}
$$

$$
\text{Quick ratio}
=
\frac{\text{Cash}+\text{Marketable securities}+\text{Receivables}}
{\text{Current liabilities}}
$$

$$
\text{Debt-to-equity}
=
\frac{\text{Total liabilities}}{\text{Equity}}
$$

$$
P/E
=
\frac{\text{Price per share}}{EPS}
$$

$$
P/B
=
\frac{\text{Price per share}}{\text{Book value per share}}
$$

---

## End-of-Part Questions

### Question 1

The central bank sells government securities and raises its short-term policy-rate target. Which result is most consistent with the intended policy direction?

A. Bank reserves and aggregate demand should increase
B. Credit conditions should tighten and interest-sensitive spending should slow
C. Government tax revenue must decrease
D. Long-term bond prices must rise

### Question 2

The general price level rose 7% last year and 3% this year. Which description is most accurate?

A. Deflation occurred this year
B. Stagflation necessarily occurred
C. Disinflation occurred because prices rose more slowly
D. The purchasing power of money necessarily increased this year

### Question 3

A U.S. investor buys an unhedged foreign security. The security rises 12% in local currency, while the foreign currency declines 10% against the dollar. What is the investor’s approximate dollar return?

A. $-2.0\%$
B. $0.8\%$
C. $2.0\%$
D. $22.0\%$

### Question 4

Which transaction is included in current-period GDP as domestic investment?

A. An investor buys existing shares on a stock exchange
B. A consumer buys a five-year-old car directly from its owner
C. A domestic company builds a new factory during the period
D. A government sends a transfer payment to a retired worker

### Question 5

A software company receives $24,000 on December 1 for 12 months of service beginning immediately. Assuming service is provided evenly and the company uses accrual accounting, how much revenue should it ordinarily recognize through December 31?

A. $0
B. $2,000
C. $12,000
D. $24,000

### Question 6

Which statement is correct?

A. Form 10-Q ordinarily contains annual audited financial statements
B. An unmodified audit opinion guarantees that the issuer is solvent
C. Form 8-K is used to report specified material events
D. An adverse opinion means the auditor could not obtain enough evidence to express any opinion

### Question 7

An investor will receive $10,000 two years from now. If the appropriate annual discount rate is 10%, what is the payment’s present value?

A. $8,264.46
B. $9,000.00
C. $10,000.00
D. $12,100.00

### Question 8

A project costs $50,000 today. The present value of all expected future cash inflows, discounted at the required return, is $54,500. Which conclusion is correct?

A. NPV is $4,500, so the project exceeds the required return under the assumptions
B. NPV is $104,500, so the project should always be accepted
C. IRR must equal $4,500
D. NPV is negative because the project requires an initial outflow

### Question 9

Two portfolios have the following characteristics:

- Portfolio A: return 11%, standard deviation 15%
- Portfolio B: return 9%, standard deviation 8%
- Risk-free rate: 3%

Which statement is correct?

A. Portfolio A has the higher Sharpe ratio because it has the higher return
B. Portfolio B has the higher Sharpe ratio
C. Both portfolios have the same Sharpe ratio
D. Sharpe ratios cannot be calculated without beta

### Question 10

A portfolio earns 12%. The risk-free rate is 2%, the market earns 10%, and the portfolio beta is 1.1. Using the CAPM benchmark, what is the portfolio’s alpha?

A. $-1.2\%$
B. $0\%$
C. $1.2\%$
D. $3.2\%$

### Question 11

A company reports:

- Cash: $50,000
- Marketable securities: $30,000
- Accounts receivable: $70,000
- Inventory: $100,000
- Prepaid expenses: $10,000
- Current liabilities: $100,000

What are the current and quick ratios?

A. Current 1.5; quick 2.6
B. Current 2.6; quick 1.5
C. Current 2.5; quick 1.6
D. Current 1.6; quick 2.5

### Question 12

A company’s product is recalled because of a manufacturing defect. The company later liquidates with insufficient assets to pay every claimant. Which statement is most accurate?

A. The recall is systematic risk, and common shareholders are paid before bondholders
B. The recall is unsystematic risk, and creditors generally have priority over shareholders
C. The recall is opportunity cost, and preferred shareholders have priority over secured creditors
D. The recall is inflation risk, and common and preferred shareholders rank equally

---

## Reasoned Answer Key

### 1. **B — Credit conditions should tighten and interest-sensitive spending should slow.**

Selling securities and raising the policy-rate target are contractionary actions. They are intended to restrain credit and aggregate demand.

- **A** reverses the intended direction.
- **C** concerns fiscal revenue and is not a necessary result of the monetary action.
- **D** is wrong because higher rates generally pressure existing bond prices downward, and “must” is too absolute.

### 2. **C — Disinflation occurred because prices rose more slowly.**

Inflation remained positive at 3%, so the price level generally continued rising, but at a slower rate.

- **A** would require a sustained decline in the general price level.
- **B** cannot be inferred without evidence of weak growth or high unemployment.
- **D** is generally false because positive inflation reduces money’s purchasing power.

### 3. **B — 0.8%.**

Use the multiplicative currency-return formula:

$$
(1.12)(0.90)-1
=
1.008-1
=
0.8\%
$$

- **A** and **C** use simple subtraction and ignore the interaction term.
- **D** incorrectly adds the gains as though both movements helped the investor.

### 4. **C — A domestic company builds a new factory during the period.**

New factory construction is current domestic investment.

- **A** is a transfer of ownership of an existing financial asset.
- **B** concerns a previously produced good, although a current dealer service would be counted if one existed.
- **D** is a transfer payment and is not itself government production, though later spending by the recipient may enter consumption.

### 5. **B — $2,000.**

One month of a 12-month service obligation has been earned:

$$
\frac{\$24{,}000}{12}
=
\$2{,}000
$$

The remaining $22,000 is ordinarily deferred revenue at December 31.

- **A** ignores the month of service already provided.
- **C** has no support in the facts.
- **D** confuses cash receipt with accrual revenue recognition.

### 6. **C — Form 8-K is used to report specified material events.**

- **A** describes the 10-K rather than the quarterly 10-Q.
- **B** is false because an audit provides reasonable assurance about financial-statement presentation, not a solvency guarantee.
- **D** describes a disclaimer more closely. An adverse opinion means the statements are materially and pervasively misstated.

### 7. **A — $8,264.46.**

$$
PV
=
\frac{10{,}000}{(1.10)^2}
=
\frac{10{,}000}{1.21}
=
\$8{,}264.46
$$

- **B** discounts only one year in a noncompound fashion.
- **C** ignores time value.
- **D** is the future value of $10,000 compounded for two years, not the present value of a future payment.

### 8. **A — NPV is $4,500, so the project exceeds the required return under the assumptions.**

$$
NPV
=
54{,}500-50{,}000
=
\$4{,}500
$$

A positive NPV means the discounted inflows exceed the initial cost.

- **B** adds instead of subtracting the cost and uses the unjustified word “always.”
- **C** confuses a dollar NPV with a percentage IRR.
- **D** ignores the larger present value of future inflows.

### 9. **B — Portfolio B has the higher Sharpe ratio.**

Portfolio A:

$$
\frac{11\%-3\%}{15\%}
=
0.53
$$

Portfolio B:

$$
\frac{9\%-3\%}{8\%}
=
0.75
$$

Portfolio B earned more excess return per unit of total volatility.

- **A** focuses only on raw return.
- **C** is contradicted by the calculations.
- **D** is false because the Sharpe ratio uses standard deviation, not beta.

### 10. **C — 1.2%.**

CAPM benchmark return:

$$
2\%+1.1(10\%-2\%)
$$

$$
2\%+1.1(8\%)
=
10.8\%
$$

Alpha:

$$
12\%-10.8\%
=
1.2\%
$$

The portfolio outperformed the model-adjusted return by 1.2 percentage points.

### 11. **B — Current ratio 2.6; quick ratio 1.5.**

Current assets:

$$
50{,}000+30{,}000+70{,}000+100{,}000+10{,}000
=
260{,}000
$$

Current ratio:

$$
\frac{260{,}000}{100{,}000}
=
2.6
$$

Quick assets:

$$
50{,}000+30{,}000+70{,}000
=
150{,}000
$$

Quick ratio:

$$
\frac{150{,}000}{100{,}000}
=
1.5
$$

Inventory and prepaid expenses are excluded from quick assets.

### 12. **B — The recall is unsystematic risk, and creditors generally have priority over shareholders.**

A manufacturing defect at one company is company-specific and therefore unsystematic. In liquidation, creditor claims generally precede preferred and common equity claims.

- **A** misclassifies the risk and reverses priority.
- **C** confuses a realized company problem with the economic concept of opportunity cost and places preferred stock ahead of secured debt.
- **D** misclassifies the risk and ignores preferred stock’s priority over common stock.

---

# Part II — Investment Vehicle Characteristics

## Introduction: Reading an Investment by Its Economic Function

An investment vehicle is a legal and economic package. Its name matters, but the Series 65 primarily tests the package: what the investor owns, how return is generated, where the investor stands in the capital structure, what can cause loss, how readily the position can be converted to cash, and how taxes affect the usable return.

For every vehicle in this part, ask seven questions:

1. **Ownership:** Is the investor a depositor, creditor, residual owner, fund shareholder, contract holder, or counterparty?
2. **Return:** Does return come from interest, dividends, rents, business growth, an index formula, option value, or price appreciation?
3. **Risk:** Is the dominant exposure credit, interest rates, inflation, equity markets, leverage, currency, liquidity, or contract complexity?
4. **Priority:** What claims must be paid before this investor receives anything?
5. **Liquidity:** Can the holder redeem with the issuer, trade on an exchange, negotiate a private sale, or only wait for maturity?
6. **Tax:** Is the return generally interest, a dividend, capital gain, pass-through income, tax-exempt income, or tax-deferred buildup?
7. **Portfolio role:** Is the vehicle reasonably used for reserves, income, growth, inflation protection, diversification, speculation, or hedging?

A product characteristic can create both a benefit and a risk. A callable bond may pay a higher coupon, but the issuer can remove that coupon when rates fall. A closed-end fund can give exchange liquidity to an illiquid strategy, but the shares may trade far below portfolio value. An annuity can defer current taxation, but its expenses, surrender period, insurer credit, and withdrawal tax treatment can reduce its usefulness.

Suitability cues appear throughout this part. They identify characteristics an adviser should notice; they are not substitutes for a complete client analysis.

---

## 1. Cash and Cash Equivalents

### 1.1 What “cash” means in a portfolio

Cash includes currency and balances available for immediate payment, such as demand deposits. A **cash equivalent** is a short-term, highly liquid holding that can ordinarily be converted into a known amount of cash with little price risk. In accounting, that label often refers to instruments acquired with three months or less remaining to maturity. In portfolio conversation, it is sometimes used more broadly, so an adviser should identify the actual instrument rather than rely on the label.

Cash can serve several legitimate functions:

- paying current expenses;
- funding an emergency reserve;
- meeting a known near-term obligation;
- supporting withdrawals during a market decline;
- reducing total portfolio volatility; and
- holding proceeds temporarily while a long-term allocation is implemented.

Cash is not economically riskless. Its major long-horizon risk is **purchasing-power loss**. If an account earns 3% while consumer prices rise 5%, the balance rises in dollars while its real value falls.

A useful approximation is:

$$
\text{Real return} \approx \text{Nominal return} - \text{Inflation rate}
$$

The exact relation is:

$$
1+\text{Real return}
=
\frac{1+\text{Nominal return}}{1+\text{Inflation rate}}
$$

For a 3% nominal return and 5% inflation:

$$
\text{Real return}
=
\frac{1.03}{1.05}-1
\approx -1.90\%
$$

### 1.2 Cash-management tradeoffs

| Objective | Useful characteristic of cash | Countervailing concern |
|---|---|---|
| Emergency reserve | Stable nominal value and immediate access | Return may lag inflation |
| Near-term purchase | Low volatility relative to stocks and long bonds | Opportunity cost if markets rise |
| Retirement distributions | Avoids forced sales during a decline | Excess reserves can weaken long-term growth |
| Tactical reserve | Allows later deployment | Market timing may leave money idle |
| Capital preservation | Small short-term price movement | “Preservation” must be defined in nominal or real terms |

A client who says “I cannot lose any money” may mean no temporary market decline, no permanent loss, no failure to meet a goal, or no loss of purchasing power. Those are different requirements.

### 1.3 Exam cues and traps

- Cash usually has low **nominal volatility**, not zero economic risk.
- A long time horizon generally increases the importance of inflation and opportunity cost.
- A security with a short maturity can be a cash equivalent without being federally insured.
- “Guaranteed” must identify the guarantor and the exact obligation being guaranteed.

> **Memory aid:** Cash controls **volatility**; inflation controls cash’s **purchasing power**.

---

## 2. Insured Deposits and Money-Market Instruments

### 2.1 Bank and credit-union deposits

Common depository products include checking accounts, savings accounts, money market deposit accounts, a **certificate of deposit** (CD), credit-union share accounts, and share certificates.

Eligible deposits at an FDIC-insured bank are generally insured up to **$250,000 per depositor, per insured bank, per ownership category**. Separate legal ownership categories can receive separate coverage when their requirements are satisfied. Merely opening several accounts with different labels at the same bank does not necessarily create additional coverage.

Federally insured credit unions generally receive comparable protection through the National Credit Union Share Insurance Fund, administered by the NCUA.

Deposit insurance addresses failure of the insured institution within applicable limits. It does not protect against:

- inflation;
- an early-withdrawal penalty;
- balances above applicable limits;
- loss on stocks, bonds, mutual funds, or annuities sold at a bank;
- market loss on a negotiable instrument sold before maturity; or
- fraud against an individual account holder.

> **Exam trap:** A bank money market **deposit account** may be FDIC-insured. A money market **mutual fund** is a security, not an insured bank deposit.

### 2.2 Certificates of deposit

A traditional retail CD is a time deposit. It normally provides a stated or formula-based rate, principal at maturity, insurance within applicable limits, and a penalty for early withdrawal.

A **brokered CD** is distributed through a brokerage firm. It can differ materially from a CD obtained directly from a bank:

- the investor may need to sell it in a secondary market rather than redeem early;
- its price can fall when market rates rise;
- the market may be thin;
- the CD may be callable by the bank; and
- insurance depends on the issuing bank, ownership records, and aggregation rules.

A brokered CD can therefore produce a market gain or loss before maturity. A **negotiable CD** is a marketable, often large-denomination bank obligation used in the institutional money market.

### 2.3 The money market

Money-market instruments are short-term debt claims. Short maturity generally reduces interest-rate sensitivity, but it does not make every issuer equally creditworthy or every instrument insured.

| Instrument | Typical issuer or transaction | Economic form | Important risks |
|---|---|---|---|
| Treasury bill | U.S. Treasury | Short-term government debt, commonly issued at discount | Inflation and small market-price risk; very low credit risk |
| Commercial paper | Corporation | Unsecured short-term note | Issuer credit and refinancing risk |
| Negotiable CD | Bank | Marketable time deposit | Bank credit, liquidity, rate risk, limited insurance |
| Banker’s acceptance | Bank-supported trade transaction | Bank’s promise to pay at maturity | Bank and transaction credit risk |
| Repurchase agreement | Dealer or financial institution | Collateralized borrowing through sale and repurchase | Counterparty, collateral, and legal risk |
| Federal funds | Depository institution | Usually overnight reserve-balance loan | Counterparty risk |
| Municipal note | State or local issuer | Short-term public financing | Fiscal, revenue, and tax risk |
| Eurodollar deposit | Bank outside the United States or offshore branch | Dollar deposit outside the domestic banking system | Bank, country, and regulatory risk |

#### Treasury bills

Treasury bills have original maturities of one year or less. Many are purchased below face value and mature at face value. Treasury interest is subject to federal income tax but is generally exempt from state and local income taxes.

#### Commercial paper

Commercial paper is generally unsecured corporate borrowing used for payroll, inventory, receivables, and other working-capital needs. High-quality paper may have low default risk, but investors rely on the company’s capacity to pay or refinance. It has neither deposit insurance nor the full-faith-and-credit backing of the United States.

#### Banker’s acceptances

A banker’s acceptance often finances international trade. A bank accepts responsibility for payment at a future date, allowing the obligation to trade substantially on the bank’s credit.

#### Repurchase agreements

In a repurchase agreement, one party sells securities and agrees to repurchase them later at a higher price. Economically, the seller has borrowed cash and pledged securities as collateral. From the cash provider’s side, the transaction is often called a reverse repo.

Risk remains if the counterparty fails, collateral value is inadequate, the collateral cannot be liquidated promptly, or the legal right to collateral is defective.

### 2.4 Money market mutual funds

A money market mutual fund is an open-end investment company holding short-term debt. It may provide transfer or check-writing features, but it is not a checking account.

Key points:

- It is a security regulated under investment-company rules.
- It seeks liquidity and principal stability.
- Some funds seek a stable $1 NAV; certain institutional funds use a floating NAV.
- A stable-value objective is not an unconditional guarantee.
- It is not protected by FDIC or NCUA deposit insurance.
- Government, Treasury, prime, and municipal funds hold different portfolios and present different credit and tax profiles.

### 2.5 Tax-equivalent yield

A tax-exempt yield must be compared with the after-tax yield on a taxable alternative. For an instrument exempt from federal income tax:

$$
\text{Tax-equivalent yield}
=
\frac{\text{Tax-exempt yield}}{1-\text{Marginal tax rate}}
$$

If a municipal money market fund yields 3.2% and the investor’s marginal federal rate is 32%:

$$
\text{Tax-equivalent yield}
=
\frac{0.032}{1-0.32}
=4.71\%
$$

Ignoring state tax and special rules, the investor would need about 4.71% on a fully taxable instrument to equal the 3.2% exempt yield.

### 2.6 Suitability cues

Insured deposits and high-quality money-market holdings can fit emergency funds, imminent purchases, temporary reserves, and low tolerance for price fluctuation. They are generally poor substitutes for diversified long-term growth assets when the client must outpace inflation over decades.

---

## 3. Fixed-Income Security Types

### 3.1 The creditor relationship

A bond investor is normally a creditor. The issuer promises interest, principal, or both under a contract. “Fixed income” does not mean fixed market value, assured profit, or absence of default.

### 3.2 U.S. Treasury securities

| Treasury security | Original maturity | Typical cash-flow pattern |
|---|---:|---|
| Treasury bill | One year or less | Issued at discount or with short-term interest convention |
| Treasury note | More than one through ten years | Semiannual coupon |
| Treasury bond | More than ten years | Semiannual coupon |
| TIPS | Various intermediate and long terms | Coupon on inflation-adjusted principal |
| STRIPS | Derived from separated Treasury payments | One zero-coupon cash flow at maturity |

Treasuries are backed by the full faith and credit of the United States. They have exceptionally low credit risk, but prices still respond to interest rates, inflation expectations, and supply and demand. An investor selling before maturity can realize a loss.

A Treasury STRIP is a separately traded principal or interest payment from an eligible Treasury security. It is bought at a discount and pays no periodic cash interest. In a taxable account, annual imputed interest can be taxable even though cash is not received until sale or maturity—an example of **phantom income**.

### 3.3 Federal agency and government-sponsored enterprise debt

Government-related issuers finance housing, agriculture, education, and other policy areas. Familiar housing-market names include **Fannie Mae** and **Freddie Mac**; Ginnie Mae has a different legal status and form of federal support. The critical distinction is the source of credit support. Some obligations have an explicit federal guarantee; others are obligations of a government-sponsored enterprise and lack the same explicit full-faith-and-credit promise. Tax treatment can also differ by issuer.

The label “agency security” therefore does not make every issue equivalent to a Treasury.

### 3.4 Mortgage-backed securities

A mortgage-backed security, or MBS, represents an interest in cash flows from a pool of mortgage loans. Borrowers’ principal and interest pass through to investors after servicing and other charges.

#### Prepayment risk

When mortgage rates fall, borrowers tend to refinance. Principal returns sooner, forcing the investor to reinvest at lower rates and removing an above-market stream of payments.

#### Extension risk

When rates rise, refinancing slows. Principal returns later, extending the holding period just when the investor would prefer to reinvest at higher rates.

#### Negative convexity

An ordinary noncallable bond generally gains more as yields fall. An MBS may not: lower rates accelerate prepayments and limit appreciation. Its expected cash flows change in an unfavorable direction for the investor.

#### Credit structure

Agency and nonagency MBS do not have identical credit support. Nonagency securities can expose investors more directly to borrower defaults, underwriting quality, geographic concentration, home prices, and tranche subordination.

A **collateralized mortgage obligation**, or CMO, redirects mortgage cash flows among tranches with different payment priorities and expected lives. Tranching reallocates prepayment and extension exposure; it does not abolish them.

### 3.5 Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities

TIPS principal adjusts with changes in the Consumer Price Index. The stated coupon rate is fixed, but the dollar coupon changes because it is applied to adjusted principal.

Suppose a TIPS has $1,000 original principal, a 2% coupon, and a 3% inflation adjustment. Adjusted principal is $1,030, and annual coupon interest becomes:

$$
\$1{,}030 \times 0.02 = \$20.60
$$

TIPS help address measured inflation but do not eliminate risk:

- CPI may not match the investor’s personal expenses;
- price can fall when real yields rise;
- inflation adjustments generally create current federal taxable income;
- deflation can reduce adjusted principal, subject to Treasury maturity rules; and
- longer TIPS can have substantial duration.

### 3.6 Asset-backed securities

Asset-backed securities, or ABS, are supported by pools of nonmortgage receivables such as automobile loans, credit-card balances, student loans, equipment leases, and other contractual payments.

Analysis can require attention to borrower defaults, collateral quality, payment speed, servicing, credit enhancement, subordination, and liquidity. Securitization changes the legal form and distributes risk among tranches; it does not erase weakness in the underlying assets.

### 3.7 Corporate bonds

Corporate bonds finance operations, acquisitions, and capital projects. Major forms include:

- **secured bonds**, supported by specified collateral;
- **mortgage bonds**, secured by real property;
- **equipment trust obligations**, supported by equipment;
- **debentures**, unsecured general obligations;
- **subordinated debentures**, junior to senior debt;
- **floating-rate notes**, whose coupons reset to a reference rate;
- **convertible bonds**, exchangeable for common shares; and
- **high-yield bonds**, carrying below-investment-grade credit ratings.

Corporate yields normally exceed Treasury yields because investors demand compensation for default, liquidity, call, and spread risk.

### 3.8 Municipal securities

Municipal securities are issued by states, territories, political subdivisions, and public authorities.

A **general obligation bond** is supported by the issuer’s taxing power. Analysis may include the tax base, employment, population, debt burden, budget practices, pension obligations, and legal constraints.

A **revenue bond** relies primarily on income from a facility, enterprise, project, or dedicated revenue source. Examples include toll roads, airports, hospitals, utilities, and water systems. Analysis may focus on demand, rate-setting power, operating costs, competition, debt-service coverage, and covenants.

Interest on many municipal bonds is exempt from federal income tax, but important qualifications apply:

- capital gains are generally taxable;
- some private-activity bond interest can affect alternative minimum tax;
- state and local treatment varies;
- an out-of-state issue may not receive a home-state exemption; and
- original-issue and market-discount rules can alter taxation.

> **Exam trap:** “Tax-exempt bond” usually describes qualifying interest, not every form of gain and not every tax jurisdiction.

### 3.9 Foreign fixed-income securities

Foreign government and corporate bonds can add currency, sovereign, political, disclosure, settlement, withholding-tax, capital-control, and liquidity risks. A dollar-denominated foreign bond removes direct exchange-rate fluctuation from its contractual dollar payments, but country and issuer risks remain. A foreign-currency bond adds the possibility that favorable local-bond performance is offset by currency depreciation.

### 3.10 Type comparison

| Security | Primary credit source | Distinctive exposure | Broad federal tax concept |
|---|---|---|---|
| Treasury | U.S. government | Rates and inflation | Federal taxable; generally state/local exempt |
| Agency or GSE | Agency or enterprise | Support varies by issuer | Varies by issuer |
| MBS | Mortgage pool and any guarantor | Prepayment and extension | Interest generally taxable |
| TIPS | U.S. government | Real yields and taxable inflation adjustment | Federal taxable; generally state/local exempt |
| ABS | Receivable pool and structure | Collateral, servicing, tranche priority | Generally taxable |
| Corporate | Corporate issuer | Default, spread, call, liquidity | Interest generally ordinary income |
| Municipal | Public issuer or project revenues | Fiscal or project risk | Interest may be federally exempt |
| Foreign | Foreign public or private issuer | Currency, country, law | Depends on investor and jurisdiction |

## 4. Fixed-Income Characteristics and Valuation

### 4.1 Bond anatomy

A conventional bond has several core terms:

- **Par value:** the principal amount, often $1,000.
- **Coupon rate:** annual stated interest as a percentage of par.
- **Coupon payment:** periodic cash interest.
- **Maturity:** the date principal is due.
- **Indenture:** the contract governing the debt.
- **Trustee:** the party acting for bondholders under the indenture.
- **Market price:** the amount at which the bond currently trades.
- **Yield:** a return measure based on price and cash flows.

A $1,000 bond with a 6% coupon pays:

$$
\text{Annual coupon}=\$1{,}000\times 0.06=\$60
$$

With semiannual payments, each coupon is $30.

### 4.2 Price and yield move in opposite directions

**Fixed-income discounted cash flow** analysis values each promised payment at a market-required rate. A bond’s promised cash flows are ordinarily fixed. If the market demands a higher return, those cash flows are discounted more heavily and their present value falls. If the required return falls, present value rises.

> **Core rule:** Bond prices and market yields move in opposite directions.

For a $1,000 one-year zero-coupon bond at a 5% required yield:

$$
P=\frac{\$1{,}000}{1.05}=\$952.38
$$

If required yield rises to 8%:

$$
P=\frac{\$1{,}000}{1.08}=\$925.93
$$

The promised $1,000 did not change. The market’s discount rate changed.

The value of a conventional coupon bond is:

$$
P
=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\frac{C}{(1+y/m)^t}
+
\frac{F}{(1+y/m)^n}
$$

where $P$ is price, $C$ is each coupon payment, $F$ is face value, $y$ is annual required yield, $m$ is payment periods per year, and $n$ is total payment periods.

### 4.3 Premium, par, and discount

| Price | Relationship | Standard yield implication |
|---|---|---|
| Above par | Premium | Coupon rate exceeds YTM |
| At par | Par | Coupon rate equals YTM |
| Below par | Discount | Coupon rate is below YTM |

For an ordinary positive-coupon, noncallable bond:

- At a **discount**: coupon rate $<$ current yield $<$ YTM.
- At a **premium**: coupon rate $>$ current yield $>$ YTM.
- At **par**: coupon rate = current yield = YTM.

Assuming no default or unusual feature, price tends toward par as maturity approaches. This is **pull to par**.

### 4.4 Current yield

Current yield compares annual coupon income with current market price:

$$
\text{Current yield}
=
\frac{\text{Annual coupon}}{\text{Current market price}}
$$

A $1,000-par 6% bond pays $60. At $960:

$$
\text{Current yield}
=
\frac{\$60}{\$960}
=6.25\%
$$

Current yield omits the gain or loss between purchase price and redemption value, coupon reinvestment, and call provisions. It is incomplete as a total-return measure.

### 4.5 Yield to maturity, yield to call, and yield to worst

**Yield to maturity**, or YTM, is the discount rate equating price with promised cash flows through maturity. Its standardized calculation generally assumes no default, holding to maturity, and reinvestment of coupons at the computed yield. The reinvestment assumption may not occur, so YTM is not guaranteed realized return.

**Yield to call**, or YTC, assumes redemption on a specified call date at the applicable call price. A premium callable bond can display a high coupon and a respectable YTM but a substantially lower YTC. If rates have fallen, the call scenario can be more realistic.

**Yield to worst** is the lowest yield among specified contractual redemption scenarios, assuming no default. It counters the temptation to quote only the most favorable yield.

### 4.6 Calls, puts, and sinking funds

A **call provision** permits the issuer to redeem debt before maturity. The issuer tends to call high-coupon debt after rates decline and refinance more cheaply. The investor faces reinvestment risk, limited price appreciation, and an uncertain holding period. A call premium only partially compensates for these disadvantages.

A **put provision** lets the holder require repayment on stated dates or after specified events. It generally benefits the investor and can reduce price sensitivity.

A **sinking fund** requires periodic retirement of part of an issue. It can support credit quality by reducing the final payment, but selected holders may have their bonds retired earlier than expected.

### 4.7 Coupon and zero-coupon bonds

A coupon bond distributes periodic interest. A zero-coupon bond is bought at a large discount and pays face value at maturity.

A zero can match a known future liability and has no coupon-reinvestment risk. It also has high interest-rate sensitivity, no current cash income, issuer credit exposure, and possible annual tax on imputed interest. A zero-coupon bond’s Macaulay duration equals its maturity because all value is received in one final cash flow.

### 4.8 Duration intuition

Duration summarizes the weighted timing of cash flows and provides a first-order estimate of interest-rate sensitivity. Modified duration gives the approximation:

$$
\%\Delta P
\approx
-\text{Modified duration}\times\Delta y
$$

If modified duration is 6 and yield rises 0.50 percentage point:

$$
\%\Delta P
\approx
-6\times0.005=-3\%
$$

An equal yield decline suggests an approximately 3% gain. The estimate is less precise for large rate changes or securities whose expected cash flows change with rates.

All else equal:

- longer maturity increases duration;
- lower coupon increases duration;
- lower yield increases duration;
- a zero has more duration than a coupon bond of the same maturity; and
- calls or mortgage prepayments can alter effective duration.

> **Memory aid:** **Longer, lower, later** means more rate sensitivity: longer maturity, lower coupon, later cash flows.

**Convexity** measures curvature. An ordinary option-free bond has positive convexity: a given yield decline produces a somewhat larger gain than the loss caused by an equal yield increase. Callable bonds and MBS can have negative convexity in relevant ranges because declining rates trigger redemption or prepayment.

### 4.9 Maturity and reinvestment

Longer bonds ordinarily have greater price sensitivity because principal is distant. Shorter instruments have less duration but can have greater reinvestment risk because cash must be reinvested sooner and more often.

A bond ladder staggers maturities, combining periodic liquidity and reinvestment opportunities. It does not eliminate default, inflation, or market risk.

### 4.10 Priority and liquidation

A simplified capital structure is:

1. secured senior debt;
2. unsecured senior debt;
3. subordinated debt;
4. preferred stock; and
5. common stock.

Actual recovery depends on collateral value, contractual subordination, bankruptcy costs, legal claims, and the issuer’s assets. Higher priority improves the claim but never guarantees full recovery.

### 4.11 Bond ratings and credit spreads

**Bond ratings** published by credit-rating agencies express opinions about an issuer’s capacity to pay. **Investment grade** is generally BBB− or Baa3 and above; **below investment grade** is generally BB+ or Ba1 and below.

A rating:

- is an opinion, not a guaranty;
- can lag market information;
- does not measure duration;
- does not assure liquidity; and
- may differ among agencies.

A downgrade usually pressures price because investors demand more yield. A **credit spread** measures extra yield over a benchmark of comparable maturity:

$$
\text{Credit spread}=\text{Bond yield}-\text{Benchmark yield}
$$

If a corporate bond yields 7.2% and a similar Treasury yields 4.5%, the spread is 2.7 percentage points, or 270 basis points.

Spread widening may reflect weaker issuer credit, recession concern, illiquidity, sector stress, or general risk aversion. If the benchmark is unchanged, a wider spread means a lower bond price.

### 4.12 Liquidity, inflation, and tax

A sound issuer’s bond can still be hard to sell. Small issues, unusual structures, private placements, distressed bonds, and infrequently traded municipals can have wide bid-ask spreads or stale quotations.

Fixed nominal payments lose purchasing power during inflation. Floating-rate notes reduce some rate exposure but retain credit risk and may have reset caps or imperfect reference rates. TIPS address measured inflation but retain real-rate and market risk.

| Cash-flow source | Broad federal tax concept |
|---|---|
| Corporate interest | Ordinary income |
| Treasury interest | Federal taxable; generally state/local exempt |
| Qualifying municipal interest | May be federally exempt |
| Taxable original-issue discount | Generally accrued before cash receipt |
| Sale or redemption difference | May create capital gain/loss or special discount treatment |
| TIPS inflation adjustment | Generally current federal taxable income |
| Foreign interest | Generally taxable; withholding or foreign-tax-credit issues can arise |

### 4.13 Convertible bonds

A convertible bond can be exchanged for a stated number of common shares.

$$
\text{Conversion ratio}
=
\frac{\text{Par value}}{\text{Conversion price}}
$$

For a $1,000 bond convertible at $40:

$$
\text{Conversion ratio}=\frac{\$1{,}000}{\$40}=25\text{ shares}
$$

If common stock is $44:

$$
\text{Conversion value}=25\times\$44=\$1{,}100
$$

A bond trading at $1,155 has a $55 premium over conversion value. Convertibles combine debt value and an equity option, and usually pay less interest than comparable nonconvertible debt. They can lose value because rates rise, credit weakens, or the stock falls. The analytical “bond floor” is not a contractual guarantee.

### 4.14 Fixed-income suitability cues

Fixed income can support income, capital preservation, diversification, and liability matching, but structure matters:

- Long duration is usually a poor match for a near-term spending need.
- High-yield debt is not a cash substitute.
- A premium callable issue can disappoint an investor expecting years of high coupons.
- A tax-exempt bond may waste its advantage inside a tax-deferred account.
- A foreign-currency bond may conflict with a need for stable dollar principal.
- Employer bonds can compound career and portfolio concentration.

---

## 5. Equity Types and Shareholder Features

### 5.1 Common stock

Common stock represents residual ownership. It can provide voting rights, dividends when declared, appreciation, and a claim on residual assets after senior obligations. Shareholders generally have limited liability, so their maximum direct loss is the amount invested, while upside is theoretically unlimited.

Common dividends are not contractual. A profitable board can retain earnings, and an unprofitable company can temporarily fund a dividend from existing cash. Dividend history does not guarantee continuation.

### 5.2 Domestic and foreign shares

A domestic company can have international economic exposure through foreign sales and operations. Direct foreign equity adds potential currency, political, accounting, withholding-tax, settlement, disclosure, capital-control, and custody risks.

An **American depositary receipt**, or ADR, is issued by a depositary bank and represents foreign shares. ADRs trade and settle in dollars, but they retain the foreign issuer’s business and country exposure and may carry depositary fees. Sponsored and unsponsored programs differ in issuer participation and disclosure.

### 5.3 Preferred stock

Preferred stock is equity with debt-like features. It generally ranks ahead of common stock for dividends and liquidation but behind creditors.

| Preferred feature | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Cumulative | Omitted dividends accumulate before common dividends resume |
| Noncumulative | Omitted dividends do not accumulate |
| Participating | Holder may receive extra distributions under stated conditions |
| Callable | Issuer may redeem the shares |
| Convertible | Holder may exchange into common shares |
| Adjustable-rate | Dividend changes according to a formula |
| Perpetual | No stated maturity |

Preferred shares often have limited voting rights. A fixed dividend makes them rate-sensitive, while issuer weakness can impair both dividend and principal. A call feature lets the issuer redeem an expensive dividend after rates fall. **Convertible preferred stock** adds a holder’s right to exchange preferred shares for common shares under stated terms, combining preferred dividend and liquidation-priority features with potential common-equity upside. After conversion, the investor gives up preferred priority and generally becomes exposed to common-share dilution; issuance or conversion of additional shares can also dilute existing common holders.

> **Exam trap:** Cumulative dividends in arrears have priority over common dividends, but they are not identical to a bondholder’s contractual interest claim.

### 5.4 Voting and control

Under **statutory voting**, votes are cast separately for each director position. Under **cumulative voting**, a shareholder can concentrate director votes, potentially helping minority holders elect representation.

Companies can issue classes with different voting power. One class may have one vote per share, another superior founder voting rights, and another no ordinary vote. Economic ownership and control need not be proportional.

### 5.5 Preemptive and antidilution rights

A **preemptive right** permits an existing holder to purchase a proportional part of a new issuance, helping preserve percentage ownership. The right must be created by law or corporate documents; it is not universal.

**Antidilution protection** adjusts conversion or exercise terms for specified events such as stock splits, stock dividends, or certain below-price issuances. It protects a defined mathematical relationship. It does not prevent all dilution, compensate for poor business performance, or stop market-price declines.

### 5.6 Liquidation priority

Creditors stand ahead of equity. Preferred owners stand ahead of common owners. Common shareholders receive only residual value. A company can have valuable operations but leave common holders with nothing if its assets cannot satisfy senior claims.

### 5.7 Restricted stock and restricted securities

“Restricted stock” often refers to compensation shares subject to vesting or forfeiture. Generally, absent a special election, compensation income is recognized when vesting occurs, based on fair market value less any amount paid.

A timely **Section 83(b) election** can cause tax at transfer rather than vesting. This may be beneficial if value later rises, but harmful if shares fall or are forfeited because tax was accelerated.

“Restricted securities” can also mean privately acquired securities subject to resale limits. Public resale may require a holding period, current information, volume restrictions, legal review, or another exemption. Limited transferability reduces liquidity and can justify a valuation discount.

### 5.8 Dividends and important dates

The typical sequence is:

1. **Declaration date:** the board announces a dividend.
2. **Ex-dividend date:** a buyer on or after this date does not receive the upcoming distribution.
3. **Record date:** the corporation identifies holders entitled to payment.
4. **Payable date:** cash or property is distributed.

Price may adjust downward by approximately the dividend on the ex-date, although market movement can obscure the effect.

A cash distribution may be a qualified dividend, nonqualified dividend, return of capital, or liquidating distribution. Qualified dividends can receive preferential federal rates if applicable requirements are met. Return of capital generally reduces tax basis before producing gain.

A stock dividend changes share count without itself creating wealth. If 100 shares at $50 receive a 10% stock dividend, the investor owns 110 shares, but price should adjust approximately so total value remains unchanged absent other market changes.

### 5.9 Incentive and nonqualified stock options

An **incentive stock option**, or ISO, is an employee option satisfying statutory rules. Generally there is no regular ordinary income at exercise, but the spread can be an alternative-minimum-tax adjustment. If shares are held more than one year after exercise and at least two years after grant, a qualifying sale generally receives capital-gain treatment. An earlier sale is a disqualifying disposition and can create ordinary compensation income.

For a typical **nonqualified stock option**, or NQSO, the exercise spread is ordinary compensation income and usually subject to withholding. Later movement from the exercise-date value is capital gain or loss when the shares are sold.

| Feature | ISO | NQSO |
|---|---|---|
| Typical recipient | Employee | Employee or another service provider |
| Regular tax at exercise | Generally none; possible AMT adjustment | Ordinary compensation on spread |
| Potential treatment at sale | Full appreciation can become capital gain if holding tests are met | Post-exercise change is capital |
| Employer deduction | Generally associated with a disqualifying disposition | Generally associated with compensation income |

Employer shares and options create a special concentration concern: salary, benefits, career prospects, and investments can depend on the same company.

### 5.10 Equity suitability cues

Common stock can support long-horizon growth but has no promised maturity value. Preferred stock can support income but can combine rate sensitivity, call exposure, weak growth, and equity subordination. Foreign shares may improve diversification while introducing currency and jurisdictional risks. Restricted shares and employee options can be illiquid and concentrated. The proper question is not “Is equity good?” but “Which claim, on which issuer, at what valuation, in what amount, for what client need?”

## 6. Equity Valuation

### 6.1 Price is not value

Market price is the amount at which willing buyers and sellers currently transact. **Intrinsic value** is an analyst’s estimate based on future benefits and risk. Because future cash flows and discount rates are uncertain, valuation is a range of reasoned estimates rather than a fact waiting to be discovered.

A strong company can be a poor investment at an excessive price. A weak company can appear statistically cheap because its earnings, assets, or dividend are deteriorating. The Series 65 distinction is between methods and inputs, not a claim that one method forecasts perfectly.

### 6.2 Fundamental analysis

Fundamental analysis examines information such as:

- sales growth and customer concentration;
- gross, operating, and net profit margins;
- free cash flow;
- assets, liabilities, and leverage;
- competitive advantages and industry structure;
- management and governance;
- economic growth, interest rates, and inflation; and
- the price paid relative to expected benefits.

A **top-down** analyst begins with the economy, then industry, then company. A **bottom-up** analyst begins with company-specific prospects and valuation.

Common measures include:

$$
\text{Earnings per share}
=
\frac{\text{Net income available to common}}{\text{Weighted-average common shares}}
$$

$$
\text{Price/earnings ratio}
=
\frac{\text{Market price per share}}{\text{Earnings per share}}
$$

$$
\text{Dividend yield}
=
\frac{\text{Annual dividend per share}}{\text{Market price per share}}
$$

$$
\text{Book value per share}
=
\frac{\text{Common shareholders' equity}}{\text{Common shares outstanding}}
$$

$$
\text{Return on equity}
=
\frac{\text{Net income}}{\text{Average common equity}}
$$

Each ratio needs context:

- A low P/E can mean undervaluation or expected earnings collapse.
- A high dividend yield can result from a plunging price before a dividend cut.
- High return on equity can reflect operating excellence or unusually heavy debt.
- Book value may be informative for an asset-heavy bank but less so for a software company built on human and intellectual capital.
- Negative earnings make a conventional P/E unhelpful.

Analysts often compare an issuer with its own history, direct competitors, an industry, and the broad market.

### 6.3 Dividend discount model

The dividend discount model values common stock as the present value of expected dividends. Under constant perpetual growth:

$$
P_0=\frac{D_1}{k-g}
$$

where $P_0$ is estimated value now, $D_1$ is next year’s expected dividend, $k$ is required return, and $g$ is perpetual growth.

If the expected next dividend is $3, required return is 10%, and perpetual growth is 4%:

$$
P_0=\frac{\$3}{0.10-0.04}=\$50
$$

The model requires $k>g$. It is highly sensitive to its assumptions. If required return rises to 11%, estimated value becomes:

$$
\frac{\$3}{0.11-0.04}=\$42.86
$$

It is most intuitive for mature dividend-paying firms. It is less directly useful for a company that pays no dividend or whose distributions are unstable.

### 6.4 Discounted cash-flow valuation

An **equity discounted cash flow** valuation estimates present value from expected future cash. A general discounted-cash-flow model is:

$$
\text{Value}
=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}\frac{CF_t}{(1+r)^t}
+
\frac{\text{Terminal value}}{(1+r)^n}
$$

The analyst may use dividends, free cash flow to equity, or free cash flow to the firm. When valuing an enterprise, debt, cash, preferred claims, and other adjustments are needed to reach common-equity value.

DCF is conceptually rigorous because it connects value to cash. Its weakness is input uncertainty. Forecast growth, margins, reinvestment, discount rates, and terminal value can change the answer dramatically. A long spreadsheet does not eliminate estimation risk.

### 6.5 Relative valuation

Relative valuation compares price with a financial measure or peer group. Common multiples include:

- price/earnings;
- price/book value;
- price/sales;
- price/free cash flow; and
- enterprise value/EBITDA.

A lower multiple is not automatically better. Differences can be justified by growth, profitability, leverage, cyclicality, accounting quality, governance, or risk. The method also fails when an entire comparison group is overvalued or undervalued.

### 6.6 Technical analysis

Technical analysis studies market-generated information, especially price and trading volume. Concepts include:

- **trend:** persistent general direction;
- **support:** a zone where buying historically emerged;
- **resistance:** a zone where selling historically emerged;
- **moving average:** a smoothed average of recent prices;
- **breakout:** movement beyond a prior range;
- **relative strength:** performance compared with another asset or benchmark;
- **breadth:** participation of securities in a market move; and
- **volume confirmation:** increased activity supporting a price move.

Technical analysis emphasizes supply, demand, and behavior rather than business cash flows. Historical patterns can fail, and frequent trading can increase costs and taxes.

| Fundamental analysis | Technical analysis |
|---|---|
| Estimates economic or intrinsic value | Studies price, volume, and market behavior |
| Uses financial statements and economic data | Uses market-generated data |
| Often emphasizes long-term ownership | Often informs timing and trend decisions |
| Depends on business and discount-rate assumptions | Depends on behavioral and pattern assumptions |

The approaches are not mutually exclusive. An adviser might use fundamentals to choose a security and technical evidence to manage entry or risk.

### 6.7 Valuation traps

- A price decline does not by itself make a stock cheap.
- A high share price does not mean a company is expensive; valuation depends on per-share economics and share count.
- A stock split changes units, not total enterprise value.
- Past earnings growth does not guarantee future growth.
- A model’s precision can exceed the reliability of its assumptions.
- Diversification reduces issuer-specific risk; it does not correct overpayment for an entire market.

---

## 7. Offerings: IPOs, Follow-Ons, and Blank-Check Companies

### 7.1 Initial public offerings

An initial public offering, or IPO, is an issuer’s first registered public sale of equity. The process commonly includes preparation of registration materials, due diligence, investor marketing, pricing, allocation, and exchange or market trading.

An offering can contain:

- **primary shares**, sold by the company, with proceeds going to the issuer; and
- **secondary shares**, sold by existing holders, with proceeds going to those holders.

IPO risks include:

- little public-company history;
- uncertain valuation;
- a small public float;
- allocation conflicts;
- volatile early trading;
- expiration of insider lockups;
- optimistic projections;
- concentrated founder voting power; and
- an underdeveloped secondary market.

A registration statement and prospectus provide disclosure. SEC effectiveness does not mean the SEC has approved the investment’s merits or guaranteed the truth of every assertion.

### 7.2 Follow-on and secondary offerings

A public issuer may later sell additional shares in a follow-on offering. Newly issued shares raise company capital but dilute existing percentage ownership. Dilution is not necessarily destructive: if the company invests new capital at attractive returns, value per share can ultimately increase.

“Secondary offering” can also describe existing holders selling their shares. The issuer receives no proceeds from those selling-holder shares.

> **Exam trap:** A **secondary offering** is a distribution of shares. The **secondary market** is the trading market for previously issued securities.

### 7.3 Rights offerings and private placements

A rights offering gives existing holders transferable or nontransferable rights to buy shares, often at a subscription price below market. It can raise capital while allowing owners to preserve proportional ownership. A holder who neither exercises nor sells a valuable transferable right may suffer avoidable dilution.

A private placement is offered under an exemption from public registration. It can involve limited disclosure, transfer restrictions, investor-qualification standards, concentration, valuation uncertainty, and little liquidity. Exemption from registration does not mean exemption from antifraud law.

### 7.4 Blank-check and blind-pool companies

A blank-check or blind-pool company raises money before identifying a complete operating plan or specific acquisition. Investors depend heavily on the sponsor’s skill, integrity, incentives, and discipline.

Risks include uncertain use of proceeds, limited operating history, sponsor conflicts, dilution, speculative acquisitions, and incomplete information at the time of investment.

### 7.5 SPACs

A special purpose acquisition company, or SPAC, is a blank-check company created to conduct an IPO and later combine with an operating business.

A common sequence is:

1. A sponsor forms the SPAC.
2. The SPAC sells units, often containing common shares and warrants.
3. IPO proceeds are placed in a trust account.
4. The sponsor searches for a target within a stated period.
5. Investors receive disclosures and consider the proposed business combination.
6. Public shareholders may have redemption rights under the governing terms.
7. The combination closes, producing a public operating company, or the SPAC liquidates if it fails to complete a transaction in time.

Important risks include:

- sponsor promote and compensation;
- warrant and financing dilution;
- conflicts in target selection;
- deadline pressure to complete a transaction;
- different incentives for sponsors, redeeming holders, and continuing holders;
- uncertain valuation of the post-merger business;
- transaction expenses and reduced trust cash after redemptions; and
- weak post-combination performance.

Trust and redemption provisions can reduce certain risks. They do not make SPAC securities bank deposits or assure that an investor buying in the market can recover the purchase price.

### 7.6 Offering suitability cues

New offerings can be appropriate for investors who understand uncertainty, tolerate volatility, and can absorb loss. They are generally a weak match for emergency reserves or investors who equate a new listing with a government-endorsed investment. Restricted offerings demand particular attention to liquidity, valuation, disclosure, and investor qualification.

---

## 8. Pooled Investments: Legal Structures and Trading Mechanics

### 8.1 Why investors pool capital

Pooling can provide diversification, professional management, administration, custody, and access to markets or strategies that would be difficult to reproduce individually. It can also create expenses, tax distributions, tracking differences, liquidity mismatches, and less control over individual holdings.

### 8.2 Open-end mutual funds

An open-end investment company continuously issues and redeems shares. A properly submitted purchase or redemption receives the **next** computed net asset value, with any applicable sales charge. This is forward pricing.

Core characteristics include:

- transactions with the fund rather than intraday exchange trading;
- NAV generally calculated once each business day;
- redeemability at NAV, subject to lawful restrictions and fees;
- professional management or index tracking;
- operating expenses and possible distribution charges; and
- pass-through of income and realized gains.

The number of fund shares expands with purchases and contracts with redemptions.

### 8.3 Closed-end funds

A closed-end investment company usually sells a fixed number of shares in an offering. Thereafter, shares trade between investors on an exchange or in the market. Market price is determined by supply and demand and can be above or below NAV.

A closed-end fund can employ leverage and hold less liquid assets without meeting daily shareholder redemptions in the same manner as an open-end fund. That structural benefit is paired with market-price risk.

If a closed-end fund’s NAV is $20 and market price is $17:

$$
\text{Discount}
=
\frac{\$17-\$20}{\$20}
=-15\%
$$

The fund trades at a 15% discount. A narrowing discount can add to return; a widening discount can create loss even if NAV is stable.

### 8.4 Unit investment trusts

A unit investment trust, or UIT, generally holds a fixed portfolio selected at creation and has a stated termination date. Unlike an actively managed fund, it ordinarily has no board-directed manager continuously trading the portfolio.

UIT features may include:

- units representing a proportional interest;
- a relatively static securities portfolio;
- pass-through income and principal;
- sponsor-created secondary-market or redemption arrangements; and
- a finite life.

A fixed portfolio reduces manager discretion but cannot actively replace a deteriorating holding except under limited governing provisions.

### 8.5 Exchange-traded funds

An ETF is generally an open-end fund or UIT whose shares trade intraday on an exchange. Large institutional **authorized participants** can create or redeem creation units, often using baskets of securities. This arbitrage mechanism normally keeps market price near NAV, but premiums and discounts can still occur.

ETF investors face:

- market-price fluctuation;
- bid-ask spreads;
- brokerage and trading costs;
- tracking difference;
- possible premiums or discounts;
- underlying-asset liquidity risk; and
- closure or delisting risk.

ETFs can be index-based or actively managed. “ETF” describes a trading and legal structure, not a risk level. An ETF may hold Treasury bills, emerging-market stocks, commodities, options, or leveraged swaps.

### 8.6 Open-end, closed-end, UIT, and ETF comparison

| Feature | Open-end mutual fund | Closed-end fund | UIT | ETF |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Investor transaction | With fund at next NAV | In market at negotiated price | Redeem or secondary arrangement | In market intraday |
| Share supply | Continuously changes | Generally fixed after offering | Fixed units, finite life | Retail shares trade; creation units change supply |
| Pricing | NAV after order | Premium or discount to NAV | Based on trust value and charges | Market price, usually near NAV |
| Management | Active or index | Active or index | Generally fixed portfolio | Active or index |
| Intraday trading | No | Yes | Usually limited | Yes |
| Key structural risk | Redemption and tax effects | Persistent discount and leverage | Static portfolio | Spread, tracking, premium/discount |

### 8.7 Private funds

Private funds commonly include hedge funds, private-equity funds, venture-capital funds, private-credit funds, and other partnerships exempt from registration as public investment companies.

Characteristics may include:

- investor eligibility requirements;
- private offering documents rather than a retail prospectus;
- limited transferability;
- lockups, gates, or infrequent withdrawal windows;
- performance-based compensation;
- leverage, derivatives, short selling, or concentrated positions;
- manager valuation of hard-to-price holdings; and
- less frequent disclosure.

The strategy may diversify a public portfolio, but the fund itself can be concentrated, leveraged, opaque, expensive, and illiquid. A “hedge fund” is not necessarily hedged against loss.

### 8.8 Real estate investment trusts

A REIT owns or finances income-producing real estate and seeks to satisfy tax-law requirements that allow pass-through treatment at the entity level.

Major economic forms are:

- **equity REIT:** owns properties and earns rents;
