- **mortgage REIT:** owns or finances real-estate debt and earns spread income; and
- **hybrid REIT:** combines property and mortgage exposure.

A REIT generally must distribute at least 90% of its taxable income to shareholders to maintain REIT tax treatment. This rule does not mean 90% of accounting profit, cash flow, or total return, and it does not guarantee a dividend.

REIT risks include property cycles, vacancies, tenant credit, leverage, interest rates, refinancing, management, geographic or sector concentration, and regulatory change.

#### Listed REITs

Listed REITs trade on exchanges, providing visible prices and ordinary market liquidity. Price may differ from the value of underlying properties, and daily volatility can resemble other equities.

#### Nontraded REITs

Nontraded REITs do not trade on a national exchange. They may offer periodic repurchase programs, but those programs can be capped, modified, or suspended. Additional concerns include high selling compensation, valuation estimates rather than continuously discovered prices, conflicts, distributions partly funded from offering proceeds or borrowing, and a long, uncertain exit.

> **Exam trap:** Ownership of real estate does not make a nontraded REIT liquid, price-stable, or principal-guaranteed.

### 8.9 Pooled-vehicle suitability cues

A broad, low-cost fund can efficiently diversify a modest account. A sector fund may be diversified within one industry yet concentrated in the portfolio. A private fund or nontraded REIT may be unsuitable for a client who needs predictable access to cash. Exchange trading improves the liquidity of fund shares, but it cannot make a stressed underlying market perfectly liquid.

## 9. Pooled-Investment Features and Evaluation

### 9.1 Net asset value

A pooled vehicle’s net asset value is its assets minus liabilities, divided by shares outstanding:

$$
\text{NAV per share}
=
\frac{\text{Market value of assets}-\text{Liabilities}}{\text{Shares outstanding}}
$$

Suppose a fund has $525 million of assets, $25 million of liabilities, and 20 million shares:

$$
\text{NAV}
=
\frac{\$525\text{ million}-\$25\text{ million}}{20\text{ million}}
=\$25
$$

NAV is not the same as an exchange-traded share price. Open-end mutual-fund transactions occur at next NAV, adjusted for applicable sales charges. Closed-end funds and ETFs trade at market prices that can differ from NAV.

### 9.2 Forward pricing and market pricing

A mutual-fund order entered before the fund’s pricing time receives that day’s subsequently calculated NAV; an order after the cutoff receives the next business day’s price. The investor does not lock in the last published NAV.

ETF and closed-end orders execute intraday at available market prices. Limit orders can control execution price but do not assure execution. Market orders prioritize execution over price.

### 9.3 Share classes

Share classes generally hold the same portfolio but allocate distribution charges, service fees, sales loads, and expenses differently.

Historically common mutual-fund patterns include:

- **Class A:** front-end sales load, often lower ongoing distribution expense;
- **Class B:** no front-end load, contingent deferred sales charge, higher ongoing expense, sometimes conversion to another class;
- **Class C:** little or no front-end load, possible short CDSC, and relatively high ongoing distribution expense; and
- **institutional/adviser classes:** generally no retail sales load and lower expense, with eligibility or account requirements.

Class labels are not perfectly standardized. The prospectus and fee table control. The lowest initial charge is not necessarily the lowest total cost for the intended holding period.

### 9.4 Sales loads and the public offering price

A front-end load is stated as a percentage of the public offering price, not NAV:

$$
\text{POP}
=
\frac{\text{NAV}}{1-\text{Sales-charge percentage}}
$$

If NAV is $19 and the front-end charge is 5%:

$$
\text{POP}
=
\frac{\$19}{0.95}
=\$20
$$

The $1 difference is 5% of the $20 offering price.

A **no-load** mutual fund has no front-end or deferred sales load meeting the regulatory definition, but it still can have management, administrative, transfer-agent, and other operating expenses. “No-load” does not mean no-cost.

### 9.5 Contingent deferred sales charges

A contingent deferred sales charge, or CDSC, is imposed when shares are redeemed within a specified period and commonly declines over time. The applicable base often follows prospectus rules, such as the lesser of original cost or current value. Reinvested distributions may be excluded, and shares can be redeemed in an order designed to minimize the charge.

A declining CDSC creates a surrender-like liquidity cost. It should be compared with the fund’s annual expenses and the expected holding period.

### 9.6 Rule 12b-1 fees

A 12b-1 fee is paid from fund assets for distribution and sometimes shareholder-service expenses under an approved plan. Because it is deducted from assets, it lowers investor return even though it does not appear as a separate invoice.

Recurring percentage fees compound. A small annual expense difference can create a large dollar difference over a long horizon.

### 9.7 Breakpoints, rights of accumulation, and letters of intent

Class A funds often reduce front-end sales charges at investment **breakpoints**. Relevant methods may include:

- **single purchase:** the current investment reaches a breakpoint;
- **rights of accumulation:** eligible existing holdings count toward the breakpoint;
- **letter of intent:** the investor states an intention to invest a threshold amount over a specified period, commonly 13 months; and
- **household or account aggregation:** related eligible accounts may be combined under prospectus rules.

A letter of intent is generally not a binding promise to complete all purchases, but shares can be held in escrow and redeemed to recover the proper charge if the investor does not reach the stated amount.

**Breakpoint sales**—recommending a purchase just below a threshold without a legitimate reason—can impose avoidable cost and raise serious conduct concerns.

### 9.8 Expense ratio and total cost

The expense ratio measures annual operating expenses as a percentage of average net assets. It generally includes management and administrative expenses and applicable 12b-1 fees, but not every investor cost. Brokerage costs incurred inside the portfolio, bid-ask spreads, loads, account charges, taxes, and market impact may not all appear in the stated ratio.

Costs matter most in comparison with economically similar alternatives. A higher-cost strategy must overcome its cost disadvantage before producing superior net return.

### 9.9 Liquidity and redemption risk

Liquidity differs by structure:

- Open-end fund shares are normally redeemable with the fund at NAV.
- ETF and closed-end shares are sold in the market.
- UIT liquidity depends on redemption and sponsor arrangements.
- Private funds can have lockups, notice periods, gates, or side pockets.
- Nontraded REIT repurchases may be limited or suspended.

A pooled vehicle can promise frequent shareholder liquidity while owning less liquid assets. In stress, forced sales can harm remaining investors, market prices can disconnect from NAV, or lawful liquidity tools can limit withdrawals.

### 9.10 Taxation of pooled investments

Regulated investment companies generally pass through income and realized gains. Investors may receive:

- ordinary dividends reflecting interest and nonqualified income;
- qualified dividend income when requirements are met;
- capital-gain distributions;
- tax-exempt interest dividends; and
- return-of-capital distributions.

A shareholder can owe tax on a distribution that is automatically reinvested. A new investor can also buy just before a capital-gain distribution, receive taxable income, and see NAV decline by approximately the distribution—sometimes called **buying the distribution**.

Sale or redemption creates the investor’s own capital gain or loss based on sale proceeds and adjusted basis. Reinvested distributions add to basis; failing to include them can lead to double taxation economically.

#### Turnover and tax efficiency

High portfolio turnover can realize gains and increase transaction costs. Index mutual funds and ETFs often have relatively low turnover. Many ETFs can use in-kind creation and redemption processes to reduce taxable capital-gain distributions, but they are not immune to distributions or tax.

#### REIT distributions

REIT distributions often contain ordinary-income components and can include capital gain or return of capital. The precise treatment depends on the distribution and current law. A high stated REIT yield should not be assumed to be a qualified dividend.

#### Tax location

Tax treatment can affect account placement:

- Taxable bonds produce ordinary interest and may benefit from tax deferral in a retirement account.
- Municipal bonds may lose their exemption’s value inside a tax-deferred account.
- High-turnover funds can create current taxable distributions.
- Assets with strong appreciation potential may benefit from long-term capital-gain treatment in taxable accounts, depending on the client’s plan.

Tax location is a portfolio decision, not a universal rule; withdrawal rules, estate objectives, future tax rates, and account access also matter.

### 9.11 Premiums and discounts

For an exchange-traded fund:

$$
\text{Premium or discount}
=
\frac{\text{Market price}-\text{NAV}}{\text{NAV}}
$$

A positive result is a premium; a negative result is a discount.

ETF arbitrage normally constrains deviations, especially when holdings are liquid and transparent. Deviations can widen when underlying markets are closed, assets are difficult to value, authorized participants withdraw, or markets are stressed.

Closed-end discounts can persist for years because there is no routine daily redemption at NAV. Discount size may reflect performance, fees, leverage, distribution policy, investor sentiment, tax issues, or governance.

> **Exam trap:** Buying a closed-end fund at a discount does not assure a profit. NAV can fall, and the discount can widen.

### 9.12 Diversification benefits and limits

A pooled vehicle can reduce unsystematic issuer risk by holding many securities. Diversification is weaker when holdings share the same economic exposure. Fifty regional-bank stocks are not diversified across industries; twenty high-yield funds may own many of the same bonds.

Fund-level diversification also does not ensure portfolio-level diversification. An investor may own several funds that duplicate the same large companies.

### 9.13 Common fund risks

| Risk | How it appears in a pooled vehicle |
|---|---|
| Market risk | Underlying holdings decline |
| Credit risk | Issuers default or spreads widen |
| Duration risk | Bond portfolio falls as yields rise |
| Liquidity risk | Holdings or fund shares become hard to trade |
| Manager risk | Decisions lag the benchmark or violate expectations |
| Tracking risk | Index fund differs from index return |
| Concentration risk | Sector, country, style, or issuer dominates |
| Leverage risk | Borrowing or derivatives magnify movement |
| Tax risk | Distributions create unexpected liability |
| Operational risk | Pricing, custody, cyber, model, or trading failures |
| Currency risk | Foreign holdings change in dollar value |
| Structure risk | Premium, discount, gate, or redemption terms harm access |

### 9.14 Benchmarks and indexes

A benchmark should resemble the strategy’s opportunity set and risk. A U.S. small-cap value fund should not be judged solely against a large-cap growth index. A municipal-bond fund needs a tax-exempt fixed-income comparison of similar maturity and credit quality.

An index is a mathematical portfolio. It may be weighted by:

- market capitalization;
- price;
- equal weight;
- fundamental measures; or
- another rules-based method.

Index return can be quoted as price return or total return. Total return includes reinvested distributions and is normally the appropriate comparison with a fund’s total return.

No investor buys an index directly. An index fund incurs fees, transaction costs, taxes, sampling differences, cash drag, and rebalancing effects, producing **tracking difference**. **Tracking error** describes variability of return differences over time.

### 9.15 Manager tenure and performance interpretation

A fund’s long record is less informative if the current manager has a short tenure or the strategy changed. Evaluation should consider:

- performance under the current decision makers;
- consistency of process;
- risk taken to earn returns;
- style drift;
- asset growth and capacity;
- team turnover;
- benchmark appropriateness; and
- performance over complete market cycles.

Past performance is not predictive by itself. Strong recent return may reflect luck, factor exposure, concentration, leverage, or a market environment favorable to the style.

### 9.16 Memory aids and traps

- **Open-end:** investor transacts at next NAV.
- **Closed-end:** market closes the price above or below NAV.
- **ETF:** exchange price plus creation/redemption mechanism.
- **UIT:** generally fixed portfolio and finite life.
- **Load:** sales compensation; **expense ratio:** recurring fund cost.
- A high distribution rate is not necessarily a high economic return; it can include return of capital.
- Several funds do not guarantee diversification if their holdings overlap.
- Manager tenure should match the record being credited to the manager.

---

## 10. Derivatives: Definitions, Payoffs, and Risks

### 10.1 What a derivative is

A derivative is a contract whose value depends on an underlying asset, rate, index, event, or other reference. Derivatives can hedge risk, obtain exposure efficiently, generate income, or speculate. Their notional exposure can greatly exceed the cash initially paid, making leverage a central concern.

### 10.2 Options

A **call option** gives its owner the right, but not the obligation, to buy the underlying asset at the strike price by or at expiration, depending on contract style. A **put option** gives the owner the right to sell.

The buyer pays a **premium**. The seller, or writer, receives the premium and assumes an obligation if assigned.

- American-style options can generally be exercised before expiration.
- European-style options can generally be exercised only at expiration.

These labels describe exercise style, not geography.

### 10.3 Option payoff basics

At expiration, ignoring transaction costs and taxes:

$$
\text{Long call payoff}=\max(0,S_T-K)
$$

$$
\text{Long call profit}=\max(0,S_T-K)-C
$$

$$
\text{Long put payoff}=\max(0,K-S_T)
$$

$$
\text{Long put profit}=\max(0,K-S_T)-P
$$

where $S_T$ is underlying price at expiration, $K$ is strike price, $C$ is call premium, and $P$ is put premium.

A call with a $50 strike purchased for $4 has a $54 expiration break-even:

$$
K+C=\$50+\$4=\$54
$$

If stock closes at $62, intrinsic value is $12 and profit is $8 per share. A standard equity contract commonly represents 100 shares, so gross profit is $800.

A put with a $50 strike bought for $3 has a $47 break-even:

$$
K-P=\$50-\$3=\$47
$$

At a $40 stock price, intrinsic value is $10 and profit is $7 per share.

### 10.4 Basic position profiles

| Position | Directional view | Maximum gain | Maximum loss at expiration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Long call | Bullish | Theoretically unlimited | Premium paid |
| Long put | Bearish/protective | Substantial, limited by underlying reaching zero | Premium paid |
| Covered call | Neutral to moderately bullish | Capped above strike plus premium | Large stock loss, partly offset by premium |
| Protective put | Bullish with downside protection | Stock upside less premium | Limited below strike plus premium |
| Uncovered call | Neutral/bearish | Premium received | Theoretically unlimited |
| Cash-secured put | Willing to buy at effective lower price | Premium | Large if stock falls toward zero |

A **covered call** owns the stock and writes a call. It generates premium but surrenders upside beyond the strike. It is not full downside protection.

A **protective put** owns stock and buys a put, creating a floor during the option term at the cost of the premium.

### 10.5 Option value and risk variables

Option premium consists of intrinsic value and time value. Important influences include:

- underlying price relative to strike;
- time to expiration;
- expected volatility;
- interest rates;
- dividends; and
- market liquidity.

Time value generally decays as expiration approaches. Higher expected volatility generally raises both call and put values because it increases the chance of a large favorable move for the holder.

Options can expire worthless. Writers can be assigned before expected. Thinly traded contracts can have wide spreads. Multi-leg strategies add execution, assignment, and model risk.

### 10.6 Warrants

A warrant is a long-lived right, usually issued by a corporation, to buy the issuer’s stock at a stated exercise price. Unlike an exchange option written between market participants, exercise of a company warrant generally causes the issuer to create shares, potentially diluting existing holders.

Warrants can have long expirations, leverage, low liquidity, and no dividend or voting rights before exercise. They may be attached to bonds, preferred shares, or SPAC units to make an offering more attractive.

### 10.7 Futures

A futures contract is a standardized exchange-traded agreement to buy or sell an underlying asset or financial reference at a future time. Contracts use a clearinghouse, daily marking to market, and margin.

Futures margin is a performance bond rather than a down payment on ownership. Because the required margin is a fraction of notional value, small underlying moves can produce large percentage gains or losses on posted capital.

If losses reduce the account below maintenance margin, the investor must add funds or the position can be liquidated. Futures can settle physically or in cash.

A producer can sell futures to hedge a price decline; a user of a commodity can buy futures to hedge a price increase. A hedge can reduce one risk while introducing basis risk—the hedge and actual exposure may not move identically.

### 10.8 Forward contracts

A forward is a customized over-the-counter agreement to transact in the future at a specified price. Unlike a standardized exchange future, a forward generally has negotiated quantity, quality, date, and settlement terms.

Customization can improve hedge fit but adds counterparty credit risk, less transparency, and limited ability to exit. Collateral and netting provisions can reduce, not eliminate, these concerns.

### 10.9 Futures and forwards compared

| Feature | Futures | Forwards |
|---|---|---|
| Venue | Exchange | Over the counter |
| Terms | Standardized | Customized |
| Credit intermediation | Clearinghouse | Direct counterparty exposure |
| Cash flows | Daily mark-to-market | Commonly settlement at maturity, subject to contract |
| Liquidity | Often active in major contracts | Depends on counterparty and terms |
| Transparency | Relatively high | Lower |

### 10.10 Derivative suitability cues

Derivatives can be appropriate when the client understands the contract, exposure, liquidity, worst-case outcome, collateral needs, and monitoring requirement. A hedge must correspond to the actual risk. An uncovered option or leveraged future can be inconsistent with capital preservation even if the initial dollar outlay is small.

> **Memory aid:** An option **buyer has a right**; an option **writer has an obligation**.

> **Exam trap:** Limited premium paid by an option buyer does not imply limited risk for the option writer.

## 11. Alternative Investments and Complex Products

“Alternative” is not a single asset class. It is a broad label for investments outside traditional publicly traded stocks, bonds, and cash. The label can encompass partnerships, private credit, real estate, commodities, managed futures, structured notes, and specialized exchange-traded products. The common analytical needs are careful review of liquidity, valuation, leverage, fees, manager dependence, legal form, and tax reporting.

### 11.1 Limited partnerships

A limited partnership has at least one general partner and one or more limited partners.

- The **general partner** manages the enterprise and ordinarily has broad authority and potential general liability, although a separate limited-liability entity often serves in this role.
- A **limited partner** contributes capital and generally has limited liability if legal requirements are observed, but normally has limited management authority.

Investment programs can use limited partnerships for real estate, energy, equipment leasing, private equity, venture capital, private credit, and other ventures.

Potential benefits include specialized management, access to nonpublic assets, pass-through tax treatment, and return patterns that may differ from public markets. Risks include:

- long holding periods;
- transfer restrictions;
- capital calls;
- uncertain valuation;
- concentrated assets;
- leverage;
- general-partner conflicts;
- complicated fees and waterfalls;
- uncertain cash distributions;
- business failure; and
- delayed Schedule K-1 reporting.

#### Pass-through taxation

A partnership generally does not pay federal income tax as a corporation would. Instead, items of income, gain, loss, deduction, and credit pass through to partners. A partner can owe tax on allocated income even when the partnership distributes no cash. This is another form of phantom income.

Loss deductions can be constrained by basis, at-risk, passive-activity, and other rules. An investor should not treat advertised tax benefits as equivalent to economic profit.

#### General and limited partner incentives

Private partnerships often charge a management fee plus a performance allocation, or **carried interest**. Distribution waterfalls may give investors a preferred return before the manager shares profits. Terms vary. Incentives can motivate strong results but may also encourage risk-taking, delayed valuation recognition, or transactions benefiting the manager.

### 11.2 Exchange-traded notes

An exchange-traded note, or ETN, is an unsecured senior debt obligation whose return is linked to an index or strategy, less applicable fees. It is not a fund holding a segregated portfolio of index assets.

ETN return depends on:

1. the performance formula;
2. the issuer’s creditworthiness;
3. fees and any embedded financing costs;
4. market price relative to indicative value; and
5. redemption and maturity terms.

Potential advantages include close formula-based index exposure and, in some cases, reduced tracking disruption from portfolio rebalancing. Important risks include issuer default, call or early-redemption provisions, thin trading, premium/discount movement, complex tax treatment, and path-dependent formulas.

> **Exam trap:** An ETF shareholder owns an interest in a pooled portfolio. An ETN holder is an unsecured creditor of the issuing institution.

If the linked index rises but the ETN issuer fails, the investor has a claim in the issuer’s capital structure, not ownership of the index components.

### 11.3 Leveraged and inverse funds

A leveraged fund seeks a multiple of an index’s return over a stated period, commonly one day. An inverse fund seeks the opposite of the index’s return over that period. Examples of daily objectives include +2×, −1×, or −3×.

These objectives generally reset daily. Over multiple days, compounded results depend on the path of returns and can differ substantially from the stated multiple of the index’s cumulative return.

Suppose an index starts at 100, rises 10%, then falls 9.09%. It returns to 100:

- Day 1: $100 \times 1.10 = $110.
- Day 2: $110 \times 0.9091 \approx $100.

A +2× daily fund, ignoring expenses and tracking differences, behaves approximately as follows:

- Day 1: $100 \times 1.20 = $120.
- Day 2: the index return is −9.09%, so fund return is approximately −18.18%.
- Ending value: $120 \times 0.8182 \approx $98.18.

The index is flat, but the leveraged fund loses about 1.82%. Volatility and repeated rebalancing caused the difference.

Leveraged and inverse funds involve:

- leverage and derivatives;
- daily reset and compounding risk;
- potentially severe loss in volatile markets;
- tracking difference;
- financing and transaction costs;
- counterparty exposure; and
- a need for active monitoring.

They can be useful for a knowledgeable investor’s short-term tactical or hedging objective. They are generally not ordinary long-term buy-and-hold index substitutes.

### 11.4 Structured products

A structured product combines a debt obligation with one or more derivatives. Its payoff may reference a stock, index, interest rate, commodity, currency, basket, or multiple underlyings.

Common terms include:

- **participation rate:** percentage of positive reference performance credited;
- **cap:** maximum credited return;
- **buffer:** initial range of loss absorbed before investor loss;
- **barrier:** level that changes the payoff if breached;
- **knock-in or knock-out:** feature activated or terminated by an event;
- **autocall:** early redemption after specified conditions;
- **contingent coupon:** payment only when a condition is met; and
- **principal protection:** contractual repayment promise, often subject to conditions and issuer credit.

Consider a note with 100% upside participation, a 12% cap, and a 10% downside buffer at maturity:

- If the index gains 8%, the note may return 8%.
- If it gains 20%, the note may return only the 12% cap.
- If it loses 7%, the buffer may prevent principal loss.
- If it loses 25%, a simple 10% buffer design may impose a 15% loss.

Actual formulas vary; some barrier products impose the full underlying loss after a barrier is breached rather than only loss beyond the barrier.

Structured-product risks include:

- issuer credit risk;
- illiquidity and issuer-controlled secondary quotations;
- capped upside;
- complex and path-dependent losses;
- call risk;
- loss of dividends on the underlying;
- difficult valuation;
- tax uncertainty; and
- misunderstanding “protection.”

“Principal protected” means only what the contract says. It may apply only at maturity, only if a barrier is not breached, and only if the issuer remains able to pay. It is not synonymous with FDIC insurance.

### 11.5 Other partnership and real-asset programs

Direct participation programs may own oil and gas interests, equipment, farms, timber, or real estate. Cash flows can depend on commodity prices, operating skill, depletion, weather, tenants, financing, and tax rules. A tangible asset does not assure an active market for the partnership interest.

Private-equity and venture-capital funds seek long-term appreciation through nonpublic businesses. Returns can be irregular and valuation estimates smoothed because holdings do not trade daily. Early losses followed by later gains can create a “J-curve.” Reported low volatility may partly reflect infrequent appraisal rather than low economic risk.

Private-credit funds make loans outside public bond markets. Floating rates can support income when rates rise, but borrower leverage, weak covenants, illiquidity, valuation, and recession risk can be substantial.

### 11.6 Alternative-investment suitability cues

Alternatives demand a purpose. “Diversification” is incomplete unless the adviser explains what risk is diversified, what new risk is added, and how the holding will be valued and exited.

Important client questions include:

- Can the client meet capital calls?
- Can the client hold through a lockup?
- Is independent pricing available?
- How much loss is possible?
- Is the return dependent on one manager or issuer?
- Do fees consume the expected diversification benefit?
- Can the client handle delayed tax forms and phantom income?
- Does the position overlap with existing real-estate, employer, or business exposure?

---

## 12. Insurance Products

Insurance contracts transfer specified risks to an insurer. The insurer’s claims-paying ability is central. Guarantees depend on the issuing insurance company and the contract, not on the SEC, FDIC, or a securities exchange.

### 12.1 Annuity vocabulary

An annuity is a contract designed to accumulate value, distribute income, or both.

- **Owner:** controls contract rights and bears tax consequences.
- **Annuitant:** life used to determine certain payments.
- **Beneficiary:** receives death benefits when applicable.
- **Accumulation phase:** value builds before income payments begin.
- **Annuitization:** conversion of contract value into a payment stream.
- **Surrender charge:** charge for withdrawal or cancellation during a stated period.
- **Free-withdrawal amount:** contractually permitted withdrawal without a surrender charge.
- **Rider:** optional feature purchased or included under stated terms.

A **qualified annuity** is held within a tax-qualified retirement arrangement. A **nonqualified annuity** is funded with after-tax dollars outside such a plan.

### 12.2 Fixed annuities

A fixed annuity credits a rate declared or guaranteed under the contract. During a guarantee period, the insurer bears direct investment risk and promises the credited terms. At renewal, the rate may change subject to contract minimums.

Potential benefits include tax-deferred accumulation, nominal-value stability under the contract, and lifetime-income options. Risks and costs include:

- insurer credit risk;
- surrender charges;
- interest-rate opportunity cost;
- inflation erosion;
- limited liquidity;
- ordinary-income taxation on gain; and
- possible penalties for early distributions.

A **market value adjustment** annuity can adjust surrender value up or down when prevailing rates differ from rates at purchase. Thus, “fixed annuity” does not always mean an unchanged surrender amount.

### 12.3 Variable annuities

A variable annuity allocates value among **separate-account** investment options, often resembling mutual-fund portfolios. The owner bears market risk. Contract value changes with investment performance after expenses.

Common costs include mortality and expense risk charges, administration, underlying portfolio expenses, rider charges, and surrender charges.

Possible features include:

- tax-deferred buildup;
- a death benefit;
- lifetime annuitization choices;
- guaranteed minimum withdrawal or income riders; and
- transfers among subaccounts under contract terms.

Guarantees under riders often depend on a separate **benefit base**, not the contract’s cash value. A benefit base used to calculate guaranteed withdrawals usually cannot be surrendered as a lump sum.

A variable annuity is a security as well as an insurance product. It is generally unsuitable when the client needs near-term liquidity, cannot tolerate market decline, or already receives adequate tax deferral through a less costly account and has no need for the insurance features.

### 12.4 Indexed annuities

A fixed indexed annuity credits interest by reference to an external index while the insurer supports contract guarantees. The investor does not own the index securities and usually does not receive their dividends.

Credit can be limited by:

- a **cap** on credited return;
- a **participation rate** below 100%;
- a **spread or margin** subtracted from index change;
- averaging; or
- another formula.

If an index gains 10%, a 70% participation rate would credit 7%, absent other limits. If a 6% cap also applies, only 6% is credited.

A floor can prevent a negative index-crediting rate for the crediting period, but charges, withdrawals, surrender penalties, and rider costs can still reduce contract value. The floor does not create equity-market ownership without downside.

### 12.5 Immediate and deferred annuities

A **deferred annuity** has an accumulation period before payouts. An **immediate annuity** begins income soon after purchase, commonly within one payment interval.

Annuitization choices illustrate tradeoffs:

| Payout | Benefit | Cost or risk |
|---|---|---|
| Life only | Usually highest periodic lifetime amount | Payments generally stop at death; little legacy |
| Life with period certain | Lifetime income plus minimum payment period | Lower payment than life only |
| Joint and survivor | Continues while either covered person lives | Lower initial payment |
| Fixed period | Payments for specified time | Can end while annuitant is alive |
| Fixed amount | Chosen payment until funds exhausted | Duration uncertain |

Once irrevocably annuitized, access to principal may be limited or eliminated under the chosen option.

### 12.6 Annuity taxation

For a nonqualified annuity:

- contributions are generally after-tax and not deductible;
- earnings grow tax deferred;
- withdrawals before annuitization are generally treated as earnings first and taxed as ordinary income until gain is removed;
- recovery of basis is not taxed;
- annuitized payments are generally divided between taxable earnings and return of investment using an exclusion ratio; and
- taxable distributions before age 59½ may face a 10% federal additional tax unless an exception applies.

Transfers satisfying Section 1035 can permit tax-free exchange from one qualifying annuity to another. An exchange does not make the new product suitable and can restart surrender periods, add expenses, or forfeit valuable existing benefits.

An annuity generally does not receive the same basis step-up treatment for deferred gain as many capital assets at death. Beneficiaries can owe ordinary income tax on gain.

#### Why an annuity inside an IRA needs special scrutiny

An IRA already provides tax deferral. Placing an annuity inside it does not create additional tax deferral. The annuity must be justified by other features—such as a desired insurer guarantee or lifetime-income provision—relative to its cost and restrictions.

### 12.7 Comparing annuity forms

| Feature | Fixed | Variable | Fixed indexed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Investment risk during accumulation | Primarily insurer under contract | Owner bears separate-account market risk | Insurer supports guarantee; formula links credit to index |
| Return | Declared or guaranteed rate | Subaccount performance less expenses | Formula-based index credit |
| Security status | Generally insurance, not a security | Security and insurance contract | Depends on design and law; conventional fixed indexed form generally insurance |
| Upside | Limited to credited rate | Market participation | Limited by cap, participation, spread, formula |
| Downside | Insurer and inflation risk; surrender terms | Market loss plus fees | Contract floor subject to fees, withdrawals, surrender, insurer credit |

### 12.8 Life insurance foundations

Life insurance pays a death benefit when the insured dies while coverage is in force. The policy owner controls contractual rights, the insured is the covered life, and the beneficiary receives proceeds.

Under general federal principles, death benefits paid by reason of death are often income-tax-free to the beneficiary, but exceptions and estate-tax considerations exist. Policy withdrawals, loans, transfers for value, modified endowment contracts, and surrender can change treatment.

### 12.9 Term life insurance

Term insurance provides death protection for a stated period and normally has no cash value. It often provides the greatest initial death benefit per premium dollar.

Forms include level term, annual renewable term, decreasing term, and convertible term. Renewal premiums can rise sharply with age. Conversion may permit movement to permanent coverage without new evidence of insurability, subject to contract terms.

Term insurance can fit temporary needs such as income replacement during working years, debt coverage, or funding children’s dependency period.

### 12.10 Whole life insurance

Whole life is permanent insurance with fixed premiums, a contractual death benefit, and guaranteed cash value under the policy schedule, assuming required premiums are paid. A participating policy may pay dividends, but dividends are not guaranteed.

Policy loans use cash value as collateral. They generally are not taxable when taken from a policy that remains in force, but interest accrues, and an outstanding loan reduces death benefit. If a policy lapses or is surrendered with a loan and gain, tax can arise unexpectedly.

Whole life offers predictability but often has higher premiums and less flexibility than term insurance.

### 12.11 Universal life insurance

Universal life separates mortality charges, expenses, and interest crediting within a flexible-premium permanent policy. The owner can often vary premium payments and death benefit within limits.

Flexibility is not a promise that coverage will remain in force without adequate funding. If credited rates disappoint or insurance costs rise, cash value can erode and additional premiums may be required. Illustrations use assumptions and are not guarantees except for explicitly guaranteed elements.

### 12.12 Variable life and variable universal life

Variable life allocates cash value to separate-account investment options. The owner bears investment risk, and the policy is a security. It generally has a fixed-premium structure and a death benefit subject to a contractual minimum.

Variable universal life, or VUL, combines flexible universal-life mechanics with separate-account investment choices. Poor performance, high charges, loans, or inadequate premiums can cause lapse. VUL requires both insurance need and capacity to understand and bear securities-market risk.

### 12.13 Life-policy comparison

| Policy | Coverage term | Premium pattern | Cash value | Who bears investment risk? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Term | Stated term | Level or increasing | None | Insurer for promised death benefit |
| Whole life | Permanent if funded | Generally fixed | Guaranteed schedule plus possible dividends | Primarily insurer under guarantees |
| Universal life | Permanent if adequately funded | Flexible | Interest-crediting account | Primarily insurer, subject to policy mechanics |
| Variable life | Permanent if funded | Generally fixed | Separate accounts | Policy owner |
| Variable universal life | Permanent if adequately funded | Flexible | Separate accounts | Policy owner |

### 12.14 Modified endowment contracts and policy taxation

A life policy that fails the statutory seven-pay test can become a **modified endowment contract**, or MEC. Death-benefit treatment can remain favorable, but lifetime distributions generally lose the usual basis-first treatment: gain is distributed first, and taxable amounts before age 59½ can face an additional 10% tax unless an exception applies.

For a non-MEC policy, withdrawals up to basis are generally received first without income tax, while gain can be taxable after basis is exhausted. Surrender produces ordinary income to the extent proceeds exceed investment in the contract. Policy tax rules are technical, and loans can create severe consequences if coverage lapses.

A Section 1035 exchange can permit qualifying tax-free movement from one life policy to another, from life insurance to an annuity, or between qualifying annuities. It generally does not permit a tax-free exchange from an annuity into life insurance. Suitability still requires comparing surrender charges, contestability or suicide periods where relevant, new expenses, health underwriting, and lost guarantees.

### 12.15 Insurance suitability cues and replacement concerns

An insurance recommendation begins with an insurable need, desired guarantee, funding capacity, time horizon, and insurer quality. It should not begin with the size of the sales commission or the attractiveness of tax deferral alone.

Replacement of an existing policy or annuity demands comparison of:

- old and new surrender periods;
- insurer strength;
- guaranteed and nonguaranteed benefits;
- mortality, expense, fund, and rider charges;
- tax consequences;
- loss of grandfathered provisions;
- health or insurability changes; and
- the client’s current objective.

> **Memory aid:** **Term protects temporarily; permanent policies combine protection with cash-value mechanics.**

> **Exam trap:** A tax-free exchange avoids current recognition of gain; it does not make costs disappear or prove that replacement is advisable.

## 13. Other Assets: Commodities, Precious Metals, and Digital Assets

### 13.1 Commodities as economic inputs

Commodities are standardized or economically interchangeable goods used in production and consumption. Broad groups include:

- energy, such as crude oil and natural gas;
- industrial metals, such as copper and aluminum;
- precious metals, such as gold, silver, platinum, and palladium;
- agriculture, such as corn, wheat, soybeans, coffee, and cotton; and
- livestock.

The official subject is precious **metals**, not “medals.”

Commodity return is driven principally by changes in spot prices, futures curves, collateral income, storage economics, and investment-vehicle expenses. Unlike a productive company or interest-bearing bond, a physical commodity does not generate earnings, dividends, or contractual interest merely by being held.

Commodity prices can respond to:

- global growth;
- weather and harvests;
- wars, sanctions, and trade restrictions;
- inventory levels;
- production decisions;
- transportation and refining constraints;
- currency values;
- technology and substitution; and
- speculative positioning.

The same commodity can be volatile for different reasons at different times. Oil may respond to geopolitical supply risk, while wheat responds to weather and export controls.

### 13.2 Ways to obtain commodity exposure

#### Physical ownership

Direct ownership gives spot exposure but creates storage, insurance, transport, assay, spoilage, or security costs. Retail purchase and sale spreads can be wide. A quoted wholesale spot price may not be the investor’s executable price.

#### Futures and forwards

Commodity futures provide leveraged exposure and are commonly used by producers and consumers to hedge. Investors must understand margin, daily settlement, contract expiry, position limits, and the need to close or roll contracts to avoid delivery obligations.

#### Commodity pools and funds

A commodity fund may use futures, swaps, physical holdings, or shares of commodity-related businesses. The label alone does not reveal which exposure the investor receives. A gold-mining equity fund, for example, holds operating companies rather than bullion and adds management, labor, political, reserve, and equity-market risk.

#### Producer equities

Shares of miners, energy producers, or agricultural businesses may benefit from higher commodity prices, but company leverage, costs, hedges, capital allocation, regulation, and execution can overwhelm spot-price movement.

### 13.3 Spot prices, futures curves, and roll yield

A futures-based fund must replace expiring contracts. If later contracts are more expensive than near contracts, the curve is in **contango**. Selling a cheaper expiring contract and buying a more expensive later contract can create negative roll yield.

If later contracts are cheaper than near contracts, the curve is in **backwardation**. Rolling can create positive roll yield, though the curve can change.

A futures-based commodity product’s return can be approximated conceptually as:

$$
\text{Total return}
\approx
\text{Spot-price effect}
+
\text{Roll yield}
+
\text{Collateral yield}
-
\text{Expenses}
$$

Therefore, a crude-oil fund need not match the percentage change in the cash price of crude oil.

### 13.4 Precious metals

Gold and other precious metals are sometimes used as stores of value, crisis hedges, currency alternatives, or portfolio diversifiers. They can perform well during certain inflationary or confidence shocks, but relationships are inconsistent.

Risks include:

- no internal cash flow;
- substantial price volatility;
- long periods of weak real return;
- storage and insurance costs;
- counterfeit or purity concerns;
- dealer spreads;
- theft;
- currency and real-rate sensitivity; and
- unfavorable tax treatment relative to ordinary long-term securities in some circumstances.

For U.S. federal tax purposes, physical precious metals and certain interests backed by them can be treated as collectibles, potentially subject to a higher maximum long-term capital-gain rate than ordinary stocks. The exact vehicle and holding period matter.

> **Exam trap:** Gold can diversify some portfolios, but “tangible” does not mean stable, income-producing, or guaranteed to track inflation.

### 13.5 Digital assets

A digital asset is an electronically recorded representation of value or rights. The category can include cryptocurrencies, stablecoins, tokens, nonfungible tokens, and interests recorded through distributed-ledger systems. Legal classification depends on facts and can involve securities, commodities, payment instruments, property, or other regimes.

#### Cryptocurrencies

Cryptocurrencies use cryptographic and network protocols to record and transfer units. A holder’s economic rights differ from those of a stockholder or bondholder:

- no inherent claim on corporate assets;
- no contractual coupon;
- no board-declared dividend;
- usually no government deposit insurance; and
- value often depends heavily on adoption, scarcity rules, network confidence, and market demand.

#### Stablecoins

A stablecoin seeks to maintain a reference value, often one U.S. dollar. Mechanisms may use reserves, overcollateralized crypto assets, algorithms, or combinations. A target peg is not a guarantee. Risks include insufficient or opaque reserves, redemption restrictions, counterparty failure, runs, operational breakdown, legal action, and depegging.

#### Tokens and decentralized applications

Tokens can provide access, governance rights, payment functionality, or an economic interest in a project. Descriptions such as “utility token” do not conclusively determine legal status or economic safety. Smart-contract code can contain errors or respond unexpectedly to market conditions.

### 13.6 Digital-asset custody

Custody is central because control often depends on cryptographic keys.

- **Self-custody** gives the owner direct key control but creates risk of loss, theft, poor backup, phishing, malware, or irreversible transfer.
- **Hosted custody** places control with an exchange or custodian, adding counterparty, insolvency, operational, cybersecurity, and withdrawal risk.
- **Hot wallets** are connected to online systems and convenient but more exposed to cyberattack.
- **Cold storage** keeps keys offline and can reduce some attack exposure while adding operational and recovery challenges.

A blockchain transaction’s technical finality can make error or theft difficult to reverse. SIPC protection, where potentially relevant to a particular intermediary and asset arrangement, is not the same as protection from a digital asset’s market decline and should never be assumed.

### 13.7 Digital-asset market risks

Digital assets can involve:

- extreme volatility;
- concentrated ownership;
- manipulation and thin markets;
- leverage and cascading liquidations;
- platform failure;
- forks and protocol changes;
- smart-contract exploits;
- oracle and bridge failures;
- unclear valuation;
- regulatory change;
- fraudulent offerings;
- uncertain legal ownership in bankruptcy; and
- limited or unreliable disclosures.

A token trading continuously is not necessarily liquid in the economic sense. Order-book depth can disappear, withdrawals can be frozen, and quoted prices can differ among venues.

### 13.8 Digital-asset tax concepts

Under general IRS treatment, virtual currency held for investment is property. Selling it for dollars, exchanging it for another digital asset, or spending it can create a taxable disposition. Receipt through mining, staking, compensation, or certain distributions can create ordinary income based on facts and current guidance. Basis records are critical because frequent transfers among wallets and platforms complicate reporting.

A rise in token price does not itself create cash to pay tax arising from a separate taxable receipt. Tax rules continue to develop, so exam-level understanding should focus on property treatment and recognition upon disposition rather than assuming currency-like exemptions.

### 13.9 Commodity and digital-asset suitability cues

These assets can provide differentiated exposure, but they are generally poor sources for a known nominal liability because they lack promised maturity value. Questions include:

- Is exposure physical, futures-based, equity-based, note-based, or token-based?
- Does the client understand leverage, roll yield, custody, and counterparty risk?
- Can the client tolerate severe drawdowns and permanent loss?
- Is the allocation small enough relative to the total plan?
- Is pricing independently observable?
- Can the asset be sold and proceeds withdrawn during stress?
- Is the tax and recordkeeping burden manageable?

A speculative position can be suitable only because the client’s objectives and capacity support speculation—not because a volatile asset is fashionable or recently appreciated.

---

## 14. Integrated Comparisons, Suitability Signals, and Exam Traps

### 14.1 What the investor legally owns

| Vehicle | Investor’s position | Primary source of return | Primary claim |
|---|---|---|---|
| Insured deposit | Depositor | Interest | Bank obligation plus qualifying insurance |
| Bond | Creditor | Coupon, discount accretion, price change | Contractual debt claim |
| Common stock | Residual owner | Dividends and appreciation | Residual corporate assets |
| Preferred stock | Senior equity owner | Stated dividends and price change | Ahead of common, behind debt |
| Open-end fund | Fund shareholder | Portfolio return less costs | Redeemable interest at NAV |
| Closed-end fund/ETF | Fund shareholder trading in market | Portfolio return plus premium/discount change | Share of pooled portfolio |
| ETN | Unsecured creditor | Formula-linked payment | Issuer promise |
| Annuity | Insurance contract owner | Crediting formula or separate-account return | Insurer’s contractual obligations |
| Option | Contract holder or writer | Contingent payoff | Right or obligation under contract |
| Limited partnership | Partnership owner | Business cash flow and value | Partnership interest |
| Physical commodity | Owner of an asset | Price appreciation | The commodity itself |
| Cryptocurrency | Holder of keys or platform claim | Network-demand price change or protocol reward | Rights depend on asset and custody arrangement |

### 14.2 Liquidity spectrum

Liquidity is multidimensional: speed, certainty of execution, transaction cost, and price impact all matter.

A rough spectrum under normal conditions is:

1. insured demand deposits;
2. Treasury bills and major money-market instruments;
3. exchange-traded large-cap stocks, bonds, and broad ETFs;
4. ordinary mutual-fund redemptions at daily NAV;
5. less actively traded corporate and municipal bonds;
6. small closed-end funds, specialized ETFs, and thin options;
7. brokered products with limited secondary support;
8. nontraded REITs and interval-like redemption programs;
9. private partnerships and restricted securities; and
10. assets subject to contractual lockup, gates, or no established market.

This ordering is illustrative, not absolute. An ETF can trade throughout the day while its underlying assets are illiquid. A mutual fund offers daily redemption but may incur forced-sale costs during stress. A quoted digital-asset price can coexist with frozen withdrawals.

### 14.3 Income is not total return

A distribution can come from economic income, realized gain, borrowing, asset sales, or return of capital. High payout does not prove high return.

$$
\text{Total return}
=
\frac{\text{Income}+\text{Ending value}-\text{Beginning value}}{\text{Beginning value}}
$$

An investment begins at $100, pays $8, and ends at $91:

$$
\text{Total return}
=
\frac{\$8+\$91-\$100}{\$100}
=-1\%
$$

The 8% distribution rate coexisted with a negative total return.

### 14.4 Yield usually compensates for something

An unusually high quoted yield can reflect:

- weak credit;
- long duration;
- call risk;
- illiquidity;
- leverage;
- return of capital;
- a falling market price;
- complex contingencies;
- foreign-currency risk; or
- a temporarily distorted market.

The correct response to a high yield is “What risk or cash-flow assumption produces it?” not “How can the client obtain more?”

### 14.5 Guarantees compared

| Claim | Who or what supports it? | What it does not necessarily protect |
|---|---|---|
| FDIC/NCUA insurance | Federal insurance arrangement within limits | Inflation, excess balances, nondeposit investments |
| Treasury payment | U.S. government | Market loss before maturity, inflation |
| Bond promise | Issuer and any collateral/guarantor | Default, market value, reinvestment |
| Annuity or life guarantee | Insurance company | Insurer failure, purchasing power, nonguaranteed illustrations |
| Structured-note protection | Note issuer under precise formula | Issuer default, early sale, breached barriers |
| Fund objective | Portfolio management process | Principal or return unless a separate enforceable guaranty exists |

“Guaranteed” is incomplete unless the adviser identifies guarantor, covered amount, conditions, date, and exclusions.

### 14.6 Tax character matters

| Return source | Typical exam-level tax character |
|---|---|
| Bank or corporate interest | Ordinary income |
| Treasury interest | Federal ordinary income; generally state/local exempt |
| Qualifying municipal interest | Often federal income-tax exempt |
| Qualified corporate dividend | Potential preferential federal rate |
| Nonqualified dividend | Ordinary income |
| Long-term security appreciation | Long-term capital gain when realized |
| Mutual-fund capital-gain distribution | Long-term capital gain character to shareholder under applicable rules |
| Partnership allocation | Character passes through; tax possible without cash distribution |
| Nonqualified annuity gain | Tax-deferred buildup; ordinary income when distributed |
| Life-insurance death benefit | Often federal income-tax-free under general rule |
| Precious-metal collectible gain | Potential collectibles rate |
| Digital-asset disposition | Property gain or loss, depending on use and holding period |

Tax treatment can change with account type, investor status, holding period, transaction, and law. The exam usually tests broad distinctions, not preparation of a return.

### 14.7 Matching needs to characteristics

| Client need | Characteristics that may help | Characteristics that conflict |
|---|---|---|
| Emergency liquidity | Stable value, immediate access, insurance | Lockups, surrender charges, volatile market price |
| Known payment in two years | Short maturity, high credit quality, duration matching | Long stock horizon, leveraged fund, private partnership |
| Long-term real growth | Productive equity exposure and diversification | Excess nominal cash, capped upside without purpose |
| Current income | Sustainable cash flow, appropriate credit and duration | Distribution funded by capital erosion |
| Inflation sensitivity | TIPS, selected real assets, pricing-power equities | Long fixed nominal payments |
| Tax-exempt income | Suitable municipal exposure in taxable account | Tax-exempt bond in account already tax deferred |
| Downside hedge | Properly sized put or matched derivative | Uncovered leverage or imperfect hedge |
| Lifetime income | Insurer-backed annuitization or appropriate rider | Need for full liquidity and bequest control |

### 14.8 High-frequency exam traps

1. **Price versus yield:** Bond price up means market yield down, and vice versa.
2. **Current yield versus YTM:** Current yield ignores principal gain or loss.
3. **Coupon versus safety:** A higher coupon does not make a bond safer.
4. **Municipal exemption:** Interest may be exempt; capital gain generally is not.
5. **Preferred stock:** Senior to common, junior to every debt claim.
6. **Open-end pricing:** Next NAV, not the last reported NAV.
7. **Closed-end discount:** A discount can widen.
8. **ETF versus ETN:** Portfolio ownership versus unsecured issuer debt.
9. **No-load:** No sales load does not mean no annual expenses.
10. **Fund distribution:** Cash received is not automatically profit.
11. **Option buyer versus writer:** Right versus obligation.
12. **Futures margin:** Performance bond creating leverage, not a conventional down payment.
13. **Leveraged fund:** Daily objective, not assured long-period multiple.
14. **Fixed indexed annuity:** Index-linked credit, not ownership of index stocks.
15. **Variable annuity:** Owner bears market risk in separate accounts.
16. **1035 exchange:** Tax deferral does not prove suitability.
17. **Nontraded REIT:** Real estate backing does not create daily liquidity.
18. **ETN:** Index performance cannot cure issuer default.
19. **Digital trading:** Continuous quotation is not the same as secure custody or reliable liquidity.
20. **Diversification:** Many holdings can still share one dominant risk.

### 14.9 Compact memory map

- **Deposits:** institution owes money; insurance may apply.
- **Bonds:** issuer owes money; investor is senior to equity.
- **Common stock:** investor owns the residual.
- **Preferred:** equity income with seniority over common.
- **Funds:** own a portfolio interest; inspect structure and fees.
- **ETNs and structured notes:** issuer owes formula-based money.
- **Options:** rights and obligations with time limits.
- **Futures:** standardized leveraged commitments with daily settlement.
- **Partnerships:** pass-through ownership, illiquidity, manager dependence.
- **Annuities:** insurer contracts for accumulation or income.
- **Life insurance:** mortality protection, sometimes with cash value.
- **Commodities:** price exposure without inherent cash flow.
- **Digital assets:** protocol and custody rights with substantial market and operational risk.

---

## 15. Original Review Questions

### Question 1

A client holds $100,000 in a bank money market deposit account and $100,000 in a money market mutual fund purchased through the same bank. Which statement is most accurate?

A. Both positions are FDIC-insured because each maintains a stable value.
B. Only the deposit account is potentially FDIC-insured; the mutual fund is a security.
C. Only the mutual fund is insured because it is diversified.
D. Neither position can experience any loss of purchasing power.

### Question 2

A one-year zero-coupon bond will pay $1,000 at maturity. If its required market yield rises from 4% to 6%, its price should:

A. rise because future value is unchanged.
B. rise because a higher yield means a higher coupon.
C. fall because the maturity value is discounted at a higher rate.
D. remain unchanged because zero-coupon bonds have no rate risk.

### Question 3

A $1,000-par bond pays a 5% annual coupon and trades at $950. Its current yield is closest to:

A. 4.75%.
B. 5.00%.
C. 5.26%.
D. 5.50%.

### Question 4

Which event is most unfavorable to an investor who owns a premium-priced callable corporate bond?

A. Market rates fall sharply and the issuer calls the bond.
B. Market rates rise and the issuer leaves the bond outstanding.
C. The issuer’s credit rating improves.
D. The call protection period is extended.

### Question 5

Two option-free bonds have the same issuer, yield, and maturity. Bond X has an 8% coupon and Bond Y has a 3% coupon. Which statement is most accurate?

A. Bond X generally has greater duration because it pays more interest.
B. Bond Y generally has greater duration because more of its value arrives later.
C. Their durations must be identical because their maturities match.
D. Coupon size affects credit risk but not rate sensitivity.

### Question 6

A municipal bond yields 3.6%. For an investor in a 28% marginal federal bracket, its approximate tax-equivalent yield is:

A. 2.59%.
B. 3.60%.
C. 4.61%.
D. 5.00%.

### Question 7

Which statement about preferred stock is correct?

A. Preferred holders rank ahead of secured bondholders in liquidation.
B. Preferred dividends are always a contractual debt obligation.
C. Preferred holders generally rank ahead of common holders but behind creditors.
D. Preferred shares cannot be callable.

### Question 8

An employee exercises an NQSO when the stock’s fair market value is $45 and the exercise price is $25. Under the usual federal treatment, the $20 spread is generally:

A. tax-free basis.
B. ordinary compensation income.
C. a long-term capital gain immediately.
D. tax deferred until retirement in every case.

### Question 9

A constant-growth dividend model uses an expected next dividend of $2.40, required return of 9%, and growth of 3%. Estimated value is:

A. $26.67.
B. $40.00.
C. $48.00.
D. $80.00.

### Question 10

An IPO contains newly issued shares and shares sold by a founder. Which statement is correct?

A. All proceeds go to the issuer.
B. Proceeds from the new shares go to the issuer; proceeds from the founder’s shares go to the founder.
C. SEC registration guarantees that both groups of shares are fairly valued.
D. The founder’s shares cannot be part of an IPO.

### Question 11

An open-end mutual-fund purchase order is properly entered before the pricing cutoff. The investor ordinarily receives:

A. the prior day’s NAV.
B. the next computed NAV plus any applicable sales charge.
C. any intraday price specified by the investor.
D. the fund’s NAV only if shares trade on an exchange.

### Question 12

A closed-end fund has NAV of $25 and trades at $22.50. It trades at:

A. a 10% premium.
B. a 10% discount.
C. a 2.5% discount.
D. par because NAV determines exchange price.

### Question 13

Which action most directly helps an investor qualify for a reduced Class A mutual-fund sales charge by counting eligible existing family holdings?

A. Contingent deferred sales charge
B. Right of accumulation
C. Daily mark to market
D. In-kind redemption

### Question 14

Which statement most accurately distinguishes an ETF from an ETN?

A. Both represent insured deposits, but only an ETF trades intraday.
B. An ETF generally represents a pooled portfolio interest; an ETN is generally unsecured issuer debt.
C. An ETN holds index securities in custody, while an ETF is a corporate debenture.
D. An ETF cannot trade at a premium or discount, while an ETN can.

### Question 15

An investor pays $3 for a call with a $40 strike. Ignoring costs and taxes, the expiration break-even stock price is:

A. $37.
B. $40.
C. $43.
D. $47.

### Question 16

An index rises 10% on day one and then falls 9.09% on day two, returning approximately to its starting level. A fund seeking twice the index’s **daily** return will most likely:

A. also finish exactly unchanged.
B. gain approximately 20%.
C. lose value because of daily compounding.
D. be required to distribute the difference as a dividend.

### Question 17

Which statement about a fixed indexed annuity is most accurate?

A. The owner directly owns every stock in the referenced index.
B. The contract credits interest under an index-linked formula, often subject to caps or participation rates.
C. The contract guarantees the full total return of the index, including dividends.
D. It is identical to an index ETF except for its name.

### Question 18

Why does purchasing a variable annuity inside an IRA require special scrutiny?

A. Variable annuities are prohibited in IRAs.
B. The IRA already provides tax deferral, so the annuity must be justified by other contractual features and costs.
C. Separate accounts become FDIC-insured inside an IRA.
D. IRA ownership converts all variable-annuity withdrawals into capital gains.

### Question 19

A futures-based commodity fund can lag a commodity’s spot-price increase primarily because:

A. commodities cannot be held by pooled products.
B. rolling contracts in contango can create negative roll yield.
C. futures are never marked to market.
D. commodity funds are legally required to hold only cash.

### Question 20

Which is the clearest risk of holding cryptocurrency through an online platform rather than through self-custody?

A. The investor acquires voting rights in the platform automatically.
B. Platform insolvency, cybersecurity failure, or withdrawal suspension can impair access to the asset.
C. The digital asset becomes an FDIC-insured bank deposit.
D. Market volatility is eliminated because the platform quotes prices continuously.

## 16. Reasoned Answer Key

### 1. B — The deposit and the fund have different legal structures.

A money market deposit account is a bank deposit and can receive FDIC coverage when the bank, balance, depositor, and ownership category satisfy the rules. A money market mutual fund is an investment-company security. Its short-term portfolio and stable-NAV objective do not convert it into a deposit or provide FDIC insurance. Both can lose purchasing power to inflation, so D is also false.

### 2. C — A higher discount rate lowers present value.

The bond still promises $1,000, but the market now requires 6% rather than 4%. The current price must fall so that purchase price grows to $1,000 at the higher required return. Zero-coupon bonds can be highly rate-sensitive because all cash flow arrives at maturity.

### 3. C — Current yield is annual coupon divided by price.

The annual coupon is $1,000 × 5% = $50. Therefore:

$$
\frac{\$50}{\$950}=5.263\%\approx5.26\%
$$

The 5% coupon rate uses par as its denominator. Current yield uses market price and does not include the $50 gain if the bond matures at par.

### 4. A — A call after rates fall removes an attractive coupon.

Falling rates make refinancing appealing to the issuer. The investor receives the call price and must reinvest in a lower-rate market. Because the bond was bought at a premium, the investor can also lose some premium. Rate increases hurt market value, but they ordinarily make a call less likely; the combination in A is the classic unfavorable call-and-reinvestment event.

### 5. B — Lower coupons generally mean longer duration.

Bond Y returns less value through early coupons, leaving a larger proportion of present value in the maturity payment. Its weighted cash-flow timing is later, so its duration is generally greater. Matching maturity alone does not make durations identical.

### 6. D — Divide the exempt yield by one minus the tax rate.

$$
\text{Tax-equivalent yield}
=
\frac{0.036}{1-0.28}
=
\frac{0.036}{0.72}
=0.05=5.00\%
$$

This simplified comparison ignores state tax, alternative minimum tax, and individual circumstances.

### 7. C — Preferred stock is senior equity, not debt.

Creditors, including secured and unsecured bondholders according to their priorities, stand ahead of preferred shareholders. Preferred generally stands ahead of common for dividends and liquidation. Preferred can be callable, and even cumulative dividends are not identical to contractual bond interest.

### 8. B — The NQSO exercise spread is generally compensation.

The $20 difference between fair market value and exercise price is generally ordinary wage or compensation income at exercise. The shares then receive a basis reflecting the exercise-date value, and later movement is generally capital gain or loss on sale.

### 9. B — Apply the constant-growth dividend formula.

$$
P_0
=
\frac{D_1}{k-g}
=
\frac{\$2.40}{0.09-0.03}
=
\frac{\$2.40}{0.06}
=\$40
$$

The calculation is highly sensitive to the required-return and growth assumptions.

### 10. B — Proceeds follow the seller of each block.

The issuer receives capital from newly created primary shares. The founder receives proceeds from the existing secondary shares the founder sells. Registration supplies a disclosure framework; it does not constitute SEC merit approval or a valuation guarantee.

### 11. B — Open-end funds use forward pricing.

A timely order receives the next NAV calculated after receipt, plus an applicable front-end sales charge. The last published NAV cannot be selected after the fact, and ordinary mutual-fund shares do not trade intraday on an exchange.

### 12. B — Market price is 10% below NAV.

$$
\frac{\$22.50-\$25}{\$25}
=
\frac{-\$2.50}{\$25}
=-10\%
$$

The negative result is a discount. Closed-end exchange prices reflect supply and demand and need not equal NAV.

### 13. B — Rights of accumulation count eligible existing holdings.

A right of accumulation applies current eligible holdings toward a breakpoint. A letter of intent instead credits intended future purchases under its terms. A CDSC is a redemption charge, daily mark-to-market is a futures process, and in-kind redemption is associated with portfolio-transfer mechanics.

### 14. B — The structures create different ownership claims.

An ETF shareholder generally owns an interest in a pooled investment portfolio. An ETN holder owns unsecured debt of the issuer linked to a formula. Both can trade above or below indicative value, and neither is an insured deposit merely because it trades on an exchange.

### 15. C — A long call breaks even at strike plus premium.

The investor must recover both the $40 exercise price and the $3 premium:

$$
\$40+\$3=\$43
$$

Below $40, the call expires without intrinsic value; between $40 and $43, exercise value offsets only part of the premium.

### 16. C — Daily leverage compounds along the return path.

A +2× daily fund rises about 20% on day one, from 100 to 120. It then loses about 18.18% on day two, falling to approximately 98.18. The index returns to 100, but the leveraged fund loses roughly 1.82% before expenses and tracking difference. Its objective is twice each day’s result, not twice every multi-day cumulative result.

### 17. B — Crediting is contractual and formula-based.

A fixed indexed annuity does not give the owner index shares or index dividends. The insurer calculates credit using the chosen method, potentially applying a cap, participation rate, spread, averaging rule, or floor. Contract guarantees remain subject to insurer claims-paying ability and contract conditions.

### 18. B — An IRA already supplies tax deferral.

The annuity’s tax-deferred buildup duplicates a feature already present in the IRA. The recommendation therefore needs a separate rationale, such as a valued income or death-benefit guarantee, and that benefit must justify expenses, restrictions, and insurer exposure. Variable annuities are not categorically prohibited in IRAs, and an IRA does not turn separate accounts into insured deposits.

### 19. B — Contract rolling can subtract from return.

In contango, later-dated futures cost more than expiring near contracts. Repeatedly selling the cheaper near contract and buying the more expensive later contract can create negative roll yield. Spot-price movement is only one part of a futures-based product’s return.

### 20. B — Hosted custody adds an intermediary dependency.

The platform may fail financially, suffer theft or operational breakdown, freeze withdrawals, or have an unclear bankruptcy treatment. Continuous prices do not assure access or remove volatility. Self-custody replaces these platform risks with private-key, backup, theft, and user-error risks rather than eliminating custody risk.

---

## 17. Further Reading

These sources provide current authoritative explanations and investor alerts. Tax and regulatory rules can change; consult the latest primary materials when applying a rule.

### Cash, deposits, and short-term instruments

- [FDIC — Deposit Insurance](https://www.fdic.gov/resources/deposit-insurance/): Coverage categories, limits, and the distinction between insured deposits and investments.
- [NCUA — Share Insurance Coverage](https://ncua.gov/support-services/share-insurance-fund): Federal credit-union share-insurance principles.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Certificates of Deposit](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/investment-products/certificates-deposit-cds): Traditional and brokered CD characteristics, call provisions, and early-sale risk.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Money Market Funds](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/money-market-funds): Money market mutual-fund structure and risk.

### Bonds and municipal securities

- [SEC Investor.gov — Bonds](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/investment-products/bonds-or-fixed-income-products/bonds): Bond terms, prices, yields, credit, and calls.
- [FINRA — Bonds](https://www.finra.org/investors/investing/investment-products/bonds): Investor education on bond types and principal risks.
- [U.S. Treasury — Treasury Marketable Securities](https://treasurydirect.gov/marketable-securities/): Treasury bills, notes, bonds, TIPS, and FRNs.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Mortgage-Backed Securities](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/mortgage-backed-securities): Mortgage-pool and prepayment concepts.
- [MSRB](https://www.msrb.org/): Municipal-security structures, disclosures, pricing, and investor resources.
- [IRS — Tax-Exempt Bonds](https://www.irs.gov/tax-exempt-bonds): Federal tax framework for qualifying municipal obligations.

### Equity, offerings, and valuation inputs

- [SEC Investor.gov — Stocks](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/investment-products/stocks): Common-stock ownership, return, and risk.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Initial Public Offerings](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/initial-public-offerings-ipos): IPO mechanics and investor considerations.
- [SEC Investor.gov — SPACs](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/special-purpose-acquisition-company-spac): Blank-check structure, redemption, warrants, and conflicts.
- [SEC EDGAR](https://www.sec.gov/edgar/search/): Registration statements, prospectuses, periodic reports, and issuer disclosures used in fundamental analysis.
- [IRS Publication 525 — Taxable and Nontaxable Income](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-525): Compensation-property and stock-option tax principles.
- [IRS — Topic No. 427, Stock Options](https://www.irs.gov/taxtopics/tc427): General ISO and nonstatutory-option treatment.

### Funds, ETFs, REITs, and private investments

- [SEC Investor.gov — Mutual Funds and ETFs Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=mutual%20funds%20ETFs): Structures, NAV, fees, trading, and risks.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Mutual Fund Fees and Expenses](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/getting-started/understanding-fees): Sales loads, operating expenses, and long-term cost effects.
- [FINRA Fund Analyzer](https://tools.finra.org/fund_analyzer/): Comparative illustration of mutual-fund and ETF costs.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Closed-End Fund Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=closed-end%20funds): Exchange pricing, premiums, discounts, and leverage.
- [SEC Investor.gov — REIT Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=real%20estate%20investment%20trusts): Listed and nontraded REIT characteristics.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Hedge Funds](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/investment-products/private-investment-funds/hedge-funds): Private-fund eligibility, strategies, fees, and liquidity.
- [IRS Publication 550 — Investment Income and Expenses](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-550): Federal tax treatment of fund distributions and investment income.

### Derivatives and complex products

- [SEC Investor.gov — Options Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=options): Rights, obligations, premiums, and option risks.
- [Options Clearing Corporation — Characteristics and Risks of Standardized Options](https://www.theocc.com/company-information/documents-and-archives/options-disclosure-document): Primary options risk-disclosure document.
- [CFTC — Learn and Protect](https://www.cftc.gov/LearnAndProtect): Futures, leverage, fraud warnings, and commodity-market risks.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Exchange-Traded Note Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=exchange%20traded%20notes): ETN credit, call, market-price, and liquidity risks.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Leveraged and Inverse ETFs](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/general-resources/news-alerts/alerts-bulletins/investor-alerts/sec): Daily reset and compounding concerns.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Structured Notes](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/structured-notes): Embedded derivatives, issuer credit, and payoff complexity.

### Insurance products

- [SEC Investor.gov — Variable Annuities](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/investment-products/insurance-products/variable-annuities): Separate accounts, expenses, surrender charges, and exchanges.
- [FINRA — Annuities](https://www.finra.org/investors/investing/investment-products/annuities): Fixed, variable, and indexed annuity education.
- [NAIC — Consumer Insurance Resources](https://content.naic.org/consumer): State insurance-regulatory guidance on annuities and other insurance products.
- [NAIC — Life Insurance](https://content.naic.org/consumer/life-insurance.htm): Term and permanent policy concepts.
- [IRS Publication 575 — Pension and Annuity Income](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-575): Federal taxation of annuity payments and distributions.
- [26 U.S.C. § 1035](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/26/1035): Statutory framework for qualifying insurance-contract exchanges.

### Commodities, precious metals, and digital assets

- [CFTC — Commodity Pools and Commodity Trading Advisors](https://www.cftc.gov/LearnAndProtect/AdvisoriesAndArticles/commodity_pool_fraud.html): Pooled commodity exposure and fraud risks.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Precious Metals Fraud Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=precious%20metals%20fraud): Dealer, leverage, storage, and pricing concerns.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Crypto Assets](https://www.investor.gov/additional-resources/spotlight/crypto-assets): Investor alerts on digital-asset offerings, platforms, custody, and fraud.
- [CFTC — Digital Assets](https://www.cftc.gov/digitalassets/index.htm): Commodity-regulatory education and customer advisories.
- [IRS — Digital Assets](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/digital-assets): Federal tax guidance and reporting resources.

---

## Closing Perspective

The safest way to distinguish investment vehicles is to follow the cash flows and legal claims. Ask who owes what, who receives what, when payment occurs, what can interrupt it, and where the investor stands if the arrangement fails. Then examine price sensitivity, liquidity, expenses, taxes, and the investor’s reason for owning it.

A concise final checklist is:

- **Deposits:** verify insurance, ownership category, term, and withdrawal rules.
- **Debt:** verify issuer, priority, maturity, duration, credit, calls, and yield measure.
- **Equity:** verify voting and economic rights, priority, valuation, and concentration.
- **Funds:** verify portfolio, legal structure, NAV mechanics, fees, taxes, benchmark, and liquidity.
