- **Derivatives:** verify right or obligation, notional exposure, maximum loss, expiration, and counterparty.
- **Alternatives:** verify valuation, leverage, lockup, manager incentives, and tax reporting.
- **Insurance:** verify the insured need, guarantor, surrender terms, costs, and tax consequences.
- **Other assets:** verify the exact exposure, custody, cash-flow source, and exit mechanism.

Product names can sound reassuring, innovative, or familiar. The disciplined adviser looks through the name to the economic reality.

# Part III — Client Investment Recommendations and Strategies

## How to Use This Part

This part follows Domain 3 of the NASAA Series 65 examination content outline effective September 1, 2023: **Client Investment Recommendations and Strategies**, which accounts for 39 scored questions, or 30% of the scored examination. Its organization tracks all 11 objective groups in that domain. The discussion is original and educational; it neither reproduces actual examination questions nor substitutes for the official outline, current law, firm procedures, or advice from a qualified tax or legal professional.

The central discipline of Domain 3 is not memorizing isolated products. It is learning to move in a defensible sequence:

1. identify the legal and economic client;
2. gather and verify relevant facts;
3. translate those facts into goals, constraints, risk capacity, and risk tolerance;
4. select an appropriate strategy and account structure;
5. implement with attention to taxes, costs, liquidity, and execution;
6. monitor for changes; and
7. document the reasoning.

An investment adviser’s fiduciary duty is broader than a broker-dealer’s transaction-specific suitability analysis. A suitable investment is not automatically the best recommendation for an advisory client. The adviser must consider the client’s entire relationship, avoid or fully and fairly disclose conflicts, seek best execution where responsible for selecting execution, and provide advice in the client’s best interest. A recommendation can fail this standard even if the security is not inherently “bad.” Excess cost, avoidable tax, illiquidity, concentration, or a conflict can make an otherwise reasonable security inappropriate.

> **Tax boundary.** Series 65 candidates should understand general tax consequences well enough to recognize client constraints and identify when specialist advice is needed. Tax rates, exclusions, deductions, contribution limits, phaseouts, and estate or gift thresholds change. Unless a date is expressly stated, this text emphasizes durable concepts rather than volatile annual numbers. General tax education is not individualized tax advice.

## Domain 3 Road Map

| Objective group | Core question |
|---|---|
| 1. Client types | Who legally owns the assets, bears the tax, and has authority to act? |
| 2. Client profile | What does the client need, want, understand, and have the financial capacity to bear? |
| 3. Capital-market theory | How are risk, return, diversification, and information reflected in prices? |
| 4. Strategies, styles, and techniques | How will the portfolio pursue its objectives and control its risks? |
| 5. Tax considerations | How do ownership, account type, and transactions affect after-tax outcomes? |
| 6. Retirement plans | Which tax and distribution framework governs retirement assets? |
| 7. ERISA | What fiduciary and prohibited-transaction rules apply to covered plans? |
| 8. Special accounts | What purpose-specific tax and ownership rules apply? |
| 9. Ownership and estate techniques | Who owns, controls, receives, and transfers property? |
| 10. Trading and execution | How do orders, market roles, costs, and conflicts affect implementation? |
| 11. Performance measurement | What return was earned, for whom, over what period, and for how much risk? |

---

## 1. Client Types and Their Investment Implications

A client label is not a complete profile. “Conservative corporation,” “wealthy retiree,” and “family trust” each leave critical questions unanswered. The adviser must identify the legal form, governing documents, authorized decision-makers, beneficiaries or owners, tax regime, liquidity needs, investment horizon, and any statutory or contractual limits.

### 1.1 Individuals and sole proprietorships

An **individual** owns property in the person’s own name or through an account registration. The individual’s earned income, investment income, tax bracket, dependents, health, age, employment, estate plan, and other resources can all affect recommendations.

A **sole proprietorship** is not a separate legal entity from its owner for federal income-tax purposes. The owner generally bears the business’s profits, losses, debts, and liquidity risks personally. That fact has several portfolio consequences:

- Business cash is often volatile and may be needed on short notice.
- The owner may already have a concentrated economic exposure to one industry, region, or customer base.
- Retirement-plan opportunities may be available to a self-employed person, but employee-coverage rules matter if the business has workers.
- Personal guarantees and unlimited liability may increase the need for liquidity and insurance.

**Example — human capital is an asset.** Elena owns a cyclical construction business and wants to invest all surplus cash in home-builder stocks because she knows the industry. Her financial capital and human capital would then respond to the same economic shocks. Diversifying away from construction may be prudent even if she has superior familiarity with it.

> **Exam distinction:** A sole proprietorship may use a trade name and a separate business bank account, but that does not by itself create the liability shield of a corporation or limited liability company.

### 1.2 General and limited partnerships

A **general partnership (GP)** is an association of two or more persons carrying on a business as co-owners. General partners generally participate in management and bear personal liability for partnership obligations, subject to applicable state law. Partnership income and loss commonly pass through to partners for federal tax purposes.

A **limited partnership (LP)** has at least one general partner and one or more limited partners. The general partner manages the enterprise and generally bears broader liability; limited partners generally have liability limited to their investment if statutory conditions are observed. Modern statutes may permit limited partners to exercise specified rights without losing limited status, so do not rely on an oversimplified “no participation whatsoever” rule.

For either form, the adviser should obtain and read the partnership agreement. It may govern:

- who can open and trade an account;
- permitted investments and borrowing;
- required reserves and distributions;
- admission, withdrawal, death, or incapacity of partners;
- valuation and transfer restrictions; and
- the partnership’s duration or winding-up process.

A partnership’s investment horizon may be shorter than that of its longest-lived partner. A real-estate partnership scheduled to liquidate in three years should not ordinarily invest near-term construction reserves as if they were perpetual endowment assets.

### 1.3 Limited liability companies

A **limited liability company (LLC)** combines limited-liability features with flexible management and federal tax classification. An LLC may be member-managed or manager-managed. Depending on elections and the number of members, it may be treated for federal tax purposes as a disregarded entity, partnership, S corporation, or C corporation.

The adviser must not infer tax treatment from the letters “LLC.” Review the operating agreement, tax election, authority resolutions, and beneficial ownership. Important questions include:

- Are members permitted to withdraw capital?
- Must the LLC make tax distributions?
- Is one manager authorized to trade, or is unanimous consent required?
- Does the business need operating cash or acquisition reserves?
- Are there buy-sell obligations after a member’s death or departure?

> **Memory aid — “LLC is a shell, not a tax answer.”** Legal form tells you about state-law structure; the tax election tells you how federal income may be reported.

### 1.4 C corporations and S corporations

A **C corporation** is a separate legal and federal income-tax entity. It generally pays corporate income tax, and shareholders may also pay tax when after-tax earnings are distributed as dividends. This is commonly called double taxation. A corporation may retain earnings, but tax and business-purpose rules can affect that decision.

An **S corporation** is a corporation that has made a qualifying federal election. Income and specified items generally pass through to eligible shareholders. S corporations face eligibility and structural limits, including restrictions on shareholders and classes of stock. State treatment may differ from federal treatment.

For both forms, advisers should identify the board or officer with authority, corporate investment policy, cash-flow cycle, debt covenants, planned capital expenditures, and tax posture. Corporate cash portfolios often use tiers:

| Tier | Purpose | Typical horizon | Primary concern |
|---|---|---:|---|
| Operating | payroll, vendors, taxes | days to months | principal stability and immediate liquidity |
| Reserve | contingencies, seasonal needs | months to a few years | liquidity with modest return |
| Strategic | long-term excess capital | multi-year | total return consistent with corporate constraints |

**Example — liability matching.** A corporation must pay a known acquisition installment in nine months. A volatile equity portfolio is unsuitable for that obligation even if management has a high subjective tolerance for risk. The corporation’s **capacity** to risk that money is low because the liability is fixed and near-term.

> **Exam trap:** Corporate officers do not automatically own corporate assets. The account belongs to the corporation, and authority comes from corporate documents and resolutions, not from an officer’s share ownership alone.

### 1.5 Trusts and estates

A **trust** separates legal control from beneficial enjoyment. The **grantor** (settlor or trustor) creates and funds the trust; the **trustee** holds legal title and administers assets; **beneficiaries** receive current or future benefits. One person can occupy more than one role, but the roles remain analytically distinct.

Trust recommendations begin with the trust instrument and applicable law. Determine:

- whether the trust is revocable or irrevocable;
- whether it is a grantor or non-grantor trust for tax purposes;
- the identity and needs of income and remainder beneficiaries;
- required versus discretionary distributions;
- the trustee’s powers, delegation authority, and standard of care;
- concentration, retention, or special-asset provisions;
- termination dates and contingent interests; and
- whether an institutional co-trustee or trust protector has authority.

A trustee may need to balance current income beneficiaries against remainder beneficiaries. Maximizing current yield could erode purchasing power for future beneficiaries; maximizing long-term growth could deprive a current beneficiary of reasonable support. The governing instrument and prudent-investor principles frame the balance.

An **estate** is the collection of a decedent’s assets and obligations administered by a personal representative (executor if named under a will, administrator if appointed otherwise). An estate is temporary. Its portfolio may need liquidity for taxes, debts, expenses, specific bequests, and distributions. The representative should avoid speculative commitments that conflict with the expected administration period.

Trusts and estates can be separate income-tax taxpayers. Undistributed income may reach high marginal tax rates at lower income levels than for individuals, while distributable net income rules can carry certain income and tax character to beneficiaries. This is a specialist area: recognize the issue, gather the documents, and coordinate with counsel or a tax professional.

### 1.6 Foundations and charities

A charitable organization or private foundation is mission-driven rather than owner-driven. Its portfolio should connect spending, liquidity, donor restrictions, legal requirements, and intergenerational equity.

Key distinctions include:

- **Public charity:** generally receives broad public support and conducts charitable programs.
- **Private foundation:** generally funded by a narrower source and subject to special federal rules, including minimum-distribution and self-dealing restrictions.
- **Operating charity:** may need substantial short-term liquidity for programs and payroll.
- **Endowment:** often seeks to support current spending while preserving long-term purchasing power.

Advisers should examine the gift instrument, board-approved investment policy statement, spending formula, restricted funds, state institutional-fund law, expected donations, grant schedule, and environmental or social constraints. “Perpetual” does not mean “all equities”: annual grants and operating needs still require a liquidity plan.

**Example — sustainable spending.** A foundation with a long-term nominal return objective of 7%, expected inflation of 2.5%, investment expenses of 0.8%, and annual spending of 5% has a rough arithmetic gap:

$$
7.0\%-2.5\%-0.8\%-5.0\%=-1.3\%.
$$

If those assumptions persist, real capital declines. The board must revisit return expectations, spending, risk, or costs; the adviser should not simply promise higher returns.

### 1.7 A client-type decision table

| Client | Governing source | Tax emphasis | Frequent portfolio constraint |
|---|---|---|---|
| Individual | account agreement, personal plan | individual income, gains, estate | goals, emergency reserve, life events |
| Sole proprietor | personal and business records | owner-level pass-through | business concentration and cash volatility |
| GP/LP | partnership agreement | pass-through and partner allocations | authority, capital calls, finite term |
| LLC | operating agreement and tax election | depends on classification | member withdrawals and tax distributions |
| C corporation | charter, bylaws, resolutions | entity tax plus shareholder dividends | operating cash and debt covenants |
| S corporation | corporate documents and S election | pass-through | eligibility, distributions, working capital |
| Trust | trust instrument | grantor or fiduciary/beneficiary tax | competing beneficiary interests |
| Estate | will, court authority, applicable law | estate income and transfer taxes | temporary horizon and administration liquidity |
| Charity/foundation | charter, gift terms, IPS | exempt-purpose and excise-tax rules | spending, restrictions, mission continuity |

> **Exam trap — identity before strategy.** Never recommend from the entity label alone. Confirm legal authority, governing documents, tax classification, cash needs, and beneficiaries or owners.

---

## 2. Constructing the Client Profile

The client profile is a living decision record, not a one-time questionnaire. It must identify the client’s **financial goals and objectives** and **tax situation**, among other facts. Facts become recommendations only after the adviser reconciles goals, resources, obligations, constraints, emotions, knowledge, and time.

### 2.1 Goals: purpose before product

Goals should be specific enough to plan and monitor. “Growth” is vague. “Accumulate $750,000 in today’s dollars for retirement beginning in 18 years while maintaining a six-month emergency reserve” is actionable.

Common goals include:

- capital preservation;
- current income;
- long-term growth or capital appreciation;
- retirement funding;
- education funding;
- home purchase or business acquisition;
- philanthropy;
- estate transfer;
- tax efficiency; and
- a blend of objectives.

Rank goals as essential, important, or aspirational. A client may be able to delay a vacation home but not a required tuition payment. When resources are insufficient, the adviser should expose the tradeoff among savings, time, spending, and risk rather than disguising the shortfall with an aggressive return assumption.

### 2.2 Financial status: cash flow and balance sheet

A **cash-flow statement** covers a period:

$$
\text{Net cash flow}=\text{cash inflows}-\text{cash outflows}.
$$

Inflows may include salary, business distributions, rent, pension, Social Security, interest, and dividends. Outflows include taxes, debt service, living costs, insurance, tuition, gifts, and savings. Distinguish stable from variable cash flows and essential from discretionary spending.

A **personal balance sheet** is a snapshot:

$$
\text{Net worth}=\text{assets}-\text{liabilities}.
$$

Assets should be classified by liquidity, tax treatment, volatility, and usability. A primary residence contributes to net worth but cannot pay next month’s bills without a sale or borrowing. Restricted stock may have substantial stated value but high concentration and sale restrictions. Liabilities should include amount, rate, maturity, collateral, and whether fixed or variable.

Useful ratios include:

$$
\text{Savings rate}=\frac{\text{annual savings}}{\text{gross or net income}},
$$

with the denominator clearly defined, and

$$
\text{Debt-to-income ratio}=\frac{\text{recurring debt payments}}{\text{income}}.
$$

Ratios are prompts for analysis, not universal pass/fail rules.

**Worked cash-flow example.** Malik earns $120,000, receives $4,000 of interest, pays $32,000 in taxes, spends $70,000, and saves the remainder.

$$
\text{Net cash flow}=\$124{,}000-\$102{,}000=\$22{,}000.
$$

If his emergency reserve is deficient, directing the entire $22,000 to illiquid alternatives would ignore liquidity even if the projected long-term return is attractive.

### 2.3 Existing investments and total economic exposure

Inventory all accounts and holdings, not just assets the adviser will manage:

- taxable brokerage and bank accounts;
- employer retirement plans, IRAs, and pensions;
- annuities and cash-value insurance;
- employer stock, options, and restricted awards;
- real estate and private business interests;
- trusts and expected inheritances, treated cautiously;
- education and health accounts;
- debts with investment-like characteristics, such as a variable-rate mortgage; and
- guarantees or contingent liabilities.

Look through fund names to underlying exposures. Five funds do not create diversification if all own the same mega-cap stocks. Also integrate **human capital**. A technology employee with employer stock and technology-heavy funds is more concentrated than the brokerage statement alone suggests.

### 2.4 Taxes, Social Security, and pensions

Gather federal, state, and local tax information relevant to the recommendation: filing status, marginal bracket, capital-loss carryforwards, holding periods, basis, charitable plans, tax-deferred accounts, estimated payments, and potential state residency changes. Do not let the “tax tail wag the investment dog,” but do compare after-tax alternatives.

For Social Security, recognize that claiming age, earnings history, marital status, survivor benefits, taxation, and longevity expectations can affect retirement cash flow. The adviser should not promise a benefit amount without reliable records or current calculators.

For pensions, obtain the summary plan description and estimates. Determine whether the benefit is defined contribution or defined benefit, whether a lump sum is available, survivorship choices, inflation adjustments, plan credit risk or guaranty considerations, and how the pension’s bond-like income affects overall allocation. A secure lifetime pension may increase a client’s capacity to hold growth assets, but it does not automatically justify greater risk; liquidity, bequest goals, and tolerance still matter.

### 2.5 Risk tolerance versus risk capacity

**Risk tolerance** is the client’s psychological willingness to accept uncertainty and loss. **Risk capacity** is the client’s financial ability to absorb loss without jeopardizing essential goals. The prudent recommendation generally respects the lower of the two unless education and a revised plan legitimately change the constraint.

| Situation | Tolerance | Capacity | Planning implication |
|---|---|---|---|
| Young heir with secure income but fear of loss | low | high | educate, but do not force an aggressive portfolio |
| Retiree needing a fixed withdrawal from modest assets | high | low | capacity limits risk despite confidence |
| Long-horizon saver with stable job and calm behavior | high | high | growth allocation may be supportable |
| Near-term house buyer who enjoys speculation | high | low for house funds | segregate goal assets from risk capital |

Questionnaires are useful but incomplete. Test answers against behavior: How did the client react in prior declines? What dollar loss would change the plan? Which goal would be sacrificed? Does the spouse or co-owner agree? A client who selects “aggressive” but will sell after a 10% decline has not demonstrated high tolerance.

**Loss arithmetic matters.** A 50% loss requires a 100% gain to recover:

$$
\frac{1}{1-0.50}-1=100\%.
$$

A 20% loss requires a 25% gain:

$$
\frac{1}{1-0.20}-1=25\%.
$$

### 2.6 Nonfinancial factors

A complete profile includes factors that may not appear on a tax return.

#### ESG, mission, and values

Clients may want to include or exclude investments based on environmental, social, governance, religious, labor, weapons, fossil-fuel, community-development, or other criteria. Clarify the requested method:

- **negative screening:** exclude specified issuers or industries;
- **positive or best-in-class screening:** favor stronger performers on selected criteria;
- **ESG integration:** include financially material ESG information in analysis;
- **thematic investing:** target themes such as clean water or renewable energy;
- **impact investing:** seek measurable social or environmental outcomes alongside financial return; or
- **shareholder engagement:** vote proxies or engage issuers.

Document definitions and tradeoffs. ESG labels are inconsistent, screens can increase tracking error or concentration, and values alignment does not eliminate normal investment risk. The adviser should not substitute personal values for the client’s.

#### Experience and knowledge

Assess education, occupation, prior holdings, trading frequency, product knowledge, and understanding of loss, liquidity, leverage, taxes, and fees. Experience does not by itself establish capacity. A client who traded options for years may still lack the resources to bear a large leveraged loss.

#### Demographics and life events

Age matters because it often correlates with horizon and cash needs, but age alone is not an allocation formula. Consider dependents, health, longevity, marital status, employment stability, citizenship or residency, and foreseeable events such as marriage, divorce, birth, tuition, relocation, retirement, business sale, inheritance, disability, or death.

A life event triggers a profile review. Divorce may require beneficiary changes, liquidity, tax coordination, and a qualified domestic relations order; it should not trigger automatic trading before ownership authority is clear.

### 2.7 Behavioral finance

Behavioral finance studies systematic departures from fully rational decision-making.

| Bias or behavior | Description | Adviser response |
|---|---|---|
| Loss aversion | losses hurt more than equivalent gains please | frame ranges and downside in dollars; precommit to a plan |
| Anchoring | fixation on an initial price or number | refocus on current value and future prospects |
| Confirmation bias | seeking supporting evidence, ignoring contrary facts | present disconfirming evidence and base rates |
| Overconfidence | overstating skill or precision | use ranges, benchmarks, and trading records |
| Recency bias | overweighting recent events | show longer market histories and multiple regimes |
| Herding | following the crowd | reconnect decisions to goals and valuation |
| Familiarity/home bias | preferring known employers, sectors, or countries | quantify concentration and correlated human capital |
| Mental accounting | treating fungible dollars differently | use goal buckets carefully without ignoring total risk |
| Status quo bias/inertia | avoiding changes even when warranted | automate prudent actions and simplify choices |
| Disposition effect | selling winners too quickly and holding losers | apply tax-aware, rule-based rebalancing |
| Availability bias | overweighting vivid or easily recalled events | compare evidence and probabilities |
| Framing | changing choices based on presentation | show both gain and loss frames consistently |

Behavioral coaching is not permission to override the client. It is a method of improving informed, voluntary decisions.

### 2.8 Data gathering and verification

A sound process uses both qualitative interviews and documents. Relevant evidence may include account statements, tax returns, pay stubs, pension estimates, Social Security records, insurance policies, estate documents, business agreements, debt statements, and employee-benefit materials.

Use a loop:

1. **Ask** open-ended questions about goals and concerns.
2. **Quantify** amounts, dates, priorities, and acceptable ranges.
3. **Verify** material facts with records where practical.
4. **Reconcile** contradictions.
5. **Confirm** the resulting profile in plain language.
6. **Document** assumptions and missing information.
7. **Update** periodically and after material events.

If a client refuses necessary information, the adviser should narrow or decline the engagement rather than manufacture precision. A model cannot repair unreliable inputs.

### 2.9 Time horizon is goal-specific

The time horizon is the period until funds are needed, but many clients have multiple horizons. A retiree may need next year’s spending and also invest for a 25-year retirement or heirs. Segmenting assets by goal can clarify the allocation without losing sight of the aggregate portfolio.

A longer horizon generally increases capacity to withstand temporary volatility, but it does not guarantee recovery or make every illiquid investment appropriate. The investment’s liquidity period, the client’s legal horizon, and the goal date must align.

**Present-value illustration.** If a client needs $100,000 in five years and uses a 4% annual planning rate, the amount needed today is:

$$
PV=\frac{FV}{(1+r)^n}=\frac{\$100{,}000}{(1.04)^5}=\$82{,}192\text{ (approximately)}.
$$

The rate is an assumption, not a guaranteed return.

### 2.10 Integrated adviser workflow example

**Facts.** Priya, age 57, plans to retire at 65. She earns $190,000, has stable employment, $80,000 of cash, $620,000 in a diversified 401(k), $310,000 of employer stock with low basis, a mortgage, and a pension expected to cover half of essential retirement spending. She says she is aggressive but became distressed during the last 20% market decline. She wants to fund $60,000 of tuition in three years and avoid fossil-fuel producers.

**Analysis.**

- Tuition has a three-year horizon and low risk capacity.
- Retirement has an eight-year accumulation horizon plus a longer distribution horizon.
- Employer stock creates financial and human-capital concentration.
- The pension supports retirement income but does not fund tuition.
- Stated aggressiveness conflicts with observed tolerance.
- A fossil-fuel exclusion must be defined and its tracking effects disclosed.
- Low basis makes diversification tax-sensitive, not unnecessary.

**Possible workflow.** Reserve tuition in appropriately stable, liquid assets; preserve an emergency fund; model retirement under moderate return and longevity assumptions; create a staged, tax-aware employer-stock diversification plan; use a diversified retirement allocation consistent with demonstrated tolerance and capacity; implement the client-defined screen; coordinate estimated gains and charitable strategies with her tax professional; document assumptions and schedule annual and event-driven reviews.

> **Exam trap — facts do not vote equally.** A client’s statement “I want high returns” cannot defeat a near-term mandatory cash need. Objectives, constraints, capacity, and fiduciary duty must be reconciled.

## 3. Capital-Market Theory

Capital-market theory supplies a vocabulary for connecting risk and expected return. Its models simplify reality; they organize analysis but do not guarantee outcomes.

### 3.1 Return, variance, standard deviation, and covariance

For possible returns $R_i$ with probabilities $p_i$, expected return is:

$$
E(R)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_iR_i.
$$

**Example.** A security has a 25% chance of returning 20%, a 50% chance of returning 8%, and a 25% chance of losing 12%:

$$
E(R)=0.25(20\%)+0.50(8\%)+0.25(-12\%)=6\%.
$$

Expected return is a probability-weighted average, not a promise.

**Variance** measures average squared deviation from expected return:

$$
\sigma^2=\sum p_i[R_i-E(R)]^2.
$$

**Standard deviation** is the square root of variance:

$$
\sigma=\sqrt{\sigma^2}.
$$

Because standard deviation is expressed in return units, it is commonly used as a measure of total volatility. A higher standard deviation means a wider dispersion of possible returns, not necessarily a lower expected return.

**Covariance** indicates whether two assets tend to move together. **Correlation** standardizes covariance between $-1$ and $+1$:

$$
\rho_{AB}=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(A,B)}{\sigma_A\sigma_B}.
$$

- $+1$: perfectly positively correlated;
- $0$: no linear relationship; and
- $-1$: perfectly negatively correlated.

Diversification benefits rise as correlation falls, all else equal. Zero correlation can still diversify; perfect negative correlation is not required.

For a two-asset portfolio with weights $w_A$ and $w_B$:

$$
E(R_p)=w_AE(R_A)+w_BE(R_B),
$$

and

$$
\sigma_p^2=w_A^2\sigma_A^2+w_B^2\sigma_B^2+2w_Aw_B\sigma_A\sigma_B\rho_{AB}.
$$

**Diversification calculation.** Asset A and B each have a 10% standard deviation, equal weights, and zero correlation:

$$
\sigma_p=\sqrt{(0.5)^2(0.10)^2+(0.5)^2(0.10)^2}=7.07\%.
$$

The weighted average volatility would be 10%, but portfolio volatility is lower because the assets do not move in lockstep.

### 3.2 Systematic and nonsystematic risk

**Systematic risk** affects broad markets and cannot ordinarily be eliminated through diversification. Examples include recessions, interest-rate changes, inflation shocks, and geopolitical events. **Nonsystematic risk** is issuer- or industry-specific and can be reduced through diversification. Examples include a factory fire, product failure, or a single company’s management scandal.

**Beta** estimates sensitivity to market movements:

$$
\beta_i=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(R_i,R_m)}{\operatorname{Var}(R_m)}.
$$

Interpretation, subject to estimation error:

- $\beta=1.0$: moves with the market on average;
- $\beta>1.0$: greater market sensitivity;
- $0<\beta<1.0$: lower positive sensitivity;
- $\beta=0$: no estimated linear market sensitivity; and
- negative beta: tends to move opposite the market.

Beta measures systematic risk relative to the selected market proxy. It does not measure total risk, credit risk, liquidity risk, or the size of a possible loss.

### 3.3 Modern Portfolio Theory

**Modern Portfolio Theory (MPT)**, associated with Harry Markowitz, evaluates portfolios rather than isolated securities. Investors are assumed to prefer more expected return and less variance. The **efficient frontier** consists of portfolios offering the highest expected return for each level of risk, or the lowest risk for each expected return.

A portfolio below the frontier is inefficient because another portfolio offers more return for the same risk or less risk for the same return. The frontier is model-dependent: expected returns, volatilities, and correlations are estimates that can change, especially during stressed markets.

Introducing a risk-free asset produces a line from the risk-free rate tangent to the efficient frontier. In the standard model, the tangent portfolio is the optimal risky portfolio. The **Capital Market Line (CML)** describes efficient combinations of the risk-free asset and the market portfolio, using **total risk (standard deviation)** on the horizontal axis.

> **Exam distinction:** CML uses standard deviation and applies to efficient portfolios. The Security Market Line uses beta and can evaluate individual securities or portfolios.

### 3.4 Capital Asset Pricing Model

The **Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM)** states that expected return equals the risk-free rate plus compensation for systematic risk:

$$
E(R_i)=R_f+\beta_i[E(R_m)-R_f].
$$

The bracketed term is the **market risk premium**.

**Example.** If the risk-free rate is 3%, expected market return is 9%, and a security’s beta is 1.2:

$$
E(R_i)=3\%+1.2(9\%-3\%)=10.2\%.
$$

The **Security Market Line (SML)** plots required or expected return against beta. A security plotting above the SML offers more expected return than CAPM requires for its beta and may be described as undervalued under the model. A security below the SML may be described as overvalued.

**Alpha** is actual or forecast return beyond that explained by a benchmark or model. Jensen’s alpha can be written:

$$
\alpha_i=R_i-[R_f+\beta_i(R_m-R_f)].
$$

If the security returned 12% in the preceding CAPM example while the inputs represented realized period returns, estimated alpha would be:

$$
12\%-10.2\%=1.8\%.
$$

Positive historical alpha does not prove skill or predict future alpha. The result depends on period, benchmark, data, fees, and model.

CAPM assumptions are restrictive: frictionless markets, common expectations, rational mean-variance investors, borrowing and lending at a common risk-free rate, and beta as the relevant priced risk. Real investors face taxes, costs, illiquidity, differing horizons, and behavioral errors. On the exam, know the model; in practice, know its limits.

### 3.5 Efficient Market Hypothesis

The **Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH)** proposes that prices reflect information, with three common forms:

| Form | Information reflected in price | Claimed implication |
|---|---|---|
| Weak | past prices and volume | technical analysis should not consistently earn abnormal risk-adjusted returns |
| Semi-strong | all publicly available information | neither technical nor fundamental analysis should consistently earn abnormal risk-adjusted returns after costs |
| Strong | all public and private information | even inside information would not permit persistent abnormal returns |

The strong form is especially implausible in real markets; securities law also prohibits trading on material nonpublic information in covered circumstances. EMH does not say prices are always “correct,” that analysis is useless for every purpose, or that prices never deviate from intrinsic value. It says persistent, exploitable abnormal performance is difficult because information is rapidly incorporated and competition erodes opportunities.

> **Memory aid — WPS:** Weak = **W**hat the tape showed; semi-strong = **P**ublic information; strong = public plus **S**ecret information.

### 3.6 Theory in an adviser’s workflow

Use theory to improve questions rather than to produce false precision:

1. What risks dominate the client’s current portfolio?
2. Are holdings truly diversified, or merely numerous?
3. Which benchmark and risk factor are relevant?
4. Are expected-return inputs realistic after costs and taxes?
5. How do estimates change in a stress test?
6. Does model uncertainty call for a margin of safety?

A mean-variance optimizer may recommend extreme weights from small changes in expected returns. Constraints, resampling, broad asset classes, and judgment can make the result more robust. Fiduciary care requires understanding the tool, not worshiping its output.

---

## 4. Portfolio Strategies, Styles, and Techniques

A strategy describes how the portfolio will pursue client goals. The same technique can be prudent for one client and dangerous for another because suitability and fiduciary analysis are client-specific.

### 4.1 Strategic and tactical asset allocation

**Strategic asset allocation** sets long-term target weights based on objectives, horizon, risk, and capital-market assumptions. The portfolio is rebalanced periodically or when weights breach bands.

**Tactical asset allocation** temporarily departs from strategic targets to exploit perceived short- or intermediate-term opportunities. Tactical decisions add timing, forecast, turnover, tax, and tracking risk.

**Example.** A strategic policy is 60% global equities and 40% bonds, with a five-percentage-point rebalancing band. A 67/33 portfolio triggers sales of equities and purchases of bonds to restore policy. A manager who deliberately moves to 70/30 because equities are forecast to outperform is making a tactical decision.

Rebalancing can be:

- calendar-based;
- threshold- or band-based;
- cash-flow-based, using contributions or withdrawals;
- tax-aware, harvesting losses or locating trades in sheltered accounts; or
- a combination.

Rebalancing controls drift and can enforce “sell high, buy low,” but it does not guarantee profit and may incur taxes and costs.

### 4.2 Active and passive management

**Active management** attempts to outperform a benchmark or achieve another objective through security selection, sector allocation, market timing, duration management, or other deviations. Potential advantages include customization, tax management, risk control, and opportunities in less efficient markets. Challenges include higher costs, turnover, manager risk, and the arithmetic that active investors collectively hold the market before costs and underperform it after costs.

**Passive management** seeks to replicate or closely track an index or rules-based exposure rather than select securities based on forecasts. Potential advantages include transparency, diversification, low cost, tax efficiency, and style consistency. Challenges include tracking error, index construction flaws, concentration in large constituents, and no automatic protection from market declines.

Passive does not mean risk-free, and an index is not unmanaged in an absolute sense: someone defines eligibility, weighting, reconstitution, and corporate-action rules.

### 4.3 Growth, value, income, and capital appreciation

**Growth investing** emphasizes companies expected to expand earnings, revenue, or cash flow faster than the market. Growth stocks often trade at high valuation multiples and can be sensitive to disappointments and interest rates.

**Value investing** seeks securities priced cheaply relative to fundamentals such as earnings, book value, sales, or cash flow. A low multiple may signal opportunity or genuine deterioration—a “value trap.”

**Income investing** emphasizes cash distributions such as bond interest, preferred dividends, common dividends, or real-estate distributions. High yield can reflect high risk. Dividends can be reduced, and bond income may not offset principal loss.

**Capital appreciation** focuses on increase in market value. It overlaps with growth but is an objective, not necessarily a single style. A value stock bought below intrinsic value may also produce capital appreciation.

| Style | Primary emphasis | Frequent risk |
|---|---|---|
| Growth | future expansion | valuation compression and forecast error |
| Value | price below assessed worth | fundamentals may remain impaired |
| Income | current distributions | reaching for yield; inflation; rate or credit risk |
| Capital appreciation | increase in value | volatility and uncertain realization date |

### 4.4 Diversification

Diversification spreads exposure across issuers, industries, asset classes, regions, currencies, maturities, credit qualities, and strategies. Its purpose is to reduce avoidable concentration, especially nonsystematic risk. It cannot eliminate market risk or guarantee against loss.

Common false diversification includes:

- owning many funds with overlapping holdings;
- holding several employers in one cyclical local industry;
- owning stocks and high-yield bonds that both fall in a credit crisis;
- treating multiple properties in one city as geographic diversification; and
- ignoring a client’s employer stock, pension sponsor, or business exposure.

Correlation often rises during crises. Diversification should be tested under stress, not judged only by calm-period averages.

> **Memory aid — “different labels are not different risks.”** Look through wrappers to economic drivers.

### 4.5 Sector rotation

**Sector rotation** shifts exposure among economic sectors based on expected business-cycle, valuation, earnings, interest-rate, or momentum conditions. A traditional cycle narrative might favor economically sensitive sectors early in an expansion and defensive sectors near contraction, but cycles are not mechanically predictable. Rotation can create concentration, timing errors, turnover, and tax costs. It is tactical and active unless embedded in a fixed rules-based methodology.

### 4.6 Dollar-cost averaging

**Dollar-cost averaging (DCA)** invests a fixed dollar amount at regular intervals regardless of price. It buys more shares when prices are low and fewer when prices are high.

**Example.** A client invests $600 over three months:

| Month | Price | Amount invested | Shares purchased |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | $20 | $200 | 10 |
| 2 | $10 | $200 | 20 |
| 3 | $25 | $200 | 8 |
| **Total** |  | **$600** | **38** |

Average cost per share is:

$$
\frac{\$600}{38}=\$15.79.
$$

The arithmetic average of prices is $(20+10+25)/3=\$18.33$, but the relevant cost is total dollars divided by shares.

DCA encourages discipline and reduces the risk of choosing one unfortunate entry date. **It does not assure a profit, prevent loss, or guarantee a lower cost than lump-sum investing.** The investor needs financial ability and willingness to continue during declines. If markets generally rise, immediately investing an available lump sum may have a higher expected return because more money is invested sooner. DCA can nevertheless be behaviorally or risk-management appropriate.

### 4.7 Options: puts and calls

A **call** gives its buyer the right, but not the obligation, to buy the underlying asset at the strike price by or at expiration, depending on contract style. A **put** gives its buyer the right to sell. The option writer assumes the corresponding obligation if exercised.

At expiration, ignoring transaction costs:

$$
\text{Long call profit}=\max(0,S_T-K)-C,
$$

$$
\text{Long put profit}=\max(0,K-S_T)-P,
$$

where $S_T$ is terminal stock price, $K$ is strike, $C$ is call premium, and $P$ is put premium.

**Long call example.** Buy one call with a $50 strike for a $4 premium. Each standard equity contract generally represents 100 shares.

- Break-even at expiration: $50+$4=$54$.
- If stock is $62, profit per share is $62-$50-$4=$8$, or $800 per contract.
- If stock is at or below $50, the option expires worthless and loss is the $400 premium.

**Protective put.** An investor owns stock at $60 and buys a $55 put for $2. The put establishes a floor near $53 before costs for the holding period: $55 strike less $2 premium. It is insurance, not free protection.

**Covered call.** An investor owns the underlying shares and writes calls. Premium provides limited income and downside cushion, but upside is capped above the strike and substantial stock downside remains.

**Uncovered or naked call.** A writer without the underlying stock can face theoretically unlimited loss as the stock rises. This is generally unsuitable for clients unable to bear severe loss and requires appropriate approval.

| Position | Typical purpose | Maximum loss at expiration, simplified |
|---|---|---|
| Long call | leveraged upside | premium |
| Long put | downside speculation or hedge | premium |
| Covered call | income, willingness to sell at strike | large stock loss less premium |
| Protective put | insure stock downside | stock cost minus strike plus premium |
| Naked call | premium income/speculation | theoretically unlimited |

Options involve expiration, volatility, assignment, liquidity, tax, and complexity. “Limited loss” for an option buyer can still mean a 100% loss of premium.

### 4.8 Leverage and margin

**Leverage** uses borrowed funds or derivatives to magnify exposure. It magnifies gains and losses and may create interest expense, margin calls, forced liquidation, and path dependence.

**Example.** An investor buys $100,000 of stock using $50,000 equity and $50,000 borrowed, ignoring interest. If the stock rises 10% to $110,000, equity becomes $60,000—a 20% gain on original equity. If the stock falls 10% to $90,000, equity becomes $40,000—a 20% loss. The asset moved 10%; investor equity moved 20%.

A margin account’s equity is:

$$
\text{Equity}=\text{market value}-\text{debit balance}.
$$

The equity percentage is:

$$
\text{Equity percentage}=\frac{\text{equity}}{\text{market value}}.
$$

If maintenance requirements are breached, the broker-dealer can demand funds or liquidate positions, sometimes without consulting the customer. House requirements may be stricter than regulatory minimums.

### 4.9 Volatility-management techniques

Volatility management may include:

- reducing equity or credit exposure;
- increasing high-quality short-duration assets;
- diversifying risk factors;
- rebalancing;
- buying protective options;
- using collars;
- volatility-targeting, which scales exposure down when measured volatility rises;
- trend-following or managed futures;
- holding cash reserves to avoid forced sales; and
- matching near-term liabilities with stable assets.

Every method has tradeoffs. Option hedges cost premiums; volatility targeting can sell after declines and repurchase after rebounds; cash reduces participation in rising markets; trend strategies can whipsaw. The client should understand which risk is being reduced and what new risks or costs are introduced.

**Sequence-of-returns risk** is especially important during withdrawals. Two portfolios can have the same average return but different retirement outcomes if one suffers losses early while distributions remove capital. A spending reserve, flexible withdrawals, diversification, and a suitable allocation may mitigate—but not eliminate—this risk.

### 4.10 Inverse and leveraged-inverse strategies

An **inverse fund** generally seeks the opposite of a benchmark’s return for a specified period, commonly one day. A leveraged inverse fund seeks a multiple of the opposite daily return. Daily reset and compounding can cause long-horizon performance to differ substantially from the simple inverse or multiple of the benchmark’s cumulative return.

**Path-dependence example.** An index begins at 100, rises 10% to 110, then falls 9.09% back to 100. A daily -1x fund begins at 100, falls 10% to 90, then gains 9.09% of 90, ending near 98.18—not 100. The index’s cumulative return is zero, while the inverse fund loses about 1.82%, before expenses and tracking effects.

Inverse products may be useful for short-term hedging or tactical exposure when understood and monitored. They are not automatically appropriate long-term hedges. Examine objective, reset period, compounding, derivatives, volatility, liquidity, costs, and the client’s ability to monitor.

### 4.11 High-frequency trading

**High-frequency trading (HFT)** uses automated systems, high-speed data, and rapid order submission or cancellation to pursue small, repeated opportunities. Strategies may include market making, statistical arbitrage, or latency-sensitive trading. HFT can add liquidity and tighten quoted spreads in some conditions, but it can also raise concerns about fleeting liquidity, market complexity, operational risk, unequal speed advantages, and instability.

For a typical advisory client, HFT is market-structure context rather than a personal investment strategy. Do not confuse HFT with ordinary algorithmic execution such as breaking a large order into smaller pieces. An adviser still evaluates execution quality, information leakage, venue conflicts, and total costs.

### 4.12 Selecting among strategies: a fiduciary matrix

| Client need | Potential technique | Required caution |
|---|---|---|
| long-term policy discipline | strategic allocation and rebalancing | inputs change; taxes and costs matter |
| low-cost broad exposure | passive diversified funds | index concentration and tracking error |
| customization or loss harvesting | active or direct indexing | fees, turnover, benchmark discipline |
| regular saving from income | DCA | no profit assurance; must continue through declines |
| insure concentrated stock temporarily | protective put or collar | premium, capped upside, tax and contract terms |
| meet spending in 12 months | cash/liability matching | inflation and opportunity cost |
| tactical bearish view | inverse exposure | daily reset, compounding, monitoring |
| enhance income | covered calls | capped upside and retained downside |

> **Suitability versus fiduciary duty.** Showing that a strategy fits a risk questionnaire is only a beginning. An adviser should also compare reasonably available alternatives, costs, taxes, liquidity, complexity, conflicts, and the client’s overall portfolio. A high-cost product can be “suitable” in a broad sense yet fail a fiduciary best-interest analysis when a materially better alternative is reasonably available.

## 5. Tax Considerations in Investment Advice

Taxes affect what a client keeps, when cash becomes available, and which account or owner should hold an asset. They should be integrated into the plan without pretending that the adviser is providing individualized legal or tax advice beyond the adviser’s competence.

### 5.1 Durable tax principles

Federal tax analysis generally asks five questions:

1. **Who is the taxpayer?** Individual, corporation, trust, estate, partnership, or another entity?
2. **What is the item?** Interest, dividend, capital gain, compensation, tax-exempt income, or return of capital?
3. **When is it recognized?** Currently, upon sale, upon distribution, or under a special rule?
4. **What is the basis and holding period?** These affect gain, loss, and character.
5. **Which account or wrapper holds it?** Taxable, tax-deferred, tax-free if qualified, or tax-exempt?

Tax laws change and state rules differ. An adviser may educate a client about general consequences, collect tax facts, model alternatives, and coordinate with professionals. The adviser should not draft legal instruments, guarantee a tax result, or give individualized tax advice without appropriate qualification.

### 5.2 Marginal, average, and effective rates

A **marginal tax rate** is the rate applied to the next dollar of taxable income. An **average or effective rate** is total tax divided by a chosen income measure.

**Example.** If a taxpayer owes $18,000 on $100,000 of taxable income, the average rate is 18%. The marginal rate may be higher or lower depending on the bracket applying to the next dollar. Investment decisions involving an additional dollar of income generally focus on the marginal rate, plus phaseouts, surtaxes, state taxes, and other interactions.

Progressive brackets do not mean all income is taxed at the top marginal rate. Each band is generally taxed at its applicable rate.

### 5.3 Interest and dividends

Most taxable bond and bank interest is taxed as ordinary income when recognized. Interest on qualifying municipal obligations is generally exempt from federal income tax, but capital gains on sale are not automatically exempt. State treatment varies, and private-activity bond interest can have alternative minimum tax implications.

Compare a tax-exempt yield with a taxable yield using the **tax-equivalent yield**:

$$
\text{Tax-equivalent yield}=\frac{\text{tax-exempt yield}}{1-\text{marginal tax rate}}.
$$

If a municipal bond yields 3.2% and the relevant marginal rate is 32%:

$$
\frac{3.2\%}{1-0.32}=4.706\%.
$$

A taxable bond must yield about 4.71% before tax to equal the 3.2% federal tax-exempt yield, assuming equal risk, maturity, liquidity, and no other tax differences. Those assumptions often fail; yield alone never establishes superiority.

**Qualified dividends** received by eligible noncorporate taxpayers and meeting applicable requirements may receive preferential long-term capital-gain rates. **Nonqualified dividends** are generally taxed at ordinary-income rates. A fund distribution labeled “dividend” economically may contain ordinary dividends, qualified dividends, capital-gain distributions, or return of capital; use the tax reporting, not the marketing label.

A **return of capital** generally is not immediately taxed to the extent of basis; it reduces basis. Once basis reaches zero, additional distributions may produce gain. Return of capital is not automatically either good or bad—it may reflect tax-deferred economics, depreciation, or simply a fund returning investor money.

### 5.4 Capital gains, losses, holding periods, and netting

For a taxable disposition:

$$
\text{Realized gain or loss}=\text{amount realized}-\text{adjusted basis}.
$$

The amount realized is generally sale proceeds net of relevant selling costs. Basis usually begins with cost and is adjusted under tax rules. A realized gain is not necessarily recognized if a specific nonrecognition rule applies, but the exam usually focuses on ordinary taxable sales.

Capital gain or loss is generally **short term** when the asset is held one year or less and **long term** when held more than one year. Short-term net gains generally receive ordinary-income treatment; long-term net gains may receive preferential rates for noncorporate taxpayers. Special assets can have special rates or character rules.

A simplified individual capital-gain netting sequence is:

1. net short-term gains and losses;
2. net long-term gains and losses;
3. net the short-term and long-term results if they have opposite signs; and
4. apply the resulting character and any limitation or carryforward rules.

Individuals may generally deduct net capital losses against ordinary income only up to a statutory annual limit, with unused losses carried forward, subject to current law. Corporations have different capital-loss rules and should not be assumed to receive the individual deduction.

**Example.** A client has a $9,000 short-term gain and a $14,000 long-term loss. Netting produces a $5,000 net long-term capital loss. The amount currently deductible against ordinary income is subject to the applicable statutory limit; the remainder may carry forward.

### 5.5 Basis methods and adjustments

Basis prevents the same economic amount from being taxed twice. Common basis rules include:

- purchased security: cost plus applicable acquisition adjustments;
- reinvested dividends: each reinvestment generally creates additional basis;
- gift: often carries over donor basis for gain, with special dual-basis rules if fair market value at the gift is below donor basis;
- inherited property: generally receives basis tied to fair market value at death or an authorized alternate valuation date, subject to exceptions and current law;
- return of capital: reduces basis;
- stock split: total basis generally stays the same while basis per share changes; and
- wash sale: a disallowed loss generally adjusts basis of replacement securities.

**Stock-split example.** An investor buys 100 shares for $6,000 and receives a 2-for-1 split. The investor now owns 200 shares with total basis still $6,000, or $30 per share.

**Gift-basis example.** Donor basis is $40,000 and fair market value on the gift date is $55,000. For a later gain sale, the donee generally uses the $40,000 carryover basis. If fair market value on the gift date had instead been $30,000, dual-basis rules could cause one basis for gain and another for loss; seek tax guidance rather than applying a one-line shortcut.

**Inherited-basis distinction.** Lifetime gifting may transfer low basis, while property included in a decedent’s estate often receives a basis adjustment at death. Estate inclusion, basis adjustment, and transfer tax are related but not identical questions.

### 5.6 Wash sales and tax-loss harvesting

The federal **wash-sale rule** generally disallows a loss when a taxpayer sells a security at a loss and acquires the same or a substantially identical security within a period beginning 30 days before and ending 30 days after the sale. The disallowed loss generally is added to replacement basis and the holding period carries over.

Tax-loss harvesting realizes losses to offset gains or, within limits, other income. Risks include:

- violating wash-sale rules across the client’s accounts, spouse’s transactions, or certain retirement-account purchases;
- changing market exposure;
- realizing later gains because basis is lower;
- transaction costs and spreads;
- letting tax motives override investment merit; and
- using an allegedly different security that is economically inappropriate.

A tax loss is a deferral or timing asset, not free money. Its value depends on current and future rates, character, holding period, and whether the taxpayer can use it.

### 5.7 Alternative Minimum Tax

The **Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT)** is a parallel federal tax calculation intended to limit the benefit of certain exclusions, deductions, and preferences. A taxpayer generally pays the greater applicable liability after statutory mechanics and credits. Candidates should recognize that items treated favorably under the regular tax can be treated differently under AMT. Interest from some private-activity municipal bonds is a classic investment-related concern. Exemptions and phaseouts change, so avoid memorizing undated amounts.

### 5.8 Taxation by client form

#### Individuals

Individuals may face ordinary-income rates, preferential rates on qualifying long-term capital gains and dividends, limits on capital-loss deductions, net investment income tax in applicable cases, state taxes, and basis or holding-period rules. Account location and realization timing can affect after-tax wealth.

#### C corporations

A C corporation generally pays tax at the entity level. Dividends paid to shareholders generally are not deductible by the corporation and can be taxed again to shareholders. Corporate capital losses generally may offset only capital gains, with carry rules determined by current law. Corporations can have special rules such as the dividends-received deduction for qualifying dividends from domestic corporations.

#### S corporations and partnerships

These are generally pass-through regimes. Tax items flow to owners, often whether or not equivalent cash is distributed. Owners may need tax distributions to pay liability. Basis, at-risk, passive-loss, and distribution rules can limit losses or create gain. The entity account’s liquidity strategy should account for tax distributions and capital calls.

#### Trusts and estates

Trusts and estates may deduct certain amounts distributed and beneficiaries may report items carried out under distributable net income rules. Retained taxable income can reach high brackets quickly. Capital gains are often retained at the fiduciary level but governing documents, local law, and tax rules matter. Grantor trusts may be taxed to the grantor instead. Never infer tax treatment solely from “revocable” or “irrevocable” without review.

#### Charitable entities

Tax-exempt status does not make every transaction tax-free. Unrelated business taxable income, debt-financed income, private-foundation excise taxes, self-dealing, excess business holdings, jeopardizing investments, and taxable expenditures may matter. Coordinate with charity counsel and tax advisers.

### 5.9 Tax-deferred and tax-free account location

**Asset location** decides which investments sit in taxable, tax-deferred, or potentially tax-free accounts. A general framework—not a universal rule—is:

- tax-inefficient ordinary-income assets may benefit from sheltered accounts;
- tax-efficient broad equity exposure may fit taxable accounts because gains can be deferred and losses harvested;
- highest expected-return assets may be attractive in Roth accounts because qualified growth may escape tax; and
- municipal bonds usually lose their federal tax-exemption advantage inside tax-deferred accounts.

But liquidity, required distributions, creditor rules, beneficiary plans, state law, and available fund menus can override tax efficiency. Compare the whole household rather than optimizing each account in isolation.

**After-tax yield.** For taxable ordinary interest:

$$
\text{After-tax yield}=\text{pre-tax yield}(1-t).
$$

A 5.5% taxable yield at a 30% marginal rate produces:

$$
5.5\%(1-0.30)=3.85\%.
$$

### 5.10 Estate and gift tax fundamentals

The federal transfer-tax system includes gift tax during life, estate tax at death, and generation-skipping transfer tax for certain transfers to younger generations. Durable concepts include:

- the **donor** is generally responsible for gift tax;
- an annual exclusion may shelter qualifying present-interest gifts up to a changing annual amount;
- direct qualifying payments of tuition to an educational institution or medical expenses to a provider may receive special treatment;
- taxable gifts above exclusions generally reduce the donor’s available lifetime transfer-tax exemption before generating out-of-pocket gift tax;
- gift splitting by spouses requires conditions and proper reporting;
- the marital deduction generally defers transfer tax for qualifying transfers to a citizen spouse;
- a charitable deduction may apply to qualifying transfers;
- property included in a gross estate is valued under estate-tax rules and may receive a basis adjustment under income-tax rules; and
- portability may allow a surviving spouse to use a deceased spouse’s unused exclusion if a timely, proper estate-tax election is made.

Annual exclusions, lifetime exemptions, rates, and filing requirements are volatile and must be checked for the relevant year.

**Present versus future interest.** The annual gift-tax exclusion generally requires a present interest—the recipient has immediate use, possession, or enjoyment. A future interest generally does not qualify merely because the transfer is irrevocable.

**Gift-tax example without a volatile threshold.** A donor transfers publicly traded shares worth more than the current annual exclusion to an adult child. The amount above available exclusions is a taxable gift that generally consumes lifetime exemption first; a gift-tax return may be required even if no immediate gift tax is paid. The child’s income-tax basis is determined under gift-basis rules, not automatically fair market value.

### 5.11 Adviser tax workflow

1. Identify account owner and tax classification.
2. Obtain basis, holding period, loss carryforwards, and relevant bracket information.
3. Compare pre-tax and after-tax outcomes.
4. Consider realization, asset location, charitable giving, and distribution sequencing.
5. Stress test future rates rather than assuming today’s rate forever.
6. Coordinate complex matters with a CPA, enrolled agent, or attorney.
7. State assumptions and obtain client approval.
8. Never promise that a strategy “avoids tax” unless the law and facts support that precise statement.

> **Exam trap:** Tax avoidance through lawful planning is not the same as illegal tax evasion. But a tax-efficient recommendation can still be unsuitable if it sacrifices liquidity, diversification, or economic merit.

---

## 6. Retirement Plans and Accounts

Retirement arrangements differ in who establishes them, who contributes, whether contributions are pretax or after-tax, how earnings grow, when distributions are taxed, whether required minimum distributions apply, and which fiduciary regime governs.

### 6.1 Qualified and nonqualified plans

A **qualified plan** meets Internal Revenue Code requirements and receives tax advantages. Employer contributions generally may be deductible subject to law; plan earnings grow tax-deferred; employees generally are not taxed on employer contributions when made; and distributions are generally taxed when received, except for qualified Roth amounts or other basis. Qualified plans must follow participation, vesting, funding, nondiscrimination, reporting, and distribution rules as applicable.

Common qualified plans include:

- defined benefit pension plans;
- profit-sharing plans;
- money purchase pension plans;
- stock bonus plans; and
- 401(k) plans.

A **nonqualified deferred-compensation plan** does not meet qualified-plan requirements. It can selectively cover executives and provide flexible benefits, but usually lacks the same tax, funding, vesting, ERISA, and creditor protections. Benefits may remain the employer’s unsecured promise and can be exposed to employer insolvency. Constructive receipt, economic benefit, and Internal Revenue Code Section 409A rules are important.

> **Exam distinction:** “Nonqualified” does not mean illegal. It means the arrangement does not qualify for the same tax-favored treatment and rules as a qualified retirement plan.

### 6.2 Defined benefit versus defined contribution

A **defined benefit (DB) plan** promises a formula-based retirement benefit, often related to salary and service. The employer bears primary investment and actuarial risk. Funding assumptions, longevity, interest rates, and plan health matter.

A **defined contribution (DC) plan** defines contributions to an individual account; the eventual benefit depends on contributions, investment performance, fees, and withdrawals. The participant generally bears investment risk.

| Feature | Defined benefit | Defined contribution |
|---|---|---|
| Promise | retirement benefit formula | contribution/account balance |
| Investment risk | primarily employer/plan sponsor | primarily participant |
| Portability | often less portable | generally more portable |
| Typical example | traditional pension | 401(k), profit-sharing account |
| Retirement income | often annuity, sometimes lump sum | withdrawals or purchased annuity |

### 6.3 Traditional IRA

A **traditional individual retirement arrangement (IRA)** accepts eligible contributions subject to current limits. Contributions may be deductible, partially deductible, or nondeductible depending on compensation, income, filing status, and workplace-plan coverage. Earnings generally grow tax-deferred.

Distributions generally contain taxable ordinary income except for recovery of after-tax basis. If an owner has basis in any traditional, SEP, or SIMPLE IRAs, the federal pro rata rule generally prevents selecting only the after-tax dollars for conversion or withdrawal. Form 8606 is important for tracking basis.

Premature distributions may face an additional tax unless an exception applies. Traditional IRA owners generally must begin required minimum distributions under the age rules then in effect. Beneficiary distribution rules depend on beneficiary type, relationship, age, disability or chronic illness status, and the owner’s death date; current law should be checked.

**Deduction versus contribution.** Eligibility to contribute and eligibility to deduct are different questions. A covered, higher-income worker may make a nondeductible contribution even when the deduction is limited, if otherwise eligible.

### 6.4 Roth IRA

A **Roth IRA** is funded with after-tax contributions; contributions are not deductible. Qualified distributions are generally federal income-tax free. A qualified distribution generally requires satisfaction of a five-tax-year rule and a qualifying event such as reaching the applicable age, death, disability, or qualifying first-home use within statutory limits.

Roth IRA ordering rules generally treat distributions as coming first from regular contributions, then conversions, then earnings. This can make return of regular contributions tax- and penalty-free, but conversion amounts and earnings have distinct rules.

Original Roth IRA owners generally are not subject to lifetime required minimum distributions under current federal law. Beneficiaries are subject to post-death rules.

Income limits can restrict direct Roth IRA contributions. A Roth conversion moves eligible pretax retirement amounts into Roth status and generally creates current taxable income. Conversion analysis should consider current and expected future rates, cash available to pay tax, time horizon, estate goals, Medicare and other income-related effects, and legislative uncertainty. A conversion is not automatically beneficial merely because tax-free growth sounds attractive.

### 6.5 Traditional versus Roth decision framework

| Question | Favors traditional treatment, all else equal | Favors Roth treatment, all else equal |
|---|---|---|
| Current versus future marginal rate | current rate expected higher | future rate expected higher |
| Need current deduction | high | low |
| Time for tax-free compounding | less decisive | long horizon can help |
| Ability to pay tax from outside funds | not needed for contribution | helpful, especially for conversion |
| Desire to reduce owner RMDs | less favorable | Roth IRA generally more favorable |
| Legislative/tax diversification | pair with Roth assets | pair with traditional assets |

The future is uncertain. Holding both pretax and Roth assets can create **tax diversification**, allowing flexible distribution planning.

### 6.6 Solo 401(k): traditional and Roth features

A **one-participant or Solo 401(k)** covers a business owner with no common-law employees other than a spouse, subject to plan rules. The owner may contribute in two capacities:

- as employee through elective deferrals; and
- as employer through nonelective or profit-sharing contributions.

Limits, compensation calculations, aggregation across plans, deadlines, and filings change. A self-employed client must account for self-employment-tax adjustments and plan documents. If an eligible common-law employee is hired, the plan may need to cover the employee and cease functioning as a simple owner-only arrangement.

Traditional elective deferrals are generally pretax and taxable when distributed. A plan may permit designated Roth deferrals, contributed after tax with potentially tax-free qualified distributions. Employer contribution Roth treatment has evolved under recent legislation and plan implementation; verify current rules and the plan document.

A Solo 401(k) can allow larger contributions than an IRA for some owners and may permit loans if the plan allows, but it brings administration, deadlines, investment constraints, and potential filing duties.

> **Exam trap:** “Solo” describes eligible participation, not a different species of tax law. A business with eligible non-spouse employees cannot simply exclude them to preserve an owner-only plan.

### 6.7 Rollovers and transfers

A **direct trustee-to-trustee transfer** moves IRA assets directly between custodians and generally avoids withholding and the once-per-12-month limit that can apply to certain indirect IRA rollovers.

A **direct rollover** from an employer plan sends eligible rollover funds directly to another eligible plan or IRA. An **indirect rollover** pays the participant, who generally must complete the rollover within the applicable period; mandatory withholding may apply to eligible employer-plan distributions, requiring the participant to replace withheld funds to roll over the full amount.

The decision to roll assets from an employer plan to an IRA is fiduciary advice when given by an adviser. Compare:

- fees and expenses;
- services and investment options;
- institutional pricing;
- creditor protection;
- plan loan implications;
- withdrawal flexibility;
- required minimum distributions;
- access to stable-value or employer stock features;
- penalty exceptions; and
- consolidation convenience.

Do not recommend a rollover merely to create advisory assets.

### 6.8 Retirement distribution risks

Retirement planning addresses:

- longevity risk;
- inflation risk;
- sequence-of-returns risk;
- health and long-term-care costs;
- tax-rate and legislative risk;
- cognitive decline and fraud;
- withdrawal sustainability;
- market, credit, and liquidity risk; and
- bequest or charitable goals.

A fixed percentage is not automatically sustainable. Spending policies may use guardrails, floors and ceilings, required-minimum-distribution methods, essential-versus-discretionary segmentation, or partial annuitization.

**Real spending example.** If a retiree needs $60,000 today and inflation averages 3%, estimated nominal need after ten years is:

$$
FV=\$60{,}000(1.03)^{10}=\$80{,}635\text{ (approximately)}.
$$

### 6.9 Beneficiaries and retirement assets

A retirement account generally passes according to its valid beneficiary designation, not merely under a will. Spousal consent can be required for certain employer-plan beneficiary choices. Trusts as beneficiaries introduce complex look-through, distribution, control, and tax issues. Advisers should identify stale designations after marriage, divorce, birth, death, or estate-plan revision and coordinate changes, but should not draft the trust or provide legal conclusions beyond competence.

---

## 7. ERISA Fiduciary Standards, Section 404(c), IPSs, and Prohibited Transactions

The Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 (**ERISA**) is a federal law governing many private-sector employee benefit plans. Governmental plans and certain church plans generally are exempt, and individual IRAs are not ERISA plans merely because they are retirement accounts, though other federal rules apply.

### 7.1 Who is an ERISA fiduciary?

Fiduciary status is functional. A person is a fiduciary to the extent the person:

- exercises discretionary authority or control over plan management;
- exercises authority or control over plan assets;
- renders investment advice for a fee under the applicable standard; or
- has discretionary authority over plan administration.

Titles do not control. A person can be a fiduciary for one function but not another.

### 7.2 Core fiduciary duties

ERISA fiduciaries generally must act:

- **solely in the interest** of participants and beneficiaries;
- for the **exclusive purpose** of providing benefits and defraying reasonable plan expenses;
- with the care, skill, prudence, and diligence of a prudent person familiar with such matters;
- by diversifying plan investments to minimize large-loss risk unless clearly prudent not to do so; and
- in accordance with plan documents insofar as the documents comply with ERISA.

These duties are often summarized as loyalty, prudence, diversification, and obedience to plan documents.

Prudence focuses heavily on process. A poor outcome is not conclusive proof of breach, and a good outcome does not cure a reckless process. Fiduciaries should investigate alternatives, understand fees, monitor service providers and investments, document decisions, and act when circumstances change.

### 7.3 ERISA Section 404(c)

ERISA **Section 404(c)** and Department of Labor regulations can provide limited relief to fiduciaries of qualifying participant-directed individual account plans for losses that are the direct and necessary result of a participant’s exercise of control. It is not a blanket immunity.

A compliant structure generally requires, among other things:

- genuine participant control;
- a broad range of investment alternatives with materially different risk and return characteristics;
- opportunities to give investment instructions with appropriate frequency;
- specified information and disclosures; and
- proper operation under the regulation.

Even when Section 404(c) applies, fiduciaries remain responsible for prudent selection and monitoring of the menu, reasonable fees, required disclosures, and other fiduciary functions. A sponsor cannot place a plainly imprudent, excessively costly menu before participants and shift all responsibility to them.

> **Memory aid — “404(c) covers choices, not the choice architecture.”** Participant control may protect against consequences of the participant’s allocation; it does not erase fiduciary responsibility for building and monitoring the menu.

### 7.4 Investment policy statement

An **investment policy statement (IPS)** documents the framework for making and monitoring investment decisions. ERISA does not universally mandate a written IPS in every circumstance, but a well-designed IPS can evidence a prudent process.

A plan IPS may address:

- plan purpose and objectives;
- fiduciary and service-provider roles;
- participant demographics and plan design;
- investment-menu structure;
- selection criteria;
- benchmarks;
- fees and share-class review;
- monitoring frequency and watch-list rules;
- replacement and mapping procedures;
- qualified default investment alternatives;
- participant communication; and
- documentation and amendment.

An IPS is not a “set and forget” promise. If drafted too rigidly and then ignored, it can become evidence of poor governance. Use principles that guide judgment and follow the document or amend it through proper process.

### 7.5 Prohibited transactions

ERISA and the Internal Revenue Code prohibit specified transactions between a plan and a **party in interest** or **disqualified person**, absent an exemption. Common categories include:

- sale, exchange, or leasing of property;
- lending money or extending credit;
- furnishing goods, services, or facilities;
- transfer or use of plan assets for a party in interest;
- fiduciary self-dealing;
- acting for a party with interests adverse to the plan; and
- receiving consideration from a party dealing with the plan in connection with plan transactions.

Statutory and administrative exemptions permit many ordinary arrangements if conditions are met, such as reasonable service contracts for necessary services and no more than reasonable compensation. The existence of an exemption is not a shortcut; every condition must be satisfied.

**Example — conflict.** A plan adviser recommends an affiliated fund that pays the adviser more than a comparable alternative. This raises loyalty, compensation, disclosure, prohibited-transaction, and exemption questions. Calling the fund “suitable” does not resolve them.

### 7.6 Fiduciary monitoring workflow

1. Identify every fiduciary function and responsible person.
2. Read plan and trust documents.
3. Establish objectives and menu architecture.
4. Gather comparable fee, performance, risk, service, and share-class data.
5. Decide through a prudent, documented process.
6. Deliver participant and fiduciary disclosures.
7. Monitor investments, providers, costs, and plan demographics.
8. Correct problems and update documents.

**Fee example.** Two otherwise comparable funds have expense ratios of 0.70% and 0.10%. On $10 million, the annual expense difference is approximately:

$$
\$10{,}000{,}000(0.007-0.001)=\$60{,}000.
$$

The cheaper fund is not automatically prudent—the services or exposure may differ—but fiduciaries should understand and document why participants pay more.
