> **Exam trap:** ERISA fiduciary obligations are not reduced to ordinary suitability. They impose duties of loyalty and prudence, and conflicts require substantive analysis, not just a signature on a disclosure.

## 8. Special-Purpose Accounts: Education, Custodial, and Health

Special accounts receive favorable treatment because they serve a defined purpose. The benefit comes with ownership, contribution, distribution, beneficiary, and investment rules. Never recommend an account merely because it is “tax advantaged”; first match the statutory purpose to the client’s goal.

### 8.1 Section 529 plans

Section **529 qualified tuition programs** are state or educational-institution programs that generally fall into two categories:

- **education savings plans**, in which contributions are invested for future qualified expenses; and
- **prepaid tuition plans**, in which the purchaser acquires tuition credits or units under plan terms.

Contributions are not federally deductible. Earnings grow federal income-tax deferred, and distributions are generally federally tax free when used for qualified education expenses under current law. States may offer deductions, credits, creditor protection, or other benefits, often conditioned on using the home-state plan. State recapture can apply after a nonqualified withdrawal or outbound rollover.

A 529 education savings account generally has an **account owner** who controls investments and distributions and a **designated beneficiary** whose qualified expenses can be paid. The owner can usually change the beneficiary to another qualifying family member without current federal income-tax consequences, subject to applicable rules. This control distinguishes a 529 from an irrevocable gift under UTMA or UGMA.

Qualified expenses have expanded over time and can include specified postsecondary tuition, fees, books, supplies, equipment, certain room and board, computers, registered apprenticeship expenses, limited student-loan repayment, and limited K–12 tuition under federal rules. Definitions and dollar limits change, and state conformity can differ. Verify the year and jurisdiction.

Contributions are completed gifts for federal gift-tax purposes even though the owner generally retains control. A special election may allow a contribution to be spread ratably over five years of annual exclusions. Later gifts during that period and the contributor’s death can affect the result; use current tax guidance.

Nonqualified distributions generally allocate between contributions and earnings. The earnings portion is generally subject to income tax and an additional federal tax unless an exception applies. Exceptions can remove the additional tax without necessarily removing income tax.

**529 coordination trap.** The same expense cannot generally be used twice to obtain both a tax-free 529 distribution and an education tax credit. Coordinate payments, scholarships, credits, and account withdrawals.

**529 rollover developments.** Current law permits limited rollovers from certain long-held 529 accounts to a Roth IRA for the beneficiary if detailed conditions are satisfied, including account-age, contribution-age, annual IRA contribution, compensation, and lifetime limits. This is not a universal escape hatch for excess funding; verify all current requirements.

### 8.2 Coverdell Education Savings Accounts

A **Coverdell Education Savings Account (ESA)** is a custodial or trust account for qualified education expenses. Contributions are nondeductible, earnings grow tax deferred, and qualified distributions are generally tax free. Compared with a 529, a Coverdell generally has:

- a relatively low annual contribution limit;
- contributor income restrictions;
- broader potential K–12 qualified expenses under federal law;
- wide investment flexibility depending on custodian; and
- age-based contribution and distribution rules, with exceptions for special-needs beneficiaries.

The beneficiary generally owns the economic benefit, while a responsible individual directs the account under the governing agreement. Excess contributions and funds remaining after the applicable age can cause tax consequences unless properly distributed or rolled to an eligible family member.

| Feature | 529 education savings plan | Coverdell ESA |
|---|---|---|
| Federal contribution deduction | no | no |
| Qualified federal distributions | generally tax free | generally tax free |
| Contribution ceiling | high plan aggregate limits; gift rules matter | low annual statutory limit |
| Contributor income restriction | generally none federally | yes |
| Investment changes | limited by tax rules and plan menu | often broader, custodian-dependent |
| K–12 use | federally limited categories/amounts | broader qualifying K–12 categories |
| Control | account owner | responsible individual under custodial/trust terms |

### 8.3 UTMA and UGMA custodial accounts

The **Uniform Transfers to Minors Act (UTMA)** and **Uniform Gifts to Minors Act (UGMA)** are state-law frameworks for irrevocable transfers to a minor without a formal trust. The custodian manages property for the minor until the statutory termination age.

General distinctions:

- UGMA traditionally covers money, securities, and certain financial assets.
- UTMA generally permits a broader range, potentially including real estate, partnership interests, and other property, depending on state adoption.
- The gift is irrevocable and belongs beneficially to the minor.
- The custodian must use the property for the minor, not reclaim it for the donor.
- At the statutory age, the beneficiary gains control and may spend the assets for any purpose.
- Earnings are taxable under rules applicable to the minor, including the “kiddie tax” where relevant.
- Custodial assets can have unfavorable treatment in needs-based financial-aid formulas compared with parent-owned 529 assets.

**Example — control matters.** A parent wants to fund college but insists that the child not control unused money at age 18 or 21. A UTMA may conflict with that objective because the transfer is irrevocable and terminates under state law. A parent-owned 529 may better preserve control, though plan, tax, aid, and expense rules still require comparison.

> **Exam distinction:** A custodian controls the account temporarily but does not own it personally. The minor is the beneficial owner.

### 8.4 Health Savings Accounts

A **Health Savings Account (HSA)** is available to an eligible individual covered by a qualifying high-deductible health plan and not disqualified by other coverage, enrollment, or dependency status. The HSA is individually owned and portable; it is not “use it or lose it.”

HSAs can have a federal **triple tax advantage**:

1. eligible contributions may be pretax or deductible;
2. earnings grow tax deferred; and
3. distributions for qualified medical expenses are tax free.

Employer contributions count toward the annual limit. Contribution limits, high-deductible-plan thresholds, catch-up rules, and eligibility are indexed and must be checked for the year.

A nonqualified distribution is generally taxable and, before the statutory age or other exception, subject to an additional tax. After that age, nonmedical distributions generally avoid the additional tax but remain taxable, making the HSA resemble a traditional retirement account for those withdrawals. Qualified medical distributions can remain tax free if properly substantiated.

HSAs have no federal required minimum distributions during the owner’s life. A spouse beneficiary may generally continue the account as an HSA; a nonspouse beneficiary generally faces different, less favorable treatment at death.

**Investment workflow.** Before investing an HSA aggressively, determine whether the client will use it for current medical costs, can pay those costs from cash flow, has retained receipts, understands investment risk, and has a sufficient cash reserve. A long horizon can support growth, but an imminent deductible creates a liquidity need.

### 8.5 Purpose-account comparison

| Account | Owner/control | Intended use | If used outside purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| 529 | account owner controls for beneficiary | qualified education | earnings generally taxed and may face additional tax |
| Coverdell | responsible individual administers for beneficiary | qualified education | earnings generally taxed and may face additional tax |
| UTMA/UGMA | minor owns; custodian manages temporarily | benefit of minor, then unrestricted | custodian cannot redirect; beneficiary controls at termination |
| HSA | eligible individual owns | qualified medical expense | generally taxable; possible additional tax |

> **Memory aid — “POT”: Purpose, Ownership, Termination.** For every special account ask: What statutory **purpose** earns the tax benefit? Who owns or controls it? What happens at **termination**, distribution, or changed circumstances?

---

## 9. Ownership Forms and Estate-Planning Techniques

Ownership determines control during life, creditor exposure, tax reporting, and transfer at death. Estate planning coordinates ownership, beneficiary designations, wills, trusts, and lifetime transfers. Advisers should recognize structures and flag inconsistencies, but legal documents and conclusions belong with qualified counsel.

### 9.1 Joint tenancy with right of survivorship

**Joint tenancy with right of survivorship (JTWROS)** gives co-owners undivided interests and provides that a deceased owner’s interest generally passes automatically to the surviving joint owner or owners, outside probate. Traditional common-law formation refers to unities of time, title, interest, and possession, though state rules vary.

JTWROS can simplify transfer but creates risks:

- each owner may have present rights in the property;
- adding an owner may constitute a gift;
- a joint owner’s creditors or divorce may affect the asset;
- survivorship can defeat a will’s contrary direction;
- one owner may be able to withdraw funds;
- basis consequences depend on contributions, marital status, jurisdiction, and inclusion; and
- convenience accounts can be misregistered as beneficial joint ownership.

**Exam trap:** A will does not ordinarily redirect property that validly passes by survivorship.

### 9.2 Tenancy in common

**Tenancy in common (TIC)** gives each owner an undivided fractional interest, which need not be equal. There is no automatic right of survivorship. At death, an owner’s interest passes under a will, trust, beneficiary mechanism if available, or intestacy law. An owner generally can transfer that owner’s interest, subject to agreements and property law.

TIC is useful when co-owners want separate, inheritable shares. It can also create management, liquidity, valuation, and partition disputes.

| Feature | JTWROS | Tenancy in common |
|---|---|---|
| Equal interests | commonly required | not required |
| Survivorship | yes | no |
| Decedent’s will controls share | generally no | generally yes, subject to other arrangements |
| Probate of share | generally avoided | generally required unless another device applies |

### 9.3 Tenancy by the entirety

**Tenancy by the entirety (TBE)** is a form of ownership available in some states only to married couples and, in some jurisdictions, only for specified property. It includes survivorship and treats spouses as a legal unit for ownership purposes. One spouse often cannot unilaterally sever or transfer the interest. Creditor protection may be stronger against a creditor of only one spouse, but varies significantly by state and does not necessarily protect against joint creditors, federal claims, or fraudulent transfers.

Do not assume all states recognize TBE or that it covers brokerage accounts. Verify local law and account eligibility.

### 9.4 Payable-on-death and transfer-on-death designations

A **payable-on-death designation**, also called **pay on death (POD)** in some outline terminology, commonly applies to bank or deposit accounts. A **transfer-on-death (TOD)** registration—also written **transfer on death (TOD)**—commonly applies to securities accounts and, under some state laws, other assets. During life, the owner generally retains control and can change the beneficiary. At death, the asset passes to the named beneficiary outside probate, subject to valid designation and law.

POD/TOD designations do not:

- create current co-ownership merely by naming a beneficiary;
- replace planning for incapacity;
- automatically coordinate with tax or trust goals;
- override spousal or community-property rights in every case; or
- ensure a beneficiary can prudently manage assets.

A beneficiary who predeceases the owner, a minor beneficiary, divorce, or conflicting estate documents can create unintended outcomes. Use primary and contingent beneficiaries and review them after life events.

### 9.5 Beneficiary designations

Retirement accounts, life insurance, annuities, POD/TOD accounts, and some benefit plans pass by contract or registration. A beneficiary designation generally controls over a will. The adviser should help inventory designations, but should not make assumptions about legal effect.

Review:

- primary and contingent beneficiaries;
- percentages totaling 100%;
- per stirpes versus per capita treatment if offered;
- spouse-consent requirements;
- minor or special-needs beneficiaries;
- trusts named by exact legal title and date;
- effects of divorce or remarriage;
- charitable beneficiaries; and
- deaths of named beneficiaries.

**Per stirpes** generally directs a deceased beneficiary’s share down that beneficiary’s family branch. **Per capita** generally divides property among surviving persons at the stated generation or class, depending on drafting. Terminology varies; the governing form controls.

### 9.6 Trusts as estate-planning tools

A trust can manage assets during incapacity, control distributions, protect a vulnerable beneficiary, coordinate tax planning, and avoid probate for properly titled assets. It is not effective merely because it was signed; assets often must be retitled or beneficiary designations coordinated.

#### Revocable living trust

A revocable living trust generally allows the grantor to amend or revoke it and often serves as trustee initially. It can provide continuity at incapacity and avoid probate for funded property. Because the grantor retains control, it ordinarily does not by itself remove assets from the grantor’s taxable estate or protect them from the grantor’s creditors.

#### Irrevocable trust

An irrevocable trust generally cannot be freely revoked, though modification may be possible under its terms or law. Properly designed irrevocable trusts may pursue transfer-tax, asset-protection, insurance, charitable, or beneficiary-control objectives. The cost is relinquished control, administrative complexity, fiduciary duties, and potentially compressed trust income-tax brackets.

#### Testamentary trust

A testamentary trust arises under a will at death. It can manage inheritances but generally does not avoid probate for the property passing through the will.

#### Special-needs trust

A properly designed special-needs trust can supplement, rather than supplant, means-tested public benefits. First-party and third-party trusts have different funding, payback, and tax rules. This demands specialized legal advice.

### 9.7 Wills and intestacy

A **will** directs probate property, nominates a personal representative, may nominate guardians, and can create testamentary trusts. It does not generally control assets passing by survivorship, beneficiary designation, or a funded living trust.

**Intestacy** is the statutory distribution scheme when a person dies without a valid will for probate property. It is not the state “taking everything” in ordinary circumstances; statutes distribute to relatives in a prescribed order, with escheat only when no qualifying heir exists.

A **pour-over will** directs remaining probate assets to a trust but those assets ordinarily still pass through probate before entering the trust.

### 9.8 Qualified Domestic Relations Orders

A **Qualified Domestic Relations Order (QDRO)** is a domestic-relations order that recognizes an alternate payee’s right to receive all or part of benefits under a qualified retirement plan subject to ERISA’s QDRO rules. It is commonly used in divorce for a spouse, former spouse, child, or dependent.

A divorce decree alone may not direct a plan administrator to divide the benefit. The order must contain required information, cannot require benefits or forms not available under the plan, and must be qualified by the plan administrator. Tax and rollover treatment can depend on the alternate payee and distribution.

QDROs generally apply to qualified employer plans, not to IRAs. An IRA transfer incident to divorce uses different tax rules and must be structured carefully. Do not tell a client to withdraw and hand over retirement cash; that can create avoidable tax and penalties.

### 9.9 Donor-advised funds

A **donor-advised fund (DAF)** is a charitable giving account maintained by a sponsoring public charity. The donor makes an irrevocable charitable contribution, may qualify for a current deduction subject to law, and retains advisory privileges over grants and investments. The sponsoring charity has legal control and final authority.

Potential benefits include:

- bunching several years of gifts into one tax year;
- donating appreciated property and potentially avoiding realization of embedded gain by the donor;
- centralized recordkeeping;
- anonymous grants where available; and
- separating the timing of the deduction from later grant recommendations.

Limitations include:

- the contribution is irrevocable;
- grants generally must go to eligible charities;
- donors cannot receive more than incidental benefits;
- DAFs cannot ordinarily satisfy a donor’s enforceable personal pledge without careful analysis;
- fees and investment options apply; and
- sponsoring-organization policies govern succession and inactive accounts.

**Appreciated-stock example.** A client owns public stock worth $40,000 with $10,000 basis, held long term. Donating the shares to a DAF may allow a deduction based on fair market value subject to limits and may avoid the client’s capital-gain realization. Selling first and donating cash generally realizes the $30,000 gain. The adviser must coordinate appraisal, holding-period, deduction-limit, and charity-acceptance rules with tax professionals.

### 9.10 Estate-plan coordination map

| Asset | Likely controlling transfer mechanism | Common mistake |
|---|---|---|
| Individually owned brokerage account | will/intestacy unless TOD or trust ownership | assuming the will avoids probate |
| JTWROS account | survivorship | trying to redirect share by will |
| IRA/401(k) | beneficiary designation and plan law | stale beneficiary after divorce |
| Life insurance | beneficiary designation | naming a minor outright without planning |
| Funded revocable trust asset | trust instrument | signing trust but never retitling asset |
| DAF contribution | sponsoring charity owns after gift | treating it as still donor-owned |
| TIC interest | will, trust, or intestacy | assuming co-owner receives it automatically |

### 9.11 Adviser workflow after a life event

After marriage, divorce, birth, incapacity, business sale, inheritance, or death:

1. pause transactions if authority or ownership is uncertain;
2. identify the legal owner and authorized actor;
3. inventory titles and beneficiary designations;
4. compare them with the estate plan;
5. quantify liquidity, tax, and concentration effects;
6. coordinate with attorney, tax adviser, trustee, executor, and plan administrator;
7. implement only valid instructions; and
8. document the review.

> **Exam trap — probate avoidance is not estate-tax avoidance.** An asset can pass outside probate yet remain in the decedent’s gross estate. Probate, income-tax basis, creditor rights, and estate tax are separate analyses.

## 10. Trading Terminology, Orders, Market Roles, Costs, and Best Execution

A sound recommendation can be harmed by poor implementation. Trading decisions determine whether, where, when, and at what total cost the portfolio obtains exposure. Advisers should understand order mechanics even when a broker-dealer or custodian performs execution.

### 10.1 Bid, ask, spread, and quotations

The **bid** is the price at which a buyer is willing to buy; the **ask** or offer is the price at which a seller is willing to sell. Normally:

$$
\text{Bid}<\text{Ask}.
$$

The **bid-ask spread** is:

$$
\text{Spread}=\text{Ask}-\text{Bid}.
$$

If a stock is quoted $49.90 bid and $50.10 ask, the spread is $0.20. A customer buying immediately generally trades near the ask; a customer selling immediately generally trades near the bid. For 100 shares, the quoted full-spread difference is $20, though actual execution can improve or worsen based on market conditions and order handling.

A narrow spread generally indicates greater liquidity and lower immediate trading cost. Spreads often widen for thinly traded securities, large orders, volatile markets, or information-sensitive events.

**Market depth** describes quantities available at various prices. A displayed quote may cover only a small quantity. A large market order can “walk the book,” filling at progressively worse prices.

### 10.2 Long and short positions

A **long position** benefits if the asset rises and loses if it falls. The ordinary long stockholder’s maximum loss is generally the amount invested, absent leverage or other obligations.

A **short sale** involves selling borrowed securities, hoping to buy them later at a lower price and return them. Simplified profit is:

$$
\text{Short profit}=\text{short-sale proceeds}-\text{cover cost}-\text{expenses}.
$$

**Example.** An investor shorts 100 shares at $40 and covers at $31. Ignoring borrow fees, commissions, and dividends:

$$
100(\$40-\$31)=\$900\text{ profit}.
$$

If the stock instead rises to $58:

$$
100(\$40-\$58)=-\$1{,}800.
$$

A short seller’s potential loss is theoretically unlimited because the stock price can rise without a fixed ceiling. The short seller must pay equivalent distributions to the lender, may face borrow charges and recall, must satisfy margin requirements, and can be forced to cover. A **short squeeze** occurs when rising prices and scarce borrow prompt short sellers to buy, adding upward pressure.

“Short against the box,” writing a call, buying a put, and selling short stock are distinct strategies with different rights, obligations, taxes, and risks.

### 10.3 Cash and margin accounts

In a **cash account**, the customer pays in full for purchases by settlement under applicable rules. In a **margin account**, the broker-dealer lends part of the purchase price or permits other secured credit.

Margin involves several layers:

- Federal Reserve initial-margin requirements under Regulation T where applicable;
- self-regulatory organization maintenance requirements;
- higher broker-dealer “house” requirements;
- interest on the debit balance; and
- the firm’s contractual liquidation rights.

**Maintenance call calculation.** A client buys $80,000 of stock with $40,000 cash and a $40,000 margin loan. The stock falls to $55,000. Equity is:

$$
\$55{,}000-\$40{,}000=\$15{,}000,
$$

and equity percentage is:

$$
\frac{\$15{,}000}{\$55{,}000}=27.27\%.
$$

If the applicable house maintenance requirement is 30%, required equity is $16,500. The shortfall is $1,500. The firm may demand funds or liquidate enough securities according to its agreement and rules. It need not wait for the client to choose what to sell.

> **Exam trap:** A margin call is not a loan-extension promise. The firm can raise house requirements and may liquidate positions without advance consultation if the agreement permits.

### 10.4 Principal and agency transactions

A broker-dealer acts as **agent** when it arranges a transaction between the customer and another party. This is an **agency trade**. Compensation is commonly a commission, though pricing and disclosure vary.

A broker-dealer acts as **principal** when it buys from or sells to the customer for its own account. This is a **principal trade**. Compensation may be embedded in a **markup** on a sale to the customer or a **markdown** on a purchase from the customer. A dealer can provide liquidity by holding inventory but also has an economic interest opposite the customer in the transaction.

An investment adviser engaging in a principal transaction or agency cross transaction with an advisory client faces specific Investment Advisers Act requirements and fiduciary conflict analysis. Disclosure must be sufficiently specific for informed consent; blanket labels do not necessarily cure the conflict.

| Capacity | Broker-dealer role | Common compensation | Core conflict question |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agent | finds counterparty/execution | commission | did routing and cost serve customer? |
| Principal | trades from own inventory/account | markup or markdown | did dealer’s inventory/profit affect recommendation or price? |
| Adviser selecting broker | directs or recommends execution venue | advisory fee; possible soft-dollar or affiliate benefit | was total execution in client’s best interest? |

### 10.5 Market orders

A **market order** instructs immediate execution at the best reasonably available price. It prioritizes execution, not price. In a liquid, stable market, execution may be near the displayed quote. In a fast or thin market, the price can differ sharply from the last trade or quote.

Use a market order when prompt execution is more important than price certainty and liquidity is adequate. It can be hazardous around halts, openings, news, or illiquid securities.

### 10.6 Limit orders

A **buy limit order** sets the maximum price the investor will pay. A **sell limit order** sets the minimum price the investor will accept.

- Buy 100 XYZ limit $25: execute only at $25 or lower.
- Sell 100 XYZ limit $30: execute only at $30 or higher.

A limit order controls price but does not guarantee execution. A market may touch the limit while earlier orders exhaust available shares. Time priority and venue rules matter.

> **Memory aid:** Buy limit is a **ceiling**; sell limit is a **floor**.

### 10.7 Stop and stop-limit orders

A **stop order** becomes a market order when the stop price is reached or triggered under the venue’s rules. A sell stop is often placed below the current market to limit loss or protect profit; a buy stop is often above market to cover a short or enter on upward movement.

Once triggered, execution is likely but price is not guaranteed. A gap can produce a fill far from the stop.

A **stop-limit order** becomes a limit order when triggered. It controls the acceptable price but may not execute in a fast market.

**Example.** Stock trades at $50. A client enters a sell stop at $45. Overnight bad news causes the next available trade to be $38. The stop becomes a market order and may fill near $38, not $45. A $45 stop-limit with a $44 limit might not execute at all if the market opens below $44.

| Order | Execution priority | Price control | Principal risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market | high | low | slippage |
| Limit | lower | high | no execution |
| Stop | high after trigger | low after trigger | gap through stop |
| Stop-limit | lower after trigger | high | no execution after trigger |

### 10.8 Time-in-force and conditional terminology

Common instructions include:

- **Day order:** expires if not executed that trading day.
- **Good-til-canceled (GTC):** remains open under broker policy until executed or canceled; firms may impose expiration.
- **Immediate-or-cancel (IOC):** execute all or part immediately and cancel the remainder.
- **Fill-or-kill (FOK):** execute the full order immediately or cancel it entirely.
- **All-or-none (AON):** execute the full quantity, but not necessarily immediately.
- **At the open/close:** seeks execution at or near the specified auction, subject to rules.

A **not-held** order gives the broker time-and-price discretion and generally reduces responsibility for immediate execution under its terms, but it does not eliminate duties of fair dealing or best execution.

### 10.9 Settlement, trade date, and record date

The **trade date** is when buyer and seller agree. The **settlement date** is when cash and securities are exchanged. The standard settlement cycle for many U.S. securities changed to T+1 in May 2024; candidates should use the rule in force on the relevant date rather than an old mnemonic.

For dividends:

- **declaration date:** board declares dividend;
- **record date:** holders of record are entitled;
- **ex-dividend date:** stock trades without entitlement to the declared dividend; and
- **payment date:** dividend is paid.

Settlement-cycle changes can alter the relationship between record and ex-dividend dates. The exam should test concepts under current rules.

### 10.10 Order handling and payment for order flow

A broker may route orders to exchanges, alternative trading systems, wholesalers, market makers, or other venues. **Payment for order flow (PFOF)** is compensation or another economic benefit a broker receives for routing customer orders to a particular venue.

PFOF can support zero-commission trading and may coexist with price improvement. It also creates a conflict because the venue paying the broker may not always provide the best customer outcome. “Commission-free” does not mean cost-free; investors can bear spreads, price impact, missed execution, market-data effects, and other charges.

The relevant question is execution quality, including:

- price improvement or disimprovement;
- likelihood and speed of execution;
- fill rate and size;
- opportunity for better prices;
- market impact;
- order type and security characteristics;
- venue reliability; and
- explicit and implicit costs.

Disclosure of PFOF does not by itself prove best execution. Firms must perform the reviews required by applicable law and FINRA rules.

### 10.11 Best execution

An investment adviser responsible for selecting broker-dealers has a fiduciary duty to seek **best execution** of client transactions. Best execution does not invariably mean the lowest commission or best displayed price. It seeks the most favorable total result under the circumstances.

Relevant factors include:

- commission or spread;
- execution price and improvement;
- order size and market impact;
- speed, likelihood, and quality of execution;
- broker financial responsibility;
- research and execution services;
- responsiveness and custody services;
- confidentiality and information leakage;
- ability to handle difficult trades; and
- conflicts, referrals, soft dollars, or affiliated routing.

The adviser should periodically and systematically evaluate execution, compare alternatives, aggregate trades fairly, allocate partial fills under a fair policy, disclose material conflicts, and maintain records.

**Soft dollars.** An adviser may receive eligible research or brokerage services from a broker using client commissions within the statutory safe harbor if conditions are met. The adviser must make a good-faith determination that commissions are reasonable in relation to eligible services and must address fiduciary conflicts. Office rent, travel, entertainment, and ordinary overhead are not transformed into eligible research merely by being useful.

### 10.12 Trading costs and implementation shortfall

Explicit costs include commissions, ticket charges, exchange fees, taxes, borrow fees, and margin interest. Implicit costs include bid-ask spread, market impact, delay, and opportunity cost from unexecuted orders.

A useful framework is **implementation shortfall**: the difference between the return of a theoretical “paper” portfolio at a decision price and the return actually achieved after execution and costs. The exact formula depends on methodology, but the concept captures more than commission.

**Example.** An adviser decides to buy 1,000 shares at a $20.00 decision price. The order fills at an average $20.12 and incurs a $10 explicit fee. Simplified shortfall versus decision value is:

$$
1{,}000(\$20.12-\$20.00)+\$10=\$130.
$$

That is 0.65% of the $20,000 decision value. A nominally zero commission would still leave $120 of price slippage.

### 10.13 Trade allocation and aggregation

Aggregating client orders can improve execution and reduce costs. Advisers need a fair allocation policy, especially for partial fills or scarce offerings. Favoring proprietary accounts, employees, or high-fee clients is inconsistent with fiduciary obligations.

A defensible policy may use pro rata allocation, random allocation, rotational access, or another neutral method appropriate to the facts. Deviations should be justified and documented. The goal is fairness over time, not necessarily identical results in every trade.

### 10.14 Trading workflow example

An adviser must sell 50,000 shares of a thinly traded small-cap stock held across 30 client accounts after a strategy change.

A prudent process may include:

1. confirm that the recommendation and tax effects apply to each client;
2. check restrictions, liquidity, average volume, spreads, and news;
3. determine whether aggregation serves clients;
4. select order type and execution broker based on total execution quality;
5. avoid revealing the entire order if doing so would increase impact;
6. follow the pre-established allocation policy;
7. compare fills with relevant benchmarks;
8. disclose or manage conflicts; and
9. document exceptions.

Simply entering a 50,000-share market order because it is “fast” could impose severe and avoidable market impact.

---

## 11. Measuring Investment Performance

Performance measurement asks more than “Did the account go up?” A useful result specifies the period, cash flows, fees, taxes, benchmark, risk, annualization, and methodology. Different measures answer different questions.

### 11.1 Holding-period return and total return

**Holding-period return (HPR)** includes income and price change over the holding period:

$$
HPR=\frac{P_1-P_0+D}{P_0},
$$

where $P_0$ is beginning value, $P_1$ is ending value, and $D$ is distributions received.

**Example.** Stock is purchased for $50, pays a $2 dividend, and is sold for $56:

$$
HPR=\frac{\$56-\$50+\$2}{\$50}=16\%.
$$

Price return alone is 12%; total holding-period return is 16% because it includes income.

For a bond or fund, **total return** likewise includes income plus realized and unrealized price changes, adjusted for cash flows under the chosen method. Current yield is not total return.

### 11.2 Arithmetic and geometric average returns

The **arithmetic mean** is:

$$
\bar R_A=\frac{R_1+R_2+\cdots+R_n}{n}.
$$

The **geometric mean** or compound annual growth rate for equal annual periods is:

$$
\bar R_G=\left[\prod_{t=1}^{n}(1+R_t)\right]^{1/n}-1.
$$

**Example.** A portfolio gains 50% then loses 50%.

Arithmetic mean:

$$
\frac{50\%+(-50\%)}{2}=0\%.
$$

But $100 becomes $150, then $75. Geometric annual return is:

$$
(1.5\times0.5)^{1/2}-1=\sqrt{0.75}-1=-13.40\%.
$$

The arithmetic mean is often used as an estimate for a single future period; the geometric mean describes compounded historical growth. Volatility makes geometric return less than arithmetic return unless periodic returns are equal.

### 11.3 Annualized return

Annualization converts a multi-period result into an equivalent yearly compounded rate:

$$
R_{annualized}=(1+R_{period})^{1/n}-1,
$$

where $n$ is the number of years in the period.

If an investment grows 21% over two years:

$$
(1.21)^{1/2}-1=10\%.
$$

Do not multiply a multi-month return mechanically when compounding is required. A 2% monthly return annualizes to:

$$
(1.02)^{12}-1=26.82\%,
$$

not 24%, assuming reinvestment at the same monthly rate. Annualizing a very short or unusual period can mislead and is governed by performance-presentation rules.

### 11.4 Time-weighted return

**Time-weighted return (TWR)** removes the effect of external cash-flow timing by dividing the measurement period at each external flow, calculating subperiod returns, and geometrically linking them:

$$
TWR=\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+r_j)-1.
$$

TWR is generally appropriate for evaluating a manager who does not control client deposits and withdrawals.

**Example.** An account begins with $100,000 and grows to $110,000. The client then contributes $90,000, making $200,000. By period end it falls to $180,000.

Subperiod returns are:

$$
r_1=\frac{110{,}000-100{,}000}{100{,}000}=10\%,
$$

$$
r_2=\frac{180{,}000-200{,}000}{200{,}000}=-10\%.
$$

Linked TWR:

$$
(1.10)(0.90)-1=-1\%.
$$

The portfolio had a -1% time-weighted return even though ending wealth exceeds the initial $100,000 because the client added $90,000.

### 11.5 Dollar-weighted return and internal rate of return

**Dollar-weighted return (DWR)** reflects the size and timing of external cash flows. It is the **internal rate of return (IRR)** that sets net present value of cash flows to zero:

$$
0=\sum_{t=0}^{n}\frac{CF_t}{(1+r)^t}.
$$

Signs must be from one consistent perspective. A client contribution is commonly treated as an outflow from the client; withdrawals and ending value are inflows.

DWR measures the investor’s actual experience and is affected by decisions to add or withdraw money. In the TWR example, much more money was exposed during the -10% subperiod than the +10% subperiod, so DWR is worse than TWR.

**Simple one-year IRR.** A client invests $10,000 at the start and receives $11,200 at year end:

$$
-10{,}000+\frac{11{,}200}{1+r}=0,
$$

so $r=12\%$.

With multiple irregular cash-flow dates, software generally solves IRR iteratively; an XIRR-style method uses actual dates. Certain cash-flow patterns can produce multiple IRRs or no meaningful solution, so methodology should be disclosed.

> **Exam distinction:** TWR evaluates the portfolio manager by neutralizing client cash-flow timing. DWR/IRR measures the investor’s realized experience and gives more weight to periods when more dollars are invested.

### 11.6 Expected return

A portfolio’s expected return is the weighted average of asset expected returns:

$$
E(R_p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_iE(R_i).
$$

A portfolio has 60% in equities expected to return 8% and 40% in bonds expected to return 4%:

$$
E(R_p)=0.60(8\%)+0.40(4\%)=6.4\%.
$$

Correlation affects portfolio risk, not this weighted-average expected-return calculation. Expected return is forward-looking and uncertain; realized return will differ.

### 11.7 Nominal, real, after-tax, and after-tax real return

A **nominal return** is not adjusted for inflation. The exact **real return** is:

$$
1+R_{real}=\frac{1+R_{nominal}}{1+\pi},
$$

or

$$
R_{real}=\frac{1+R_{nominal}}{1+\pi}-1,
$$

where $\pi$ is inflation.

A 7% nominal return with 3% inflation gives:

$$
\frac{1.07}{1.03}-1=3.8835\%.
$$

The shortcut $7\%-3\%=4\%$ is an approximation.

For an ordinary taxable return taxed at rate $t$, a simple **after-tax return** is:

$$
R_{after-tax}=R(1-t).
$$

If a 6% return is fully taxed currently at 25%, after-tax return is 4.5%. If inflation is 2%, exact after-tax real return is:

$$
\frac{1.045}{1.02}-1=2.451\%.
$$

Real portfolios may contain deferred gains, qualified dividends, losses, return of capital, and multiple tax rates, so the simple formula may not apply to the whole return.

### 11.8 Current yield and bond yield distinctions

For a bond:

$$
\text{Current yield}=\frac{\text{annual coupon interest}}{\text{current market price}}.
$$

A $1,000 par bond with a 6% coupon pays $60 annually. If market price is $900:

$$
\text{Current yield}=\frac{\$60}{\$900}=6.67\%.
$$

Current yield ignores capital gain or loss at maturity, reinvestment, and time value. **Yield to maturity (YTM)** is the discount rate equating the present value of promised cash flows to price, assuming payments and reinvestment conditions implicit in the calculation. **Yield to call (YTC)** assumes redemption at a call date and price. For a callable premium bond, yield to call can be more relevant than YTM.

For stock:

$$
\text{Dividend yield}=\frac{\text{annual dividends per share}}{\text{market price per share}}.
$$

A high current yield can result from a falling price and may signal risk rather than value.

### 11.9 Risk-adjusted performance measures

#### Sharpe ratio

The **Sharpe ratio** measures excess return per unit of total risk:

$$
\text{Sharpe}=\frac{R_p-R_f}{\sigma_p}.
$$

If a portfolio returns 9%, the risk-free rate is 3%, and standard deviation is 12%:

$$
\text{Sharpe}=\frac{9\%-3\%}{12\%}=0.50.
$$

Sharpe is useful when evaluating an entire portfolio because it uses total risk. Higher is better, all else equal. It can be unstable with non-normal returns, illiquid pricing, or negative excess returns.

#### Treynor ratio

The **Treynor ratio** measures excess return per unit of systematic risk:

$$
\text{Treynor}=\frac{R_p-R_f}{\beta_p}.
$$

If the same portfolio has beta 0.8:

$$
\text{Treynor}=\frac{9\%-3\%}{0.8}=7.5\%.
$$

Treynor is most meaningful for a well-diversified portfolio where nonsystematic risk is largely removed.

#### Jensen’s alpha

**Jensen’s alpha** compares realized return with CAPM-required return:

$$
\alpha_p=R_p-[R_f+\beta_p(R_m-R_f)].
$$

Suppose $R_p=11\%$, $R_f=3\%$, $R_m=9\%$, and $\beta_p=1.1$:

$$
\alpha=11\%-[3\%+1.1(6\%)]=1.4\%.
$$

#### Sortino ratio

The **Sortino ratio** divides excess return over a target by downside deviation rather than total standard deviation:

$$
\text{Sortino}=\frac{R_p-R_{target}}{\text{downside deviation}}.
$$

It does not penalize upside volatility in the same way as Sharpe. Results depend strongly on the target and downside methodology.

#### Information ratio

The **information ratio** measures active return per unit of tracking error:

$$
\text{Information ratio}=\frac{R_p-R_b}{\sigma(R_p-R_b)}.
$$

If a manager outperforms a benchmark by 2% with tracking error of 4%:

$$
IR=\frac{2\%}{4\%}=0.50.
$$

This evaluates consistency of active return relative to a benchmark, not total portfolio risk.

### 11.10 Other risk and diagnostic measures

- **Tracking error:** standard deviation of active returns relative to a benchmark.
- **R-squared:** proportion of return variation statistically associated with benchmark variation; a low value can make beta and alpha relative to that benchmark less meaningful.
- **Maximum drawdown:** largest peak-to-trough decline over a period.
- **Upside/downside capture:** performance in benchmark up or down periods.
- **Value at Risk (VaR):** modeled loss threshold at a confidence level and horizon; it does not describe the size of loss beyond the threshold.
- **Standard deviation:** total variability, not just harmful variability.

**VaR wording example.** A one-day 95% VaR of $100,000 means the model estimates that losses should exceed $100,000 on about 5% of days under its assumptions. It does **not** mean the maximum possible one-day loss is $100,000.

### 11.11 Benchmarks

A benchmark should be:

- relevant to the strategy and client objective;
- investable or at least representable;
- specified in advance;
- measurable and transparent;
- consistent through the comparison period; and
- appropriately risk- and currency-matched.

Common benchmark errors include comparing a balanced portfolio only with a stock index, comparing tax-free bonds with taxable bonds without adjustment, changing the benchmark after underperformance, ignoring style drift, and comparing net client returns with gross index returns.

A custom blended benchmark can mirror strategic weights. For a 60/40 portfolio, a simplified benchmark return is:

$$
R_b=0.60R_{equity\ index}+0.40R_{bond\ index}.
$$

If the equity index returns 10% and bond index 2%, the static blended benchmark returns 6.8%. Rebalancing convention and cash flows should be specified.

### 11.12 Gross, net, pre-tax, and after-tax presentation

**Gross-of-fee return** is before specified investment-management fees; **net-of-fee return** subtracts relevant fees. Both may still treat trading costs or underlying fund expenses differently depending on methodology. A clear presentation defines all deductions.

Pre-tax performance may be appropriate for tax-exempt accounts or manager comparison, but a taxable client experiences after-tax wealth. After-tax measurement requires assumptions or actual tax lots and can be client-specific.

The adviser should disclose:

- whether returns are gross or net;
- which fees and expenses are included;
- whether income is reinvested;
- valuation and cash-flow conventions;
- benchmark and currency;
- tax assumptions;
- use of model, back-tested, hypothetical, or actual performance; and
- material limitations.

### 11.13 Performance attribution

Attribution asks **why** performance differed from a benchmark. A common framework separates:

- **allocation effect:** overweighting or underweighting segments that performed differently;
- **selection effect:** choosing securities that out- or underperformed within segments; and
- **interaction effect:** combined allocation and selection impact.

Fixed-income attribution may examine duration, yield-curve positioning, sector allocation, credit quality, and security selection. Attribution is diagnostic, not proof of skill.

### 11.14 Performance workflow example

A client says, “My account earned only 5%, but the S&P 500 earned 12%, so the adviser failed.” The portfolio is 50% global stocks, 40% bonds, and 10% cash, designed to fund distributions with moderate risk.

A proper review should:

1. confirm the exact dates and cash flows;
2. calculate TWR for manager evaluation and DWR for client experience;
3. use a pre-specified blended benchmark rather than an all-stock index;
4. compare gross and net results consistently;
5. examine volatility, drawdown, and risk-adjusted return;
6. assess whether the allocation met the client’s objective and constraints;
7. explain attribution and costs; and
8. revisit the plan if goals or tolerance changed.

Underperforming an all-stock index can be expected for a moderate portfolio in a strong equity year. That alone does not establish poor advice. Conversely, beating the index through undisclosed leverage would not prove prudent management.

### 11.15 Performance-measure selection table

| Question | Most relevant measure |
|---|---|
| How did one investment perform including income? | holding-period/total return |
| What was compounded annual growth? | geometric/annualized return |
| How did the manager perform independent of client flows? | time-weighted return |
| What return did the client’s dollars experience? | dollar-weighted return/IRR |
| What was return after inflation? | real return |
| What income does a bond produce relative to current price? | current yield |
| What excess return was earned per unit of total risk? | Sharpe ratio |
| What excess return was earned per unit of beta? | Treynor ratio |
| What return exceeded CAPM expectation? | Jensen’s alpha |
| How consistent was active return versus benchmark? | information ratio |

> **Memory aid — “S-T-A-I-R.”** **S**harpe uses **s**tandard deviation; **T**reynor uses be**t**a; **A**lpha compares with CAPM; **I**nformation ratio uses active return and tracking error; **R**eal return removes inflation.

## Integrated Recommendation Workflow and Exam Distinctions

Domain 3 questions often combine several objectives. The following framework helps separate facts that may look similar.

### The GATHER–PLAN–ACT–REVIEW cycle

**GATHER**

- identify client, owner, authority, and governing documents;
- inventory assets, liabilities, cash flows, taxes, benefits, and existing exposures;
- define goals, priority, amounts, and dates;
- assess risk tolerance, risk capacity, knowledge, values, and behavior; and
- verify material information.

**PLAN**

- distinguish near-term liabilities from long-term capital;
- select strategic allocation and liquidity reserve;
- compare account types, ownership, tax location, costs, and alternatives;
- choose benchmarks and monitoring rules; and
- identify matters requiring legal or tax professionals.

**ACT**

- obtain informed client approval;
- use suitable order types and seek best execution;
- manage allocation, conflicts, taxes, and implementation costs;
- update beneficiaries or registrations through valid procedures; and
- document reasons and assumptions.

**REVIEW**

- measure TWR, DWR, risk, benchmark-relative results, fees, and taxes;
- rebalance under policy;
- monitor products, service providers, plans, and conflicts;
- update after life events or material market and legal changes; and
- revise the IPS or plan when facts—not recent performance alone—warrant it.

### High-frequency exam distinctions

| Do not confuse | Correct distinction |
|---|---|
| Risk tolerance and capacity | willingness versus financial ability to bear loss |
| Cash flow and balance sheet | activity over a period versus position at a point in time |
| Suitable and fiduciary-best-interest | basic fit versus broader duty including costs, conflicts, alternatives, and relationship |
| Strategic and tactical allocation | long-term target versus temporary deviation |
| Diversification and number of holdings | different economic risks versus merely many labels |
| DCA and guaranteed profit | regular fixed-dollar purchases versus no assurance of gain or loss protection |
| Call buyer and writer | right to buy versus obligation to sell if exercised |
| Put buyer and writer | right to sell versus obligation to buy if exercised |
| Current yield and total return | current income divided by price versus income plus price change |
| TWR and DWR | manager result neutralizing flows versus investor result weighted by flows |
| Sharpe and Treynor | total risk versus beta |
| CML and SML | efficient portfolios/standard deviation versus securities or portfolios/beta |
| Weak and semi-strong EMH | past market data versus all public information |
| Traditional IRA contribution and deduction | permission to contribute versus ability to deduct |
| Qualified and nonqualified plan | tax-code status, not lawful versus unlawful |
| 529 owner and beneficiary | controller versus person whose education is funded |
| UTMA custodian and owner | fiduciary manager versus minor beneficial owner |
| Joint tenancy and tenancy in common | survivorship versus inheritable fractional share |
| Will and beneficiary designation | probate instruction versus contractual/nonprobate transfer |
| Probate avoidance and estate-tax exclusion | transfer procedure versus tax inclusion |
| Market and limit order | execution priority versus price control |
| Stop and stop-limit | market order after trigger versus limit order after trigger |
| Principal and agent | dealer’s own account versus arranging for another party |
| Lowest commission and best execution | one explicit cost versus best total result |
| Expected and realized return | probability/model estimate versus actual outcome |

### Ten traps resolved in one sentence each

1. **Young does not automatically mean aggressive:** the goal may be near-term, employment unstable, or tolerance low.
2. **A high yield is not free income:** price, credit, call, leverage, and distribution sustainability matter.
3. **Tax deferral is not always tax reduction:** future rates, required distributions, and loss of capital-gain character matter.
4. **A trust is not one tax category:** grantor status, distributions, governing terms, and beneficiary interests control.
5. **An index fund is not risk-free:** it bears the index’s market and concentration risks.
6. **A stop price is not a guaranteed fill price:** after triggering, a stop becomes a market order.
7. **A 404(c) plan is not fiduciary-free:** menu selection, monitoring, costs, and disclosures remain fiduciary functions.
8. **A DAF is not the donor’s private account:** the sponsoring charity has legal control after the irrevocable gift.
9. **A margin account can be liquidated without the client choosing the securities:** contractual and regulatory rights protect the lender.
10. **Outperformance is not enough:** compare risk, benchmark, taxes, fees, leverage, cash flows, and mandate.

### Comprehensive adviser case

**Client.** Redwood Design LLC is taxed as an S corporation. Its two members, spouses ages 51 and 49, employ eight people. The company has $900,000 in cash, of which $400,000 is needed for payroll, taxes, and a studio purchase within 12 months. Revenue is cyclical and tied to commercial real estate. The spouses have $1.4 million of retirement assets, $500,000 of taxable assets heavily concentrated in a real-estate investment trust, and college goals for two children. They describe themselves as aggressive after a strong market year. They want environmentally screened investments, recently inherited low-basis stock, and have outdated wills and retirement beneficiaries.

**Profile conclusions.**

- The LLC’s S election means pass-through tax and possible tax-distribution needs; the operating agreement determines authority.
- The $400,000 liability pool has a short horizon and low risk capacity.
- Business income, the REIT, and the planned studio create correlated real-estate exposure.
- Recent bullishness may reflect recency bias and does not establish tolerance.
- Education, retirement, business, and estate assets have distinct goals and horizons.
- An environmental screen needs a client-defined policy, not a generic ESG label.
- Inherited stock generally raises basis-at-death questions; records must be verified before sale.
- Eight employees make an owner-only Solo 401(k) generally inappropriate for the company if employees are eligible.
- Wills do not fix stale retirement beneficiary designations.

**Plan architecture.**

1. Keep operating and acquisition funds in a liability-matched liquidity tier.
2. Develop a diversified strategic policy for long-term business reserves.
3. Analyze household exposure across business, real estate, REIT, and financial accounts.
4. Diversify through cash flows and tax-aware sales rather than ignoring concentration because of gain.
5. Compare 529 plans, Coverdell accounts, and taxable savings based on control, expenses, state benefits, and qualified uses.
6. Review the employer retirement plan under qualified-plan and ERISA rules, including eligible employees and fiduciary process.
7. Define environmental exclusions and disclose tracking and concentration effects.
8. Send estate documents, basis issues, and entity-tax questions to qualified counsel and tax professionals.
9. Update valid beneficiary designations after legal review.
10. Establish TWR, DWR, blended benchmark, drawdown, fee, and review reporting.

**Implementation.** The adviser should seek best execution on concentrated-stock sales, aggregate and allocate fairly where appropriate, avoid market orders that would unduly move thin securities, and document tax-lot choices. DCA could help behaviorally when investing future cash, but it does not guarantee a better outcome. Options might temporarily hedge a concentrated position, but premium, liquidity, tax, and complexity must be compared with direct diversification.

**Monitoring.** Review business cash monthly or quarterly, household plan at least annually, and all elements after revenue shocks, property purchase, employee changes, education enrollment, death, disability, or tax-law change.

---

## 25 Original Multiple-Choice Questions

These questions are original applications of the concepts in this part. They are not actual NASAA questions and are not intended to imitate protected exam wording.

### Question 1

A client owns a cyclical oilfield-services sole proprietorship, receives nearly all income from it, and proposes investing her retirement account entirely in energy producers. Which concern should an adviser address first?

A. Retirement accounts may never own energy stocks.
B. Her human capital and financial capital would be exposed to related risks.
C. A sole proprietor is legally separate from the business.
D. Energy stocks always have negative beta.

### Question 2

A client says he can emotionally tolerate a 40% decline. He must use the invested funds for a noncancelable home closing in six months. Which statement is most accurate?

A. High tolerance makes an equity portfolio appropriate.
B. His risk capacity for these funds is low despite high stated tolerance.
C. Time horizon is irrelevant when tolerance is high.
D. Dollar-cost averaging guarantees enough money at closing.

### Question 3

An LLC asks an adviser to open an investment account. Which document is most directly useful for determining who may authorize transactions?

A. A member’s personal will
B. The LLC operating agreement and authorization resolutions
C. A beneficiary’s tax return
D. The adviser’s Form ADV only

### Question 4

A two-asset portfolio has equal weights. Each asset has a 10% standard deviation and their correlation is zero. Approximately what is portfolio standard deviation?

A. 0%
B. 5%
C. 7.07%
D. 10%

### Question 5

The risk-free rate is 2%, expected market return is 8%, and a stock’s beta is 1.5. Under CAPM, the stock’s expected return is:

A. 8%
B. 9%
C. 11%
D. 14%

### Question 6

Which statement best describes semi-strong-form market efficiency?

A. Prices reflect only historical price and volume data.
B. Prices reflect all publicly available information.
C. Prices reflect no fundamental information.
D. Investors may legally profit from material nonpublic information.

### Question 7

An investor contributes $300 each month to a stock fund regardless of its price. Which statement is correct?

A. The method guarantees a profit if continued for at least five years.
B. It guarantees a lower cost than immediate lump-sum investment.
C. It purchases more shares at lower prices and fewer at higher prices but cannot assure profit.
D. It eliminates systematic risk.

### Question 8

A client owns 100 shares at $60 and buys one put with a $55 strike for a $2 premium. The principal purpose is to:

A. create unlimited upside with no cost.
B. establish downside protection for a premium.
C. obligate the client to buy more shares at $55.
D. eliminate all loss below $60.

### Question 9

An index rises 10% one day and falls 9.09% the next, ending approximately where it started. Why can a daily inverse fund nevertheless lose money over the two days?

A. Inverse funds are prohibited from using derivatives.
B. Daily reset and compounding create path-dependent returns.
C. Inverse funds always track monthly returns.
D. The benchmark’s beta becomes zero.

### Question 10

A municipal bond yields 3.6%. For a client with a relevant 28% marginal rate, what is the approximate federal tax-equivalent yield, assuming comparable risk and no other tax effects?

A. 2.59%
B. 3.60%
C. 4.61%
D. 5.00%

### Question 11

A client buys stock for $8,000, reinvests $600 of taxable dividends, and later sells all shares for $11,000. Ignoring other adjustments, the gain is:

A. $2,400
B. $3,000
C. $3,600
D. $8,600

### Question 12

Which statement about a traditional IRA is correct?

A. Every eligible contribution is fully deductible.
B. Contribution eligibility and deduction eligibility are separate questions.
C. Qualified distributions are always tax free.
D. Original owners are never subject to required minimum distributions.

### Question 13

A self-employed consultant has no employees other than a spouse. Which feature can distinguish a Solo 401(k) from an IRA?

A. It may permit contributions in both employee and employer capacities.
B. It never requires a plan document.
C. It permits unlimited annual contributions.
D. It remains owner-only after eligible common-law employees are hired.

### Question 14

Which statement about ERISA Section 404(c) is most accurate?

A. It relieves plan fiduciaries of every duty once participants choose investments.
B. It can provide limited relief for losses resulting from participant control if regulatory conditions are satisfied, but menu selection and monitoring remain fiduciary functions.
C. It applies automatically to every IRA.
D. It permits a fiduciary to receive undisclosed compensation from any fund on the menu.

### Question 15

A parent contributes to a parent-owned 529 education savings account for a child. Which statement is generally correct?

A. The child immediately controls all investment and distribution decisions.
B. The contribution is federally deductible without limit.
C. The account owner generally controls the account, and qualified distributions may be federally tax free.
D. Any nonqualified distribution is entirely tax free because contributions were after tax.

### Question 16

Which feature most clearly distinguishes a UTMA custodial account from a parent-owned 529 plan?

A. UTMA property remains revocable by the donor.
B. The minor beneficially owns the UTMA property and gains control at the state-law termination age.
C. UTMA earnings are always federally tax free.
D. UTMA funds may be redirected to any sibling at the custodian’s discretion.

### Question 17

Which statement about a Health Savings Account is correct under general federal principles?

A. The balance is forfeited at the end of every year.
B. Qualified medical distributions may be tax free, and the account is portable.
C. Enrollment in any health plan automatically establishes eligibility.
D. Nonmedical distributions are always tax free after the owner leaves employment.

### Question 18

Two unrelated investors want unequal ownership percentages and want each share to pass under the owner’s estate plan rather than automatically to the co-owner. Which registration is generally the closest fit?

A. JTWROS
B. Tenancy by the entirety
C. Tenancy in common
D. POD with each other as beneficiaries

### Question 19

A divorcing participant wants part of an ERISA-covered qualified plan paid to a former spouse. Which mechanism is specifically designed for this purpose?

A. A 529 rollover
B. A qualified domestic relations order
C. A wash-sale election
D. A transfer-on-death registration

### Question 20

A stock closes at $50. A client enters a sell stop at $45. Overnight news causes the next available trade to occur at $38. What is the most likely result after the stop is triggered?

A. The order must execute at exactly $45.
B. The order becomes a market order and may execute near $38.
C. The order becomes a $45 limit order and cannot execute below $45.
D. The order is canceled automatically because the market gapped.

### Question 21

A broker-dealer sells a bond from its own inventory to a customer. In that transaction, the broker-dealer acts primarily as:

A. an agent compensated only by a disclosed commission.
B. a principal that may earn a markup.
C. a trustee for the issuer.
D. a beneficiary under the customer’s account.

### Question 22

An adviser selects a broker that charges a slightly higher commission but consistently provides materially better prices and fills for the adviser’s large, difficult trades. Which statement is most accurate?

A. Best execution is necessarily violated because another broker charges less.
B. Best execution considers the total result, not commission alone.
C. Payment for order flow eliminates the duty to review execution.
D. The adviser need not disclose material routing conflicts.

### Question 23

An account earns 10% before a large client contribution and loses 10% after the contribution. Which method is generally preferred to evaluate the manager independent of the client’s decision about when to contribute?

A. Current yield
B. Dollar-weighted return
C. Time-weighted return
D. Tax-equivalent yield

### Question 24

Portfolio A returns 10%, the risk-free rate is 2%, and Portfolio A’s standard deviation is 16%. What is its Sharpe ratio?

A. 0.08
B. 0.50
C. 0.625
D. 8.00

### Question 25

A bond with $1,000 par value pays a 5% annual coupon and trades at $800. Its current yield is:

A. 5.00%
B. 6.25%
C. 8.00%
D. 10.00%

---

## Reasoned Answer Key

### 1. **B — Her human and financial capital would share related risks.**

The client’s income, business equity, and proposed retirement holdings could all suffer from the same energy-sector downturn. That is concentration viewed at the household level. **A** is false because retirement accounts are not categorically barred from energy stocks. **C** reverses the legal point: a sole proprietorship generally is not separate from its owner. **D** is false; energy equities do not inherently have negative beta. The adviser should quantify the exposure, preserve business liquidity, and diversify avoidable sector risk rather than equating familiarity with safety.

### 2. **B — Risk capacity is low despite stated tolerance.**

Risk tolerance measures willingness; risk capacity measures ability to bear loss without impairing a goal. A noncancelable six-month obligation gives these dollars a short horizon and little capacity for market loss. **A** and **C** allow psychological confidence to override a binding financial constraint. **D** is false because dollar-cost averaging does not guarantee principal, return, or the needed closing amount. A liability-matched, liquid allocation is generally more defensible, while separately identified long-term assets may have a different allocation.

### 3. **B — Review the operating agreement and authorization resolutions.**

An LLC’s governing and authorization documents show whether it is member- or manager-managed and who may bind the entity or direct investments. A member’s will may matter after death but does not ordinarily establish current LLC trading authority, so **A** is wrong. A beneficiary’s tax return is irrelevant to entity authority, making **C** wrong. Form ADV discloses information about the adviser, not the client entity’s authorized persons, so **D** is wrong. Tax classification should also be verified, but it is a separate question from authority.

### 4. **C — Approximately 7.07%.**

For equal weights, equal 10% standard deviations, and zero correlation:

$$
\sigma_p=\sqrt{(0.5)^2(0.10)^2+(0.5)^2(0.10)^2}=0.0707.
$$

Zero correlation creates diversification but not a riskless portfolio, so **A** is wrong. **B** omits the square-root relationship. **D** would ignore the diversification benefit. Perfect negative correlation with suitable weights could theoretically produce lower or even zero volatility, but that is not the fact pattern.

### 5. **C — 11%.**

CAPM gives:

$$
E(R)=R_f+\beta(E(R_m)-R_f)=2\%+1.5(8\%-2\%)=11\%.
$$

**A** merely repeats the market return. **B** does not apply beta to the market risk premium correctly. **D** incorrectly multiplies beta by the full market return and then adds the risk-free rate. CAPM compensates beta exposure to the **market risk premium**, not to the entire market return. The result is a model estimate, not a guaranteed outcome.

### 6. **B — Prices reflect all publicly available information.**

Semi-strong EMH includes historical market data and all other public information, including financial statements and public news. **A** describes weak-form efficiency. **C** contradicts the theory. **D** is both conceptually wrong for strong efficiency and legally dangerous: trading on material nonpublic information can violate securities law. Semi-strong efficiency suggests that fundamental and technical analysis should not consistently produce abnormal risk-adjusted returns after costs using public information, but it does not claim that prices never err.

### 7. **C — DCA changes share quantity but assures no profit.**

A fixed dollar amount buys more shares when price is lower and fewer when it is higher. That can enforce savings discipline and reduce single-entry timing risk. It does not eliminate market risk or guarantee gain, so **A** and **D** are false. It also does not guarantee a better average cost than investing a lump sum immediately; if prices rise steadily, the lump sum benefits from earlier exposure, making **B** false. The client must be able and willing to continue purchases during declines.

### 8. **B — The put buys downside protection for a premium.**

The long put gives the stockholder the right to sell at $55 during the option’s exercise terms, creating a floor near $53 after accounting for the $2 premium, before costs and tax effects. **A** ignores the premium and misstates the payoff. **C** describes an obligation associated with writing a put, not buying one. **D** is false because the investor can lose from $60 down to the protected floor and pays the premium. The hedge is temporary and depends on contract terms and counterparty clearing arrangements.

### 9. **B — Daily reset makes the return path dependent.**

An inverse fund generally targets the inverse of a benchmark for **each day**, then resets. Percentage gains and losses apply to changing bases, so multi-day compounding can create a loss even when the index returns to its starting level. **A** is false because derivatives are commonly used. **C** uses the wrong measurement period. **D** is unrelated; beta is not what causes the arithmetic divergence. Higher volatility and longer holding periods can magnify the difference between stated daily objective and cumulative inverse performance.

### 10. **D — 5.00%.**

Tax-equivalent yield is:

$$
\frac{3.6\%}{1-0.28}=\frac{3.6\%}{0.72}=5.00\%.
$$

**A** multiplies rather than divides by the after-tax fraction. **B** is simply the tax-exempt yield. **C** results from an incorrect denominator. The comparison is only a first screen: credit, maturity, call risk, liquidity, state tax, alternative minimum tax exposure, and transaction costs must also be comparable. A higher tax-equivalent yield does not make the municipal bond suitable by itself.

### 11. **A — $2,400.**

Reinvested taxable dividends generally purchase additional shares and add $600 to basis. Adjusted basis is therefore $8,600, and gain is:

$$
\$11{,}000-\$8{,}600=\$2{,}400.
$$

**B** ignores the reinvested-dividend basis and would tax the same economic amount twice. **C** adds rather than subtracts the adjustment. **D** is the adjusted basis, not gain. Accurate reinvestment records are essential, particularly when older lots transfer between custodians.

### 12. **B — Contribution and deduction eligibility are separate.**

A person with eligible compensation may be able to contribute to a traditional IRA even when workplace-plan coverage and income limit the deduction; the contribution can be nondeductible and creates basis that must be tracked. **A** confuses contribution with deduction. **C** describes qualified Roth treatment, not ordinary traditional IRA distributions. **D** is generally false because original traditional IRA owners are subject to required minimum distribution rules under the applicable starting-age law.

### 13. **A — The owner may contribute in employee and employer capacities.**

A one-participant 401(k) can accept elective deferrals by the owner as employee and employer contributions based on eligible compensation, within annual and aggregate limits. **B** is false because a plan document and administration are required. **C** is false because statutory limits apply. **D** is false: hiring eligible common-law employees can trigger coverage and nondiscrimination responsibilities. The plan is not a device for excluding eligible workers simply by retaining the “Solo” label.

### 14. **B — Section 404(c) offers conditional, limited relief.**

When regulatory conditions are satisfied, Section 404(c) can relieve fiduciaries from liability for losses that are the direct and necessary result of a participant’s exercise of control. It does not erase the duty to prudently select and monitor the investment menu, control reasonable expenses, provide required information, or operate the plan properly. That makes **A** too broad. **C** is false because an ordinary IRA is not automatically an ERISA participant-directed plan. **D** would raise fiduciary loyalty, disclosure, compensation, and prohibited-transaction concerns; Section 404(c) does not authorize undisclosed self-interest.

### 15. **C — The owner controls; qualified distributions can be tax free.**

In a typical 529 education savings plan, the account owner selects among permitted investment options, requests distributions, and can often change the beneficiary to an eligible family member. Federal law generally provides tax-deferred growth and tax-free qualified distributions. **A** confuses the beneficiary with the controlling owner. **B** is false because 529 contributions are not federally deductible, although a state may offer a benefit. **D** is false because the earnings portion of a nonqualified distribution is generally taxable and may face an additional tax, even though contributed principal was after-tax money.

### 16. **B — The minor owns UTMA property and eventually controls it.**

A transfer under UTMA is generally irrevocable. The custodian manages the property as a fiduciary, but the minor is the beneficial owner and receives control at the termination age under applicable state law. Therefore **A** is false: the donor ordinarily cannot reclaim the gift. **C** is false because custodial-account income is taxable under applicable rules, potentially including the kiddie tax. **D** is false because the custodian cannot substitute another sibling as owner merely because family priorities change. A parent-owned 529 generally preserves greater owner control and beneficiary flexibility.

### 17. **B — Qualified medical withdrawals may be tax free and the HSA is portable.**

An HSA belongs to the individual, carries forward from year to year, and generally follows the owner between employers. Qualified medical distributions can be federal income-tax free. **A** confuses an HSA with a use-it-or-lose-it arrangement such as some flexible spending accounts. **C** is false because eligibility generally requires qualifying high-deductible health-plan coverage and no disqualifying coverage or status. **D** is false: leaving employment does not make nonmedical withdrawals tax free; tax and possible additional-tax rules depend on the owner’s age and applicable exceptions.

### 18. **C — Tenancy in common.**

TIC permits unequal undivided interests and has no automatic right of survivorship, so each owner’s interest can pass through that owner’s estate plan. **A** usually includes survivorship and commonly equal interests. **B** is generally limited to married couples in recognizing jurisdictions and also includes survivorship. **D** would deliberately create a nonprobate beneficiary transfer to the other owner, contrary to the stated desire to have each estate plan control. The parties should also use a co-ownership agreement addressing expenses, decisions, transfers, and partition.

### 19. **B — A qualified domestic relations order.**

A QDRO is the mechanism under ERISA and the Internal Revenue Code for recognizing an alternate payee’s right to specified qualified-plan benefits after divorce or another covered domestic-relations matter. **A** concerns education and Roth IRA rollover rules, not divorce. **C** concerns capital-loss recognition. **D** is an account-registration transfer at death. A divorce decree that is not qualified under the plan’s procedures may be insufficient, and IRAs use different transfer-incident-to-divorce rules.

### 20. **B — The triggered stop becomes a market order.**

A standard sell stop prioritizes execution after its trigger, not a guaranteed price. If the first liquid market is around $38, the order may fill near that price despite a $45 stop. **A** incorrectly treats the stop as a guarantee. **C** describes a stop-limit order, which would add a limit price. **D** is false because a gap ordinarily triggers rather than automatically cancels a stop under the applicable handling terms. The adviser should explain the tradeoff: a stop can execute badly; a stop-limit can fail to execute.

### 21. **B — The broker-dealer is acting as principal.**

Selling from its own inventory means the dealer is the customer’s counterparty and may earn a markup embedded in the price. **A** describes agency capacity, in which the broker arranges a trade with another party and may charge a commission. **C** and **D** have no connection to execution capacity. Principal status creates inventory and pricing conflicts that require fair dealing and applicable disclosure. When an investment adviser itself enters a principal trade with an advisory client, additional fiduciary and statutory consent requirements can apply.

### 22. **B — Best execution concerns the most favorable total result.**

Commission is one factor, but a broker’s price improvement, fill probability, speed, market impact, capability, financial responsibility, and service can justify a higher explicit charge for difficult orders. Thus **A** reduces the analysis improperly to commission. **C** is false: PFOF creates a routing conflict and does not eliminate execution review. **D** is false because material conflicts must be fully and fairly disclosed and addressed consistently with fiduciary duty. The adviser should periodically compare execution quality and document the basis for the broker selection.

### 23. **C — Time-weighted return.**

TWR divides the period around external cash flows and geometrically links subperiod returns, neutralizing the client’s choice to add a large amount before the loss. That makes it the conventional manager-evaluation measure when the manager does not control cash-flow timing. **B**, DWR or IRR, gives more weight to the loss period because more dollars were invested; it better describes the client’s dollar experience. **A** measures annual income relative to price. **D** converts a tax-exempt yield into a taxable-equivalent yield. Both TWR and DWR can be useful, but they answer different questions.

### 24. **B — 0.50.**

The Sharpe ratio is excess return divided by standard deviation:

$$
\frac{10\%-2\%}{16\%}=\frac{8\%}{16\%}=0.50.
$$

**A** is merely excess return expressed as a decimal and omits risk scaling. **C** divides the total return, rather than excess return, by standard deviation. **D** is the percentage-point excess return without dividing by risk. Sharpe uses total volatility, so it is useful for an entire portfolio; Treynor would divide by beta instead.

### 25. **B — 6.25%.**

Annual coupon interest is $1,000 times 5%, or $50. Current yield is:

$$
\frac{\$50}{\$800}=6.25\%.
$$

**A** is the coupon rate based on par, not current price. **C** incorrectly divides price by par or otherwise reverses the relationship. **D** overstates annual income. Current yield ignores the $200 gain if the bond is redeemed at par, time to maturity, reinvestment, default, and call risk. Yield to maturity or yield to call is more comprehensive for those cash-flow assumptions.

---

## Final Memory Framework

Use **CLIENT FIRST** to organize a recommendation:

- **C — Capacity and tolerance:** distinguish ability from willingness.
- **L — Legal owner and authority:** identify entity, trustee, custodian, fiduciary, or beneficiary roles.
- **I — Income, expenses, assets, and liabilities:** build cash flow and balance sheet.
- **E — Ends and events:** quantify goals, time horizons, life events, and values.
- **N — Nonfinancial behavior and knowledge:** account for biases, experience, and communication needs.
