- **T — Taxes and transfer design:** seek after-tax efficiency without giving unauthorized tax or legal advice.
- **F — Fiduciary alternatives:** compare cost, liquidity, risk, complexity, conflicts, and reasonably available choices.
- **I — Implementation:** select suitable orders, allocate fairly, and seek best execution.
- **R — Risk and return measurement:** use the right return, benchmark, and risk-adjusted metric.
- **S — Special accounts and statutes:** apply retirement, ERISA, education, custodial, and health-account rules.
- **T — Track and update:** monitor performance, products, service providers, beneficiaries, and changed facts.

The exam may isolate one calculation or definition, but professional advice integrates all of them. The best answer usually begins with the client’s verified facts, respects legal authority and constraints, compares realistic alternatives, states what is not guaranteed, and leaves a clear record of why the action served the client.

## Further Reading — Primary Sources

The following official materials are starting points. Rules, thresholds, and agency interpretations change; consult current editions and amendments.

### NASAA

- [NASAA — Uniform Investment Adviser Law Examination (Series 65) Study Guide](https://www.nasaa.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/NASAA-Series-65-Exam-Study-Guide.pdf) — official content outline effective September 1, 2023 and the controlling scope for this part.
- [NASAA — Investment Adviser Guide](https://www.nasaa.org/industry-resources/investment-advisers/) — state investment-adviser regulatory resources and links.

### Securities and Exchange Commission

- [SEC — Commission Interpretation Regarding Standard of Conduct for Investment Advisers](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/interp/2019/ia-5248.pdf) — fiduciary duty, best interest, conflicts, care, loyalty, and best execution.
- [SEC — Investment Adviser Public Disclosure](https://adviserinfo.sec.gov/) — Form ADV and adviser background research.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Asset Allocation, Diversification, and Rebalancing](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/getting-started/asset-allocation) — investor education on portfolio construction.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Types of Orders](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/how-stock-markets-work/types-orders) — market, limit, and stop-order basics.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Margin: Borrowing Money to Pay for Stocks](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/general-resources/news-alerts/alerts-bulletins/investor-bulletins/margin) — margin risks and forced liquidation.
- [SEC Investor.gov — 529 Plan Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=529%20plans) — ownership, qualified use, state benefits, fees, and plan comparison.
- [Federal Register — Regulation Best Execution Proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2023/01/27/2022-27644/regulation-best-execution) — official rulemaking record useful for understanding execution-quality policy; this is a proposal, not an adopted final rule.

### Internal Revenue Service

- [IRS Publication 550, Investment Income and Expenses](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-550) — interest, dividends, basis, gains, losses, wash sales, and investment income.
- [IRS Publication 590-A, Contributions to Individual Retirement Arrangements](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-590-a) — traditional and Roth IRA contribution and deduction rules.
- [IRS Publication 590-B, Distributions from Individual Retirement Arrangements](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-590-b) — distributions, required minimum distributions, beneficiaries, and Roth ordering.
- [IRS Publication 560, Retirement Plans for Small Business](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-560) — self-employed and small-business retirement plans.
- [IRS — One-Participant 401(k) Plans](https://www.irs.gov/retirement-plans/one-participant-401k-plans) — owner-only plan contributions and employee-coverage consequences.
- [IRS Publication 969, Health Savings Accounts and Other Tax-Favored Health Plans](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-969) — HSA eligibility, contributions, and distributions.
- [IRS Publication 970, Tax Benefits for Education](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-970) — 529, Coverdell, credits, and coordination rules.
- [IRS Publication 559, Survivors, Executors, and Administrators](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-559) — estate administration, income-tax, and basis concepts.
- [IRS Publication 551, Basis of Assets](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-551) — cost, gift, inherited, and adjusted basis.
- [IRS — Estate and Gift Taxes](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/estate-and-gift-taxes) — current transfer-tax forms, thresholds, and guidance.
- [IRS Publication 575 — Pension and Annuity Income](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-publication-575) — QDRO-related tax treatment and retirement-distribution fundamentals.
- [IRS — Donor-Advised Funds](https://www.irs.gov/charities-non-profits/charitable-organizations/donor-advised-funds) — sponsoring organizations, donor advice, and prohibited benefits.

### U.S. Department of Labor

- [DOL — Meeting Your Fiduciary Responsibilities](https://www.dol.gov/agencies/ebsa/about-ebsa/our-activities/resource-center/publications/meeting-your-fiduciary-responsibilities) — ERISA loyalty, prudence, diversification, plan documents, fees, and monitoring.
- [DOL — 29 CFR § 2550.404c-1, Participant-Directed Individual Account Plans](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-29/subtitle-B/chapter-XXV/subchapter-F/part-2550/section-2550.404c-1) — official Section 404(c) regulation.
- [DOL — Fiduciary Education Campaign](https://www.dol.gov/agencies/ebsa/employers-and-advisers/plan-administration-and-compliance/fiduciary-responsibilities) — fiduciary resources for plan sponsors and service providers.
- [DOL — Meeting Your Fiduciary Responsibilities](https://www.dol.gov/agencies/ebsa/about-ebsa/our-activities/resource-center/publications/meeting-your-fiduciary-responsibilities) — fiduciary duties and prohibited-transaction fundamentals.
- [DOL — Understanding Retirement Plan Fees and Expenses](https://www.dol.gov/agencies/ebsa/about-ebsa/our-activities/resource-center/publications/understanding-retirement-plan-fees-and-expenses) — plan fee categories and fiduciary review.

### FINRA

- [FINRA Rule 5310 — Best Execution and Interpositioning](https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/5310) — broker-dealer best-execution obligations and execution-quality considerations.
- [FINRA Rule 2230 — Customer Account Statements](https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/2230) — statement requirements relevant to client records.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Margin Account Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=margin%20accounts) — leverage, margin calls, and liquidation risk.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Short-Selling Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=short%20selling) — borrowing, covering, and loss risks.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Types of Orders](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/how-stock-markets-work/types-orders) — market, limit, stop, and stop-limit orders.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Payment-for-Order-Flow Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=payment%20for%20order%20flow) — routing compensation and investor considerations.
- [SEC Investor.gov — Investment-Performance Resources](https://www.investor.gov/search?keys=investment%20performance) — return, fees, benchmarks, and comparisons.

### Using primary sources effectively

For examination preparation, use the NASAA outline to identify what can be tested and the agency materials to confirm current concepts. For practice, retrieve the version effective for the transaction year, read definitions and exceptions, and document the source used. An IRS publication explains general federal tax rules but is not a substitute for the Internal Revenue Code, regulations, rulings, and individualized professional advice. Investor bulletins simplify rules and should be paired with governing statutes, regulations, and firm procedures when acting for a client.


---

# Part IV — Laws, Regulations, and Ethical Practices

## How to use this part

Domain 4 is the legal and ethical core of the Series 65. The NASAA study guide dated **September 1, 2023** assigns this domain **39 of the 130 scored questions (30%)** and divides it into eight groups. The underlying NASAA test specifications say **Effective June 12, 2023**. This part follows the eight-group structure in that official guide while identifying later legal developments through **August 11, 2026**.

This is an educational synthesis, not legal advice and not a substitute for a jurisdiction's current statutes, rules, orders, forms, interpretive releases, or counsel. The Uniform Securities Act of 1956 (the **USA**) and NASAA model rules are models. They have no force merely because NASAA adopted them; a state must enact or adopt them, and may change them. When a question says only “under the Uniform Securities Act,” apply the tested 1956 model as amended by NASAA. In practice, check the actual jurisdiction.

> **Licensure warning.** Passing the Series 65 is normally only one prerequisite for state investment adviser representative registration. It does not itself register or license anyone, authorize advisory activity, create an affiliation with a firm, or satisfy filing, fee, background, bonding, continuing-education, or other state requirements.

### The legal hierarchy

1. **Federal statutes** enacted by Congress include the Securities Act of 1933 (**Securities Act** or **SA**), Securities Exchange Act of 1934 (**Exchange Act** or **SEA**), Investment Company Act of 1940 (**ICA**), and Investment Advisers Act of 1940 (**Advisers Act** or **IAA**).
2. **SEC rules** implement federal statutes. In this part, a citation such as Rule 204-2 means 17 C.F.R. § 275.204-2 unless otherwise stated.
3. **State statutes and rules** govern matters not preempted by federal law and preserve broad antifraud and enforcement powers.
4. **The 1956 USA and NASAA models** are exam sources and legislative templates, not automatically binding law.
5. **Firm policies and contracts** may be stricter than law, but cannot waive mandatory investor protections.

### Five regulated roles that must not be collapsed

| Role | Core function | Typical registration locus | Key distinction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Federal covered adviser** | Advisory firm registered with the SEC under IAA §203 | SEC; state notice filings may remain | The firm is not state-registered as an IA, but states retain antifraud power and may register qualifying IARs with an in-state place of business. “Federal covered” means SEC-registered—not simply federally exempt. |
| **State investment adviser** | Advisory firm required to register under state law | One or more state administrators, usually through IARD | The firm itself is registered; its IARs generally need their own registrations. |
| **Investment adviser representative (IAR)** | Natural person who performs specified advisory, solicitation, portfolio-management, or supervisory functions | State(s), generally via Form U4/CRD | An IAR is an individual, not the advisory firm. SEC rules define which supervised persons of federal covered advisers are IARs for state-law allocation. |
| **Broker-dealer (BD)** | Person in the business of effecting securities transactions for others or for its own account | SEC/SRO and applicable states | A firm or sole proprietor; distinct from its individual agents. Incidental advice with no special compensation may fall outside the IA definition. |
| **Agent** | Individual representing a BD or issuer in effecting or attempting securities purchases or sales | State(s), generally via Form U4/CRD | An agent is an individual; not every officer of a BD or issuer is an agent unless the person actually meets the functional definition. |

### Person registration is not security registration

A lawful sale may require answers to two independent questions:

- **May this person act?** Analyze IA, IAR, BD, and agent registration or an exclusion/exemption.
- **May this security be offered or sold this way?** Analyze federal and state security registration, federal covered status, an exempt security, or an exempt transaction.

A registered agent can sell an unregistered security only if the security or transaction has a valid route around registration. A registered security does not authorize an unregistered agent to sell it.

### Four vocabulary distinctions

| Term | Meaning | What survives? |
|---|---|---|
| **Exclusion from a definition** | The person or instrument is outside the defined category if every condition is met. | Other laws and antifraud provisions may still apply. The claimant bears the burden under USA §402(d). |
| **Registration exemption** | The person, security, or transaction is within a definition but need not register under the particular provision. | Antifraud law generally survives. Conditions must be satisfied. |
| **Exempt security** | The security itself is exempt from state registration under USA §402(a); later sales commonly retain that status while the exemption remains applicable. | Antifraud provisions survive. Agents and BDs may still require registration. |
| **Exempt transaction** | A particular offer or sale is exempt under USA §402(b). | The exemption does not attach permanently to the security; the next transaction needs its own analysis. Antifraud provisions survive. |

---

## Group 1 — Regulation of Investment Advisers

### 1. The definitional test: advice, business, compensation

USA §401(f) and IAA §202(a)(11) use the same central architecture. A person is an investment adviser when the person, **for compensation**, is **in the business** of:

- advising others, directly or through publications or writings, about the value of securities or the advisability of investing in, purchasing, or selling securities; or
- as part of a regular compensated business, issuing analyses or reports concerning securities.

The state model expressly reaches financial planners and others who provide securities advice as an integral component of financially related services or hold themselves out as providing compensated securities advice. Compensation can be direct or indirect and need not be labeled an “advisory fee.” A bundled planning charge, referral benefit, product-related economic benefit, or other value can satisfy the concept. Advice need not result in a transaction.

A useful exam screen is **ABC**:

1. **Advice or analysis about securities?**
2. **Business?** Is the person holding out, providing advice with regularity, or receiving transaction-linked compensation that makes advice part of a business?
3. **Compensation?** Is any economic benefit received for the advisory activity, directly or indirectly?

If all three are present, start with IA status, then test exclusions and registration exemptions.

#### Federal and state definitional exclusions

| Category | Federal IAA §202(a)(11) | USA §401(f) exam treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Bank/trust institution | Qualifying banks and certain bank holding companies are generally excluded, with a special rule for advice to registered investment companies. | Bank, savings institution, or trust company excluded. |
| Listed professional | Lawyer, accountant, engineer, or teacher whose advice is **solely incidental** to the profession. | Same core four professions and solely-incidental condition. |
| Broker-dealer | Advice must be solely incidental to brokerage and there must be **no special compensation** for it. | Same two-part concept for a BD or agent. |
| Bona fide publisher | Federal text refers to a bona fide newspaper, news magazine, or business/financial publication of general and regular circulation. Personalized, timed, or tout-like advice can defeat the exclusion. | Bona fide publication or service, including electronic delivery, that does not advise based on each client's specific situation. |
| Government-securities adviser | Federal exclusion for advice limited to specified U.S. government obligations. | The amended tested USA does not retain this as a separate exclusion. |
| Credit-rating organization | Qualifying NRSRO, unless it recommends securities or manages securities assets for others. | Not an express model exclusion. |
| Family office | A family office satisfying SEC Rule 202(a)(11)(G)-1. | Only if the Administrator designates it or state law otherwise excludes/exempts it. |
| Federal covered adviser | Not an exclusion from the federal definition; it is an SEC-registered IA. | Expressly excluded from the state-law IA definition because federal registration preempts state IA registration. |
| IAR | A federal “supervised person” may or may not be an IAR under Rule 203A-3; IAR is principally a state registration concept. | An IAR is excluded from the definition of IA. |
| Other designated persons | SEC may exclude persons not within congressional intent. | Administrator may designate persons not within the state-law intent. |

> **Exam trap — exclusion versus exemption.** A lawyer whose occasional securities advice is solely incidental is outside the IA definition. An intrastate adviser under IAA §203(b)(1) is an adviser but may be exempt from SEC registration if every condition is satisfied. The legal paths are different.

### 2. Federal versus state adviser registration

IAA §203 generally requires an adviser using the mails or interstate commerce in connection with its business to register with the SEC unless an exemption applies. IAA §203A allocates most advisers between federal and state registration and largely preempts state registration of SEC-registered advisers. It does **not** erase state antifraud power, permitted notice filings and fees, or state IAR registration.

#### The AUM decision tree, current as of August 11, 2026

“Regulatory assets under management” are reported on Form ADV and generally encompass securities portfolios for which the adviser provides continuous and regular supervisory or management services, calculated under Form ADV instructions. Do not substitute net capital, revenue, or number of clients.

```text
Is the adviser exempt from registration?
├─ Yes → Follow the exemption and any reporting/state duties.
└─ No
   ├─ Adviser to a registered investment company or elected BDC?
   │  └─ Yes → SEC registration route regardless of ordinary AUM band.
   ├─ Eligible for a Rule 203A-2 exception or other federal route?
   │  └─ Yes → SEC registration permitted/required as applicable.
   └─ Apply home-state and RAUM rules:
      ├─ Below $25m → ordinarily state level if regulated or required
      │  to be regulated as an IA in the principal-office state.
      ├─ $25m to below $100m → mid-sized: ordinarily state level if
      │  the home state requires registration and subjects the IA
      │  to examination.
      ├─ $100m to below $110m → optional SEC-registration buffer for
      │  a mid-sized adviser covered by Rule 203A-1.
      └─ $110m or more → SEC-registration tier, absent another rule.

Already SEC-registered?
└─ No required withdrawal until RAUM falls below $90m.
```

The **$100 million/$110 million/$90 million** framework is Rule 203A-1's current buffer. It prevents needless switching around one point. It does not mean every adviser with $100 million may ignore home-state law, nor that an exempt adviser must register. A state-registered adviser that becomes eligible for SEC registration generally applies within **90 days after filing** the annual Form ADV amendment reporting eligibility. An SEC registrant that reports ineligibility generally files Form ADV-W within **180 days after fiscal year-end**, unless it becomes eligible again.

A mid-sized adviser below $100 million is not barred from SEC registration if its principal-office state does not require it to register or does not subject it to examination. Which states satisfy those facts can change; test the statutory condition, not a memorized state list.

#### Rule 203A-2 exceptions to the federal-registration prohibition

As of August 2026, the rule permits SEC registration for these categories even when the ordinary allocation would bar it:

- **Pension consultants** advising plan assets of at least **$200 million**, calculated under the rule's 12-month/90-day methodology.
- **Related advisers** controlling, controlled by, or under common control with an SEC registrant eligible for SEC registration, with the same principal office and place of business; 25% voting/profit rights create a rebuttable control presumption under the rule.
- **Newly formed advisers** with a reasonable expectation of eligibility within **120 days**, provided the Form ADV undertaking and timely withdrawal conditions are met.
- **Multi-state advisers** that otherwise must register in **15 or more states**, with Form ADV representations, records, and withdrawal duties if the number drops below 15 and no other federal basis remains.
- **Internet investment advisers.** Following the 2024 amendment, the adviser must provide advice to **all clients exclusively** through an operational interactive website while relying on the exception, retain supporting records for at least five years, and satisfy the affiliation limitation. The platform must provide ongoing software-generated digital advice to more than one client; a mostly human practice with a website is not enough.

#### Statutory SEC-registration exemptions

Do not confuse an exemption from SEC registration with the §203A allocation:

- **Intrastate adviser, §203(b)(1):** all clients reside in the adviser's principal-office state; no advice about securities listed on a national exchange; and the adviser does not advise a private fund. State registration may still be required.
- **Insurance-company-only adviser, §203(b)(2).**
- **Foreign private adviser, §203(b)(3):** no U.S. place of business; fewer than 15 U.S. clients plus U.S. private-fund investors; less than $25 million attributable U.S. AUM (unless adjusted by rule); no general U.S. public holding out; and no registered-fund or elected-BDC client.
- Specified **charitable**, **employee-benefit-plan**, **CFTC-registered commodity trading adviser**, **SBIC**, and **RBIC** categories under §203(b).
- **Venture-capital-fund adviser, §203(l):** advises only qualifying venture capital funds. This is not shorthand for every startup or private-equity fund.
- **Private-fund-adviser exemption, §203(m):** advises solely private funds and has **less than $150 million in private-fund assets under management in the United States**, calculated under SEC rules.

Advisers relying on §203(l) or §203(m) are commonly **exempt reporting advisers (ERAs)**. “Exempt” does not mean invisible. Rule 204-4 requires specified portions of Form ADV through IARD, the filing fee, amendments under the form instructions, and a final report on ceasing business, losing ERA status, or applying for SEC registration. They remain subject to applicable antifraud, pay-to-play, examination, recordkeeping/reporting, and state requirements.

NASAA's **Registration Exemption for Investment Advisers to Private Funds Model Rule** supplies a possible state exemption, but only in adopting jurisdictions and only on its conditions. State definitions, disqualifications, notice reports, fees, beneficial-owner qualifications, audited-financial-statement conditions, and venture-capital treatment can differ. Never assume the federal $150 million exemption automatically supplies a state exemption.

### 3. Notice filing is not state IA registration

A federal covered adviser registers with the SEC by Form ADV. A state generally may not require that firm to register as an IA, but may require a **notice filing** consisting of SEC-filed documents, consent to service of process, and a fee where federal law permits. Under USA §§201(f) and 202(b), the federal covered adviser files before conducting advisory business in the state, subject to the model's institutional-client exception. IARD commonly transmits the notice.

A notice filing:

- does not convert the federal adviser into a state-registered adviser;
- does not imply administrator approval;
- does not preempt state antifraud investigations; and
- does not remove state registration of an IAR with a place of business in the state.

### 4. Form ADV and the registration lifecycle

| Filing/document | Function |
|---|---|
| **Form ADV Part 1A** | Check-the-box and schedule information about ownership, business, clients, AUM, custody, disciplinary events, affiliations, and eligibility. Filed through IARD. |
| **Part 2A brochure** | Plain-English narrative about services, fees, methods, risks, discipline, other business, conflicts, brokerage, custody, discretion, voting, and financial information. |
| **Part 2B supplement** | Information about supervised persons providing advice, including education, business background, discipline, other activities, compensation, and supervision. Generally delivered, not filed with the SEC. |
| **Part 3 / Form CRS** | Brief relationship summary for SEC registrants serving retail investors; separate from the full brochure. |
| **Form ADV-W** | Withdrawal from SEC or state adviser registration. |
| **Form ADV-E** | Accountant filings relating to custody surprise examinations. |

Under federal Rule 204-1, Parts 1 and 2 are amended at least annually within **90 days after fiscal year-end**, and more often when Form ADV instructions require. Part 3 follows its instructions. A material change should trigger the specific amendment and client-disclosure analysis; “wait until annual renewal” is not a safe general rule. Under USA §203(d), a registrant corrects materially inaccurate or incomplete filed information promptly; a federal covered adviser follows the SEC amendment timing for SEC-filed documents.

An application is disclosure, not approval. Under IAA §203(c), the SEC generally must grant registration or begin denial proceedings within **45 days** after filing, subject to statutory procedures and extensions. Under USA §202(a), absent a denial order or pending proceeding, registration becomes effective at noon on the **30th day after filing**, unless the Administrator accelerates it or defers effectiveness after an amendment. These are separate systems.

### 5. Brochures: federal rule versus NASAA model rule

| Question | SEC Rule 204-3 | NASAA Model Rule 203(b)-1 |
|---|---|---|
| Initial Part 2A delivery | Before or at contract execution. | At least **48 hours before** contracting, **or** at signing if the client may terminate without penalty within **five business days**. |
| Annual delivery | Within **120 days** of fiscal year-end, if material changes: updated brochure or material-changes summary offering a free brochure. | Similar 120-day updated brochure/supplements or summary-and-offer framework; no delivery if no material change. |
| Supplement | Before or when the supervised person begins advisory service; for teams over five, supplements for five with greatest day-to-day responsibility. | Required for specified direct-contact advisers and persons exercising discretion, subject to the adopted rule. |
| Common exception | Registered fund/qualifying BDC; impersonal advice under $500 annually. | Same basic model categories. |

> **Exam trap.** The “48 hours or five business days” rule is the NASAA state model, not the current SEC brochure rule. A question naming federal law calls for federal timing.

### 6. Books, records, reports, and examinations

IAA §204 authorizes SEC records and examinations. Rule 204-2 requires SEC-registered or required-to-register advisers to keep true, accurate, current records, including journals and ledgers; order memoranda; bank records; bills and financial statements; written communications about recommendations, orders, funds, and performance; discretionary-account lists and powers; contracts; advertisements and substantiation; brochures and delivery records; compliance policies and annual reviews; custody reports; pay-to-play records; Code of Ethics records; and specified Regulation S-P records.

The general federal retention rule is **at least five years from the end of the fiscal year of the last entry**, with the first two years in an appropriate adviser office, subject to special periods for organizational records and particular items. Electronic storage must permit prompt, legible retrieval, safeguards, indexing, and duplicate storage. Social media, texts, chats, and email are judged by content, not platform: business communications can be records.

USA §203 authorizes the Administrator to prescribe records, disclosure, financial reports, correcting amendments, and reasonable periodic, special, or other examinations. NASAA's recordkeeping and financial-reporting rules are models; jurisdictions may adopt different versions. The official 2023 outline names Model Rules 203(a)-2 and 203(c)-1. NASAA adopted revised model recordkeeping rules in May 2026, but a state's law changes only upon adoption. On an exam question expressly naming an older model, follow the named tested model; in practice, verify the current state rule.

Common state-model concepts include maintaining comparable business, communication, client, discretionary, custody, contract, advertising, personal-trading, and financial records; making them available to the Administrator; and filing required balance sheets or audited statements when custody, substantial prepayment, or financial impairment creates investor risk.

### 7. Minimum finances and custody-sensitive reporting

Under NASAA's traditional Model Rule 202(d)-1, often tested as a model baseline:

- an adviser with custody generally maintains at least **$35,000 net worth**;
- an adviser with discretionary authority but not custody generally maintains at least **$10,000 net worth**; and
- an adviser accepting more than **$500 per client six months or more in advance** generally maintains positive net worth.

A deficient adviser generally notifies the Administrator by the close of the next business day and files a financial-condition report by the close of the following business day. Bonding, home-state compliance, custody definitions, fee-deduction safeguards, and financial statement requirements depend on the adopted text. These are **model figures**, not universal federal requirements or guaranteed current law in every state.

### 8. Supervision is a substantive duty

USA §204 permits discipline for failure reasonably to supervise IARs or employees. Federal IAA §203(e)(6) similarly supports discipline for supervisory failure, with a defense framework where a reasonable compliance system existed, responsibilities were reasonably discharged, and the supervisor lacked reason to believe the system was being ignored.

Reasonable supervision is risk-based and evidenced, not a job title. It includes:

- licensing and role mapping before activity begins;
- review of advice, trading, fees, advertising, complaints, outside activity, political contributions, personal trading, and custody indicators;
- written policies tailored to the business;
- training, testing, escalation, discipline, and remediation;
- preservation and surveillance of approved communication channels; and
- oversight of vendors and remote offices without treating outsourcing as abdication.

**Scenario 1 — the growing robo-adviser.** A new firm has $8 million RAUM and expects $120 million within four months. It has no state registration yet. Rule 203A-2 may permit the 120-day federal route if the firm reasonably expects eligibility, makes the Form ADV undertaking, and withdraws on time if eligibility fails. It cannot merely announce that growth is likely and operate unregistered.

**Scenario 2 — the “federal exemption.”** A private-fund manager with $80 million U.S. private-fund AUM relies on §203(m). It is not a “federal covered adviser” merely because federal law supplies an exemption. It is an ERA with federal reports and must separately analyze every relevant state's private-fund-adviser rule.

---

## Group 2 — Regulation of Investment Adviser Representatives

### 1. State-law IAR definition

USA §401(g) defines an IAR as an individual associated with a state-registered or required-to-register adviser—or an individual with a place of business in the state associated with a federal covered adviser—who:

1. makes recommendations or otherwise renders securities advice;
2. manages client accounts or portfolios;
3. determines which recommendations or advice should be given;
4. solicits, offers, negotiates for, or sells advisory services; or
5. supervises employees performing those functions.

An IAR is a **natural person**. A clerical employee who performs none of these functions is not transformed into an IAR merely by working at an adviser. A title does not control; functions do.

### 2. Rule 203A-3 for supervised persons of federal advisers

For federal-state allocation, a supervised person of an SEC adviser is an IAR only if both tests apply:

- the person has **more than five** natural-person clients other than “excepted persons” (therefore at least six); and
- more than **10%** of all the person's clients are such non-excepted natural persons.

A qualified client under Rule 205-3 is an excepted person for this count. Non-U.S. clients need not be counted. Even if both numerical tests are met, the person is not an IAR under this rule if the person does not regularly solicit, meet, or communicate with clients, or provides only impersonal advice.

This federal numerical test does **not** replace the USA's functional definition for personnel of a state-registered adviser. It identifies IARs of federal covered advisers for the scope of permitted state registration.

### 3. Place of business and de minimis rules

Rule 203A-3 defines an IAR's place of business as an office where the IAR regularly provides advisory services, solicits, meets, or communicates with clients, plus any location held out publicly for those activities. A home office or coworking site can qualify; labels do not defeat facts.

IAA §203A(b) permits a state to register an IAR of a federal covered adviser when the IAR has a place of business there. Under USA §201(c), an out-of-state IA/IAR with **no place of business** in the state has a model exemption if its only in-state clients are listed institutional clients, or if during the preceding 12 months it has no more than **five** noninstitutional clients there. This is the state adviser/IAR de minimis concept.

Do not merge three different numbers:

| Rule | Number | What it tests |
|---|---:|---|
| USA §201(c) out-of-state de minimis | No more than **5** noninstitutional clients in 12 months | Whether an IA/IAR without an in-state place of business needs state registration. |
| Rule 203A-3 federal-adviser IAR definition | More than **5** non-excepted natural-person clients **and** over **10%** of all clients | Whether a supervised person of an SEC adviser is an IAR for state allocation. |
| Rule 203A-2 multi-state adviser | **15** or more states | Whether an adviser may choose SEC registration to avoid many state IA registrations. |

### 4. Registration process: U4, examination, affiliation

An IAR generally files **Form U4** through CRD/IARD under the firm's sponsorship and pays state fees. Form U4 discloses identity, residences, employment, outside activities, criminal events, regulatory actions, civil matters, customer complaints/arbitrations, judgments/liens, and financial events. It is a continuing disclosure document: reportable changes require timely amendment under the applicable instructions and state law.

NASAA's model examination rule generally recognizes the Series 65, or the Series 7 plus Series 66, subject to state timing and waiver provisions. Certain professional designations may support a waiver in an adopting jurisdiction. A waiver is not automatic nationwide, and an exam waiver is not a registration waiver.

USA §§201–204 provide the model lifecycle:

- the IAR must be registered unless an exemption applies;
- an adviser generally may not employ an unregistered IAR when registration is required;
- the federal covered adviser's in-state IAR may be state-registered;
- association and termination notices must be filed promptly by the party assigned under the model;
- registration normally expires December 31 unless renewed;
- material information must remain accurate; and
- the Administrator may examine qualifications and discipline, deny, suspend, revoke, bar, censure, limit, or cancel on statutory grounds with required process.

Under the model, an IAR's registration is not effective while the person is not associated with the relevant adviser. Passing an exam while unaffiliated is not authority to solicit clients.

### 5. IAR continuing education and post-registration conduct

NASAA's IAR Continuing Education Model Rule has been adopted in many, but not all, jurisdictions. Where applicable, an IAR generally completes annual products-and-practice and ethics/professional-responsibility credits, with reciprocity and reporting rules determined by the adopted version. Requirements can change; check the IAR's registration state, not merely the firm's home state.

Post-registration obligations include:

- keeping Form U4 current;
- renewing and paying fees;
- satisfying applicable CE;
- using only approved titles, channels, marketing, and fee arrangements;
- disclosing conflicts and outside activities;
- following custody, discretion, privacy, personal-trading, and political-contribution controls; and
- remaining within the scope of firm supervision.

**Scenario 3 — six clients, but only 8%.** A portfolio specialist at an SEC adviser has six ordinary natural-person clients and 94 institutional clients. Six is more than five, but 6% is not more than 10%; Rule 203A-3 does not classify the specialist as an IAR on those facts.

**Scenario 4 — five clients and an office.** An out-of-state IAR has only two clients in State X but regularly meets them from an advertised State X office. The five-client de minimis route requires **no place of business** in the state, so the small client count does not save the person.

## Group 3 — Regulation of Broker-Dealers

### 1. Broker, dealer, and broker-dealer

USA §401(c) defines a broker-dealer as a person engaged in the business of effecting securities transactions for the account of others **or for its own account**. Federal law separates the functions:

- A **broker** effects securities transactions for others.
- A **dealer** buys and sells securities for its own account as part of a regular business.
- A firm can act in both capacities and commonly is called a broker-dealer.

The USA excludes an agent, an issuer, qualifying banks/savings institutions/trust companies, and a person with no place of business in the state whose in-state activity is limited to specified institutional counterparties or certain existing out-of-state customers. The ordinary investor trading frequently for a personal account is not necessarily “in the business” as a dealer; business status turns on the total facts.

IAA §202 incorporates federal Exchange Act concepts of broker and dealer for the advisory-law analysis. The BD's advisory exclusion requires both **solely incidental advice** and **no special compensation**. A BD charging a separate planning fee cannot invoke that exclusion merely because it also executes trades.

### 2. Underwriter, market maker, associated person

- A **Securities Act underwriter** includes a person who buys from an issuer with a view to distribution, offers or sells for an issuer in connection with a distribution, or participates directly or indirectly in such an undertaking. For this definition, “issuer” reaches controlling, controlled, and commonly controlled persons. A routine investor who buys with investment intent is not an underwriter merely because the investor later resells, but distribution intent and restricted-security rules matter.
- An Exchange Act **market maker** is a dealer that regularly holds itself out as willing to buy and sell a security for its own account on a regular or continuous basis. Market makers quote bid and ask prices, supply liquidity, and earn a spread while bearing inventory risk.
- An **associated person of a broker-dealer** broadly includes partners, officers, directors, branch managers, comparable persons, controlling or controlled persons, and employees, generally excluding personnel whose functions are solely clerical or ministerial. “Associated person” is broader than state “agent”; the latter requires the securities-sales function.

### 3. Registration and post-registration

USA §201 makes it unlawful to transact business in the state as a BD without registration or to employ an unregistered agent when registration is required. The firm generally files Form BD and its agents Form U4 through CRD, supplies consent to service, pays fees, and becomes subject to the Administrator's qualification, financial, bonding, examination, and disciplinary authority. Federal BDs also register with the SEC, join an applicable SRO such as FINRA, and comply with Exchange Act rules. Federal registration does not automatically displace state BD and agent registration in the way SEC IA registration preempts state IA registration.

Post-registration duties include:

- updating Forms BD and U4 and reporting material events;
- maintaining net capital, customer-protection, and financial-reporting compliance;
- supervising associated persons and branches;
- books and records under Exchange Act Rules 17a-3 and 17a-4 and applicable state law;
- confirmations, account records, communications, complaints, order tickets, blotters, financial ledgers, and supervisory records;
- fair dealing, best execution, Regulation Best Interest for recommendations to retail customers, and applicable FINRA/MSRB rules; and
- annual renewal and fees.

USA §203 lets the Administrator prescribe BD records and financial reports, but federal law limits duplicative state requirements for federally registered BDs. The Administrator may inspect records and coordinate with the SEC and SROs.

> **Capacity trap.** A principal trade means the firm trades from or into its own inventory. An agency trade means it acts for the customer. Capacity affects compensation, disclosure, conflicts, and confirmations. Calling every transaction “agency” because an individual agent spoke to the customer is wrong.

**Scenario 5 — the planning desk.** A BD offers occasional allocation observations with trades and receives only normal brokerage compensation. The solely-incidental/no-special-compensation exclusion may apply. If it launches a separately priced ongoing portfolio-advice program, it must analyze IA registration and fiduciary duties in addition to BD obligations.

---

## Group 4 — Regulation of Broker-Dealer Agents

### 1. Functional definition

Under USA §401(b), an **agent** is an individual, other than a BD, who represents a BD or issuer in effecting or attempting to effect securities purchases or sales. Solicitation can qualify even if no sale closes. The employer relationship and actual function control—not titles such as “consultant,” “finder,” “vice president,” or “relationship manager.”

A partner, officer, or director is an agent only if the person otherwise performs agent functions. Conversely, a commissioned salesperson can be an agent despite a non-sales title.

### 2. Individuals representing issuers

An individual representing an issuer is not an agent under the tested model for specified transactions, including:

- transactions in certain exempt securities listed in USA §402(a);
- exempt transactions under §402(b);
- specified federal covered offerings under Securities Act §18(b)(3) and §18(b)(4)(D); and
- transactions with existing employees, partners, or directors when no commission or other remuneration is paid for solicitation.

This is not a blanket “issuer employee” exemption. The exact security/transaction category and remuneration condition matter. Rule 506 issuer representatives, finders, and transaction-based compensation require careful federal and state analysis; using the word “finder” does not create an exemption.

### 3. Registration, Form U4, and association

USA §§201–204 generally require agent registration and prohibit a BD or issuer from employing an unregistered agent when registration is required. Form U4 supplies the individual's uniform application and continuing disclosure record. The firm files association/termination information, supervises activity, and commonly files Form U5 at termination under the CRD system.

Under the USA model:

- an agent's registration is effective only while associated with the registered BD or relevant issuer;
- beginning and terminating an association requires prompt notice;
- registrations expire December 31 unless renewed;
- the Administrator may require examinations and assess statutory disqualification grounds; and
- a transfer to a different BD does not carry an automatic free-standing license—the association and state registration must be effective.

Passing the Series 7, Series 63, or another exam does not by itself register an agent, just as passing the Series 65 does not register an IAR.

### IA/IAR versus BD/agent quick test

| Conduct | Firm-level category | Individual category |
|---|---|---|
| Ongoing compensated securities advice | IA | IAR if functions/registration tests are met |
| Effecting customer securities transactions | BD | Agent |
| Both advice and execution | May require dual IA and BD status unless an exclusion applies | May be both IAR and agent |
| Clerical processing only | Neither merely because of employment | Ordinarily neither IAR nor agent |

**Scenario 6 — the issuer's paid finder.** A startup promises 4% of money raised to a person who introduces retail investors. The “finder” label is not protective. Attempting to effect sales for transaction-based compensation strongly raises agent and federal broker-registration issues, and the offering itself needs a registration or exemption analysis.

---

## Group 5 — Regulation of Securities and Issuers

### 1. Security and issuer

USA §401(m) defines security broadly: notes, stocks, bonds, debentures, evidence of indebtedness, profit-sharing interests, transferable shares, **investment contracts**, voting-trust certificates, security certificates of deposit, certain oil/gas/mining interests, options, warrants, rights, and related interests. Substance prevails over labels.

The federal **Howey** investment-contract analysis asks whether there is an investment of money in a common enterprise with a reasonable expectation of profits to be derived from the entrepreneurial or managerial efforts of others. Digital assets, real-estate arrangements, memberships, and revenue interests require facts, not category slogans.

A fixed insurance policy or fixed annuity promising fixed-dollar benefits is generally outside the USA security definition. A variable annuity or variable life product shifts investment risk and is a security as well as an insurance product.

An **issuer** is a person that issues or proposes to issue a security. USA §401(h) provides special issuer rules for trust certificates and unit-type vehicles and generally treats oil, gas, or mining fractional interests as having no issuer for that definition. “Nonissuer” means a transaction not directly or indirectly for the issuer's benefit.

### 2. The two-layer offering analysis

```text
Step 1: Is the instrument a security?
├─ No → Securities-registration analysis ends, but other law applies.
└─ Yes
   Step 2: Is there an offer or sale using federal jurisdictional means?
   ├─ Yes → Securities Act §5 registration or federal exemption.
   └─ Continue state analysis whenever state jurisdiction exists.

Step 3: In the state, is it:
├─ a federal covered security/transaction? → no state registration;
│  permitted notice/fees and antifraud may remain.
├─ an exempt security under USA §402(a)? → no state registration.
├─ an exempt transaction under USA §402(b)? → that transaction exempt.
└─ none of the above? → register under USA §§302, 303, or 304.

Step 4: Separately register/qualify every required BD, agent, IA, and IAR.
Step 5: Apply antifraud law in every route.
```

### 3. Federal registration under Securities Act §5

Absent an exemption:

- **Pre-filing period:** §5(c) generally prohibits offers before a registration statement is filed.
- **Waiting period:** after filing but before effectiveness, offers may occur within statutory and rule limits, but sales cannot be completed; a written offer must be a compliant prospectus.
- **Post-effective period:** sales may close, and required final prospectus delivery must accompany or precede delivery of the security.

A registration statement provides issuer, management, business, risk, use-of-proceeds, capitalization, financial, and offering information. SEC effectiveness is not a merit endorsement. Material misstatements and omissions can create civil, administrative, and criminal exposure.

### 4. Federal covered securities and Securities Act §18

Section 18 preempts state registration, qualification, and merits review for covered securities, principally:

- securities listed or authorized for listing on qualifying national exchanges, plus specified equal or senior securities of the same issuer;
- securities issued by registered investment companies;
- offers or sales to SEC-defined qualified purchasers; and
- specified exempt transactions, importantly **Rule 506** offerings.

States retain antifraud authority and, for categories permitted by §18, may require a notice filing, consent to service, sales information, and fees. Exchange-listed covered securities generally cannot be burdened with state notice fees. “Covered” does not mean regulator-approved or fraud-proof.

### 5. State registration methods under USA §§301–305

USA §301 makes it unlawful to offer or sell a security in the state unless it is state-registered, exempt, or federal covered.

| Method | Best conceptual use | Core idea |
|---|---|---|
| **Registration by filing, §302** | Seasoned issuers meeting the model's eligibility conditions | A streamlined state filing based on established issuer and disclosure history. |
| **Registration by coordination, §303** | Offering simultaneously registered under the Securities Act | State registration coordinates with the federal registration statement and can become effective with it if state conditions are met. |
| **Registration by qualification, §304** | Any security; often offerings not eligible for filing/coordination | The most comprehensive state disclosure route; Administrator may require extensive issuer, promoter, capitalization, offering, and financial information. |

USA §305 supplies generally applicable provisions: who may file, fees, effective date, ongoing reports, amendments, escrow/impoundment conditions, and effectiveness of outstanding offerings. The Administrator may issue stop orders under §306 on statutory grounds and required process. Registration generally covers the specified offering, not every future issuance by the issuer.

### 6. Regulation D

Regulation D is a set of federal exemptions, not a registration form and not immunity from antifraud law.

#### Rule 506(b)

- No dollar cap.
- No general solicitation or general advertising.
- Unlimited accredited investors and up to **35 nonaccredited purchasers in any 90-calendar-day period** under the current rule text, with each nonaccredited purchaser meeting the rule's sophistication standard, counted under its provisions.
- Prescribed information must be furnished to nonaccredited purchasers; accredited investors must have access to requested information as required by the rule.
- Resale restrictions and bad-actor disqualification apply.

#### Rule 506(c)

- No dollar cap and general solicitation is permitted.
- **Every purchaser** must be accredited.
- The issuer must take **reasonable steps to verify** accredited status; a checked box alone is not automatically enough.
- Resale restrictions and bad-actor disqualification apply.

Rule 506 offerings are federal covered transactions under §18. States cannot require substantive registration, but can require a Form D-type notice, consent, and fee and can enforce antifraud law.

#### Rule 504

Rule 504 permits eligible issuers to offer up to **$10 million in a 12-month period**, subject to issuer eligibility, integration, solicitation, resale, disclosure, and bad-actor rules. Unlike Rule 506, Rule 504 is not categorically a federal covered security under §18. State registration or a state exemption must be found. General solicitation and freely tradable securities are allowed only through specified state-law routes; “Rule 504” alone is not a public-advertising license.

#### Form D and accredited investors

Form D is generally due within **15 calendar days after the first sale** in a Regulation D offering. It is a notice; filing it does not create an exemption if substantive conditions fail.

Accredited-investor categories under Rule 501 include, among others, natural persons with qualifying net worth or income, persons holding designated professional credentials (currently including Series 7, 65, and 82), knowledgeable employees for investments in their private fund, and qualifying entities. The familiar natural-person tests use net worth over **$1 million excluding the primary residence**, or income over **$200,000 individually/$300,000 jointly** in each of the two preceding years with a reasonable expectation for the current year. These figures and categories are changeable federal rules; date them when used in practice.

### 7. USA §402 exempt securities

The model list includes, subject to its exact terms:

- U.S., state, and local governmental securities and guarantees;
- Canadian and qualifying foreign-government obligations;
- securities issued or guaranteed by qualifying banks, savings institutions, trust companies, credit unions, and regulated insurers;
- specified railroad, public-utility, and regulated holding-company securities;
- qualifying nonprofit-organization securities;
- highly rated short-term commercial paper maturing within nine months, in at least $50,000 denominations under the model; and
- qualifying employee-benefit-plan investment contracts with required notice.

The old model also lists specified exchange securities, but federal §18 now supplies controlling preemption for qualifying national-exchange securities. Always reconcile older USA text with federal law.

### 8. USA §402 exempt transactions

Important model transactions include:

- an isolated nonissuer transaction;
- qualifying manual/secondary-market nonissuer transactions;
- an unsolicited nonissuer order through a registered BD, with possible customer acknowledgment;
- transactions between issuer and underwriter or among underwriters;
- fiduciary transactions by executors, administrators, sheriffs, marshals, receivers, bankruptcy trustees, guardians, or conservators;
- bona fide pledge transactions not designed to evade the Act;
- offers or sales to listed financial institutions, institutional buyers, or BDs;
- a limited-offeree private transaction directed to no more than **10 noninstitutional persons in the state in 12 months**, with investment intent and no solicitation commission, subject to Administrator adjustment or conditions;
- qualifying preorganization subscriptions, existing-security-holder offers, and certain offers after federal and state filings but before effectiveness.

The USA number 10 is a **state model transaction exemption**. It is not Rule 506(b)'s 35-purchaser concept and not the IA five-client de minimis rule.

The Administrator may deny or revoke certain exemptions for a specific security or transaction prospectively, with process. The claimant bears the burden of proving an exemption or definitional exception. No §402 exemption turns fraud into lawful conduct.

### 9. Investment companies and private funds

ICA §3(a) generally captures an issuer that is or holds itself out as primarily engaged in investing, reinvesting, or trading securities, or that owns “investment securities” exceeding **40% of total assets** on an unconsolidated basis, excluding government securities and cash items from the statutory calculation. Registration under the ICA adds a separate federal regulatory layer.

Common private funds rely on exclusions from the investment-company definition:

- **§3(c)(1):** generally no more than 100 beneficial owners and no public offering; or
- **§3(c)(7):** securities owned exclusively by qualified purchasers and no public offering.

A private fund is not a registered investment company. Its interests are still securities, its offering needs an exemption, its adviser needs a separate registration/exemption analysis, and antifraud rules apply.

### 10. State antifraud authority

USA §101 prohibits devices, schemes, or artifices to defraud; material misstatements or omissions; and fraudulent or deceitful acts, practices, or courses of business in connection with an offer, sale, or purchase. USA §102 applies analogous and fiduciary-oriented prohibitions to compensated advisory activity and solicitation.

Fraud under USA §401(d) is not limited to common-law deceit. Registration, a federal covered status, an exempt security, an exempt transaction, or an excluded person does not create a fraud exemption. Securities Act §18 expressly preserves state antifraud enforcement over covered securities.

**Scenario 7 — Rule 506(c) online ad.** A startup posts a public social-media solicitation, reasonably verifies every purchaser as accredited, files Form D, satisfies bad-actor and other Rule 506(c) conditions, and files state notices. The public ad is not inherently fatal under 506(c). If one unverified nonaccredited purchaser buys, however, the offering cannot be rescued by calling it 506(b), because 506(b) bars general solicitation.

**Scenario 8 — exempt Treasury security.** A state-registered agent lies about the yield on a U.S. Treasury. The security's exemption from state registration does not protect the lie; state and federal antifraud rules remain.

**Scenario 9 — private sale count.** An issuer directs offers to 11 ordinary residents of a state and pays a commission to solicit them. The tested USA §402(b)(9) model exemption fails both its offeree ceiling and remuneration condition, even if only four people buy. Count offers, not merely purchasers, under that model.

## Group 6 — Remedies and Administrative Provisions

### 1. The Administrator's roles

The “Administrator” is the state official or agency designated to administer that state's securities law. Depending on the jurisdiction, the title may be commissioner, secretary, director, division, or commission. Under the tested USA, the Administrator has several distinct kinds of power:

| Power | Typical source | Important limit |
|---|---|---|
| Rulemaking and forms | USA §412 | A **rule** is generally applicable. It must be within delegated authority, published, and satisfy public-interest/investor-protection standards and state administrative procedure. An order cannot substitute for a rule of general application. |
| Registration administration | §§202, 204, 302–306 | May process, condition, accelerate, deny, suspend, revoke, bar, censure, limit, or cancel as authorized. Registration is not merit approval. |
| Examinations | §203(e) | May conduct reasonable periodic, special, or other examinations of registrant records, including outside the state, and cooperate with other regulators. |
| Investigations and subpoenas | §407 | May investigate publicly or privately, inside or outside the state, require statements, administer oaths, subpoena witnesses, and demand relevant records. A court enforces a disobeyed subpoena and may punish contempt. |
| Administrative enforcement | §§204, 306, 408 | May enter authorized registration orders and issue cease-and-desist orders, including specified summary relief followed by due process. |
| Judicial enforcement | §408 | May ask a court for injunction, restraining order, mandamus, receiver/conservator, rescission, restitution, or disgorgement. The Administrator does not personally issue a court injunction or imprison a defendant. |
| Criminal referral | §409 | May refer evidence to the attorney general or prosecutor; the prosecutor brings the criminal case. |
| Interpretive/no-action relief | §413 | May issue interpretive opinions or decline enforcement in specified facts; relief is not a blanket amendment of the statute. |

### 2. Investigations are broader than final adjudications

The Administrator need not wait for a completed violation. USA §407 permits investigation when a person **has violated or is about to violate** the Act, to aid enforcement, or to inform rules and forms. An investigation can be private, need not be limited to in-state evidence, and can demand sworn statements and documents.

A subpoena is not self-enforcing. If a witness refuses, the Administrator applies to the appropriate court for an order compelling attendance, testimony, or production. Violation of the court's order—not simply disagreement with the agency—can produce contempt. The model addresses compelled testimony and immunity, but constitutional and local rules vary.

### 3. Denial, suspension, revocation, bar, censure, limitation, cancellation, withdrawal

Under USA §204, a disciplinary order against a person registration generally requires **both**:

1. the action is in the public interest; **and**
2. a statutory ground exists.

Grounds include materially deficient applications; willful violations of state or listed federal commodities/securities law; specified recent convictions; injunctions; other regulatory orders; dishonest or unethical practices; insolvency in the proper case; lack of qualification; failure reasonably to supervise; and unpaid fees. A technical ground does not automatically require the maximum sanction.

Key procedural distinctions:

- **Summary postponement or suspension** may issue pending a final determination. The Administrator must promptly notify affected parties and, if a written request is received, set the matter for hearing within the model's **15-day** period.
- A final non-summary order generally requires prior notice, opportunity for hearing, and written findings and legal conclusions.
- **Cancellation** is administrative housekeeping for a person no longer existing or doing business, adjudicated incompetent, under conservatorship/guardianship, or unlocatable. It is not a disciplinary finding and is not interchangeable with revocation.
- **Withdrawal** under the model generally becomes effective after **30 days**, unless accelerated or a proceeding is pending or instituted. Withdrawal does not necessarily outrun jurisdiction; the model permits a qualifying proceeding within one year after effective withdrawal for specified willful violations.
- A securities registration statement may be denied, suspended, or revoked under §306 for grounds relating to disclosure, legality, fraud, unreasonable compensation, offering terms, or other statutory matters. Apply the securities-registration provision rather than assuming a person-registration sanction.

### 4. Cease-and-desist orders and court remedies

When a person has engaged or is about to engage in a violation, USA §408 permits the Administrator to:

- issue a **cease-and-desist order**, with or without prior hearing subject to due process; and/or
- bring a court action seeking an injunction or other authorized equitable relief.

On a proper showing, a court may order temporary or permanent injunctive relief, mandamus, a receiver or conservator, rescission, restitution, or disgorgement. A receiver protects and marshals assets; restitution aims to restore victims; rescission unwinds a transaction; disgorgement removes ill-gotten gains. These remedies can coexist with administrative sanctions, private civil claims, and criminal prosecution.

### 5. Criminal liability

USA §409's model text punishes a person who willfully violates the Act (with a knowledge condition for a materially false §404 filing) or willfully violates a rule or order. The tested 1956 model states a maximum **$5,000 fine, three years' imprisonment, or both**, and contains an optional five-year charging limitation. Those old model numbers are **not a forecast of any actual state's current penalties**; enacted statutes may be far higher and materially different.

“Willfully” in this regulatory setting generally requires intentional conduct—awareness of what one is doing—not necessarily evil motive or knowledge of the precise rule. The model supplies a protection against imprisonment for violating a rule or order when the defendant proves no knowledge of it. The Administrator refers evidence; a criminal court imposes punishment after prosecution and conviction.

### 6. Civil liability under USA §410

The model creates express private remedies rather than an unlimited implied claim for every statutory breach.

#### Buyer claims against sellers

A seller can be liable for selling in violation of specified registration/licensing/representation provisions or for a material misstatement or omission when the buyer did not know the truth and the seller cannot prove lack of knowledge despite reasonable care. The buyer may seek rescission—consideration plus statutory interest, costs, and reasonable attorney fees, less income received, upon tender—or corresponding damages if the buyer no longer owns the security.

#### Advisory-client claims

A compensated adviser can be liable for specified unregistered, fraudulent, unlawful-contract, or misleading conduct. The client may recover advisory consideration and loss due to the advice, plus the model's interest, costs, and reasonable attorney fees, less income received from the advice.

#### Secondary liability and defenses

Controlling persons, partners, officers, directors, comparable persons, materially aiding employees, BDs, and agents can be jointly and severally liable unless they prove they did not know and, with reasonable care, could not have known the operative facts. Contribution is available among persons liable under the model.

The tested model's outside limits bar suit after the earlier of:

- **three years** after the sale contract or rendering of advice; or
- **two years** after discovery of the facts constituting the violation.

A proper written rescission offer can cut off a later claim if not accepted or rejected within the model's 30-day mechanics. Contract clauses purporting to waive compliance are void. Actual state limitation periods, damages, interest, tender rules, class remedies, and secondary liability vary.

### 7. Judicial review

Under USA §411, a person aggrieved by a final order may petition the designated court within **60 days** after entry. The court reviews the administrative record under the statutory standard and may affirm, modify, enforce, or set aside the order. Filing the petition does **not** automatically stay the order; the court must grant a stay.

### 8. Territorial jurisdiction

USA §414 avoids a simplistic “both people were in the state” test.

For offers and sales, relevant provisions apply when an offer to sell is made in the state or an offer to buy is made and accepted there. An offer is made in the state when it originates there or is directed there and received there. Acceptance is in the state when properly directed and first communicated there under the model. Bona fide out-of-state publications with the specified general circulation and out-of-state radio/television broadcasts receive limited exclusions.

For advisory conduct, USA §§102, 201(c), and the relevant §405 prohibition apply when an act instrumental to the prohibited conduct occurs in the state, whether or not either party is physically present. Email, video meetings, websites, and remote work can create multistate facts.

Applicants file irrevocable consent to service of process. A nonresident engaging in actionable conduct can be deemed to appoint the Administrator for service even without a filed consent, subject to required notice mechanics.

> **Administrator-power traps**
>
> - The Administrator can investigate outside the state but cannot prosecute conduct with no statutory jurisdictional nexus.
> - The Administrator can issue a cease-and-desist order; a **court** issues an injunction.
> - The Administrator can refer a criminal case; a **prosecutor** charges and a court sentences.
> - Registration may be denied only through authorized grounds and procedure, not because the Administrator dislikes an otherwise lawful investment.
> - Cancellation is not revocation, and withdrawal is not erasure of past misconduct.

**Scenario 10 — remote solicitation.** An unregistered adviser in State A sends personalized securities advice for a fee to a client who receives it in State B. Physical absence from State B is not dispositive. The communication and compensated advisory acts can create State B jurisdiction, subject to exclusions, de minimis rules, and federal preemption.

**Scenario 11 — “the state approved us.”** An issuer tells prospects that a filed registration proves the Administrator found its projections accurate. USA §405 makes that representation unlawful. Filing and effectiveness are disclosure/registration events, not an endorsement.

---

## Group 7 — Communications with Clients and Prospects

### 1. Required disclosures flow from fiduciary duty

An adviser must make full and fair disclosure of material facts so a client can give informed consent. What is material depends on whether a reasonable client would consider it important. Form ADV is a disclosure system, not the outer boundary of fiduciary duty.

Common required subjects include:

- services, strategy, material risks, limitations, and monitoring;
- fee formula, billing practices, other charges, and compensation;
- conflicts from commissions, revenue sharing, proprietary products, affiliates, referrals, soft dollars, principal or cross trades, aggregation/allocation, side-by-side management, and personal trading;
- disciplinary events and financial impairment where required;
- custody, discretion, account authority, proxy voting, and brokerage practices;
- material changes to personnel or business;
- the capacity in which the firm acts in a transaction; and
- facts needed to make earlier statements not misleading.

“Disclosure” is not magic. A conflict that cannot be fully and fairly disclosed or cannot be managed consistently with the client's best interest must be eliminated or the adviser must refrain from the conduct. Buried boilerplate, vague “may” language when a conflict actually exists, and consent obtained after harm are inadequate.

### 2. Registration is not approval

USA §405 establishes four exam rules:

1. Filing an application, notice, or registration statement does not mean the Administrator found it accurate or complete.
2. Effective registration does not mean the Administrator approved the person or security.
3. An exemption or exception does not imply merit review or recommendation.
4. It is unlawful to tell a prospect otherwise.

The same practical lesson applies federally. “SEC-registered,” “state-registered,” “Series 65-qualified,” and “Form ADV filed” may be factual if accurate, but cannot be presented as a governmental seal of quality, safety, expertise, or performance.

### 3. Guarantees and performance promises

USA §401(e) uses “guaranteed” to mean guaranteed as to payment of principal, interest, or dividends. A legitimate third-party guarantee must be accurately described, including the guarantor and limits. It does not make a security risk-free.

Advisers and agents must not guarantee against loss, guarantee repurchase at a set price, promise a particular return, or imply that past or hypothetical performance assures future results. A contractual undertaking to refund an advisory fee under defined service conditions is different from guaranteeing market performance, but it still must be fair and clearly described.

### 4. Advisory contracts

#### USA §102(c)

Unless the Administrator permits otherwise, the state-model advisory contract must provide in writing that:

- compensation is not based on a share of capital gains or appreciation;
- the adviser cannot assign the contract without the other party's consent; and
- a partnership adviser will notify the other party within a reasonable time of a partnership-membership change.

An asset-based fee measured by account value at specified dates is not inherently a prohibited performance fee. “Assignment” includes direct or indirect transfer of the contract and transfer of a controlling voting block, subject to the model's minority-partnership changes.

#### Advisers Act §205

An SEC-registered or required-to-register adviser's contract may not contain a prohibited performance-fee term, may not permit assignment without client consent, and—if the adviser is a partnership—must require reasonably prompt notice of membership changes. Section 205 permits specified fulcrum-fee and statutory arrangements and authorizes qualified-client exceptions.

Effective **June 29, 2026**, the SEC's inflation-adjusted Rule 205-3 qualified-client dollar tests are:

- at least **$1,400,000 under management with the adviser** immediately after contracting; **or**
- net worth of **more than $2,700,000** immediately before contracting.

Other Rule 205-3 routes include qualified-purchaser status and specified knowledgeable adviser executives/employees. For the natural-person net-worth test, exclude the primary residence as an asset; generally exclude mortgage debt up to home value as a liability, but include excess mortgage debt and specified recent increases. Private investment companies may require look-through to equity owners. Existing contracts satisfying the rule when entered generally receive transition protection; a new party is tested under the rule then in effect.

These figures come from SEC Order 2026-08480 and are time-sensitive. Older texts showing $1.1 million/$2.2 million are stale for new contracts after the 2026 effective date.

#### NASAA contract-content rule

The official outline cites NASAA's **Contents of Investment Advisory Contract Model Rule under USA 2002 §502(c)**. In adopting jurisdictions, a written contract generally identifies services, term, advisory fee and formula, prepaid-fee refund, discretion, and assignment; prohibits impermissible performance fees and waivers; and requires disclosure of compensation from securities sales or other conflicts as the adopted text provides. This is a model built under the 2002 Act even though the exam otherwise tests the 1956 Act. State adoption and wording control.

A hedge or exculpation clause cannot mislead a client into believing nonwaivable rights or fiduciary duties disappeared. Arbitration clauses do not themselves waive substantive law, but enforceability, required disclosure, and forum rules vary.

### 5. SEC Marketing Rule 206(4)-1

The Marketing Rule applies to an adviser **registered or required to register with the SEC**. A state adviser instead follows state statutes and adopted advertising/ethics rules, although federal antifraud law may also apply. Some older state models are stricter than the federal rule, especially about testimonials.

#### What is an advertisement?

The rule generally covers:

- a direct or indirect adviser communication to **more than one person** offering securities-advisory services to prospects/private-fund investors or new services to current clients/investors; and
- a one-to-one communication if it includes **hypothetical performance**, subject to specified exclusions; plus
- a compensated testimonial or endorsement.

Excluded categories include extemporaneous live oral communications; required regulatory notices reasonably designed to satisfy the requirement; and specified unsolicited one-to-one hypothetical-performance responses and one-to-one communications to private-fund investors.

The medium does not decide coverage. A website, email campaign, podcast, webinar, video, social post, direct message, app notification, slide deck, or influencer statement can be an advertisement.

#### Seven general prohibitions

An advertisement may not:

1. contain a material false statement or misleading omission;
2. make a material factual claim the adviser lacks a reasonable basis to substantiate on SEC demand;
3. create a materially false or misleading implication or inference;
4. discuss potential benefits without fair-and-balanced treatment of material risks and limitations;
5. refer to specific advice in a manner that is not fair and balanced;
6. include/exclude performance or choose time periods unfairly; or
7. otherwise be materially misleading.

#### Testimonials and endorsements

The federal rule conditionally permits them. Required safeguards generally include:

- clear, prominent disclosure of client/nonclient status, compensation, and brief material conflicts;
- disclosure of material compensation terms and conflicts;
- adviser oversight and a reasonable basis to believe compliance;
- a written agreement describing scope and compensation; and
- disqualification screening.

For **de minimis compensation of $1,000 or less over the preceding 12 months**, the written-agreement and disqualification conditions are excepted, but disclosure, oversight, general prohibitions, and other law remain. The rule also has tailored affiliated-person, registered-BD, and Rule 506 offering exceptions. A paid “like,” ranking, referral link, or influencer post can be an endorsement even without traditional testimonial language.

#### Third-party ratings

The adviser needs a reasonable basis to believe the survey makes favorable and unfavorable responses equally easy and is not engineered for a predetermined result. The advertisement clearly and prominently identifies the rating date/period, rating provider, and related compensation. Buying an award without explaining the payment is a classic problem.

#### Performance advertising

Key conditions include:

- show net performance whenever gross performance is shown, with at least equal prominence, comparable format, same periods, and same methodology;
- for non-private-fund portfolios/composites, show one-, five-, and ten-year performance with equal prominence through a sufficiently recent year-end, substituting life-of-portfolio where shorter;
- never imply SEC review or approval of the calculation;
- present related performance without cherry-picking, subject to narrow exclusions that cannot materially inflate results;
- pair extracted performance with, or offer promptly, total-portfolio performance;
- use hypothetical performance only with audience-relevance policies plus criteria/assumption and risk/limitation information; and
- use predecessor performance only when responsible personnel, account similarity, completeness, and disclosure conditions are satisfied.

### 6. Social media, email, digital messaging, and websites

Apply four questions to every communication:

1. **Content:** Is it advice, an offer of service, a testimonial, performance, a conflict disclosure, or a business record?
2. **Speaker:** Did the firm create it, pay for it, adopt it, entangle itself with it, or merely permit independent speech?
