3. **Audience:** One person, more than one, current clients, prospects, private-fund investors, or the public?
4. **Controls:** Was it reviewed where required, supervised, balanced, substantiated, archived, and corrected if stale?

A firm can become responsible for third-party content by endorsing, editing, paying for, or selectively featuring it. Linking to independent material is not automatically adoption, but context and control matter. Employee personal accounts can become business channels. Deleting a post does not satisfy recordkeeping.

Email and messaging used for advice, orders, recommendations, complaints, performance, custody, or client business can be required records. Firms should prohibit unapproved ephemeral channels or capture them. A website must be monitored for stale AUM, personnel, fees, awards, links, and disclosures. Cybersecurity controls must not alter records needed for regulatory access.

### 7. Books and review

Rule 204-2 requires copies of advertisements, records of testimonial/endorsement disclosures and compliance bases, rating questionnaires when obtained, performance calculation support, intended-audience records for hypothetical performance, and related communications. The usual federal period is five years, first two in an appropriate office, under the rule's specific timing.

The SEC Marketing Rule does not impose a universal regulator preapproval or filing requirement for every ad. Firm compliance policies may require pre-use review. A state Administrator may require filing of sales or advertising literature under USA §403 or an adopted rule, subject to exemptions and federal preemption. Do not equate internal principal review, state filing, and SEC approval.

**Scenario 12 — five-star ad.** An SEC adviser pays a client $1,500 for a five-star video and posts it without conflict disclosure. This exceeds the federal de minimis threshold and lacks required disclosures/agreement/oversight. Even a $500 payment would not erase disclosure, oversight, and general antifraud duties.

**Scenario 13 — one-person backtest.** An adviser emails backtested returns to one ordinary prospect. The one-to-one format does not automatically escape the Marketing Rule because hypothetical performance receives special treatment. The adviser needs the applicable audience, methodology, risk, and record controls.

**Scenario 14 — stale website.** A state adviser accurately posted “$90 million AUM” two years ago but now manages $20 million and continues displaying it. A statement true when first posted can become misleading; supervision requires periodic review and correction.

## Group 8 — Ethical Practices and Fiduciary Obligations

### 1. The adviser's fiduciary duty

The Advisers Act does not reduce fiduciary duty to a checklist. Sections 206(1) and 206(2), interpreted by the Supreme Court and SEC, impose a federal fiduciary duty on advisers. The duty is principles-based, applies to the entire adviser-client relationship, and comprises a **duty of care** and **duty of loyalty**. State common law, USA §102, NASAA unethical-practice rules, trust law, ERISA, contract, and professional duties can add obligations.

#### Duty of care

The adviser must provide advice in the client's best interest based on a reasonable understanding of the client's objectives. This commonly requires:

- reasonable inquiry into financial situation, tax status, objectives, experience, risk tolerance, liquidity needs, time horizon, restrictions, and other relevant circumstances;
- reasonable investigation and due diligence into investments, strategies, costs, risks, data, and service providers;
- advice suitable for and in the best interest of the client, not merely “not unsuitable”;
- best execution when selecting BDs and executing client trades; and
- monitoring at the frequency and scope agreed upon and reasonably implied by the relationship.

For institutional clients, the inquiry may focus on mandate, governing documents, legal constraints, liquidity, risk budget, and investment policy. A one-time consultant's monitoring obligation differs from an ongoing discretionary manager's, but neither may misdescribe the scope.

#### Duty of loyalty

The adviser may not subordinate the client's interest to its own. It must make full and fair disclosure of material conflicts so the client can provide informed consent. Disclosure must be specific enough for the client to understand the conflict, its incentives, and its effect. If informed consent cannot be achieved or the conflict remains inconsistent with the client's best interest, the adviser must eliminate or avoid it.

The fiduciary duty cannot be waived wholesale by contract. Sophistication can affect how duties are shaped and disclosed, but “client agrees adviser owes no fiduciary duty” is not a safe harbor.

### 2. Compensation, fees, and commissions

Compensation creates incentives and must be reasonable, accurately calculated, and fully disclosed. Relevant questions include:

- Is the fee asset-based, hourly, flat, subscription, transaction-based, wrap, incentive-based, or mixed?
- What assets are included? Are cash, leverage, margin debit, household assets, or held-away assets counted?
- When and how is the fee valued and prorated?
- Is it charged in advance or arrears, and how are refunds computed?
- What additional custody, brokerage, fund, platform, product, or tax costs does the client bear?
- Does the adviser receive commissions, trails, revenue sharing, referral payments, spreads, markups, gifts, or noncash benefits?
- Does one choice pay the adviser more than a reasonably available alternative?

An asset-based fee is not a performance fee merely because account value rises and the dollar fee rises. It can still be excessive or conflicted. Charging both advisory fees and commissions is not categorically unlawful, but the dual role and incentive to trade or recommend costlier products require clear disclosure and best-interest analysis.

Misconduct includes billing on the wrong balance, failing to rebate prepaid fees after termination, charging undisclosed household minimums, valuing illiquid assets opportunistically, applying an unearned breakpoint, or calling a transaction-based payment a “consulting fee” to evade BD registration.

#### Performance fees

USA §102(c) and IAA §205(a)(1) begin with a prohibition on compensation based on a share of capital gains or appreciation, then allow statutory or rule-based exceptions. Under federal Rule 205-3, a qualified client can enter a performance-fee contract. As stated in Group 7, the current tests effective June 29, 2026 include at least **$1.4 million managed by the adviser** or net worth **over $2.7 million**, as well as other qualifying routes. The primary residence is excluded under the rule's special net-worth calculation.

NASAA Model Rule 102(f)-3 provides a state performance-compensation exemption with disclosure and client-qualification conditions. A state may update its thresholds on a different schedule. Never import the federal qualified-client figures into a state question unless the adopted state rule does so.

A **fulcrum fee** adjusts symmetrically above and below a base fee according to performance against an appropriate index. An arrangement that rewards gains without a proportionate downside adjustment is not a fulcrum fee merely because it mentions a benchmark.

### 3. Pay-to-play

SEC Rule 206(4)-5 combats political contributions used to obtain government advisory business. It applies to SEC-registered or required-to-register advisers, exempt reporting advisers, and specified exempt advisers.

Core rules:

- A contribution by the adviser or a covered associate to an official who can influence adviser selection generally triggers a **two-year ban on receiving compensation** for advisory services to that government entity.
- A contribution can follow an employee into the firm. A nonsoliciting new covered associate generally has a six-month lookback; a solicitor can carry the full two-year exposure.
- An adviser or covered associate may not pay a solicitor for government business unless the solicitor is qualifying adviser personnel or a qualifying “regulated person.”
- They may not coordinate or solicit contributions to relevant officials or payments to relevant state/local political parties.
- Indirect conduct is prohibited if direct conduct would violate the rule.
- Advising a covered investment pool in which a government entity invests is treated as advising the government entity.

A natural-person covered associate's contribution does not trigger the two-year compensation ban if aggregate contributions to one official per election do not exceed **$350** when the contributor was entitled to vote for the official, or **$150** when not entitled to vote. A narrow returned-contribution cure requires discovery within four months, an amount no greater than $350, return within 60 calendar days, and annual/per-person limits. Do not assume every refund cures a violation.

**MSRB Rule G-37** is a separate municipal-securities/municipal-advisory pay-to-play regime. Its de minimis contribution exception traditionally permits a municipal finance professional to contribute up to **$250 per election** to an official for whom the person is entitled to vote, while covered contributions can trigger a two-year business ban. Do not substitute G-37's $250 for the SEC adviser's $350/$150 limits.

### 4. Soft dollars and Exchange Act §28(e)

Client brokerage commissions are client assets. Section 28(e) supplies a limited safe harbor when an investment manager with discretion causes an account to pay a BD more than the lowest available commission and determines in good faith that the commission is reasonable in relation to the value of eligible **brokerage and research services**, viewed in the particular transaction or the manager's overall responsibilities.

A disciplined analysis asks:

1. Is the product or service eligible research or brokerage under §28(e)?
2. Does it provide lawful, substantive assistance to investment decision-making or execution?
3. If it has mixed eligible and ineligible uses, has the adviser made a reasonable allocation and paid the ineligible portion itself?
4. Did the adviser make the required good-faith value determination, seek best execution, keep support, and disclose the practice and conflict?

Potentially eligible research includes advice, analyses, or reports about securities, issuers, industries, economic factors, portfolio strategy, or performance. Eligible brokerage relates to effecting transactions and functions incidental to execution, clearance, and settlement. Rent, furniture, travel, salaries, mass-market office equipment, entertainment, general marketing, and ordinary overhead are not transformed into research by paying with commissions.

The safe harbor is not a free-spending rule and does not excuse excessive commissions, poor execution, undisclosed conflicts, or using one client's commissions to buy benefits unrelated to investment responsibilities.

**Scenario 15 — mixed-use terminal.** A market-data terminal supplies eligible research and also performs the adviser's payroll. The adviser must make and document a reasonable allocation and pay hard dollars for the noneligible payroll use. Charging all of it to commissions is not protected simply because the terminal also displays research.

### 5. Custody: identify it before applying safeguards

Custody means holding client funds or securities, directly or indirectly, **or having authority to obtain possession**. Under federal Rule 206(4)-2, custody includes:

- physical possession, unless inadvertently received assets are returned promptly and in any event within **three business days**;
- authority, including a general power of attorney, to withdraw client funds or securities;
- serving as general partner, managing member, trustee, or a comparable role giving legal ownership or access; and
- a related person's custody in connection with advisory services.

A check payable to a third party is not possession of client funds under the rule. Authority only to deduct advisory fees is custody, but receives a limited exception from the surprise-examination requirement when conditions are met. Trading discretion alone generally is not custody because it authorizes trades, not withdrawal for the adviser's benefit; a broad transfer power can be custody.

#### Federal qualified-custodian framework

Client assets generally must be maintained by a **qualified custodian**:

- a qualifying bank or FDIC-insured savings association;
- a registered BD holding assets in customer accounts;
- a registered futures commission merchant for the permitted assets; or
- a qualifying foreign financial institution that customarily holds financial assets and segregates customer assets.

Accounts must be in each client's name or in accounts containing only client assets under the adviser's name as agent or trustee. If the adviser opens the account, it promptly notifies the client of custodian identity, address, and manner of holding and urges comparison of adviser and custodian statements where applicable.

The adviser must have a reasonable basis after due inquiry to believe the qualified custodian sends at least **quarterly statements** directly to clients, showing positions and transactions. Custodied assets generally receive an annual surprise examination by an independent public accountant at an irregular, unannounced time. The accountant files Form ADV-E and rapidly reports discrepancies and engagement termination as the rule requires.

If the adviser or related person itself is the qualified custodian, enhanced protections include a PCAOB-registered and inspected accountant and an annual internal-control report. Operationally independent related custodians receive a tailored exception from surprise examination, not from every safeguard.

#### Federal exceptions and special cases

- Mutual-fund shares may be held by the transfer agent instead of another qualified custodian.
- Certain privately offered, uncertificated, issuer-recorded, transfer-restricted securities are excepted from the qualified-custodian requirement; pooled vehicles must use the audit route to rely on it.
- Custody solely from fee-deduction authority is excepted from surprise examination, not from the rest of the rule.
- A pooled vehicle can use the annual-audit route: PCAOB-registered/inspected independent accountant, GAAP financials distributed generally within **120 days** after fiscal year-end, plus liquidation audit. This substitutes for specified statements and surprise-exam requirements.
- Registered investment company accounts are outside this custody rule.

NASAA Model Rule 102(e)(1)-1 provides a state custody framework with notice, qualified-custodian, direct-statement, surprise-exam or audited-pool safeguards and fee-deduction conditions. States may modify it. The state minimum-financial rules described in Group 1 can add net-worth or bond consequences.

> **Custody trap.** Using an unaffiliated custodian does not prove the adviser lacks custody. Withdrawal authority, fee deduction, trustee status, or a related custodian may still create custody.

### 6. Discretion and trading authorization

**Discretion** is authority to decide what security, amount, action, or strategy to trade without contemporaneous client approval. An adviser can have discretion without custody. Under common NASAA unethical-practice rules, discretionary authority must be granted in writing within **10 business days after the first transaction** placed under oral discretion. Authority limited to **time or price**—after the client specifies the security and amount—is ordinarily not treated as discretionary authority, but it generally expires at the end of the business day unless renewed in writing.

Best practice is written authorization before the first discretionary trade. The agreement should define account, scope, limits, options/margin/short authority, voting, cash transfers, and termination. A client's broad objective does not authorize trades outside the mandate.

For a BD agent, discretionary power ordinarily requires prior written customer authorization and written firm acceptance under applicable SRO rules. Firm approval does not eliminate suitability, best-interest, churning, or fiduciary duties.

### 7. Prudent investor standards and suitability

The Uniform Prudent Investor Act applies primarily to trustees and turns prudence into a portfolio-process standard rather than hindsight about one security. Core concepts include:

- reasonable care, skill, and caution;
- portfolio-level risk and return suited to trust purposes, terms, distribution needs, and circumstances;
- diversification unless special circumstances reasonably justify otherwise;
- impartiality among beneficiaries;
- only reasonable and appropriate costs;
- reasonable verification of relevant facts; and
- prudent delegation, selection, scope, and monitoring of agents.

An investment can be risky yet prudent in a diversified portfolio; a “safe” concentration can be imprudent. Compliance is judged when the decision was made, not solely by outcome.

For advisers, “suitability” is a floor-like exam word, but the fiduciary standard asks whether advice is in the client's best interest after reasonable inquiry. For BDs recommending to retail customers, Regulation Best Interest imposes disclosure, care, conflict, and compliance obligations. A dual registrant must identify its capacity and apply the correct standard to the relationship and recommendation.

### 8. Anti-money laundering and sanctions

Broker-dealers are financial institutions under the Bank Secrecy Act and must maintain risk-based AML programs, customer identification, customer due diligence, suspicious activity reporting, currency reporting where applicable, independent testing, training, and designated responsibility, along with OFAC sanctions controls.

FinCEN's 2024 final rule would have brought many SEC-registered advisers and ERAs into the BSA AML/CFT and SAR framework, but FinCEN postponed its effective date from January 1, 2026 to **January 1, 2028** while conducting further review. Therefore, as of August 11, 2026, do **not** state that the new federal adviser AML-program rule is already operative. Advisers nevertheless have antifraud, fiduciary, sanctions, identity-theft, custody, privacy, and suspicious-fact escalation duties and may be contractually subject to custodian or fund AML controls. State law and other business lines can independently apply.

Exam questions may test AML principles rather than this implementation date: know the client and beneficial owners as required, risk-rate relationships, monitor red flags, preserve records, escalate rather than tip off a suspected subject, and never facilitate evasion.

### 9. Principal and agency-cross transactions

IAA §206(3) distinguishes two conflicts:

- **Principal trade:** adviser knowingly sells from its own account to a client or buys into its own account from the client.
- **Agency cross:** adviser, or a qualifying affiliate, acts as broker for the person on the other side of the adviser's client trade.

Absent an exception, before completion of each principal or agency-cross trade, the adviser discloses in writing the capacity in which it acts and obtains client consent. Blanket consent does not satisfy the ordinary principal-trade rule.

Federal Rule 206(3)-2 and NASAA Model Rule 102(f)-1 provide a conditional agency-cross framework. Core protections include:

- prospective written client consent after full written conflict disclosure;
- a written confirmation at or before completion describing nature, date, remuneration information, and other required facts;
- at least annual disclosure of transaction count and total commissions/remuneration;
- conspicuous notice that consent is revocable; and
- no cross where the adviser or affiliated person recommended the transaction to **both** buyer and seller.

The rule does not waive best execution or fiduciary duty. Agency cross is not principal trading: in the cross, the adviser brokers both sides but does not itself own the security.

**Scenario 16 — power of attorney.** A client authorizes an adviser to trade any security and to wire funds to any bank account the adviser designates. The first grant is discretion; the second creates authority to obtain possession and therefore custody. Both sets of safeguards must be analyzed.

**Scenario 17 — affiliated seller.** An adviser recommends that Client A buy stock from the adviser's proprietary account. This is a principal trade, not an agency cross. Written capacity disclosure and transaction-specific consent before completion are central; a general contract clause is not enough.

### 10. Client loans and loans from clients

NASAA unethical-practice models generally prohibit an adviser or IAR from borrowing money or securities from a client unless the client is a BD, an affiliate of the adviser, or a financial institution engaged in lending; they also generally prohibit lending to a client unless the adviser is a financial institution in the business of lending or the client is an affiliate. The precise adopted exceptions vary.

The conflict is acute: an indebted adviser may temper advice to preserve the loan, misuse confidential financial information, or expose a client to credit risk outside the advisory mandate. A wealthy or sophisticated client does not erase the issue. Ordinary margin loans from a client's unaffiliated BD and bank credit obtained on arm's-length terms are not adviser-client loans merely because the adviser knows about them.

### 11. Sharing profits and losses

An agent or associated person generally may not share directly or indirectly in a customer's account profits or losses except under a written arrangement approved by the firm and, under applicable BD rules, in proportion to the person's financial contribution, with tailored rules for immediate family. An adviser/IAR promising to reimburse losses or take a slice of gains can create an unlawful guarantee, undisclosed performance compensation, custody, and conflict.

A properly disclosed Rule 205-3 incentive fee is not ordinary “sharing in the account,” but it must satisfy the performance-fee rules. Waiving a fee after poor service differs from agreeing to absorb market losses. Never use a personal check to make a complaining client whole outside firm procedures; escalate the complaint.

### 12. Confidentiality and privacy

An adviser must protect client identity, holdings, transactions, financial circumstances, tax information, credentials, communications, and material nonpublic information. Disclosure is generally limited to client authorization, lawful regulatory or judicial demands, service providers under appropriate controls, and other legal bases. Discussing a recognizable client in an elevator or using an account screenshot in a pitch can breach duty even if the client's name is cropped.

Controls include least-privilege access, encryption, secure disposal, clean-desk and remote-work practices, vendor diligence, identity verification before disbursement, call-back procedures, phishing resistance, and prompt access termination. Confidentiality survives termination of the relationship.

### 13. Insider trading and material nonpublic information

Information is **material** if a reasonable investor would consider it important or it would significantly alter the total mix of information. It is **nonpublic** until disseminated broadly enough and absorbed by the market. Trading or tipping in breach of a duty can violate Exchange Act §10(b) and Rule 10b-5. Misappropriation theory reaches a person who misuses confidential information entrusted by its source, even without a direct duty to the issuer's shareholders.

An adviser should maintain policies under IAA §204A reasonably designed to prevent misuse of material nonpublic information: restricted/watch lists, information barriers, need-to-know access, preclearance, surveillance, escalation, and training. A mosaic assembled from public and lawfully obtained immaterial nonpublic pieces is not automatically insider information; the source, duty, and materiality of each piece matter.

> **Tipping trap.** The tipper need not trade personally. Communicating material nonpublic information for an improper personal benefit can create tipper liability, and a tippee who knows or should know of the breach can incur liability by trading or tipping onward.

### 14. Selling away and outside business activity

**Selling away** occurs when an agent or associated person participates in a securities transaction outside the regular scope of firm employment without required notice and approval. It can include recommending, referring, negotiating, receiving compensation, or facilitating a private offering. No compensation does not necessarily remove the notice duty.

An **outside business activity** is a separate employment, board, consulting, ownership, or compensated role that may create conflicts or time commitments. BD and IA rules differ, but both require truthful disclosure and firm review where applicable. A private securities transaction is not cured merely because it was listed as an outside business. “My friend’s startup” and “my own LLC” are not exceptions.

For IARs, outside accounts and activities also raise fiduciary, personal-trading, custody, valuation, allocation, referral-compensation, Form U4, Form ADV, and Code of Ethics concerns. Firm approval does not exempt the transaction from securities registration or antifraud law.

### 15. Manipulation, front-running, and allocation abuse

Market manipulation includes wash trades, matched orders, marking the close, painting the tape, spoofing, layering, pump-and-dump schemes, false rumors, and transactions designed to create a false appearance of activity, price, or supply/demand. Intent and facts control; a real change in beneficial ownership for a bona fide investment purpose is different from a wash trade.

**Front-running** means trading for oneself or a favored account ahead of a known client order or recommendation to benefit from its expected market impact. Related abuses include:

- allocating profitable trades to personal/favored accounts and losses to clients;
- delaying allocations until results are known;
- cherry-picking IPOs or scarce offerings;
- placing personal trades inconsistent with client recommendations without disclosure/review;
- cross trading to move losses or inflate valuation; and
- excessive aggregation or bunching that disadvantages some clients.

Fair allocation policies should be established before outcomes are known, consistently applied, documented, and disclosed. Aggregation can improve execution, but costs and partial fills must be allocated fairly.

### 16. Personal securities transactions, outside accounts, and Code of Ethics

Rule 204A-1 requires an SEC-registered or required-to-register adviser to establish, maintain, and enforce a written Code of Ethics that:

- sets fiduciary conduct standards;
- requires compliance with federal securities laws;
- requires access-person holdings and transaction reporting plus adviser review;
- requires prompt reporting of code violations to the chief compliance officer or a route that ultimately reaches the CCO; and
- requires delivery of the code/amendments and written acknowledgments.

An **access person** is a supervised person with access to nonpublic client transaction or reportable-fund holdings information, or who makes or accesses nonpublic recommendations. When advice is the firm's primary business, directors, officers, and partners are presumed access persons.

#### Holdings and transaction reports

| Report | Deadline | Data currency/content |
|---|---|---|
| Initial holdings | Within **10 days** after becoming an access person | Information current no more than **45 days** before status began; reportable securities and relevant brokerage/bank accounts. |
| Annual holdings | At least once in each 12-month period | Information no more than **45 days** old. |
| Quarterly transactions | No later than **30 days after quarter-end** | All reportable-security transactions during the quarter, with security, date, type, amount, price, intermediary, and submission date. |

Exceptions include accounts over which the person has no direct or indirect influence/control, automatic investment plans, and timely duplicate confirmations/statements. “Reportable security” excludes direct U.S. government obligations, specified bank and high-quality short-term instruments, money-market funds, most unaffiliated open-end funds, and qualifying UITs invested solely in them. Municipal securities, ETFs, closed-end funds, and individual stocks generally are not excluded merely because they seem routine.

Access persons need preapproval before acquiring beneficial ownership in an **IPO or limited offering**. A one-person adviser need not report to or approve itself but must preserve the records. Rule 204-2 generally requires code, violation, acknowledgment, access-person, report, and approval records for five years under its detailed timing.

Firms often go further with duplicate statements, restricted lists, blackout periods, minimum holding periods, gifts/entertainment logs, and preclearance. These policy requirements are enforceable internally even when not universal legal minima.

**Scenario 18 — spouse's account.** An access person's spouse buys a reportable stock in a jointly supported household account. Beneficial-ownership rules can attribute the interest to the access person; omitting it because the account title names only the spouse is unsafe.

### 17. Due diligence and reasonable basis

Due diligence is proportional to the recommendation and cannot be outsourced blindly. A reasonable process may include:

- understanding issuer business, management, capitalization, financials, legal/regulatory risks, offering terms, use of proceeds, conflicts, liquidity, custody, valuation, and exit constraints;
- testing source reliability and reconciling inconsistencies;
- understanding product structure, embedded leverage, fees, tax consequences, counterparties, and scenario behavior;
- investigating managers, service providers, auditors, administrators, custodians, disciplinary histories, and references;
- documenting assumptions and limitations; and
- monitoring material changes when the mandate includes ongoing advice.

A glossy deck, third-party rating, sponsor reputation, auditor name, or regulator filing is not a substitute for inquiry. An adviser need not guarantee every fact, but cannot ignore red flags or repeat claims without a reasonable basis. For complex or illiquid products, inability to understand the product is a reason not to recommend it.

### 18. Excessive trading and churning

**Churning** is excessive trading in a controlled account undertaken to generate compensation or other benefit. Analysis is contextual: turnover, cost-to-equity ratio, transaction frequency, in-and-out trading, client's objectives, account size, strategy, and the representative's control all matter. No single numerical threshold proves every case.

A fee-based account can be abused by “reverse churning”—placing a low-activity client in an ongoing asset-based fee arrangement without meaningful services. A commission account can be inappropriate for an active advisory program; a wrap account can be too expensive for buy-and-hold assets. Fiduciary duty requires ongoing review of account type and total cost, not merely individual trades.

### 19. Political contributions beyond the rule minimum

Rule 206(4)-5 and MSRB G-37 set regulatory floors. Firms may impose lower preclearance limits, prohibit contributions, cover candidates beyond the minimum, or require household reporting to avoid indirect evasion. False contribution reports can be a separate books, supervision, Form ADV, and ethics violation. Federal-election contributions can matter when the candidate also holds a qualifying state or local office.

### 20. Vulnerable adults and financial exploitation

NASAA's **Model Act to Protect Vulnerable Adults from Financial Exploitation** is a template, not automatic law. It generally protects an “eligible adult,” commonly a person age **65 or older** or an adult protected under the adopting jurisdiction's adult-protective-services law. Definitions and ages vary.

The model framework generally:

- requires a qualified person of a BD or IA who reasonably believes exploitation may have occurred, is occurring, was attempted, or will be attempted to notify the state securities regulator and adult protective services;
- permits notification to previously designated third parties unless the third party is suspected;
- permits a temporary delay of a disbursement when the statutory reasonable-belief and reporting conditions are met;
- requires internal review, regulator/APS notice, record retention, and access; and
- provides qualified immunity for good-faith actions complying with the statute.

Under the model text, a firm delaying a disbursement generally gives written notice to authorized account parties other than a suspected exploiter and notifies adult protective services and the state regulator within **two business days**. It continues its review and reports results generally within **seven business days**. The ordinary delay ends no later than **15 business days**, unless an agency requests an extension up to **25 business days** or a court/other state authority orders otherwise. Enacted states may change the age threshold, transaction scope, notice recipients, deadlines, immunity, and whether reporting or holds are mandatory or permissive.

A delay is protective, temporary, and procedural—not confiscation. It does not authorize an IAR to ignore a competent client's wishes merely because the IAR dislikes them. Red flags include sudden wire requests, new “friends,” isolation, fear, unusual secrecy, changed beneficiaries, unexplained powers of attorney, caregiver coercion, unpaid bills despite resources, and abrupt liquidation of prudent holdings.

The response should prioritize safety without ageism: pause if authorized, verify through trusted channels, escalate internally, report to required agencies, preserve records, avoid alerting a suspected exploiter, and continue appropriate client communication.

**Scenario 19 — caregiver wire.** An 82-year-old client, prompted by a new caregiver, requests an unusual overseas wire and sounds frightened during call-back. The representative should not diagnose incapacity or secretly move the funds. The firm should invoke its exploitation protocol, make required/authorized reports, consider a lawful temporary delay, contact an unimplicated trusted person if permitted, and document the basis.

### 21. Regulation S-P: privacy, safeguards, incident response

Regulation S-P governs privacy and safeguarding duties for covered SEC-regulated financial institutions, including **SEC-registered investment advisers**. A purely state-registered adviser is not covered merely by being an IA, although state privacy, breach, cybersecurity, and NASAA-derived rules may apply. Regulation S-P addresses privacy notices, limits on disclosure of nonpublic personal information, safeguards for customer information, disposal, and—after the 2024 amendments—incident response and notification.

The amendments in **89 Fed. Reg. 47,688**, effective August 2, 2024, set compliance for larger entities on **December 3, 2025** and smaller entities on **June 3, 2026**. For registered advisers, the release generally uses $1.5 billion RAUM to divide larger from smaller entities. By August 11, 2026, both compliance dates have elapsed.

Covered institutions must maintain written policies and procedures reasonably designed to:

- protect against anticipated threats or hazards and unauthorized access/use;
- detect, respond to, and recover from unauthorized access to or use of customer information;
- assess the nature and scope of an incident and identify affected information/systems;
- contain and control incidents; and
- notify affected individuals when the rule's conditions are met.

Customer notice must be given as soon as practicable, generally no later than **30 days after awareness** that unauthorized access to or use of customer information occurred or is reasonably likely, subject to the rule's risk-of-harm determination, content, Attorney General delay, and other provisions. Policies for service providers must be reasonably designed to require the provider to notify the institution as soon as possible, but no later than **72 hours after becoming aware** of a qualifying breach, so the institution can carry out its own response. The 72-hour provider notice is not a substitute for the institution's customer-notice analysis.

The amendments also broaden safeguards and secure-disposal coverage, require incident and vendor-contract documentation, and integrate Rule 204-2 records for advisers. A firm cannot delegate legal responsibility to a cloud provider.

Privacy notices and opt-out rights depend on the relationship, information-sharing practices, exceptions, and annual-notice rules. Regulation S-P is distinct from Regulation S-ID identity-theft red flags, state breach laws, and general cybersecurity obligations; multiple regimes can apply to one incident.

### 22. Cybersecurity controls

A reasonable program aligns with the firm's information, systems, access, vendors, clients, and threat profile. Core controls include:

- governance, inventory, classification, and risk assessment;
- multifactor authentication, least privilege, privileged-access management, encryption, and secure configuration;
- patching, endpoint protection, logging, monitoring, and tested backups;
- secure software development and change control;
- employee screening, phishing training, and payment-verification procedures;
- vendor diligence, contractual security/notice terms, monitoring, and exit plans;
- incident response, tabletop exercises, forensics, legal/regulatory escalation, and communication plans; and
- secure retention and destruction consistent with books-and-records duties.

Cyber claims in marketing or disclosures must be accurate. Calling controls “military-grade,” “unhackable,” or fully compliant without support can itself be misleading. A material cyber incident or known risk can require disclosure beyond technical remediation.

### 23. Business continuity and succession planning

NASAA Model Rule 203(a)-1 requires state advisers in adopting jurisdictions to establish, implement, and maintain written business-continuity and succession plans based on the business model. A sound plan addresses at least:

- protection, backup, and recovery of books and records;
- alternate communications with clients, employees, vendors, regulators, and counterparties;
- relocation or remote operations after loss of office or systems;
- assignment of duties during disruption or death/incapacity of key personnel; and
- minimizing service interruption and facilitating an orderly transition or termination.

**Disaster recovery** restores technology, data, facilities, and operations. **Business continuity** preserves critical client service through disruption. **Succession planning** addresses ownership, authority, client consent, key-person death/disability, books, fees, custody, and transfer or wind-down. They overlap but are not synonyms.

Test the plan: contact trees, alternate decision makers, custodian access, payroll, cyber recovery, vendor dependencies, regulatory filing, and client notification. Store copies so the same event cannot destroy both production and backup. Review after material business changes and exercises.

Advisory contracts requiring consent to assignment complicate succession. A purchase agreement cannot silently transfer client contracts contrary to IAA §205 or state law. The successor must also have effective registrations, notices, IAR affiliations, custody arrangements, privacy authority, and disclosures.

**Scenario 20 — founder incapacity.** A sole adviser is hospitalized, holds all system credentials, and has no authorized successor. A generic data backup does not solve trading, billing, client communication, custody, registration, or contract-assignment issues. A compliant succession plan identifies temporary authority and an orderly consent-based transition or wind-down before the event.

### 24. Integrated conflict checklist

Before recommending, trading, advertising, billing, or accepting authority, ask:

1. **Capacity:** IA/IAR, BD/agent, principal, broker, trustee, solicitor, or some combination?
2. **Client:** What are the client's objectives, constraints, sophistication, and vulnerabilities?
3. **Compensation:** Who pays whom, how much, when, and what behavior does payment reward?
4. **Affiliations:** Is the product, custodian, BD, solicitor, rating provider, or counterparty related?
5. **Authority:** Advice only, time/price discretion, full discretion, fee deduction, transfer power, custody, voting, or trustee power?
6. **Information:** What must be investigated, disclosed, protected, updated, or reported?
7. **Execution/allocation:** Best execution, fair allocation, principal/cross status, soft dollars, and personal-trading priority?
8. **Registration:** Are firm, individual, security, and transaction requirements each satisfied?
9. **Records/supervision:** Can the firm prove its reasonable basis, consent, review, and retention?
10. **Remedy:** If facts change or a red flag appears, should the firm disclose, recuse, reject, pause, report, correct, refund, or terminate?

---

## Master decision tables and exam traps

### Who regulates whom?

| Fact pattern | Firm registration | Individual registration | State power retained |
|---|---|---|---|
| IA with $40m RAUM; home state requires and examines | Ordinarily state IA | IAR under state functional definition | Full state registration, examination, antifraud, discipline |
| IA with $115m RAUM, no exemption | SEC IA | States may register qualifying IARs with in-state place of business | Notice/fee where permitted; antifraud; IAR registration |
| SEC IA falls to $95m RAUM | May remain SEC-registered in buffer | Same IAR analysis | Same retained powers |
| SEC IA falls below $90m and no exception | Transition to state registration under Rule 203A-1 timing | State IAR registration | State becomes firm regulator after transition |
| Sole private-fund adviser with $100m U.S. private-fund AUM | May rely on §203(m) ERA exemption if all conditions met | State law controls individual status | Federal reporting/antifraud plus state private-fund/adviser rules |
| Out-of-state state IA, no office, four ordinary clients in State X | USA model de minimis may exempt State X registration | Similar IAR analysis | Antifraud and jurisdiction remain; actual state rule may differ |
| IAR has one client but advertised office in State X | Place of business defeats de minimis route | State X registration ordinarily required if IAR definition met | Registration and antifraud |

### Security route versus transaction route

| Fact | Classification | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| U.S. Treasury | Exempt security under USA model | Security registration not required; persons and fraud still regulated |
| Sale to a bank | Exempt transaction | Only that sale is exempt; security does not become permanently exempt |
| NYSE-listed common stock | Federal covered security | State registration preempted; state antifraud remains |
| Rule 506 offering | Federal covered transaction/security for the qualifying offer/sale | State notice, consent, and fee may remain; antifraud remains |
| Rule 504 offering | Federal registration exemption, not categorically §18-covered | Separate state registration/exemption needed |
| Registered security sold by unregistered agent | Security route satisfied, person route not | Sale can violate agent-registration law |

### High-frequency traps

1. **Passing is not licensing.** Examination, registration, affiliation, and authorization are separate.
2. **Federal covered adviser means SEC-registered.** An ERA is federally exempt, not a federal covered adviser.
3. **Notice filing is not state IA registration.** It does not imply approval.
4. **Five is not six.** USA de minimis allows no more than five noninstitutional clients with no place of business; Rule 203A-3 begins at more than five and also requires more than 10%.
5. **$100m is a buffer edge, not a universal command.** $100m–<$110m permits SEC registration for a covered mid-sized adviser; below $90m triggers withdrawal analysis for an SEC registrant.
6. **Exempt is not antifraud-free.** Neither exempt security nor exempt transaction shields deception.
7. **Registration of the security does not register the seller.** Analyze persons independently.
8. **State model is not every state's law.** State brochure, custody, net worth, CE, private-fund, vulnerable-adult, and remedy rules vary.
9. **Federal and state brochure timing differ.** SEC: before or at contracting. NASAA model: 48 hours before or five-business-day penalty-free termination.
10. **Discretion is not automatically custody.** Withdrawal/possession authority is the custody question.
11. **Fee deduction is custody.** It may avoid surprise exam under a federal exception; it does not cease being custody.
12. **A testimonial is not federally banned per se.** The current SEC Marketing Rule conditionally permits it; an adopted state model may differ.
13. **Gross performance needs net performance.** Equal prominence and comparable periods/methodology matter.
14. **Performance-fee numbers changed.** For new Rule 205-3 contracts from June 29, 2026: $1.4m under management or net worth over $2.7m, subject to the full rule.
15. **Pay-to-play limits are regime-specific.** SEC adviser rule: $350/$150; MSRB G-37: different $250 concept.
16. **The Administrator does not imprison.** Prosecutors charge and courts sentence.
17. **Cancellation is not discipline.** It removes deadwood; revocation is a sanction.
18. **Withdrawal does not erase jurisdiction.** Proceedings may continue or be instituted under statutory timing.
19. **Form D is a notice, not the exemption.** Substantive Regulation D conditions control.
20. **AML date matters.** The adviser AML final rule was postponed to January 1, 2028; BD AML duties already apply.

### Terminology note — “registration by notification”

Some older statutes and prep materials call the seasoned-issuer route **registration by notification**. In the NASAA-amended 1956 USA tested by the September 2023 guide, §302 is titled **registration by filing**. Treat “notification” as the historical label when a question uses it; do not confuse it with a federal covered security's **notice filing**, which is a state filing preserved despite federal preemption.

---

## Practice Examination — 30 Original Questions

These questions are original teaching questions, not actual or recalled NASAA examination content. Choose the best answer under the law identified in each question. Unless a question specifies an actual jurisdiction, “Uniform Securities Act” means the tested 1956 USA as amended by NASAA, and “NASAA model rule” means the model rather than automatically enacted law.

### Questions 1–15

**1.** Apex Advice LLC is registered with the SEC and has $96 million of regulatory assets under management after filing its annual updating amendment. It has no other basis for SEC registration. Which statement best describes its status under Rule 203A-1?

A. It must withdraw immediately because it is below $100 million.
B. It may remain SEC-registered because required withdrawal generally begins below $90 million.
C. It must register in every state and terminate SEC registration on the amendment date.
D. It automatically becomes an exempt reporting adviser.

**2.** A supervised person of an SEC-registered adviser has eight non-excepted natural-person clients and 92 institutional clients. The person regularly meets those eight individuals. Under Rule 203A-3, which result is best?

A. The person is an IAR because more than five natural-person clients is sufficient by itself.
B. The person is not an IAR because the non-excepted natural-person clients are not more than 10% of all clients.
C. The person is an IAR because institutional clients are excluded from the denominator.
D. The person is exempt only if all eight natural persons are accredited investors.

**3.** An adviser has no place of business in State M. During the preceding 12 months it advised four ordinary resident individuals and three banks in State M. Under the USA model's adviser de minimis provision, which statement is most accurate?

A. All seven clients count, so registration is required.
B. Only the four noninstitutional clients count, so the model exemption may apply.
C. Banks count only when they have less than $1 million in assets.
D. The exemption applies even if the adviser opens an advertised office in State M.

**4.** A private-fund manager advises only private funds and manages $70 million of private-fund assets in the United States. It properly relies on Advisers Act §203(m). Which label is most accurate?

A. Federal covered adviser
B. State-registered adviser in every state by federal command
C. Exempt reporting adviser, subject to specified Form ADV reporting and other applicable law
D. Person excluded from the federal definition of investment adviser

**5.** A financial columnist publishes the same regularly scheduled, nonpersonalized market newsletter to 20,000 subscribers. Subscription fees are the only compensation. Which fact most strongly supports the publisher exclusion from the investment-adviser definition?

A. The newsletter discusses only securities with rising prices.
B. The newsletter has general and regular circulation and is not tailored to subscriber circumstances.
C. The columnist holds a journalism degree.
D. The newsletter includes a disclaimer that all advice is guaranteed.

**6.** A state-registered adviser gives a prospective client its brochure at contract signing. The contract permits termination without penalty for five business days. Under NASAA Model Rule 203(b)-1, which statement is best?

A. The timing can satisfy the model because of the penalty-free five-business-day termination right.
B. Delivery must always occur 48 hours after signing.
C. The rule requires delivery only after the client funds the account.
D. The timing violates the model because only SEC advisers may deliver at signing.

**7.** An individual works for an issuer and receives transaction-based compensation to solicit the public for a nonexempt offering. The issuer calls the individual a “community ambassador.” Under the USA, the strongest conclusion is:

A. Issuer employees can never be agents.
B. The title controls, so the individual is not an agent.
C. The solicitation function and compensation raise agent-registration requirements; no blanket issuer-employee exclusion applies.
D. The individual is automatically a broker-dealer rather than an agent.

**8.** A registered agent recommends a security whose state registration statement is effective. The agent is not registered in the customer's state and has no exemption. Which is correct?

A. Effective security registration cures the agent's deficiency.
B. The sale may violate person-registration law even though the security is registered.
C. Only the issuer can be liable.
D. The agent is covered by the security's registration for 30 days.

**9.** An issuer conducts a Rule 506(c) offering through public online advertising. One purchaser is not accredited, although the issuer reasonably verified all other purchasers. Which statement is best?

A. The offering satisfies Rule 506(c) because fewer than 35 purchasers are nonaccredited.
B. Rule 506(b) automatically replaces Rule 506(c).
C. Rule 506(c) is unavailable because every purchaser must be accredited; the public solicitation also prevents simple reliance on 506(b).
D. Form D converts the purchaser into an accredited investor.

**10.** Under the USA, which is the clearest example of an **exempt transaction**, rather than an exempt security?

A. A U.S. Treasury bond
B. A qualifying bank-issued security
C. A sale of a nonexempt security to a bank
D. A qualifying Canadian government bond

**11.** A Rule 506 issuer has complied with federal exemption conditions. State Q demands substantive registration and merits review before any in-state Rule 506 sale. Which is most accurate under Securities Act §18?

A. State Q may require merits review because private offerings are never covered securities.
B. Federal law preempts substantive state registration, but State Q may retain permitted notice, fee, consent, and antifraud authority.
C. State Q loses all jurisdiction, including fraud investigations.
D. Preemption applies only if the security later lists on an exchange.

**12.** A startup directs offers to 10 ordinary residents of a state in 12 months, reasonably believes buyers purchase for investment, and pays no solicitation compensation. Under USA §402(b)(9), which statement is best?

A. The model limited-offeree exemption may apply, subject to Administrator variation or conditions.
B. The issuer may make unlimited additional offers as long as only 10 people buy.
C. The transaction is exempt only if every offeree is accredited.
D. The security becomes permanently exempt for all future resales.

**13.** The Administrator issues a subpoena in a lawful investigation. The witness refuses to produce records. Under the USA model, what is the usual next enforcement step?

A. The Administrator imposes imprisonment directly.
B. The Administrator applies to the appropriate court for an order compelling compliance.
C. The registration of every employer of the witness is automatically revoked.
D. The SEC must enforce every state subpoena.

**14.** Which action is best characterized as **cancellation**, rather than revocation?

A. Removing the registration of a firm that no longer exists
B. Punishing a firm for willful securities fraud
C. Suspending an agent for an undisclosed felony
D. Barring an IAR for dishonest practices

**15.** A security seller gives a buyer a material false statement. The security was exempt from state registration under USA §402(a). Which statement is most accurate?

A. The security exemption also exempts the misstatement from antifraud liability.
B. Antifraud and potential civil liability can apply despite the registration exemption.
C. Liability exists only if the Administrator first revokes the exemption retroactively.
D. Only federal law can reach an exempt security.

### Questions 16–30

**16.** An SEC-registered adviser tells a prospective client, “The SEC approved our registration, so it has confirmed that our strategy is safe.” Which is best?

A. Permitted if the adviser has never had a loss
B. Permitted if the statement appears only in Form ADV
C. Misleading and an unlawful implication of regulatory approval
D. Permitted for institutional prospects but not retail prospects

**17.** A new client signs a federal advisory contract on July 10, 2026. The contract pays the adviser 20% of appreciation. The client is not a qualified purchaser or knowledgeable employee and has $1.2 million with the adviser and $3 million net worth, of which $900,000 is equity in the primary residence. Ignoring other assets and liabilities, which is best under current Rule 205-3?

A. The client qualifies because gross net worth exceeds $2.7 million.
B. The client qualifies because more than $1.1 million is managed.
C. The client does not qualify on these facts because the managed amount is below $1.4 million and primary-residence equity is excluded, leaving $2.1 million.
D. Every accredited investor is automatically a qualified client.

**18.** A covered associate of an SEC adviser contributes $300 per election to a state official for whom the associate is entitled to vote. The official can select the adviser for a state pension. Assuming no indirect scheme, what is the best result under Rule 206(4)-5?

A. The contribution necessarily causes a two-year compensation ban because it exceeds $150.
B. It falls within the $350 de minimis exception for an official for whom the natural-person associate may vote.
C. It is governed only by MSRB Rule G-37's $250 amount.
D. It is exempt only if returned within 72 hours.

**19.** An adviser directs client commissions to obtain issuer research that assists investment decisions and a vacation package for the adviser. Which is best under Exchange Act §28(e)?

A. Both are protected because the same BD supplied them.
B. Neither can ever be paid with commissions.
C. Eligible research may fit the safe harbor after the required good-faith analysis, but the vacation is not eligible and must not be paid with client commissions.
D. Disclosure alone makes the vacation eligible.

**20.** An adviser has authority only to deduct the disclosed advisory fee from a client account held at an unaffiliated qualified custodian. Under federal Rule 206(4)-2, which is best?

A. The adviser has no custody because the custodian is unaffiliated.
B. Fee-deduction authority is custody, but custody solely from fee deduction can qualify for an exception from the surprise examination.
C. The adviser has discretion but never custody.
D. The adviser must hold the client's paper certificates itself.

**21.** A client tells an IAR to buy 500 shares of XYZ today and allows the IAR to choose the time and price. Which is best under common NASAA concepts?

A. This limited time-and-price authority is ordinarily not full discretion for that day.
B. The IAR has custody.
C. The IAR may instead buy any security in any amount.
D. The instruction remains open indefinitely without written authority.

**22.** An adviser proposes an agency cross transaction. The adviser will act as broker for both Client A and the seller and has recommended the trade to both parties. Which is best under the agency-cross safe-harbor concepts?

A. Prospective blanket consent permits every cross, including recommendations to both sides.
B. The conditional agency-cross rule does not permit reliance where the adviser recommends the transaction to both buyer and seller.
C. The transaction is a principal trade because the adviser is broker for both sides.
D. An annual report alone is enough; no confirmation is needed.

**23.** An access person becomes subject to an SEC adviser's Code of Ethics on March 1. By when is the initial holdings report generally due, and how current must the information be?

A. Within 10 days; current as of no more than 45 days before becoming an access person
B. Within 30 days after quarter-end; current as of 90 days earlier
C. Within 120 days; current as of fiscal year-end
D. Immediately; current that same hour

**24.** An agent recommends a private offering to customers away from the firm, receives no compensation, and gives the firm no notice. Which is best?

A. No compensation means selling-away rules can never apply.
B. Participation in an outside securities transaction can constitute selling away even without compensation and can require prior notice/approval under applicable rules.
C. Private offerings are not securities transactions.
D. Form D is a substitute for firm notice.

**25.** An IAR trades a personal account before entering a large client order the IAR knows is likely to raise the price. This is best described as:

A. Dollar-cost averaging
B. Front-running and a serious fiduciary/personal-trading violation
C. A permissible agency cross
D. Registration by coordination

**26.** A representative repeatedly buys and sells securities in a controlled client account principally to generate commissions, producing turnover and costs inconsistent with the client's conservative objective. This most strongly indicates:

A. Churning or excessive trading
B. Soft-dollar research
C. A qualified-custodian exception
D. A private-fund exemption

**27.** A qualified employee of an adviser reasonably believes an elderly client's new companion is attempting financial exploitation. Under the NASAA vulnerable-adult model, which response is most consistent with the framework?

A. Permanently confiscate the client's assets.
B. Ignore the facts unless a court first declares the client incompetent.
C. Follow internal escalation; make required regulator and adult-protective-services reports; and consider a temporary statutory delay and trusted-contact notice if conditions permit.
D. Post the allegation publicly to warn other firms.

**28.** A service provider to a covered registered adviser becomes aware of a qualifying breach of customer information. Under the amended Regulation S-P service-provider control, policies must be reasonably designed for provider notice to the covered institution:

A. Only after every affected client confirms loss
B. As soon as possible and no later than 72 hours after awareness
C. Within 30 business days
D. At the next annual updating amendment

**29.** After becoming aware of unauthorized access to customer information, a covered institution determines customer notice is required under amended Regulation S-P. Subject to the rule's conditions and exceptions, notice generally must be sent:

A. As soon as practicable, but generally no later than 30 days after awareness
B. Only at fiscal year-end
C. Within 72 hours in every case
D. Only after a criminal conviction

**30.** A sole state adviser keeps daily cloud backups but has no plan for the founder's death, client communication, temporary authority, contract assignment, or orderly wind-down. Which is best under NASAA business-continuity and succession concepts?

A. Backups alone constitute a complete succession plan.
B. A plan is unnecessary because advisory contracts transfer automatically at death.
C. The firm has addressed one disaster-recovery element but not the broader business-continuity and succession obligations.
D. The custodian automatically becomes the successor adviser.

---

## Reasoned Answer Key

**1. B.** Rule 203A-1 creates a buffer. A qualifying mid-sized adviser may register at $100 million but below $110 million, and an existing SEC registrant generally need not withdraw until RAUM is **below $90 million**. At $96 million, Apex can remain federally registered absent another disqualifying fact. It does not become an ERA; that status depends on a registration exemption such as §203(l) or §203(m).

**2. B.** Rule 203A-3 requires both more than five non-excepted natural-person clients and more than 10% of all clients in that category. Eight of 100 is 8%. Institutional clients remain in the denominator. Accredited status is not the relevant exception; the rule refers to “excepted persons” based on qualified-client status.

**3. B.** Under USA §201(c), listed institutional clients are excluded from the five-client count when the adviser has no place of business in the state. Four ordinary clients is no more than five, so the model exemption may apply. An advertised office would defeat the no-place-of-business prerequisite. Actual state adoption can vary.

**4. C.** A manager relying on §203(m) is an **exempt reporting adviser**, not an SEC-registered federal covered adviser and not excluded from the IA definition. Rule 204-4 requires Form ADV reporting. State private-fund-adviser and IAR requirements need separate analysis.

**5. B.** The publisher exclusion centers on a bona fide publication of general and regular circulation rather than personalized advice. A degree is irrelevant. A performance guarantee is a red flag, not a qualification. Selective touting can affect antifraud analysis but does not answer the central exclusion fact.

**6. A.** NASAA Model Rule 203(b)-1 allows initial brochure delivery at least 48 hours before the contract **or** at contract execution when the client may terminate without penalty within five business days. Do not import that model timing into every state or confuse it with the federal rule.

**7. C.** Agent status is functional. Representing an issuer in soliciting securities sales for transaction-based compensation falls within the basic definition unless a specific issuer-agent exclusion applies. The “ambassador” title has no legal force. Depending on the activity, federal broker issues can also arise.

**8. B.** Person registration and security registration are independent. An effective registration statement authorizes the security's offering subject to its terms; it does not license the individual selling it. The unregistered agent can violate USA §201.

**9. C.** Rule 506(c) permits general solicitation only when every purchaser is accredited and the issuer takes reasonable verification steps. Its one nonaccredited purchaser defeats that route. Rule 506(b) permits up to 35 sophisticated nonaccredited purchasers but prohibits general solicitation, so it is not an automatic fallback.

**10. C.** The sale to a bank is transaction-specific under USA §402(b). U.S. Treasury, qualifying bank-issued, and qualifying Canadian government securities are exempt securities under §402(a). A later sale of the nonexempt security needs its own transaction or registration route.

**11. B.** Rule 506 transactions are covered under Securities Act §18. State substantive registration and merits review are preempted, but permitted notice filings, fees, consent to service, and state antifraud enforcement survive.

**12. A.** The tested USA model permits offers to no more than 10 noninstitutional persons in the state during 12 months, with buyer investment intent and no solicitation remuneration, subject to Administrator modification. The rule counts offerees, not merely purchasers, and it exempts the transaction rather than permanently changing the security.

**13. B.** The Administrator can subpoena, but a court enforces noncompliance. The court may order testimony or production and punish disobedience as contempt. The Administrator does not summarily imprison the witness.

**14. A.** Cancellation addresses deadwood: a person no longer in existence or business, adjudicated incompetent, under specified protective control, or unlocatable. Revocation, suspension, and bars are disciplinary remedies based on misconduct or other statutory grounds.

**15. B.** USA exemptions remove registration burdens, not antifraud liability. A materially false sale can support enforcement and civil liability even when the security is exempt. Exemption revocation is not a prerequisite to prosecuting fraud.

**16. C.** Registration does not mean the regulator verified disclosures, approved the registrant, endorsed the strategy, or found it safe. USA §405 expressly makes inconsistent representations unlawful; federal antifraud principles reach the same misleading implication.

**17. C.** For a new contract after June 29, 2026, the current dollar routes are at least $1.4 million managed by the adviser or net worth over $2.7 million. The client's managed amount is only $1.2 million. Primary-residence equity is excluded, leaving $2.1 million under the simplified facts. Accredited investor and qualified client are distinct concepts.

**18. B.** A natural-person covered associate may contribute up to $350 per official per election when entitled to vote for that official without triggering Rule 206(4)-5's two-year compensation ban. The $150 figure applies when not entitled to vote. MSRB G-37 is a separate regime.

**19. C.** Eligible research can fit §28(e) if the manager makes the required good-faith reasonableness determination and satisfies fiduciary/best-execution duties. A vacation is neither eligible research nor brokerage. Disclosure does not turn ineligible overhead or personal benefits into safe-harbor services.

**20. B.** Authority to deduct advisory fees is authority to withdraw client funds and therefore custody. Rule 206(4)-2(b)(3) can remove the surprise-examination requirement when custody arises solely from fee deduction, but the adviser still applies the remaining custody framework and any state safeguards.

**21. A.** When the client specifies security, action, and amount, limited authority to choose time and price ordinarily is not full discretion for that business day. It does not authorize a different security or amount and does not itself confer withdrawal authority or custody.

**22. B.** The agency-cross safe harbor prohibits reliance when the adviser or affiliate recommended the transaction to both seller and buyer. The adviser is acting as broker, not as principal owner. Prospective consent, confirmations, annual reporting, revocability, best execution, and fiduciary duties also matter.

**23. A.** Initial holdings reports are due no later than 10 days after a person becomes an access person, with information current no more than 45 days before that date. Annual holdings and quarterly transactions have separate deadlines.

**24. B.** Selling away concerns participation in an outside securities transaction, and applicable rules can require notice even without compensation. Compensation often increases the problem and approval requirements, but zero compensation is not a universal escape. Form D relates to offering notice, not firm supervision.

**25. B.** Personal trading ahead of a known client order to profit from expected price impact is front-running. It violates client priority, fiduciary loyalty, personal-trading controls, and potentially antifraud law. It is not rescued by later placing the client order.

**26. A.** Excessive, controlled trading designed to generate commissions is churning. Turnover, cost-to-equity, objectives, account control, and total circumstances provide evidence. A fee account can present reverse-churning issues, but these facts describe classic commission churning.

**27. C.** The NASAA model uses reasonable belief and reporting/protective processes, not a prerequisite judicial incompetency finding. It supports regulator and adult-protective-services reports, permitted trusted-person notice, temporary delays on conditions, internal review, records, and good-faith immunity. It does not authorize confiscation or public disclosure.

**28. B.** Amended Regulation S-P requires covered-institution policies reasonably designed so a service provider gives notice as soon as possible, and no later than 72 hours after becoming aware of a qualifying breach. This provider-to-institution clock is distinct from customer notice.

**29. A.** When notice is required, the rule generally requires it as soon as practicable and no later than 30 days after awareness, subject to its risk, content, law-enforcement-delay, and other provisions. The 72-hour concept concerns service-provider notice to the institution.

**30. C.** Backups are one disaster-recovery control. Business continuity also covers people, communication, facilities, vendors, authority, and sustained service; succession addresses incapacity/death, ownership, registrations, client consent, contract assignment, and wind-down. Advisory contracts do not automatically transfer free of §205 and state requirements.

## Compact Legal Glossary

**Accredited investor.** A Regulation D investor category under Rule 501(a). It concerns offering eligibility and is not synonymous with a Rule 205-3 qualified client or ICA qualified purchaser.

**Administrator.** The state securities official or agency designated by state law. Powers come from statute and adopted rules; NASAA itself is not a multistate super-regulator.

**Agent.** An individual, other than a BD, representing a BD or issuer in effecting or attempting securities transactions. An **issuer agent** can fall outside the definition only for the transactions and conditions specified by USA §401(b) or actual state law.

**Annual updating amendment.** The Form ADV amendment generally due within 90 days after an adviser's fiscal year-end under Rule 204-1. It updates Parts 1 and 2 and triggers eligibility, brochure, state notice, fee, and transition analysis. Interim amendments may be required sooner.

**Associated person.** A broad federal relationship category for personnel and control persons of a regulated firm. Not every associated person performs functions requiring agent or IAR registration.

**Brochure.** Form ADV Part 2A narrative disclosure. A brochure supplement is Part 2B information about advisory personnel. Delivery does not eliminate other fiduciary disclosure.

**Cancellation.** Nondisciplinary administrative removal of a registration/application for such facts as nonexistence, cessation, incompetency, protective control, or inability to locate the person. Contrast revocation.

**Custody.** Holding client funds/securities or having authority to obtain possession, including through certain related persons or legal capacities. Custody is broader than physical possession.

**Dealer.** A person in the regular business of buying and selling securities for its own account. An ordinary investor is not a dealer solely because the investor trades.

**Discretion.** Authority to decide securities, amount, or action without contemporaneous client approval. Time-and-price authority after the client specifies security and amount is treated differently under common rules.

**Exempt reporting adviser.** An adviser relying on IAA §203(l) or §203(m) that is exempt from SEC registration but must file specified Form ADV reports. It is not an SEC-registered federal covered adviser.

**Federal covered adviser.** Under the tested USA, a person registered with the SEC under IAA §203. States generally cannot register the firm as an IA but retain specified notice, fee, antifraud, and IAR authority.

**Federal covered security.** A security or qualifying offer/sale covered by Securities Act §18. State substantive registration is preempted; authorized notices/fees and antifraud remain.

**Finder.** A descriptive business label, not a statutory safe harbor. Solicitation, negotiation, transaction-based compensation, regularity, and control can produce broker/agent status.

**Form U4.** Uniform application and continuing disclosure form for securities-industry individuals. Accuracy and timely amendments continue after initial registration.

**Guaranteed.** Under the USA, guaranteed as to principal, interest, or dividends. Government or third-party guarantees must be accurately delimited; adviser performance guarantees remain improper.

**Investment adviser.** A compensated person in the business of securities advice or analyses, unless a definitional exclusion applies. An exemption from registration does not remove IA status.

**Investment adviser representative.** An individual performing specified advisory, solicitation, portfolio, or supervisory functions. Rule 203A-3 defines which supervised persons of an SEC adviser are IARs for state allocation.

**Issuer.** A person that issues or proposes to issue a security, with USA special rules for certain trusts and oil/gas/mining interests.

**Market maker.** A dealer regularly holding itself out as willing to buy and sell a security for its own account on a regular or continuous basis.

**Notice filing.** A permitted state filing by a federal covered adviser or covered-security issuer. It is not substantive state registration or regulator endorsement.

**Order.** Agency action directed to one or more particular persons or matters. Contrast a rule of general applicability.

**Performance-based fee.** Compensation based on capital gains or appreciation. It begins as prohibited under USA §102/IAA §205 unless a valid exemption, such as Rule 205-3 qualified-client treatment, applies.

**Place of business.** For an IAR under Rule 203A-3, an office where the IAR regularly provides/solicits/communicates advisory services and any location held out publicly for them.

**Private fund.** An issuer that would be an investment company but for ICA §3(c)(1) or §3(c)(7). A **private fund adviser** needs its own registration or exemption analysis; the fund's offering exemption does not decide adviser status.

**Qualified client.** Rule 205-3 category relevant to performance-fee contracts and Rule 203A-3's excepted-person count. From June 29, 2026, current dollar routes include $1.4 million managed or net worth over $2.7 million, subject to the full rule.

**Qualified custodian.** A qualifying bank/savings institution, registered BD, registered FCM for permitted assets, or qualifying foreign financial institution under Rule 206(4)-2.

**Qualified purchaser.** A higher ICA sophistication/ownership category relevant to §3(c)(7) funds and one Rule 205-3 qualified-client route. It is not identical to accredited investor.

**Registration by coordination.** USA §303 state registration coordinated with a Securities Act registration statement.

**Registration by filing/notification.** USA §302 streamlined route for qualifying seasoned issuers. “Notification” is the historical name, not a federal-covered notice filing.

**Registration by qualification.** USA §304 comprehensive state route potentially available to any security.

**Revocation.** Disciplinary termination of an effective registration after statutory ground and process. Contrast cancellation and voluntary withdrawal.

**Security.** A broadly defined instrument or interest including an investment contract. Economic reality, not the label, controls.

**Solicitor.** A person who solicits/refers advisory clients or investors. The activity can cause IAR, endorsement, compensation-disclosure, pay-to-play, or broker issues depending on facts.

**Summary order.** Temporary administrative action permitted without ordinary advance process, followed by prompt notice and an opportunity for the statutorily required hearing.

**Supervised person.** An adviser's partner, officer, director, comparable person, employee, or another person providing advice on its behalf subject to supervision/control. Not every supervised person is an IAR or access person.

**Underwriter.** A person participating in a distribution as defined by Securities Act §2(a)(11), including purchasing from an issuer with a view to distribution.

**Withdrawal.** A registrant's exit filing. It does not erase prior violations or necessarily stop a pending or timely later proceeding.

---

This is the official source for Domain 4's eight groups and cited law. It warns that exam passage alone does not confer authority to transact business.

Reg. 47,688](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/06/03/2024-11116/regulation-s-p-privacy-of-consumer-financial-information-and-safeguarding-customer-information), effective August 2, 2024. This is the primary source for incident response, the general 30-day customer-notice deadline, 72-hour service-provider notice control, safeguards, disposal, and record duties.

Because URLs and implementation can change, confirm the current FinCEN rule page before operational reliance.
