48. **C.** Soliciting retail investors for transaction-based compensation strongly raises state agent and federal broker-registration issues; an issuer employee exclusion is conditional, not automatic. A and B invent label-based exclusions, and D improperly combines person registration with security registration.

49. **D.** An exempt security remains exempt while its security-level exemption applies, whereas an exempt transaction protects only that transaction and later sales require fresh analysis. A wrongly makes a transaction exemption permanent, B forgets that antifraud law survives, and C confuses security status with seller registration.

50. **D.** Rule 506(c) permits general solicitation, but every purchaser must be accredited and the issuer must take reasonable verification steps. Rule 506(b) in B bars general solicitation; A is unrelated; C is a state transaction concept, not the described Regulation D route.

51. **C.** Securities Act Section 18 preempts state registration or merits review of Rule 506 offerings but preserves state antifraud enforcement and permits specified notice filings and fees. A and B describe preempted substantive regulation, while D falsely treats filing as approval.

52. **B.** The tested state model private-transaction exemption is limited to offers directed to no more than 10 noninstitutional persons in the state in 12 months, with additional conditions. A counts the wrong event, C ignores the numerical condition, and D conflates a federal Rule 506(b) concept with state law.

53. **A.** Effectiveness of registration means regulatory filing requirements were met; it is not an endorsement, approval, or assurance of competence. Accuracy of Form U4 (B), federal status (C), or a disclaimer (D) does not cure the false implication.

54. **D.** The NASAA state brochure model permits delivery at signing if the client can terminate without penalty within five business days; otherwise delivery is generally at least 48 hours before contracting. Institutional status, fee method, and discretion in A–C are not the stated alternative.

55. **C.** The statement guarantees against loss and promises a specific return, both improper and misleading. Percentages may be used with compliant context (A), writing does not make a guarantee lawful (B), and favorable history does not assure future performance (D).

56. **B.** Effective June 29, 2026, Rule 205-3 includes a test of at least $1.4 million under management with the adviser or net worth over $2.7 million, applying the rule's special net-worth calculation. A and C are not the current tests, and D improperly counts the primary residence without applying the rule.

57. **A.** Choosing securities and quantities is discretionary trading authority, but authority to trade ordinarily does not itself let the adviser obtain client property and therefore generally is not custody. B collapses distinct concepts, C denies obvious discretion, and D makes physical possession the only form of custody.

58. **C.** Selling from the adviser's own proprietary account is a principal transaction. It creates a direct conflict and calls for applicable written capacity disclosure and transaction-specific client consent before completion. A denies the conflict, B requires the adviser to broker between clients rather than trade as owner, and D wrongly treats an exemption concept as a fiduciary waiver.

59. **B.** Material nonpublic information received through a duty breach creates serious insider-trading and tipping concerns; the proper response is to refrain and escalate under firm controls. Trading in any account (A or C) or selectively tipping clients (D) compounds the problem.

60. **D.** Custody includes authority to obtain possession of client funds or securities. A broad power to direct wires therefore triggers custody analysis and safeguards even before misuse. A is not limited time-and-price authority, B ignores possession authority, and C states the opposite of the qualified-custodian framework.


---

# Glossary

1. **Accredited investor** — A person or entity meeting specified financial, professional, or status criteria under SEC rules for certain unregistered offerings; the status does not remove antifraud protections.
2. **Accrual accounting** — Accounting method recognizing revenue when earned and expenses when incurred, rather than only when cash moves.
3. **Active management** — A strategy using security selection, timing, or allocation decisions in an effort to outperform a benchmark or achieve another objective.
4. **Agent** — Under state securities law, generally an individual representing a broker-dealer or issuer in effecting or attempting to effect securities transactions, subject to exclusions.
5. **Alpha** — Return above or below the amount implied by a selected risk model, commonly CAPM; interpretation depends on consistent benchmark and period measurement.
6. **Alternative minimum tax (AMT)** — A parallel federal tax calculation intended to limit certain preferences; current rules and municipal-bond implications must be checked by tax year.
7. **American depositary receipt (ADR)** — A U.S.-traded receipt representing interests in shares of a foreign issuer, with currency, country, custody, and issuer risks.
8. **Annuity** — An insurance contract designed for accumulation or income, commonly fixed, variable, or indexed, with fees, surrender terms, tax rules, and insurer claims-paying risk.
9. **Asset allocation** — Distribution of a portfolio among asset classes based on objectives, constraints, and capital-market assumptions.
10. **Asset-backed security (ABS)** — A security supported by cash flows from a pool of receivables or other financial assets.
11. **Balance of payments** — A record of a country’s transactions with the rest of the world, including current and financial/capital flows.
12. **Balance sheet** — A statement of assets, liabilities, and owners’ equity at a point in time, reflecting the identity assets = liabilities + equity.
13. **Basis** — The tax investment in property used to calculate gain or loss, adjusted for items specified by law.
14. **Best execution** — An adviser’s duty to seek the most favorable total result reasonably available under the circumstances, not necessarily the lowest stated commission.
15. **Beta** — A measure of an investment’s systematic sensitivity relative to a market benchmark; the market is conventionally assigned beta 1.
16. **Bid-ask spread** — The difference between the highest quoted bid and lowest quoted offer; a component of trading cost and liquidity.
17. **Bond** — A debt instrument under which an issuer promises specified payments, subject to interest-rate, credit, liquidity, call, inflation, and other risks.
18. **Broker-dealer** — A person in the business of effecting securities transactions for others or for its own account, subject to statutory exclusions.
19. **Business cycle** — Recurring expansion, peak, contraction, and trough phases in broad economic activity, without fixed duration.
20. **Call option** — A contract giving the holder the right, but not the obligation, to buy an underlying interest at a strike price before or at expiration under its terms.
21. **Capital asset pricing model (CAPM)** — Model expressing required return as the risk-free rate plus beta times the market risk premium.
22. **Cash accounting** — Accounting method generally recognizing revenue and expense when cash is received or paid.
23. **Closed-end fund** — An investment company with shares that generally trade in the secondary market, potentially at a premium or discount to NAV.
24. **Commercial paper** — Unsecured short-term corporate debt generally issued by financially strong borrowers.
25. **Common stock** — Residual corporate equity ordinarily carrying voting rights and a claim subordinate to creditors and preferred shareholders.
26. **Correlation** — Statistical measure from −1 to +1 describing how two return series move together; imperfect correlation can support diversification.
27. **Current ratio** — Current assets divided by current liabilities, a measure of short-term liquidity.
28. **Current yield** — Annual coupon or income divided by current market price; it excludes maturity value and capital gain or loss.
29. **Custody** — Holding, directly or indirectly, client funds or securities or possessing authority that enables their withdrawal, as defined by applicable regulation.
30. **Debenture** — A corporate debt obligation generally supported by the issuer’s credit rather than a specific pledged asset.
31. **Debt-to-equity ratio** — A leverage measure comparing debt or liabilities, as defined, with owners’ equity.
32. **Deflation** — A sustained decline in the general price level, increasing money’s purchasing power but potentially accompanying weak demand and debt stress.
33. **Discounted cash flow (DCF)** — Valuation method converting expected future cash flows to present value at an appropriate discount rate.
34. **Diversification** — Combining investments so issuer- or asset-specific risks may offset; it does not guarantee profit or eliminate systematic risk.

35. **Dollar-cost averaging** — Investing equal dollar amounts at regular intervals; it buys more units at lower prices but neither assures profit nor prevents loss.
36. **Dollar-weighted return** — The internal rate of return on actual portfolio cash flows, reflecting their size and timing.
37. **Duration** — A measure of fixed-income price sensitivity to yield changes and, in Macaulay form, weighted timing of cash flows.
38. **Efficient-market hypothesis (EMH)** — Hypothesis that market prices reflect information to varying degrees—weak, semistrong, or strong—limiting persistent excess returns from that information set.
39. **Equity-indexed annuity** — An insurance contract crediting interest partly by reference to an index under caps, participation rates, spreads, floors, and other contract terms; it is not direct index ownership.
40. **ERISA** — Federal law setting fiduciary, reporting, disclosure, and other standards for many private employee benefit plans.
41. **Exempt security** — A security exempt from specified registration requirements because of instrument or issuer status; antifraud provisions still apply.
42. **Exempt transaction** — A particular transaction exempt from specified securities-registration requirements because of its circumstances; the security or persons are not necessarily broadly exempt.
43. **Expected return** — Probability-weighted average of possible returns, not a guaranteed result.
44. **Federal covered adviser** — An SEC-registered or otherwise federally covered adviser generally subject to federal registration and permitted state notice filing rather than state adviser registration.
45. **Fiduciary duty** — An adviser’s principles-based duty of care and loyalty, requiring advice in the client’s best interest, conflict management and disclosure, and faithful service.
46. **Fiscal policy** — Government use of spending and taxation to influence economic activity.
47. **Form ADV** — The principal investment-adviser registration and disclosure form, with parts covering the business, disciplinary matters, conflicts, fees, services, and brochure information.
48. **Form U4** — Uniform application used for securities-industry registration or transfer of individuals and disclosure reporting through registration systems.
49. **Forward contract** — A customized bilateral agreement to transact in an asset at a future date and agreed price, carrying counterparty and liquidity risk.
50. **Futures contract** — A standardized exchange-traded obligation for future delivery or cash settlement, subject to margin and daily marking-to-market.
51. **General-obligation bond** — Municipal debt supported primarily by the issuer’s taxing power, subject to legal limits and credit quality.
52. **Gross domestic product (GDP)** — Market value of final goods and services produced within a country over a period; a broad measure of economic output.
53. **Growth stock** — Stock of a company expected to grow earnings or revenue faster than a reference group, often with high valuation and expectation risk.
54. **Hedge fund** — A private pooled vehicle that may use flexible, concentrated, short, derivative, or leveraged strategies and often offers limited liquidity.
55. **Holding-period return** — Total income and change in value over a holding period divided by beginning value.
56. **Inflation** — Sustained increase in the general price level that erodes purchasing power and influences real returns and interest rates.
57. **Insider trading** — Securities trading or tipping in breach of a duty while aware of material nonpublic information, or other conduct prohibited by applicable law.
58. **Internal rate of return (IRR)** — Discount rate making the net present value of a cash-flow series equal zero; also the basis of dollar-weighted return.
59. **Investment adviser** — A person who, for compensation, is in the business of advising others about securities, subject to statutory exclusions and exemptions.
60. **Investment adviser representative (IAR)** — A natural person performing specified advisory, client-facing, solicitation, or supervisory functions for an adviser, as defined by applicable state/federal law.
61. **Investment policy statement (IPS)** — Written articulation of objectives, constraints, responsibilities, permissible investments, monitoring, and review; its legal role depends on context.
62. **Leveraged fund** — A fund seeking a multiple of an index’s stated, often daily, return; compounding and volatility can cause material long-term divergence.
63. **Limit order** — Order to buy at no more than, or sell at no less than, a specified price; price is controlled but execution is not guaranteed.
64. **Liquidity risk** — Risk that an asset cannot be sold promptly near a reasonable estimate of value or that obligations cannot be met without material cost.
65. **Market order** — Order seeking prompt execution at the best available price, without guaranteeing the execution price.
66. **Material nonpublic information (MNPI)** — Information not broadly disseminated that a reasonable investor would likely view as important or that could significantly affect price.
67. **Modern portfolio theory (MPT)** — Framework evaluating portfolios by expected return, variance, and covariance, emphasizing efficient combinations rather than isolated securities.
68. **Monetary policy** — Central-bank actions affecting money, credit, liquidity, and interest-rate conditions.

69. **Municipal bond** — Debt issued by a state or political subdivision, authority, or qualifying entity; interest may receive tax preferences, but credit and market risks remain.
70. **Net asset value (NAV)** — Per-share value of a pooled vehicle’s assets minus liabilities, usually calculated under prescribed valuation procedures.
71. **Net present value (NPV)** — Present value of future cash flows minus initial investment; a positive NPV indicates value above the selected required return under the assumptions.
72. **Notice filing** — State filing, document, consent-to-service, and fee requirements that may apply to federal covered advisers or securities without constituting full state registration.
73. **Open-end investment company** — Mutual fund that continuously issues and redeems shares at a price based on next-computed NAV, subject to charges and policies.
74. **Opportunity cost** — Value of the best alternative forgone by choosing one use of resources over another.
75. **Option premium** — Price paid by an option buyer and received by the writer for the contract rights and obligations.
76. **Passive management** — Strategy seeking to track an index or systematic exposure with limited discretionary security selection and generally lower turnover.
77. **Pay-to-play** — Practices linking political contributions to government advisory business; specific SEC, MSRB, FINRA, and state restrictions can impose bans and controls.
78. **Preferred stock** — Equity ordinarily having dividend and liquidation preference over common stock, often with limited voting rights and rate sensitivity.
79. **Present value** — Current equivalent of a future cash flow discounted at a required rate.
80. **Price-to-book ratio** — Market price per share divided by book value per share, interpreted in industry and accounting context.
81. **Price-to-earnings ratio** — Market price per share divided by earnings per share; sensitive to growth expectations, risk, accounting, and whether earnings are meaningful.
82. **Principal transaction** — Transaction in which an adviser acts for its own account opposite a client, creating a direct conflict and specific disclosure/consent duties.
83. **Private fund** — An issuer excluded from the Investment Company Act definition under provisions such as Sections 3(c)(1) or 3(c)(7), commonly with restricted participation and liquidity.
84. **Put option** — Contract giving the holder the right, but not obligation, to sell an underlying interest at a strike price under the option’s terms.
85. **Qualified domestic-relations order (QDRO)** — Domestic-relations order satisfying federal requirements to assign specified retirement-plan benefits to an alternate payee.
86. **Quick ratio** — Cash, marketable securities, and receivables divided by current liabilities; a liquidity measure excluding inventory.
87. **Real return** — Return after inflation; exactly $(1+\text{nominal return})/(1+\text{inflation})-1$.
88. **Rebalancing** — Restoring portfolio exposures toward policy targets by trading or directing cash flows, subject to costs and taxes.
89. **Regulation Best Interest (Reg BI)** — SEC conduct standard for broker-dealers and associated persons making recommendations to retail customers, distinct from an adviser’s fiduciary duty.
90. **REIT** — Real estate investment trust holding or financing real estate under statutory requirements; may be exchange-listed or non-traded and carries market, property, rate, management, and liquidity risks.
91. **Revenue bond** — Municipal bond primarily supported by revenue from a project, facility, enterprise, or pledged stream rather than general taxing power.
92. **Risk tolerance** — Client’s psychological willingness to accept uncertainty and loss, distinct from financial risk capacity.
93. **Sharpe ratio** — Portfolio excess return over the risk-free rate divided by total standard deviation.
94. **Soft dollars** — Brokerage arrangements involving research or services purchased with client commissions, subject to best execution, disclosure, conflicts, and statutory safe-harbor conditions.
95. **Standard deviation** — Statistical measure of dispersion around an average, commonly used as total return volatility.
96. **Stop order** — Order activated after a specified stop price is reached, generally becoming a market order and therefore not guaranteeing execution price.
97. **Strategic asset allocation** — Long-term policy allocation based on objectives and assumptions, generally maintained through rebalancing.
98. **Systematic risk** — Broad market risk that diversification cannot eliminate, including market-wide rate, inflation, and economic effects.
99. **Time-weighted return** — Geometrically linked subperiod returns designed to reduce the effect of external cash-flow timing.
100. **Unsystematic risk** — Issuer-, industry-, or asset-specific risk that can generally be reduced through diversification.

# Legal and Tax Currency Statement

**Currency date: August 11, 2026.** This manuscript is organized around NASAA’s September 1, 2023 Series 65 overview and public outline effective June 12, 2023, while its administrative discussion reflects official materials reviewed through August 11, 2026. The dated review does **not** represent that every statute, regulation, model rule, state adoption, tax threshold, retirement limit, age, filing form, fee, interpretation, or judicial decision remained unchanged on the reader’s later study or test date.

Tax and retirement rules are especially date-sensitive. Dollar limits, phaseouts, deductions, credits, required-minimum-distribution rules, early-distribution exceptions, education-account uses, estate/gift exclusions, and AMT treatment may change by tax year and taxpayer facts. Model acts and NASAA model rules have no force in a jurisdiction until adopted there, and a state may modify them. Federal and state definitions, de minimis rules, exemptions, notice filings, custody duties, marketing standards, privacy rules, continuing education, and registration procedures likewise require current source checking.

Before relying on a rule, verify the latest official text and effective date and consult an appropriately qualified professional. For the exam, use NASAA’s current outline and candidate guidance if either differs from this edition.
